===== ICLE-US-IND-0001.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an important topic and clearly conveys genuine emotional engagement with the Exxon Valdez disaster. Your passion for environmental accountability comes through strongly, which is a real strength. However, the essay currently reads more as a personal reflection/narrative than an academic argument. To strengthen it as an argumentative essay, you'll need to work on structure, evidence, and argumentative technique. Below are specific areas to address.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your opening claim—"Money has been said to be the root of all evil. I feel this statement is personally true"—is a strong emotional statement, but it's too broad and abstract to serve as a thesis for an argumentative essay. What exactly are you arguing? Possible options:

Exxon's negligence, not just an accident, caused the disaster
Corporations should be legally required to meet stricter environmental/safety standards
Financial penalties alone are insufficient punishment for environmental damage

Choose one clear, arguable claim and state it explicitly in your introduction. Right now, the essay drifts between narrating your personal experience, describing the disaster, and calling for policy change, without clearly signaling which is the central argument.

Evidence and Argumentation

This is the most significant area for improvement. An argumentative essay needs to do more than assert a position emotionally—it needs to support it with verifiable evidence and address possible counterarguments.

Missing citation: You reference Exxon's public statements about spill prevention but leave a placeholder  instead of an actual quote. This gap needs to be filled with a specific, sourced statement.
Vague sourcing: Phrases like "Exxon's notes and court transcriptions" need actual citations (dates, names, specific findings) so readers can verify your claims.
Statistics need context: The "11 million gallons" figure is good, but consider comparing it to something (eg, other spills, or gallons per mile of coastline affected) to help readers grasp its scale.
Consider counterarguments: A strong argumentative essay acknowledges opposing views. For example, did Exxon offer any justification for the delayed response? Even if you reject their reasoning, addressing it makes your argument more persuasive and academically credible.

Personal Narrative vs. Academic Argument

Passages like "While I laid in my living room the evening of the spill, I watched..." are vivid and show authentic emotion, but in academic writing, personal anecdote should generally *support* an argument, not *replace* evidence. Consider:

Keeping a brief personal anecdote in the introduction to establish your motivation for the topic
Shifting the body paragraphs toward factual analysis, evidence, and logical argumentation
Reserving emotional language for moments where it reinforces (not substitutes for) your evidence

Structure

Currently, the essay follows a chronological/narrative structure. For a clearer argumentative essay, consider organizing by point:

Introduction: State thesis clearly
Body Paragraph 1: Establish that the disaster was caused by negligence (not just bad luck)
Body Paragraph 2: Show that Exxon's response compounded the damage (PR failure, slow cleanup)
Body Paragraph 3: Address counterarguments (eg, "accidents happen," "regulations existed")
Body Paragraph 4: Present your proposed solutions (double hulling, corporate accountability funds, etc)
Conclusion: Restate thesis with a call to action

Language and Style Notes (for a German-speaking writer)

A few recurring issues to watch for:

False friends/direct translations: "rape our land" is very strong—make sure this word choice is intentional and appropriate for academic register, not simply a strong emotional translation from German.
Article usage: "the Exxon corporation," "the grounding of the Exxon" — check whether "Exxon" needs "the" before it (in English, company names usually don't take an article: "Exxon owns..." not "The Exxon corporation... Exxon owns").
Word order: "many wildlife organizations, concerned citizens and others asked the all important question to Exxon" — English often prefers "asked Exxon the all-important question." Watch for this verb-object-indirect object pattern, which differs from German syntax.
Repetition: "still is," "still felt," "still are devastating" appears multiple times in one paragraph. Vary your phrasing (eg, "persist," "linger," "remain evident").
Comma splices: Sentences like "After close to two years, Exxon finally made monetary amends..." are fine, but watch for run-ons elsewhere, eg, "The Exxon Valdez Tanker, poured over 11 million gallons..." — the comma after "Tanker" is unnecessary.

Next Steps

Write one clear thesis sentence and place it at the end of your introduction.
Fill in the missing quote/citation.
Add at least one counterargument and your rebuttal.
Revise structure so each paragraph has a clear argumentative purpose (not just narrative sequence).
Proofread for article usage and repetitive phrasing.

You have strong instincts for persuasive writing—now let's channel that passion into a more rigorously argued essay. I'd be happy to review a revised draft once you've reworked the structure and evidence.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0002.1.txt =====
Feedback on "Crime Does Not Pay" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a clear, debatable topic, and your central story (the businessman who scammed elderly couples) is vivid and could be very effective. However, the essay currently reads more like a narrative anecdote than an argumentative essay. To strengthen it, you need clearer argumentative structure, more developed reasoning, and some editing for grammar and word choice. Below are suggestions organized by priority.

Strengthen the Argumentative Structure

Right now, the essay has three paragraphs that function almost like a story with a moral, rather than an argument with claims and support. Consider this structure:

Introduction: State your thesis explicitly and early. You reference "the title of this essay" with a placeholder  — this suggests the sentence got lost in editing. Make sure your thesis ("Crime does not pay") appears clearly in your introduction, not just implied.
Body paragraphs: Each paragraph should defend the thesis with a *type* of evidence or reasoning, not just one long story. Right now paragraphs 2 and 3 are really one continuous narrative split in two. Consider instead:
  - One paragraph explaining *why* people choose crime (you do this in paragraph 1 — good).
  - One paragraph giving your extended example (the businessman).
  - One paragraph offering a *second*, different kind of support — perhaps a statistic, a general observation about recidivism, or a contrasting case — to show the argument isn't based on a single story.
  - A concluding paragraph that goes beyond restating "crime does not pay" and instead reflects on broader implications (eg, What does this suggest about how society should address the "get rich quick" mentality?).

Address the Counterargument

A strong argumentative essay acknowledges the other side. Some readers might think, "But some criminals *do* get away with it and live comfortably." Consider adding a sentence or short section acknowledging that some people avoid punishment, before explaining why this doesn't undermine your overall point (eg, they live with guilt, fear of being caught, damaged relationships, etc). This will make your argument more persuasive and mature.

Develop and Diversify Your Evidence

Your one extended example is engaging, but for a more convincing essay, avoid relying on a single personal anecdote as your *only* evidence. Ask yourself:
Could you add a brief second example (a different person, or a well-known case)?
Could you cite a general truth or statistic about the low success rate of white-collar crime, or the psychological toll on families?

This would make the essay feel less like "a story I know" and more like a supported argument.

Language and Grammar Notes

You write fluently overall, but here are patterns to watch for (common transfer issues from German):

Articles: English requires articles more consistently than German in certain contexts. Example: "see and easier way" → "see an easier way." Also check phrases like "in this catigory" (should be "category" — spelling) and "societies rules" → "society's rules" (apostrophe for possessive).
Word confusion (false friends / typos):
  - "expectable" → you likely mean "acceptable."
  - "will to go about" → "willing to go about."
  - "family way very proud" → "family was very proud" (likely a typo).
  - "beleive" → "believe."
  - "catigory" → "category."
Sentence-level clarity: "Most of the people that turn to crime are either fed up with societies rules for becoming rich and famous" — this sentence needs a second option after "either," since "either...or" requires two parts. You may have meant: "Most people who turn to crime are either fed up with society's rules for becoming rich, or they grew up around crime and see it as normal."
Tense consistency: You mostly use past tense for the narrative — good — but double-check shifts, eg, "His whole family was very happy, because they finally had all the things they wanted" is fine, but proofread for consistency throughout.
Comma splices/run-ons: Some sentences join independent clauses with only a comma (eg, "This went on for about a year. The one day..." — should be "for about a year. Then one day..."). Watch for "The one day" — likely a translation issue; in English this should be "One day."

Next Steps

Rewrite your introduction with an explicit, complete thesis statement.
Reorganize body paragraphs so each has a distinct argumentative purpose.
Add a short paragraph addressing a counterargument.
Proofread carefully for spelling (catigory, beleive) and grammar (articles, possessives, run-ons).
Consider adding one more piece of evidence beyond the single anecdote.

You have strong instincts for storytelling — now let's channel that into a tighter, more persuasive argumentative structure. Good work overall!

===== ICLE-US-IND-0003.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Essay

Overall Impression

This is a thoughtful, intellectually ambitious essay that tackles a genuinely difficult question about how societies assign value to labor. Your central insight—that the debate over "equal pay for equal work" often masks deeper unexamined assumptions about which societal functions we consider valuable—is sophisticated and worth developing. The choice of childcare work as a case study is effective and concrete. However, the essay would benefit from clearer structural signposting, a more decisive conclusion, and attention to some recurring grammatical patterns.

Argument and Structure

Strengths: You successfully move from an abstract claim (paragraph 1) to a concrete example (paragraph 2) to an economic argument (paragraph 3). This is a logical progression.

Areas to strengthen:

The placeholder  – Remember to insert your actual thesis statement here before submitting. Right now the essay begins referring to "the idea" without stating what that idea is.

Define your key terms early. You introduce the crucial distinction between "economic measures" and "measures of status" only in paragraph 2. This framework is central to your entire argument and should probably be established in paragraph 1, so readers know from the start how you're defining "value."

The conclusion is circular in a way that may confuse readers. You end by saying childcare workers won't be seen as valuable until their pay improves—but earlier you argued that low pay is a *symptom* of society not recognizing their value. This creates a chicken-and-egg problem. If you intend this circularity as your point (ie, "this is a vicious cycle with no easy entry point"), say so explicitly. Otherwise, it reads as if the essay contradicts its own logic. Consider a sentence like: *"This creates a self-reinforcing cycle: low status leads to low pay, and low pay reinforces low status—making intervention difficult but not impossible."*

The claim "It is not difficult to conclude that child care workers are underpaid" is followed immediately by acknowledging the difficulty of resolving the issue. Consider rephrasing to avoid the apparent contradiction: perhaps "the underpayment of child care workers is easy to diagnose, but difficult to remedy."

Paragraph 3's connection between workforce diversity and childcare value is compressed. Spell out the causal chain more explicitly: women and men with families can only participate fully in the labor force *if* reliable childcare exists; therefore childcare enables the broader economy to function. Right now the reader has to do some inferential work to reconstruct this logic.

Language and Style

Your English is strong, but there are some patterns worth noting, some of which may reflect German sentence structure:

"profess to the belief" → should be either "profess the belief" or "profess to believe." ("Profess to" takes a verb, not a noun phrase.)

"pereniel" → spelling error: "perennial."

"as well as man with family responsibilities" → should be "men." Also consider rephrasing for parallelism: "will inevitably include women of all ages as well as men with family responsibilities."

Long, embedded subordinate clauses: Sentences like "If we recognize that a work force filled with such diversity of workers will inevitably include women – of all ages – as well as man with family responsibilities, then we see the undeniable economic connection..." are grammatically correct but very dense. This kind of nested clause structure (common in German academic writing) can overwhelm English readers. Consider breaking such sentences into two: state the premise, then state the conclusion separately.

"without involving employers or the government in the child care industry" – this phrasing is a bit ambiguous; it's unclear whether you mean the issue *cannot* be resolved without such involvement, or that resolving it *would require* such involvement, which you view as undesirable. Clarify your stance here.

Watch comma usage around parenthetical phrases—you use dashes and commas somewhat interchangeably (eg, "of all ages –" vs. surrounding commas). English typically prefers consistency: choose either dashes or commas for a given parenthetical insertion, not a mix.

Suggestions for Revision

Insert and clarify your thesis statement.
Move your economic/status distinction earlier.
Break up 2–3 of the longest sentences into shorter, clearer units.
Resolve or explicitly frame the circularity in your conclusion.
Proofread for the specific errors noted above (profess, perennial, man/men).
Consider adding one sentence near the end that offers even a tentative path forward (eg, subsidies, public campaigns, unionization) — right now the essay ends on a fairly pessimistic note without gesturing toward what "consciously raising status" might look like in practice.

Overall: This is strong analytical writing with a compelling core argument. With some structural tightening and a few grammatical corrections, this could be a very persuasive essay.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0004.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Essay: "The Value of Homemaking Labor"

Overall Impression

This is a thoughtful and timely essay that tackles a genuinely difficult question: how should society value unpaid domestic labor? Your writing shows strong critical thinking and a good ear for English rhythm and idiom. The essay would benefit most from tightening the logical structure, sharpening the thesis, and cleaning up a few grammatical issues common for German-speaking writers of English.

Thesis and Argument

Your thesis—that "home-making service" deserves some form of compensation—is clear in the introduction, but it becomes less clear by the end. The final paragraph shifts from "compensation should be made" to a much more tentative proposal ("Perhaps this is a solution worth its salt"). This softening weakens your argument. Decide whether your essay is *arguing for* a specific solution (joint paychecks) or *exploring* the problem without committing to an answer. Right now it reads as the latter, despite an introduction that promises the former. I'd suggest either (a) committing to the joint-paycheck idea and defending it more fully, addressing objections, or (b) reframing your thesis as an exploration of the *problem* of valuation, with the paycheck idea offered as one possible avenue rather than a resolution.

Structure and Development

The essay's organization is logical—problem, complication ("what is equal work?"), historical context, and proposed solution—but each section needs more development to feel complete:

Paragraph 2 asserts that career jobs "deserve" compensation but doesn't connect this claim back to your thesis. Consider explicitly stating why this matters: if marketplace jobs are valued by dollar amount, what does that logic imply about unpaid labor?
Paragraph 3 raises an excellent question (comparing a parent's 25 years of child-rearing to a neurosurgeon's 25 years of work) but doesn't pursue the comparison. What *would* fair compensation look like? You gesture at $50,000/year but drop it immediately. Push this thought further—even a rough calculation or comparison to existing caregiver wages (nannies, daycare workers) would strengthen your evidence.
Paragraph 5 introduces a compelling distinction (is the problem the culture's ambivalence about gender roles, or its market-based value system?) but leaves both possibilities unresolved. Choose one as your primary explanation, or explain how they interact.
Final paragraph: This is where the essay needs the most work. The "joint paycheck" idea is interesting but appears only in the last two sentences and isn't argued for—only floated as a question. Give it a full paragraph: how would it work practically? What problems could it solve, and what new problems might it create (eg, for unmarried homemakers, whom you mention in passing but then abandon)?

Evidence and Support

You make strong claims ("many of the ills of society... have been traced by study after study") but do not cite any actual studies. Even a general reference (a named researcher, organization, or report) would substantially strengthen your credibility. Right now, this sentence reads as an assertion rather than a supported claim.

Language and Style Notes

A few specific issues to address:

"An neurosurgeon" → should be "a neurosurgeon" (article error before consonant sound).
"He or she who works outside the home" — this phrasing is grammatically correct but stylistically awkward in English; consider simplifying to "those who work outside the home."
"It, of course, the worker is married" — this sentence is unclear; I believe you mean "if the worker is married." Revise for clarity.
"Doctors, lawyers and Indian chiefs" — this alludes to an old English-language children's rhyme ("Rich man, poor man, beggar man, thief; doctor, lawyer, Indian chief"), but the phrase can read as culturally insensitive to contemporary American readers and may distract from your argument. Consider replacing it with a more neutral example.
Idiom check: "worth its salt" is usually applied to people or things proving their value through performance (eg, "any doctor worth their salt would know this"), not typically to abstract solutions. Consider "a solution worth considering" instead.
Watch for comma splices and long sentences (eg, the final sentence of paragraph 5)—breaking these into two sentences will improve readability.

Suggestions for Revision

Sharpen your thesis statement so the ending matches its ambition.
Develop the "joint paycheck" proposal into a fuller paragraph, addressing at least one objection.
Add at least one concrete source or statistic to support your claims about societal effects of unpaid caregiving.
Proofread for article usage and sentence-length control, both common trouble spots for German-English writers, since German doesn't require articles in all the same contexts.

This essay tackles an ambitious and important question—your instincts about where the argument should go are good. The next step is following through on the ideas you raise rather than gesturing toward them.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0005.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Essay: "Money as the Root of All Evil"

Overall Impression

This is a genuinely interesting philosophical exercise—you're testing a well-known proverb against logical scrutiny, which is a sophisticated approach to argumentative writing. Your instinct to treat the saying as a claim that can be falsified through counterexample and logical extension (rather than simply agreeing or disagreeing) shows real analytical ambition. However, the essay currently reads as more of a philosophical exploration in progress than a finished, persuasive argument. Below are areas to strengthen in revision.

Argument and Structure

The core logic needs to be made more explicit. Your central move—turning the saying into a testable hypothesis ("if money causes evil, poverty should correlate with virtue")—is clever, but you move through several sub-arguments (desire vs. wealth, happiness as contentment, virtue as happiness's "counterpart") very quickly and without clear signposting. A reader unfamiliar with your reasoning will struggle to follow the chain: money→evil becomes money→desire→evil, then happiness→virtue, then desire-for-money vs. desire-for-virtue as competing forces. Each of these substitutions is a significant philosophical move that deserves its own sentence of justification, not just a claim of "must" or "would."

Watch unsupported logical necessity. Phrases like "would-must-coincide" or "It is not subtle argument, but the unfolding of history" assert conclusions rather than earning them. In academic argument, especially in English-language conventions, claims of logical necessity ("must," "immediately," "clearly") should follow demonstrated reasoning, not substitute for it. Consider walking the reader through *why* desire for money and desire for virtue would need to coincide, rather than declaring it.

There is a placeholder () left in the text. This is presumably where you intended to insert a sentence stating your alternative to the proverb—likely the most important sentence in the essay, since the next sentence refers to "a truer statement." Please make sure to complete this before submission; right now the essay's central thesis is literally missing.

The ending is strong in tone but abrupt in logic. Your final paragraph pivots to a historical claim (the powerful are "just as often the oppressors as saviors") that is compelling but arrives without transition. Consider adding a sentence that explicitly connects the abstract argument about desire/happiness to this historical observation—right now they feel like two separate essays sutured together.

Language and Style

Given that English is your second language, most of your sentence structures are ambitious and sophisticated—arguably more complex than many native speakers attempt. But this complexity sometimes obscures meaning. A few patterns to watch:

Subjunctive mood: "If money was the root of all evil" should be "If money were the root of all evil" (hypothetical/counterfactual subjunctive). This appears in your opening sentence and sets the tone for the whole piece.

Spelling errors (likely from typing speed rather than knowledge gaps): "privelaged" → *privileged*; "acruement" → *accruement* (or better: *accumulation*); "wheighed" → *weighed*; "serrendipitously" → *serendipitously*. A careful proofreading pass will catch these.

Overly compressed noun phrases, a common feature transferred from German academic style, sometimes make English sentences harder to parse. For example: "since desire—much less evil—cannot be appropriated objectively and weighed against financial aptitude" packs multiple abstract nouns into one clause. English readers often prefer these ideas unpacked across two sentences: *Desire cannot be measured objectively. Even less so can it be weighed against wealth.*

Word choice precision: "financial aptitude" is a puzzling substitute for "wealth" or "financial status"—*aptitude* suggests skill or talent, not accumulated money. Similarly, "counterpart" (virtue as happiness's counterpart) is vague; do you mean *equivalent*, *cause*, or *symptom*? Precision here will strengthen your logic, not just your prose.

Punctuation and dashes: Your em-dashes are used effectively for parenthetical asides ("desire—much less evil—cannot..."), which is a strong stylistic choice. Keep this, but ensure consistent spacing/formatting.

Suggestions for Revision

Fill in the missing thesis statement where the placeholder appears.
Add explicit transitional sentences between the four major logical moves (desire/wealth correlation → happiness as contentment → competing desires → historical conclusion).
Do a dedicated proofreading pass for spelling and subjunctive verb forms.
Consider whether "happiness" and "virtue" need clearer definitions upfront, since your entire second half depends on this specific, non-obvious equation.

You have a genuinely original argument here—it just needs firmer scaffolding so your reader can follow you to the conclusion you've already reached. Well done on tackling such an abstract topic with real philosophical seriousness.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0006.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Essay

Thank you for sharing this thought-provoking piece on Marx's egalitarian principle and its application to family, religion, and individualism. You raise genuinely interesting questions, but the essay needs revision in several key areas before its argument will be fully persuasive to an academic reader.

Missing Quotations (Critical Issue)

The placeholders () appear three times where quotations should be—Marx's famous principle, a New Testament passage, and the "giving back to society" idea. These must be filled in before the essay can be evaluated. Without the actual Marx quotation ("From each according to his ability, to each according to his needs," presumably), your reader cannot follow your reasoning, since your entire argument depends on unpacking this specific phrase. Please insert the exact quotations, with proper citations, in your revision.

Unclear Thesis and Argumentative Structure

Right now, the essay reads as a loosely connected sequence of observations rather than a sustained argument. Ask yourself: what is my *one* central claim? Your final paragraph—that Marx's egalitarian ideal is "admirable" but unachievable in Western culture—seems to be your thesis, but it arrives too late and isn't clearly signaled at the start. 

Suggestion: State this thesis explicitly in your introduction. Then, restructure the body paragraphs so each one visibly supports it. Currently, the family and religion paragraphs *seem* to support the idea that egalitarianism already exists naturally in these institutions—but then the essay pivots to argue that egalitarianism *fails* at the societal level. The relationship between these two claims (small-scale success vs. large-scale failure) needs to be made explicit. Are you arguing that egalitarianism works in intimate, kin-based contexts but breaks down when abstracted to "society" as a whole? If so, say this directly and use it as your organizing thread.

Underdeveloped Transitions

Several paragraphs shift abruptly:
The move from family (paragraph 2) to religious institutions (paragraph 3) needs a bridging sentence explaining *why* these are analogous examples.
The jump to "moral relativism" in the second-to-last paragraph feels like a new, third argument rather than a natural continuation of the "individual vs. society" tension you raised earlier. Consider merging these two ideas or clarifying how relativism specifically *worsens* the tension between individual freedom and collective good.

Argument Development

Your rhetorical questions in paragraph 4 ("who is to define what contribution merits what reward?") are effective, but they need to be followed by your own tentative answers, not left open. Right now the essay poses several sharp questions but doesn't fully commit to resolving them, which weakens the persuasive force. Even a provisional answer ("I contend that...") would strengthen your position.

Similarly, the claim "the individual is paramount in the post-modern world" is asserted without support. A brief example (a philosopher, a cultural trend, a statistic) would make this claim more convincing to an academic reader.

Language and Grammar Notes

As a non-native English speaker, your prose is generally strong, but here are some specific corrections:

"on e would understand" → "one would understand" (typo)
"who it to define" → "who is to define"
"learned primarily form" → "learned primarily *from*"
"Absent a deviation from our general nature to propagate" — this phrase is grammatically correct but very dense; consider simplifying to something like: "Unless we deviate from our natural tendency to propagate and extend ourselves through our offspring..."
Watch article usage ("the individual," "an affluent society")—minor but recurring issue typical for German speakers, since German doesn't always require articles where English does. Reading your paragraphs aloud can help you catch these.
"its own" (in "protect, provide and nurture its own") should probably be "their own," since "family" as a collective noun pairs more naturally with "their" in this context.

Conclusion

Your final line is compelling but could be stronger with a forward-looking or reflective final sentence—what does this failure mean for how we *should* think about social ethics, given that pure egalitarianism seems unattainable? This would give the essay a more satisfying close, rather than ending on a somewhat resigned note.

Overall

You have a genuinely engaging philosophical question at the heart of this essay. Your task now is to (1) restore the missing quotations, (2) sharpen and foreground your thesis, (3) strengthen the logical connections between paragraphs, and (4) polish minor grammatical issues. I'd be happy to look at a revised draft once these changes are made.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0007.1.txt =====
Feedback on "Does Crime Pay?"

Overall Impression

This is a genuinely interesting and original essay. Your central metaphor—treating crime as a business with investments, overhead, and risk—is clever and sustained effectively throughout. The writing shows good analytical thinking, and your English is strong overall, with only minor, typical L1-interference issues. Below are suggestions to strengthen both the argument and the prose.

Content and Argument

Strengthen your thesis. Right now, the essay reads more as an exploration than an argument. Your opening paragraph promises to "determine whether crime pays," but the conclusion ends with "In some cases it may"—a fair and nuanced answer, but one that needs to be stated as your actual thesis from the start. Consider revising your introduction to something like: *"Whether crime pays depends less on the size of the payout than on whether the criminal correctly calculates the full cost of doing business—and most fail to do so."* This gives your reader a clear claim to track through the essay, rather than a question they must wait to see answered.

Add concrete evidence. Your reasoning is logical, but it rests entirely on hypothetical illustrations (the jewelry store example, the "if a criminal is well educated" scenario). For an academic essay, even one that is somewhat exploratory, you would benefit from at least one or two real-world references—criminology studies on recidivism and earnings, statistics on average "returns" from specific crimes (burglary, drug dealing), or a cited case. Steven Levitt and Sudhir Venkatesh's well-known study on the economics of drug dealing gangs would fit your argument perfectly and would lend the essay academic credibility beyond the extended metaphor.

Develop the ending, don't just joke with it. The final line ("he does better than the legitimate businessman on one count: no taxes") is witty, but it appears out of nowhere and undercuts the seriousness of your argument just as you reach your conclusion. Either develop this point earlier as one more piece of your cost-benefit analysis (untaxed income is a real economic advantage worth analyzing seriously), or replace the ending with a stronger synthesis of your main claim.

Structure and Organization

Your paragraph structure is logical: capital loss → time investment → equipment/overhead → health loss → legal loss → conclusion. This works well. Two suggestions:

Signal transitions more explicitly. Phrases like "Another type of loss to be considered" are functional but repetitive. Vary your transitions to show *relationships* between ideas (eg, "Beyond material costs, a second category of loss concerns...").
Consider merging the legal-problems paragraph with the health paragraph, since you explicitly say it's "both a capital loss and a health related loss"—this suggests it may not need its own separate category, and merging it could tighten the essay.

Language and Style

Your English is impressively fluent for this level of abstract argument. A few specific corrections:

Spelling: "equiptment" → *equipment*
Missing comparative word: "he does better the legitimate businessman" → *he does better than the legitimate businessman*
Article use (a known German-English challenge): Watch definite/indefinite articles with abstract nouns. eg, "Crime, like any small business, requires an investment" is correct, but later, "Legal problems could become both a capital loss and a health related loss" would read more naturally as "*a* health-related loss" (you have this correct, but double-check hyphenation: *health-related* needs a hyphen as a compound adjective).
Comma splices/run-ons: Your sentences are sometimes long, joining multiple clauses with commas rather than periods or semicolons. For example, the first sentence of the essay is quite dense—consider breaking it into two sentences for clarity: one that sets up the hypothetical, and one that delivers the financial tally.
Word choice: "criminal work" is slightly unusual phrasing in English; "criminal enterprise" or simply "crime" would sound more natural in an academic register.
Consistency of tense/register: You shift between general present tense (correct for argument) and more narrative, hypothetical constructions ("If a person gains...cuts his hand..."). This is fine, but make sure each hypothetical is clearly marked as illustrative, not anecdotal, so the reader doesn't expect a real case.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

State your thesis explicitly and clearly in the introduction.
Add at least one piece of real-world evidence or research to support your economic analysis.
Reconsider the placement and development of your "no taxes" point.
Proofread for the specific errors noted above (equipment, comparative "than," hyphenation).
Vary paragraph transitions to avoid repetitive phrasing.

You have a strong conceptual foundation here—the "crime as business" framework is compelling and original. With a sharper thesis and some outside evidence, this essay could move from a clever thought experiment to a genuinely persuasive academic argument.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0008.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay engages with an interesting philosophical distinction—between money itself and the *love* of money—and your moral stance comes through clearly. However, the essay currently reads more like a series of restated convictions than a developed argument. To strengthen it, you need a clearer structure, more concrete evidence, and careful editing of grammar and word choice. Below are specific suggestions organized by category.

---

Introduction and Thesis

The essay opens with "This idea is completely erroneous," but the reader has no idea what "idea" you are referring to. Since this seems to be a response to a quotation or prompt (likely the biblical saying "money is the root of all evil" vs. "the love of money is the root of all evil"), you must state that prompt explicitly in your first sentence. For example:

> "It is often said that 'money is the root of all evil.' This claim, however, is only partly correct. It is not money itself but rather the *love* of money that is truly destructive."

This gives your reader the context needed to follow your argument and turns your first paragraph into a clear thesis statement rather than an unexplained reaction.

---

Structure and Development

Right now, the essay repeats its central claim (money is neutral; love of money corrupts) four or five times in slightly different words, but each repetition adds little new evidence or reasoning. Academic essays in English are usually built on distinct, well-developed points, each supported by a specific example.

Consider restructuring around three clear body paragraphs, each with a topic sentence:
Paragraph 1: Define money's legitimate function (trade, storage of value, generosity).
Paragraph 2: Explain how the love of money differs from money itself, using one specific, developed example (a real or realistic case of a family, a company, or a public figure) rather than the vague "How many times have we seen on the evening news…" You mention several examples (family, company, nation, child) but none is explored in depth. Choose one or two and explain the *mechanism*—how desire escalates into harmful behavior.
Paragraph 3: Address the cross-cultural point (dollar, yen, peseta) as evidence that this is a universal human tendency, not a cultural flaw—this is a good idea, but it needs to be connected explicitly back to your thesis.

End with a conclusion that *synthesizes* rather than repeats your opening claim—perhaps reflecting on what this distinction implies for how individuals or societies should relate to wealth.

---

Evidence and Persuasiveness

Much of your argument relies on rhetorical assertion ("this love of money urges them on…") rather than demonstration. Strengthen your credibility by:
Referencing a real example (a historical case, a study on wealth and behavior, or even a well-known corporate scandal).
Avoiding vague generalizations like "our society pays dearly" without specifying *how*.

---

Grammar and Word Choice

Since English and German differ significantly in article use, spelling conventions, and pronoun capitalization, please review these specific errors:

"capitivates" → *captivates*
"sued" → *used*
"it's needs" → *its needs* (its = possessive; it's = it is)
"he begins to hide it" → capitalize *He*
"all together" → *altogether* (meaning "entirely")
"is money the root of all evil? No." → capitalize *Is* at the start of the sentence.
"The love of whatever represents the financial exchange between parties wreaks havoc" — this sentence is grammatically fine but stylistically clunky; simplify to "The love of whatever currency represents financial exchange can wreak havoc..."

I recommend reading your essay aloud, sentence by sentence, to catch these small but noticeable errors. A grammar-checking tool (eg, Grammarly) can also help catch capitalization and possessive/contraction mistakes, though it won't fix structural issues.

---

Tone and Register

Rhetorical questions ("How many times have we seen…") and biblical/moralizing language ("captivates their minds," "root of all evil") give the essay a sermon-like tone. This can work in a personal essay, but for an academic argumentative essay, aim for a more analytical register—explain *why* and *how* your claims are true, rather than simply asserting them with emotional emphasis.

---

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Add a clear introductory context/thesis statement.
Reorganize into distinct paragraphs, each with one focused idea and supporting evidence.
Replace vague generalizations with at least one concrete, developed example.
Proofread carefully for capitalization, possessives, and word choice errors.
Adjust tone toward analytical rather than rhetorical/moralizing.

With these revisions, your essay will move from a passionate personal reflection toward a well-structured, persuasive academic argument. You have a strong central idea—now it needs a clearer framework and stronger evidence to support it.

Feedback on Your Essay: "Crime Does Not Pay"

Overall Impression

This is a thoughtful and engaging essay with a clear argumentative trajectory: you take a common cultural saying, question its validity, and marshal evidence to refute it. Your examples are varied and interesting, and the final line offers a memorable reframing ("wishful thinking" vs. "truism"). With some revision to structure, evidence, and language, this could be a strong piece of argumentative writing.

Content and Argument

Strengths:
Your organizing logic is sound: you move from individual criminals (murderers, white-collar offenders) to systemic incentives (the incarceration industry), which shows an ability to think about the issue on multiple levels.
The behavioral psychology angle ("Behavior that is not reinforced in some way will not persist") is a nice analytical move—it gives your argument a theoretical anchor beyond anecdote.

Areas to strengthen:

Verify your facts. The stockbroker's name is *Michael Milken*, not "Milliken." This kind of error undermines credibility on a point central to your argument. Similarly, "Richard Nixon... has died" – check your tense consistency and verify the "statesman" claim with a citation. Since this essay relies heavily on factual claims (15 years average sentence, $23/month wages, Indiana costs), you need sources. Where did you read these statistics? Without citations, a skeptical reader may doubt the evidence.

Define your terms early. You use "crime" to cover murder, financial fraud, and political corruption—very different categories with different incentive structures. Consider addressing this range explicitly: is your claim that crime *never* pays, or that it pays *disproportionately* for certain kinds of offenders (wealthy, white-collar, well-connected)? Right now the Nixon and Milken examples suggest a more specific and interesting thesis: that crime pays especially for the privileged, while punishment falls hardest on the poor (your opening bank-robber example gestures at this but you don't return to it).

Address the counterargument. A strong argumentative essay anticipates objections. Someone might respond: "But most criminals *are* caught and punished severely—Milken and Nixon are exceptions, not the rule." You should acknowledge this and explain why the exceptions matter, or provide evidence that they are more common than assumed.

The conclusion is underdeveloped. Your final paragraph is only one sentence. Given the richness of your evidence, expand this to explain the implications of your claim. Why does it matter if this phrase is "wishful thinking"? Does this affect how we should design punishment or justice policy?

Language and Style

Since you're writing in your second language, here are some specific points, including a few interference patterns from German:

Word order/phrasing: "It seems that a more cold-blooded statistician than I am could calculate..." – this is a bit convoluted. Consider simplifying: "A more cold-blooded statistician than I could probably calculate whether murder is 'cost-effective' if the only cost is fifteen years."

"after, not only saves" – this sentence has a typo or missing word ("after all," perhaps?). Please proofread carefully for such gaps, as they interrupt reading.

Article usage: English articles are notoriously tricky for German speakers since German's "der/die/das" system doesn't map directly onto "the/a/—". Watch for phrases like "such incarceration has become big business" vs. your correct "Incarceration has become big business" (good instinct here, but double-check throughout).

The placeholder "" should presumably be replaced by the actual phrase you're analyzing (eg, "Crime doesn't pay"). Make sure your final draft states it explicitly and perhaps in quotation marks, since your whole essay depends on the reader knowing exactly what saying you're interrogating.

Register: Your tone is appropriately academic-informal, suitable for this kind of essay. Phrases like "penniless" and "scammed the public" work well. Just ensure consistency—"Miscreants" is a nice word choice, but check it fits the overall register of the piece.

Sentence variety: You have a good mix of short punchy sentences ("If, however, crime does not pay, then why does it persist?") and longer explanatory ones. This is a real strength—keep cultivating it.

Concrete Next Steps

Fact-check and cite your sources (Milken's name, prison statistics, Indiana costs).
Sharpen your thesis: is this about crime in general, or about a *class-based* double standard in how "paying" for crime works?
Add a paragraph acknowledging counterevidence (most criminals are punished).
Expand your conclusion to explore the implications of your claim.
Proofread carefully for typos and missing words.

This essay has real potential—the core insight (that the "truism" masks inequities in how crime is punished) is compelling. Focus your revision on making that insight more explicit and better supported.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Thank you for sharing this thoughtful essay on effort and reward. You raise an interesting philosophical question, and your writing shows genuine engagement with the topic. Below is feedback organized by area, with suggestions for revision.

Argument and Structure

Your thesis — that financial reward should correspond to individual effort — is stated clearly in the introduction, which is good. However, the essay would benefit from a stronger analytical structure. Currently, the essay moves chronologically (past → present → hypothetical future) rather than building a logical case with reasons and evidence. Consider organizing paragraphs around specific *reasons* supporting your thesis (eg, "Reward motivates innovation," "Equal outcomes discourage effort," "Historical patterns confirm effort-based reward works"), rather than around time periods. This would make your argument more persuasive and easier to follow.

Additionally, the essay currently relies heavily on hypothetical reasoning ("Let's imagine...", "I would venture to guess...") rather than concrete evidence. For an academic argumentative essay, try to include specific examples — historical, economic, or sociological — to support your claims. For instance, you could reference real economic systems, studies on motivation, or historical case studies of societies with different reward structures. This will strengthen your credibility as a writer and move the essay beyond speculation.

Development of Ideas

The middle paragraphs (paragraphs 2–3) largely restate the same idea: "harder work equals greater reward." Consider what *new* insight each paragraph should add. Right now, paragraph 2 and 3 both conclude with nearly the same sentence. Try to make each paragraph build on the previous one — for example, paragraph 2 establishes the historical pattern, and paragraph 3 could then explain *why* this pattern persists despite technological change, rather than simply reasserting it.

Your fourth paragraph (the hypothetical equal society) is the most engaging part of the essay, but it also introduces strong claims — "the world would become divided or even destroyed" — without support. Consider tempering this claim or grounding it with a real-world parallel (eg, a historical example of centrally planned economies, or research on motivation and productivity).

The conclusion introduces a new and interesting idea — that reward isn't really about money but about meeting needs and wants — but this shift happens quite late and isn't fully developed. This could actually be a compelling thesis on its own. Consider whether this idea should be foreshadowed earlier in the essay, perhaps even replacing your original thesis, since it seems to be your most original insight.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

A few specific corrections to note:

"men and women should be financially reward" → should be *"financially rewarded"* (missing past participle -ed).
"took up of majority of each person's day" → *"took up the majority of each person's day"* or more naturally, *"took up most of each person's day."*
"We have many machines to held us meet our...needs" → likely a typo for *"help us meet."*
"Competitiveness and jealously would set in" → *"jealousy"* (not "jealously," which is an adverb).
"as advanced of a society as we are today" → In English, this construction should be *"as advanced a society as we are today"* (no "of").
"Persons with great technical minds are using their talent on the job and paying someone to do their labor." — This sentence is unclear; consider revising to state your meaning more directly, eg, *"People with specialized skills now use their talents professionally while paying others to perform manual labor for them."*

These are common patterns for German speakers transferring word order or prepositional structures (eg, "as...of a..." constructions, or verb-final tendencies in subordinate clauses) into English. I'd recommend reading your sentences aloud slowly — awkward phrasing often becomes audible even when it isn't visible.

Style Suggestions

Your voice is engaged and sincere, which is a strength — but for academic argumentative writing, try to reduce first-person hedging phrases like *"I feel,"* *"I don't think so,"* and *"I would venture to guess."* These weaken your authority as a writer. Instead of "I feel it is vital that...", try more assertive academic phrasing: *"This essay argues that..."* or *"It is essential that..."*

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Reorganize paragraphs around reasons, not chronology.
Add concrete evidence (examples, data, or references) to support claims.
Avoid repeating the same concluding idea across paragraphs — ensure each paragraph adds something new.
Consider developing your final insight (reward vs. needs/wants) earlier and more fully — it may be your strongest idea.
Proofread for grammar (verb forms, prepositions, and unclear sentences noted above).
Adopt a more assertive academic tone by minimizing personal hedging.

You have a strong instinct for argument and an interesting philosophical instinct — with revision focused on structure and evidence, this essay could become much more persuasive. Well done, and I look forward to seeing your revised draft!

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Thank you for sharing this thought-provoking draft. You are grappling with a genuinely difficult question—whether compensation can or should reflect "contribution to society"—and you bring in interesting historical and contemporary examples. However, the essay currently reads more like a series of loosely connected reflections than a tightly argued essay. Below are suggestions organized by area, followed by some specific language notes.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your first paragraph raises an interesting claim (compensation cannot equal "contribution to society" because contribution is subjective), but this thesis gets lost. By the final paragraph, you seem to arrive at a related but distinct conclusion: that we cannot fairly redistribute pay according to "contribution" without infringing on personal liberty. These are related ideas, but the essay needs one clear, arguable thesis stated early and revisited throughout. Ask yourself: Is your main claim (a) that contribution-based pay is impossible because contribution is subjective, or (b) that pay inequality is unavoidable in a society that values individual freedom? Choose one framing and make it your spine.

Organization and Paragraph Unity

Right now, each paragraph introduces a new example (1920s investors, migrant workers, inherited wealth, doctors) without clearly linking back to your thesis or to each other. Readers can lose track of your argument. Consider:

Adding a topic sentence to each paragraph that explicitly ties the example to your central claim.
Using transitional phrases ("This raises the question of...", "A similar problem arises when...") to show *why* you are moving from one example to the next.
The question "But do they have the right to live this way?" in paragraph 3 is compelling, but it appears without being resolved. If you pose a question, either answer it in the same paragraph or clearly flag that you will return to it.

Development and Evidence

Several claims need more support:

You mention "several great authors" who questioned 1920s investors. Name them (Fitzgerald? Sinclair Lewis?) and give a brief, specific reference. Vague appeals to authority weaken your argument.
The doctor example is a nice concrete illustration, but it appears suddenly in the final paragraph and needs to be connected explicitly to your thesis about subjectivity of contribution.
Consider addressing a counterargument more fully: someone might argue that contribution *can* be measured (eg, via market value, education level, danger of the job). Engaging with this seriously would strengthen your case.

The Conclusion

Your final paragraph tries to do the work of both evidence and conclusion at once, and the very last sentence ("we cannot give pay equal to contribution to society") arrives abruptly, without a clear synthesis of the essay's various threads (1920s wealth, migrant workers, democracy, doctors). Consider ending with a paragraph that explicitly restates your refined thesis and briefly reviews how each example supported it.

Language and Grammar Notes

Since English is not your first language, here are some patterns to watch for (many of these reflect German sentence structures being carried into English):

Articles: "In the 1920s in American" → "In the 1920s in America." German often omits articles where English requires them ("die 1920er" vs. "the 1920s")—double-check article usage throughout.
Word order/verb placement: "The answer this question lies in..." → "The answer to this question lies in..." (missing preposition, common German-to-English pitfall since German prepositions attach differently).
Spelling: "Democracry" → "Democracy."
Subject-verb agreement: "yet none of the doctors are lowering" → "yet none of the doctors is lowering" (or restructure: "yet doctors are not lowering their fees").
Word confusion: "make much too money" → "make too much money."
Punctuation with quotation marks: "equally'" — check that opening and closing quotation marks match (you used a straight closing quote after "equally," but no matching opening mark, and it's a single quote rather than double).
Comma splices: "They received money, but they contributed nearly nothing to society" is fine, but watch for other run-on constructions, eg, "If one has preconceived notions concerning one's salary, one will certainly be disappointed since some employers are sure to value..." — this is grammatically correct but quite dense; consider breaking into two sentences for clarity.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Sharpen your thesis into one clear, arguable sentence and place it at the end of your introduction.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one explicitly connects to that thesis via topic sentences and transitions.
Support your claims with named sources/examples rather than vague references.
Write a conclusion that synthesizes your argument rather than introducing a new example at the last moment.
Proofread carefully for articles, prepositions, and spelling—these small, consistent errors are the most common carryover from German syntax and are easy to fix with targeted editing.

You have a genuinely interesting argument here—now it needs a clearer skeleton to hold all these good ideas together. I'd be happy to discuss a revision plan with you in office hours.

Feedback on "Crime Pays" Essay

Overall Impression
This is a lively, confident essay with a clear point of view and an ambitious three-part structure (history, material gain, the justice system). Your voice comes through well, and the rhetorical questions are effective. However, the essay needs revision in three main areas: sharpening the logical connection between your evidence and your thesis, tightening sentence-level grammar (some of which reflects typical German-to-English interference), and proofreading for typos.

Argument and Structure

Your thesis—that "crime can be a profitable profession"—is clear, but be careful: the essay actually makes several *different* claims that aren't quite the same thing:
Some crimes are later celebrated as heroic (Boston Tea Party, Benedict Arnold)
Some criminals gain fame/notoriety after being accused (Divine Brown, O.J. Simpson)
Some criminals make direct money from books/movies
Some criminals benefit from a flawed justice system (appeals, prison amenities)

These are related but logically distinct phenomena. "Being remembered by history" (Boston Tea Party) is not the same as "profiting financially" (book deals), and "becoming famous" is not the same as "crime paying" in a strict sense—fame isn't always profit, and infamy isn't always a benefit the person wanted. Consider narrowing your thesis to focus consistently on *financial* profit, or explicitly distinguish between "fame," "fortune," and "escaping just punishment" as three separate forms of "paying." Right now, paragraph 2's historical examples don't fully match the "material gain" claim you make in paragraph 3, so the reader has to work to see how they connect.

Also reconsider the Boston Tea Party/Benedict Arnold example. You use it to show that "crime pays," but the argument you're actually making is closer to "history's judgment of an act depends on who wins the war"—which is a different and interesting point, but it doesn't clearly show that the *individuals* profited from breaking the law. Make the connection explicit, or find a cleaner example.

Evidence and Development

Your examples (Divine Brown, Oliver North, Buttafuco, O.J. Simpson, Prohibition, Las Vegas) are good real-world references, but they need more explanation for readers who may not know these cases well or may not immediately see the connection to your claim. For each example, spend a sentence explaining *how* the person specifically profited (how much, in what form) rather than assuming the reader will infer it. This will also help you meet an academic audience's expectation of demonstrated evidence rather than assertion.

The conclusion is currently very short and mostly repeats the thesis. Try expanding it slightly: acknowledge the counterargument (that punishment does exist and many criminals suffer real consequences) and then explain why, despite this, your examples show a troubling pattern. This will make your argument feel more balanced and persuasive rather than one-sided.

Language and Style

A few sentence-level issues to revise:

Pronoun agreement: "No one can argue that crime is not a means of making money, if it were not why would people commit them." — "crime" is singular but you use "them." Revise to something like: "No one can argue that crime is not a means of making money; if it were not, why would people commit crimes?"

Missing "there": "Finally, is the issue of capture and punishment" → "Finally, there is the issue of capture and punishment." (This kind of omission is common for German speakers, since German doesn't always require an expletive subject the way English does.)

Verb tense consistency: "Throughout history crime is a recurrent theme" — since you're discussing a pattern over time, consider "has been" or "has always been a recurrent theme."

Watch for article usage ("the current material gain" in your intro is a bit awkward — perhaps "the material gains of crime today").

Proofreading

There are several typos that need correction before this could be considered a polished draft:
"vents" → "events"
"Los Vegas" → "Las Vegas"
Stray fragment "tes." at the end of the second-to-last paragraph
"speak-eases" should be "speakeasies"
"convicted killers autobiographies" needs an apostrophe: "convicted killers' autobiographies"

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Clarify and narrow your thesis so all three body paragraphs support the *same* specific claim.
Add explanatory detail to your examples so the connection to "crime paying" is explicit.
Expand the conclusion to address a counterargument.
Do a careful proofreading pass for typos and pronoun/article agreement.

You have strong material here—with tighter logical connections and careful editing, this could be a very persuasive essay.

Feedback on Your Essay

Thank you for tackling such a complex and important topic. You clearly have strong opinions about feminism and gender roles, and your writing shows genuine engagement with the subject. However, the essay needs significant revision in three main areas: argument and reasoning, structure, and language. Let me address each.

Argument and Reasoning

This is the most important area to work on. Several claims in your essay are stated as fact but are not supported by evidence, and some rely on generalizations or unexamined assumptions:

"Women just can't fight in a war very well. They are not strong enough and if captured could be raped." This is a sweeping generalization. Physical strength varies among individuals, not just between sexes, and many women have served effectively in combat roles internationally. The rape argument is especially problematic — male prisoners of war are also subjected to sexual violence, so this cannot be a reason to exclude women specifically. If you want to make this argument, you need actual data (eg, physical fitness standards, historical case studies) rather than assumptions.
"Now many employers are the victims" of sexual harassment claims — this is a strong claim that needs statistical support. Without evidence, it reads as speculation and undermines your credibility.
"God would have created us all the same if this was not true." This is a theological claim, not an academic argument, and it will not be persuasive to readers who don't share your religious framework. Academic essays should rely on evidence (sociological, economic, biological research) rather than religious authority.
The link between feminism and rising divorce rates, heart attacks, and child neglect is presented as causal, but no evidence connects these trends to feminism specifically. Correlation is not causation — these trends have many possible causes (economic pressures, healthcare changes, etc).

Suggestion: For each major claim, ask yourself "How do I know this?" and "What evidence supports this?" Even one or two statistics or a reference to a study would make your argument far more convincing. Try to distinguish between your personal opinion and claims that require evidence.

Structure and Thesis

Your essay lacks a clear, consistent thesis. You begin by acknowledging that women faced real historical injustice, then pivot to criticizing feminism, then discuss combat roles, then workplace harassment, then "nature," and end with a call for equality but also a suggestion that "nature" limits what women can do. This creates a contradictory impression — are you *for* or *against* expanded roles for women?

Suggestion: Decide on one central argument (for example: "Feminism has achieved important gains, but it should allow more room for individual choice rather than imposing a single model of success on all women"). Then organize each paragraph around a specific point that supports this thesis, using topic sentences to signal the focus of each paragraph. Right now paragraphs jump between topics (combat roles, harassment, "nature," divorce statistics) without clear transitions.

Language and Style

As a non-native speaker, you're doing well overall, but here are recurring issues to review:

Word confusions: "verses" → *versus*; "piers" → *peers*; "the are not strong enough" → *they are not*.
Articles and prepositions: "the woman" when you mean women in general should usually be just *women* (no article) — German often uses articles where English does not for generalizations. eg, "a lot of office jobs are being filled by women" (not "the woman").
Sentence fragments and run-ons: "Women should be created equal, but they can only do what nature intended they to do..." has a grammar error ("intended they to do" → *intended them to do*) and ends with an unclear ellipsis. Avoid trailing off; finish your thought clearly.
Register: Phrases like "I felt that these people were crazy" are too informal and dismissive for academic writing. Academic tone requires you to engage with opposing views respectfully, even when arguing against them — eg, "I find this argument unconvincing because..."

Overall Recommendation

Revise this essay by:
Choosing one clear, defensible thesis.
Cutting or heavily qualifying claims you cannot support with evidence (combat ability, "victims" of harassment claims, religious appeals).
Finding at least 3–4 credible sources (statistics on wage gaps, studies on workplace equality, or historical accounts of the feminist movement) to ground your argument.
Reorganizing paragraphs so each one develops a single point tied to your thesis.
Proofreading carefully for the vocabulary and grammar issues noted above.

You have real insight into the tension between collective movements and individual choice — that could be a genuinely interesting thesis if developed with more care and evidence. I'd be happy to discuss thesis options with you before you begin your revision.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay: "Crime Does Not Pay"

Thank you for sharing this draft. You clearly have strong personal convictions about this topic, and your writing shows creativity, especially in the extended hypothetical scenario. However, the essay currently reads more like a personal reflection or story than an academic argumentative essay. Below are the main areas to focus on for revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Structure

Your essay opens with a personal belief ("I am a true believer...") rather than a clear, arguable thesis statement. An academic argumentative essay needs a thesis that states a specific claim you will defend with evidence and reasoning—for example: "The severity of legal punishment escalates disproportionately once a crime involves violence, demonstrating that criminal behavior carries increasingly serious consequences." Right now, your central claim ("crime does not pay") is a proverb, not an argument, and it is never clearly connected to your evidence.

Action: Rewrite your introduction with one sentence that states exactly what you will argue and how you will support it (eg, through legal consequences, escalation of criminal behavior, etc).

Evidence and Support

The essay relies almost entirely on personal opinion and a single invented hypothetical story. While illustrative examples can support an argument, academic writing requires you to engage with actual evidence—statistics on recidivism, legal statutes on sentencing, criminology research, or real case studies. Your long anecdote about the convenience store robbery is vivid, but it functions as storytelling, not argumentation. You describe what happens rather than explaining *why* it proves your point or what broader principle it illustrates.

Action: Shorten the hypothetical example significantly (a few sentences are enough) and instead use it to introduce a discussion of actual sentencing laws (eg, how "felony murder" or "attempted murder" charges work) or research on how minor offenses can escalate into serious ones.

Paragraph Organization

Your essay currently has three paragraphs, but they don't build logically toward a conclusion:
Paragraph 1: general belief that small crimes escalate
Paragraph 2: brief mention that punishment varies by severity
Paragraph 3: an extended hypothetical story

There is no paragraph that clearly analyzes the story's significance, and there is no concluding paragraph that ties everything back to your thesis. The essay ends abruptly with "Something that was supposed to be so simple ended up to be a big mess," which is too informal and does not offer a final, reasoned takeaway.

Action: Add a concluding paragraph that restates your argument and explains its broader significance (eg, what this means for how we think about deterrence, or for policy on juvenile/first-time offenders).

Tone and Register

For academic writing, the tone needs to be more formal and objective. Phrases like "a cool million," "run like hell," "guess what happens... You know what happens," and "Sounds like trouble to me" are conversational and undermine the credibility of your argument. Similarly, personal asides such as "(especially me having a 'weakness' for children)" are appropriate for a personal essay but not for an argumentative academic one.

Action: Go through the essay and remove first-person anecdotal asides and slang expressions. Replace them with more neutral, analytical language.

Sentence-Level Language Issues

Since English is your second language, here are some patterns to watch for, which are common for German speakers:

Run-on sentences: Many of your sentences string together multiple independent clauses with commas or "and," making them hard to follow (eg, the entire robbery narrative is often one long sentence). Break these into shorter, clearer sentences.
Spelling errors: committs → commits; indivudual → individual; whrn → when; convenient store → convenience store (this is a real English term, different from "convenient"); strats → starts; hurridly → hurriedly; persistant → persistent; ths → the/this.
Word order/verb forms: "someone could definitely headed for destruction" should be "someone could definitely be headed for destruction."
Article usage: Watch for missing or incorrect articles, a common challenge when transferring from German (eg, "commit crime" → "commit a crime" in most contexts).

Action: After revising content and structure, do a careful proofreading pass focused only on grammar and spelling. Reading your essay aloud can help you catch run-on sentences.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Write a clear, arguable thesis statement.
Cut the hypothetical story down significantly and support your argument with real evidence (legal facts, statistics, or scholarly sources).
Add a concluding paragraph.
Formalize your tone throughout.
Proofread carefully for spelling and sentence structure.

You have a genuine, well-considered point of view—the next step is to present it with the structure and evidence that academic argumentation requires. I look forward to seeing your revised draft!

Feedback on Your Essay: "Money is the Root of All Evil"

Overall Impression

You've chosen a thought-provoking topic and have some genuinely interesting ideas—particularly your point that money itself is neutral and it is human attitude (addiction, greed) that determines its moral impact. This is a strong thesis. However, the essay needs work in three main areas: structure and development, argumentation with evidence, and grammar/mechanics. Below are concrete suggestions.

Thesis and Structure

Your actual argument—"money itself is neither good nor bad; it is the person's attitude that makes it so"—doesn't appear until paragraph two. Consider moving this claim (or a clear preview of it) into your introduction so readers know from the start where the essay is heading. Right now the introduction focuses heavily on defining "money" and explaining its power, which delays your real thesis.

Also, the essay ends very abruptly with a single unfinished sentence ("Money does good or evil--depending on who has the money"). This reads more like a note-to-self than a conclusion. You need a proper concluding paragraph that:
restates your thesis in new words
briefly summarizes your key points (power, addiction, greed)
ends with a broader reflection (eg, what should individuals or society do with this insight?)

Argument Development

Your claims are currently quite abstract and asserted rather than demonstrated. For an argumentative essay, you should support your points with specific examples—historical figures, case studies, statistics, or even brief hypothetical scenarios. For instance:
When you mention drug use, gambling, and prostitution as "obvious problems," give a sentence of concrete illustration.
Your point about material possessions and social prestige driving greed could use a real-world example (a corporate scandal, a well-known case of fraud, etc).
Consider addressing a counterargument: some might say money enables charity, medical research, education, etc Acknowledging this and then explaining why your thesis still holds would strengthen your credibility.

This would move your essay from a series of assertions to a more persuasive, evidence-based argument.

Paragraph Organization

Each paragraph currently tries to do too much at once. Consider separating your ideas more clearly:
Paragraph 1: Introduce the proverb, complicate it, state your thesis.
Paragraph 2: Discuss the "power" of money (survival, status).
Paragraph 3: Discuss addiction as a mechanism of money's negative influence.
Paragraph 4: Discuss greed specifically, with an example.
Paragraph 5: Conclusion.

This will make your line of reasoning easier to follow and give you room to add supporting detail.

Grammar and Mechanics

A few recurring issues to address in revision:

Comma splices: "Enough money gives you the power to get whatever you need to survive, lack of money may mean you can't survive." Two independent clauses need a period, semicolon, or conjunction, not just a comma. Watch for this pattern throughout—it appears several times.

Capitalization after a colon/dash: "when the need for money becomes the driving force" should begin with a capital "W" since it starts a new sentence after the previous one ends with a period.

Apostrophe error: "Ones's attitude" should be "One's attitude" (only one apostrophe needed for the possessive).

Subject-verb agreement: "The list of addictions people develop are endless" — *list* is singular, so it should be "is endless."

Sentence fragment/missing punctuation: Your final sentence has no period and functions more like a title than a complete concluding statement. Expand it into a full sentence and paragraph.

Word choice/idiom: "the need for whatever it buys is possessing the buyer" is a bit awkward in English (this may be a direct translation from German idiom). Consider: "the need for whatever it buys takes hold of the buyer" or "controls the buyer."

Title/opening: Titles and the first line of essays are usually not left as an unattributed quotation without any signal that you're quoting it (eg, quotation marks or "As the old saying goes...").

Style

You have a tendency to start consecutive sentences with the same subject ("Money...", "The power...", "The need..."). Try varying sentence openings and lengths to improve flow. Reading your paragraphs aloud can help you catch this repetition.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Clarify and foreground your thesis early.
Add concrete examples/evidence for each main point.
Write a real conclusion.
Fix comma splices, capitalization, and the apostrophe/agreement errors noted above.
Vary sentence structure for better flow.

You have a solid conceptual foundation—now it needs fuller development and tighter mechanics. Good luck with your revision!
