Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Thank you for sharing this piece—it's clear you feel strongly about your topic, and that passion comes through. However, the essay currently reads more like a personal reflection than an academic argument. Below are some suggestions to help you strengthen it.

Clarify and Narrow Your Thesis

Your opening paragraph creates some confusion about what you're actually arguing. You say you disagree with "much of" what feminists promote, but then in your second and third paragraphs you *agree* with several core feminist goals (equal pay, breaking the glass ceiling, women's right to choose work or homemaking). This isn't necessarily a contradiction—you clarify later that your real objection is narrower (chivalry, dating norms)—but the reader doesn't know this until paragraph four. 

Suggestion: State your specific thesis early and clearly: something like, "While I support many feminist goals, I believe the movement has had an unintended negative effect on chivalry and dating etiquette." This will help you avoid seeming to attack feminism broadly when your actual argument is much more specific.

Strengthen Your Evidence

Right now, your argument rests almost entirely on personal experience and anecdotes from friends. This is valuable as illustrative material, but for an academic essay, it reads as anecdotal rather than analytical. Ask yourself:
Is there research on changing dating norms since the 1960s?
Are there sociological studies on perceptions of chivalry among different generations?
Could you cite surveys about attitudes toward paying for dates, opening doors, etc?

Even a few outside sources would let you move from "I have heard from several male friends" to a more credible, generalizable claim. Right now, statements like "women in general are offended if you open doors for them" are broad generalizations based on a small, informal sample.

Address Counterarguments

An academic argumentative essay is strengthened by acknowledging opposing views. For instance: Could confusion about dating behavior stem from changing gender roles more broadly, rather than specifically from feminism? Could some men welcome the shift toward shared financial responsibility on dates? Engaging with these possibilities—even briefly—would make your argument more persuasive and less one-sided.

Structure and Paragraphing

Some paragraphs try to do too much at once. For example, your third paragraph moves from the "two-income household" reality, to the stigma around working women, to housekeepers, to time with children—all in one paragraph. Consider breaking these into smaller, focused paragraphs, each with one clear point, followed by support.

Also, the essay's structure would benefit from a clearer roadmap: an introduction with a thesis, body paragraphs each defending one aspect of that thesis, and a conclusion that returns to the thesis rather than introducing new rhetorical moves.

Tone and Register

The conclusion shifts into direct address ("just for some consideration towards traditional values... from you men out there"), which changes the audience mid-essay. Academic essays typically maintain a consistent audience and tone throughout. Consider whether this direct address serves your argument, or whether it would be more effective to end with a reflective statement that reinforces your central claim.

Additionally, phrases like "I have been hanging around this continent for 40-plus years" and "this is bunk!" are quite colloquial. This informality can work in a personal essay, but for an academic argumentative piece, aim for a more measured tone even when your opinions are strong.

Language Notes

A few specific issues to watch for (these may reflect German sentence structures):

"stigmatism" → this should be "stigma."
"opposite-sexed dinner date" is awkward; consider simply "a date."
Several long sentences combine multiple ideas with commas where periods or semicolons would improve clarity (eg, "Men were gentlemen in the 60's, felt that the woman was someone special and usually these men had parents...").
Watch verb tense consistency—you move between past and present tense when discussing general patterns versus specific memories. Decide whether you're making a general claim (present tense: "chivalry has declined") or narrating your personal history (past tense: "I dated in the 60s").
The phrase "in the realm of date, courting, and chivalry" needs an article: "in the realm of dating, courting, and chivalry."

Summary

Your essay has a genuine, personal voice and an interesting angle, but it needs a sharper thesis, more substantiated evidence, engagement with counterarguments, and tighter paragraph structure to meet the expectations of academic argumentative writing. I'd encourage you to revise with these goals: (1) narrow and foreground your thesis, (2) add outside evidence, (3) address at least one counterargument, and (4) revise for consistent tone and paragraph focus.

I look forward to seeing your revision!

Feedback on Your Essay: "Crime Doesn't Pay"

Overall Impression

You've chosen an engaging way to open your essay by directly addressing the reader and referencing a familiar saying (though the phrase itself seems to have dropped out of your final draft — check that the placeholder  wasn't left in by mistake). Your essay identifies several genuinely interesting angles on the cost of crime: financial cost to taxpayers, psychological/moral cost to the offender, economic cost to law-abiding citizens, and social cost through stigmatization. This is a promising range of ideas. The main areas to work on are argument development, evidence, coherence between paragraphs, and some recurring grammar issues typical of German-English transfer.

Argumentation and Structure

Your four body paragraphs each introduce a different "cost" of crime, but they read as a list rather than a developed argument. Right now, each paragraph makes a claim and restates it in slightly different words, without offering concrete evidence (statistics, examples, studies, or even a specific anecdote) to support it. For an argumentative essay, this is the biggest gap: readers need to see *why* they should believe your claims, not just that you're asserting them.

Also consider that a strong argumentative essay anticipates and addresses counterarguments. For instance, someone could argue that crime *does* pay for some individuals (financial gain from theft, fraud, etc) — even if this is outweighed by other costs. Acknowledging and refuting this would strengthen your position rather than weaken it.

The moral/remorse paragraph is the weakest link, because it shifts from your central claim (crime costs *money*) to an emotional/psychological claim without a clear transition. Either connect it explicitly back to your financial thesis (eg, therapy costs, lost future earning potential due to guilt-driven withdrawal from society) or reconsider whether it belongs in this essay at all.

Finally, your essay ends abruptly after the paragraph on labeling/stigma. You need a concluding paragraph that synthesizes your points and returns to the opening phrase, showing the reader how you've proven your thesis.

Coherence and Transitions

Right now, paragraphs begin with topic sentences that don't clearly connect to the previous paragraph ("The moral aspect...", "The law-abiding citizen also...", "Once an individual..."). Add transitional phrases that show the logical relationship between sections, eg, "Beyond the financial burden on taxpayers, crime also carries a psychological cost..." This will guide your reader through your reasoning.

Grammar and Sentence-Level Issues

A few recurring problems to check systematically:

Verb agreement/tense slip: "has been repeated many times but have you ever..." → should be "has."
Spelling: "freinds" → friends; "acquantances" → acquaintances; "oppurtunity" → opportunity; "to often" → too often.
Capitalization/sentence boundaries: "...for the area. so even though..." — this should start a new sentence with a capital letter, and check whether it's a complete sentence or needs restructuring.
Missing "there": "their is no escape" → there is no escape.
Literal translation from German: "As the number of criminals increases so to will the tax money" — this phrasing mirrors German "so wird auch," but in English it should be something like "so too will the tax money needed to house them increase" (note: you need a verb at the end here, as the sentence currently lacks one).
Pronoun consistency: You switch between "his or her" and "they/their" for the same referent (eg, "the criminal will have to live with his or her actions as long as they live"). Choose one convention — either consistent singular "they" throughout, or "he or she," but don't mix within a sentence.
Redundancy: "as long as they live" after "for the rest of their lives" — trim repeated phrasing across paragraphs (you use "for the rest of their lives" twice in one paragraph).

Word Choice and Style

You rely heavily on "individual" — vary this with "offender," "perpetrator," "person," "defendant" depending on context, both for style and precision. Similarly, "actually" appears twice in the first paragraph; consider using it only once for emphasis. The phrase "we hear stories of how crime has robbed individuals" is vivid — lean into this kind of concrete, sensory language elsewhere instead of abstract claims.

Suggestions for Revision

Insert the actual proverb you're analyzing at the start.
Add one piece of concrete evidence (a statistic, real case, or cited example) per paragraph.
Write a conclusion that ties your points together and returns to your opening question.
Address at least one counterargument.
Proofread specifically for the spelling/grammar patterns listed above — reading the essay aloud can help you catch missing words like "there."

You have a solid conceptual foundation here — the next draft should focus on deepening evidence and tightening the mechanics.

Feedback Report on Argumentative Essay: "Crime and Money"

Overall Impression

Thomas, you clearly have real interest in and knowledge of your subject, and this comes through in your writing. However, the essay currently reads more like a personal reflection or classroom presentation than an academic argumentative essay. Below are the main areas to address before revision.

---

Thesis and Argument

The most significant issue is that your reader never learns what "this statement" actually is. The essay opens by referencing something the reader is expected to already know, but you never quote or state it directly. Before we can evaluate your argument, we need a clear, explicit thesis: What exactly are you arguing? Is it "Organized crime pays off far more reliably than unorganized crime because it depends on money and complicity, not chance"? State this claim directly in your introduction, and make sure every paragraph works to support it.

Related to this: much of the essay is *descriptive* (explaining what organized and unorganized crime are) rather than *argumentative* (making a claim and defending it with evidence and reasoning). An argumentative essay needs you to take a position and justify it — ideally while also acknowledging counterarguments. Right now, you assert conclusions ("This type of crime very rarely pays") without much supporting evidence or logical development.

Evidence

You use one concrete statistic — the $30 per person spent on illegal gambling — but do not cite a source. Since this number is central to your argument about the scale of organized crime's profitability, you need a citation (even informally, eg, "according to my Organized Crime & American Politics course materials"). Otherwise, the number appears anecdotal and undermines your credibility. Look for at least two or three more sourced facts or examples (specific cases, historical examples of organized crime networks, or data on prosecution/conviction rates for petty crime vs. organized crime) to strengthen your claims.

Structure

The essay would benefit from a clearer roadmap:
Introduction: State the "statement" you are examining, and present your thesis.
Body paragraph 1: Unorganized crime — definition, examples, why it "does not pay."
Body paragraph 2: Organized crime — definition, examples, why it "does pay."
Body paragraph 3 (currently missing): A direct comparison, addressing counterarguments (eg, someone might argue that many organized criminals *do* get caught, or that unorganized crime can occasionally be lucrative).
Conclusion: Restate your thesis and its implications.

Transitions like "This leads me to my next part" are too informal for academic writing. Instead, use topic sentences that explicitly connect ideas (eg, "While unorganized crime rarely generates sustained profit, organized crime succeeds precisely because it is built around money and complicity.").

The Ending

The final paragraph, in which you introduce yourself by name, age, and major, does not belong in an academic essay. Academic writing is impersonal in this sense — your credibility should come from your argument and evidence, not a biographical statement. Consider removing this entirely, or if some personal context is required by the assignment, integrate it briefly into your introduction instead (eg, mentioning your coursework as a source of expertise, which you already do effectively in paragraph 3).

Tone and Style

Academic essays require a more formal register than the one used here. A few specific issues:
Avoid addressing the reader directly ("Now that you have read this and may be trying to figure me out...").
Avoid emphasis through capitalization ("MONEY!", "BIG sum") — use precise word choice instead (eg, "a substantial sum").
Avoid casual phrases like "both sides of the track," "the best for last," and rhetorical exclamations. These suit a spoken presentation, not written academic prose.
Reconsider generalizations that could be seen as classist or unsupported ("these people are usually somewhat less educated") — even if statistically common, such claims need to be phrased carefully and, ideally, supported by evidence rather than assumption.

Language and Grammar

A few recurring issues to watch for, some of which are common transfer errors from German:
"will to deal with others" → should be "willing to deal with others."
Comma splices and run-on sentences appear frequently (eg, "The persons must be patient and will to deal with others. There are certainly other important factors..."). Review sentence boundaries carefully.
Watch article usage ("the unorganized" vs. "unorganized crime") — German often omits or adds articles differently than English requires.
"Multiply that by the number of citizens" — informal calculation reasoning; in academic writing, present statistics more precisely and cite them.

---

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Add a clear thesis statement early in the essay.
Cite your sources, especially statistics.
Restructure with clear topic sentences and a comparison/counterargument section.
Remove the personal biography at the end.
Revise for a more formal, academic tone throughout.
Proofread for run-on sentences and article usage.

You have strong raw material here — a genuine argument with real-world relevance. Tightening the structure and formalizing the tone will make this a much more persuasive academic essay.

Feedback on Your Essay

Thank you for sharing this personal and emotionally powerful story. Your essay does a good job of using a real experience to explore the argument that "crime doesn't pay." Below is feedback organized by key areas, along with suggestions to strengthen your next draft.

Content and Argument Structure

Your essay is framed as a narrative, but since this is an argumentative essay, you need to be clearer about what you are arguing and why. Currently, the thesis ("crime doesn't pay") is stated but not deeply analyzed—it appears mainly through your father's repeated saying. Consider:

Adding a clear thesis statement early on (perhaps at the end of your introduction) that explicitly states your argument: eg, "This experience taught me that despite moments of doubt, the justice system can hold criminals accountable, and crime ultimately does not pay."
Connecting your personal story to a broader claim. Right now, the essay stays very personal. An argumentative essay benefits from explaining *why* your experience matters beyond your own life—what does it teach us about justice, deterrence, or victims' rights?
Addressing counterarguments. You could strengthen your argument by acknowledging that sometimes crime *does* pay (criminals aren't always caught, plea bargains happen often) and explain why your case is meaningful despite this reality.

Organization

The chronological structure works well for storytelling, but your conclusion could be more analytical. Right now, it summarizes the story's ending rather than driving home your argument. Consider adding a final paragraph that steps back from the narrative and reflects explicitly on what this experience proves about justice systems in general, not just in your case.

Grammar and Language (Key Areas for a German-speaking Writer)

Since English and German differ in several structural ways, here are patterns to watch for:

Verb tense consistency: You shift between past and present tense in places. For example, "I have trust in the legal system now" is present tense, which works for your concluding reflection, but check that transitions between past narration and present reflection are clear and intentional.

Reported speech constructions: 
   - "my mother and father always taught me the stealing was wrong" → should be "that stealing was wrong" (missing conjunction, common German-English confusion where "dass" can sometimes be dropped in German but not in English).
   - "I always believed them and they saying that" → this sentence is incomplete and unclear. Revise to something like: "I always believed them when they said this."

Article usage: German and English handle articles differently. Check phrases like "husbands firearms" (should be "husband's firearms" — apostrophe needed for possession).

Word order with adverbs: "policemen. We kept going over things and had to try so hard" — some sentences could be tightened for a more natural flow in English (this is a common area of difficulty since German allows more flexible word order).

Past participle vs. simple past confusion: "that it was our home they stolen property came from" → should be "that the stolen property came from our home" (word order issue, likely influenced by German syntax).

Sentence-Level Suggestions

Break up some very long sentences (eg, the one describing what was stolen) for clarity and rhythm.
Watch for repeated words like "so upset," "so scared," "so hard" — consider varying your vocabulary for more sophisticated expression (eg, "devastated," "terrified," "exhausting").
Proofread for missing words: "did not think that they would have to pay" — check if this reflects your intended meaning (did you mean you *doubted* they would be punished?).

Overall Impression

Your storytelling is vivid and emotionally engaging—readers can feel your fear, frustration, and eventual relief. To transform this into a stronger *argumentative* essay, focus on:
Stating your thesis explicitly.
Connecting your personal experience to a larger claim about justice.
Considering counterarguments.
Polishing grammar, especially around reported speech and word order.

Next steps: Revise your introduction to include a clear thesis, and add a concluding paragraph that explicitly ties your experience to a broader argument about the justice system. Then do a careful proofreading pass focusing on the grammar points above.

Keep up the good work—your voice and personal insight are compelling; now let's make the argument as strong as the story!

Feedback on Your Essay: Feminism and Its Contemporary Reception

Thank you for tackling such a complex and contentious topic. Your essay shows genuine engagement with the subject and a clear personal investment in the argument. However, the essay would benefit from revision in several key areas: argumentative structure, evidence, and language precision. Below is feedback organized by these categories.

Argumentative Structure and Clarity

Your essay responds to an implied "statement," but this statement is never quoted or clearly identified. Since your reader does not have access to the prompt, you must state explicitly what claim you are addressing (eg, "The claim that feminism has done more harm than good..."). Without this, your opening paragraph is difficult to follow.

Additionally, your central argument is somewhat contradictory. You argue both that (a) feminism has been "misused" and has become something negative, and (b) the *idea* that feminism has done more harm than good is "absurd." These positions can coexist, but you need to explicitly reconcile them—perhaps by distinguishing between "the feminist movement's *achievements*" and "the feminist movement's *current reputation*." Right now, the essay reads as though you are arguing against yourself in different paragraphs. A clearer thesis stated early on—something like "While feminism's historical achievements are undeniable, its contemporary image has been damaged by a radical minority who conflate equality with dominance"—would give your essay a stronger backbone.

Use of Evidence

This is the area needing the most attention. Your essay relies heavily on assertions rather than evidence:

The reference to Susan B. Anthony needs a citation and more historical specificity. What did she say or write? When?
Phrases like "many radical women," "many people see," and "outsiders now see" are unsupported generalizations. Academic writing requires you to substantiate such claims—through survey data, quotations from feminist theorists or critics, media examples, or scholarly sources on the topic.
The claim about "how women are now taken seriously when they report sex crimes" would be much stronger with statistics or a specific historical comparison (eg, changes in reporting rates or legal reforms).

Without evidence, your argument risks sounding like opinion rather than analysis, which will weaken its persuasive power for an academic reader.

Tone and Word Choice

The description of a feminist as "a big chunky lesbian who hates all men" is a stereotype that, even when used to criticize a stereotype, risks alienating readers and undermining your credibility. If you want to reference this popular caricature, it would be more effective to frame it explicitly as a stereotype you are critiquing (eg, "One common stereotype depicts feminists as..."), and perhaps cite where this image comes from (media portrayals, popular culture, etc).

Similarly, phrases like "dirty words," "home free," and "shout from and feel safe within" are colloquial and imprecise for academic writing. Aim for more analytical, formal vocabulary throughout.

Language and Grammar (German-to-English Interference)

I noticed a few patterns common among German-speaking learners of English:

Capitalization after full stops: "by this I do not mean..." should begin with a capital letter: "By this I do not mean..."
Article usage: "A women is just as much a citizen" — should be "A woman." Be careful throughout with woman/women (singular/plural).
Possessive pronoun agreement: "every human has just as much potential to prosper... no matter what gender" — later "in her society" doesn't match; use "their" for gender-neutral reference, or be consistent.
Prepositions: "fight tooth and nail to win a moot argument and be obstinate in their belief" is fine, but "shout at the atrocities" should likely be "shout about the atrocities" or "protest against."
Run-on/comma splices: Several sentences chain clauses with commas where a period or semicolon would create clearer breaks (eg, in the third paragraph).

I recommend reading your essay aloud, which often helps catch these small grammatical slips, and using a grammar-checking tool as a supplement (not a replacement) for careful proofreading.

Paragraph Organization

Your paragraphs would benefit from clearer topic sentences that preview their content. For example, the third paragraph jumps between the popular stereotype, the "umbrella" metaphor, and outsider perceptions without a clear guiding sentence. Consider adding a topic sentence like: "The current negative stereotype of feminists stems from a minority who use the movement's name to justify extreme positions."

Summary

Your essay has a compelling personal voice and an argument with real substance, but it needs: (1) a clearer, unified thesis; (2) concrete evidence and citations; (3) more careful, formal word choice; and (4) attention to specific grammar patterns. I'd encourage you to revise with these priorities in mind, starting with explicitly stating your thesis and the claim you're responding to. This will give the entire essay a much stronger foundation.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay engages with an interesting and important question: how the public perception of "radical" feminists has overshadowed the moderate achievements of the Women's Liberation Movement. This is a promising and debatable thesis. However, the essay would benefit from clearer organization, more careful use of historical evidence, and attention to some recurring language issues. Below are specific suggestions for revision.

Thesis and Argument

Your central claim—that the fault lies not with the radicals themselves but with the *public's perception* of them—is a strong, arguable thesis. However, it doesn't appear clearly until the middle of the essay ("I contend that the fault does not lie with..."). Academic essays in English typically state the thesis explicitly in the introduction so readers know what to expect. Consider moving a clear version of this claim to the end of your first paragraph.

Also, be careful with the rhetorical question "So what am I getting at?" While this device can work in spoken or informal writing, it reads as underdeveloped in an academic essay—as though you are discovering your own argument mid-text rather than guiding the reader through it. Replace it with a transitional sentence that restates and reinforces your thesis.

Use of Historical Evidence

This is the area needing the most attention. Several of your examples raise historical accuracy concerns that could undermine your credibility with an academic reader:

Susan B. Anthony and George Sand are presented as contemporaries and as "the first radicals," but Sand's public reputation (cross-dressing, romantic scandals) was largely built in the 1830s–40s, distinct in context and nation from Anthony's American suffrage activism decades later. If you group them, explain *why*—what specifically unites their "radicalism"?
Emily Dickinson is a puzzling example of a "feminist ignored during her lifetime." Dickinson was not a political or social activist; she is generally studied as a reclusive poet, not as part of the organized women's movement. If you want an example of an overlooked *moderate* activist, choose someone actually associated with the movement (eg, Lucretia Mott, or a "second-wave" author like Betty Friedan).
Gertrude Stein is cited as a 1960s radical, but Stein died in 1946. If you mean a different figure—perhaps Gloria Steinem or another second-wave activist—please correct this, as chronological errors like this can seriously weaken your argument's credibility.

Revising these examples with historically accurate, well-chosen figures will strengthen your case considerably.

Structure and Paragraphing

The essay moves quickly between the 19th century, the 1960s, and "twenty-five years later," but the transitions between these time periods are abrupt. Consider adding brief signposting sentences ("A similar pattern reemerged in the 1960s...") to guide readers through the chronology.

Some paragraphs are only one or two sentences (eg, "This is especially true..." and "So what am I getting at?"). While short paragraphs can be used for emphasis, overusing them makes the essay feel fragmented. Try combining some of these into fuller paragraphs with clearer topic sentences and supporting detail.

The conclusion is underdeveloped. You end with a broad generalization ("Like many other aspects and movements in life...") but don't return explicitly to your thesis or suggest what should change. Consider closing with a reflection on how public perception might be improved, or what this pattern reveals about social movements more broadly.

Language and Grammar

You write fluently, but a few recurring issues (some likely influenced by German sentence structure) should be corrected:

"Whenever a movement is began" → should be "begins" (active verb needed here).
Spelling: "oppostion" → opposition; "therfore" → therefore; "readiliy" → readily.
"and and other 'liberal' causes" — remove the duplicate word.
"the public it was being aimed was not so jaded" — this sentence is unclear; try: "...if the public *at which it was aimed* were not so jaded."
Watch comma splices, eg, "This ignorance of other less aggressive feminists, made it seem..." — remove the comma before "made."

Summary of Priorities for Revision

State your thesis clearly and early.
Verify and correct historical examples for accuracy and relevance.
Improve transitions between time periods and strengthen your conclusion.
Proofread carefully for spelling, verb forms, and sentence clarity.

You have a genuinely interesting argument here—refining the evidence and tightening the structure will make it much more persuasive.

Feedback on Your Essay: Feminism's Impact on American Culture

Overall Impression

You've chosen a timely and substantive topic, and your writing shows real strengths: vivid imagery ("slammed in the faces," "same sermon, same pulpit"), a personal connection to your subject, and genuine enthusiasm for the material. However, the essay currently reads as an extended introduction rather than a complete argumentative piece. Below are the main areas to address in revision.

Scope and Promise vs. Delivery

Your opening paragraph sets up an ambitious scope—the impact of feminism on "countries around the world"—but you explicitly narrow this to America "because I myself am American." This is a reasonable rhetorical choice, but you should be more transparent about it. Consider revising your thesis to state clearly that you will focus specifically on the American case as a lens through which to understand feminism's global reach, or alternatively, follow through and address at least one other country for contrast. Right now, the essay's conclusion ("America continues to set an example for women around the world") makes a global claim that your evidence doesn't support, since you've only examined the domestic context.

The Missing Content

There's a placeholder  in your third paragraph where you intended to state what message Friedan and Steinem "preached." This is a critical gap—it's the rhetorical and argumentative center of that paragraph, and its absence weakens your characterization of these feminists' legacy. Please fill this in with a specific, quotable idea (eg, a paraphrase of their core message) before submitting.

Logical Consistency

There's a tension between two claims you make:
"No one today would say that women are not equal to men in the voting booths of America."
Later: women "still" face resentment, and access to opportunities remains "a struggle."

These statements aren't necessarily contradictory (formal legal equality vs. ongoing social equality are different things), but you need to make that distinction explicit for your reader. Right now, the shift from "equality achieved" to "battle continues" feels abrupt. Consider adding a transitional sentence that clarifies: political/legal equality was won, but social and economic equality remains contested.

Development and Evidence

Each paragraph introduces a promising idea but doesn't fully develop it:
The suffrage paragraph could benefit from a specific date, statistic, or quotation.
The Friedan/Steinem paragraph would be stronger with a concrete example of their influence (a specific policy change, court case, or cultural shift).
The final paragraph, asserting American exceptionalism, needs support—what evidence do you have that other countries look to America as a model? This claim currently feels asserted rather than argued.

As a rule for revision: each paragraph should contain at least one specific example, statistic, date, or quotation to substantiate its claim.

Language-Level Notes

Your English is fluent and largely idiomatic—impressive for a non-native writer. A few specific corrections:

"suffragates" → should be "suffragists" (this appears to be an interference error, perhaps from German "Suffragetten," though even that would translate to "suffragettes" in English, not "suffragates").
"Steinam" → should be "Steinem" (spelling of Gloria Steinem).
"Women's access... have expanded" → subject-verb agreement issue; "access" is singular, so this should be "has expanded."
The phrase "greeted with cheers or jeers from any crowd" is vivid, but check whether "any crowd" is the intended meaning—you may mean "audiences are divided," which could be phrased more directly.

Suggestions for Revision

Narrow or expand your thesis explicitly. Either commit fully to America as a case study (and say so in your intro) or add a comparative section on another country to justify the global framing.
Fill in the placeholder with a substantive statement of the feminist message you're referencing.
Add a bridging sentence between legal/political equality and the "battle continues" framing to resolve the apparent contradiction.
Strengthen each paragraph with concrete evidence—a date, a statistic, a named case or event.
Support your closing claim about America's global influence, or soften it to match the evidence you've actually presented (eg, "America offers one influential model among many, though not the only one").
Proofread for the specific spelling/agreement issues noted above.

Final Thought

You have a strong voice and clear investment in this topic—the imagery and passion come through well. The task now is to match that voice with the argumentative rigor and evidentiary support that a strong thesis requires. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression
You have chosen an interesting and debatable topic, and your basic argument—that it is the *love* of money, not money itself, that leads to evil—is a reasonable and defensible position. You also show good instincts for argumentative writing by acknowledging a counterargument (that money *can* lead to evil) before returning to your main point. However, the essay needs revision in three main areas: clarity of the central claim, logical consistency, and grammar/word choice. Below are specific suggestions.

Clarify Your Thesis from the Start
Your opening paragraph is confusing because of the placeholder "" and the ambiguous phrase "since the original subject is." Readers cannot tell which version of the saying you consider correct and which you are arguing against. Since your whole essay depends on distinguishing "money is the root of all evil" from "the *love of* money is the root of all evil," you must state this distinction explicitly and early. For example:

> "The common saying 'Money is the root of all evil' is, in fact, a misquotation. The original biblical phrase is 'The love of money is the root of all evil.' I agree with the original statement, but I disagree with the popular, shortened version, because it wrongly blames money itself rather than people's attitude toward it."

This kind of explicit framing will make your thesis unmistakable and give the essay a clear direction from paragraph one.

Check Your Logic
Some of your reasoning needs tightening:
"If money were the root of all evil, we all would have to be evil because we all have money" is a good rhetorical move, but be careful—not everyone has money, and this could be challenged. Consider softening to "most people" or explaining that having money is common and unavoidable in modern life.
The line "If one is attaining money honestly, how can honesty be associated with evil?" (you wrote "honestly" instead of "honesty") is a strong point, but it would be more persuasive if you gave a concrete example (eg, a nurse or teacher earning a salary) rather than staying abstract.
Your conclusion introduces a new idea—individual moral choice—that isn't developed earlier in the essay. To avoid feeling like a new argument appears out of nowhere, add a sentence in your third or fourth paragraph foreshadowing this idea, eg, "Ultimately, whether money leads to evil depends not on the money itself but on the choices of the person who possesses it."

Strengthen Paragraph Structure
Each paragraph would benefit from a clearer topic sentence stating its main point before you give examples. For instance, paragraph three currently begins with "It is true to say that money is the root of some evil," which is good, but paragraph four ("Other things as well can be the root of evil...") could open with a clearer transition, such as: "However, money is not the only possible cause of evil actions."

Also, consider merging or reordering paragraphs 3 and 4, since both deal with the idea that money is not inherently evil—only human attitudes are.

Language and Grammar Notes
As a German-speaking writer, you may find some of the following typical issues in your draft:
Typos/spelling: "wold" → *would*; "honestly" → *honesty* (in "how can honesty be associated with evil").
Article usage: English often requires articles where German does not. eg, "If money was the root of all evil" → "If money were the root of all evil" (subjunctive for hypothetical conditions—note *were*, not *was*).
Word order/phrasing: "worth-while causes are sometimes being used to satisfy an individual's greed" is slightly awkward; consider: "worthwhile causes are sometimes exploited to satisfy an individual's greed."
Repetition: The phrase "root of evil" appears many times. Try synonyms such as *cause*, *source*, *origin* to add variety.
Comma splices: "Some have more than others; some have less than others" is correctly punctuated, but check other sentences for similar constructions that might need periods or conjunctions instead of commas.

Suggestions for the Conclusion
Your final question ("will he choose to be morally decent, or will he choose to be evil?") is a strong rhetorical closing, but tie it back explicitly to your thesis about *love* of money versus money itself. For example:

> "In conclusion, money itself is neutral; it is the individual's love for it—or lack of self-control—that determines whether it becomes a root of evil."

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Fix the placeholder and clarify your thesis in paragraph one.
Add concrete examples to support abstract claims.
Improve paragraph transitions and topic sentences.
Proofread carefully for spelling, articles, and subjunctive verb forms.
Connect your conclusion explicitly back to your opening argument.

With these revisions, your essay will have a much clearer argumentative thread and more polished language. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback on Your Essay

Thank you for sharing this thought-provoking essay on money and power. You clearly have strong opinions and interesting ideas, but the essay needs revision in several areas to communicate your argument more effectively. Below is feedback organized by content/argument, structure, and language.

Content and Argument

Missing context: Your essay opens with "this saying," but you never state what saying you are responding to. Since this is an argumentative essay, your reader needs to know from the first sentence what claim you are agreeing with, disagreeing with, or complicating. Please add a clear reference to the quotation (likely "Money is the root of all evil") in your introduction.

Unclear or shifting thesis: This is the most important issue to address. Your essay moves through several related but distinct ideas — the necessity of money in modern life, the corrupting nature of power, the value of earned versus unearned wealth, and the idea that money should serve us rather than control us. Each of these is interesting, but as written they read as separate mini-essays rather than a single, developed argument. Ask yourself: what is the ONE claim you want the reader to accept by the end? Right now it seems to be something like "money itself is not evil, but our relationship to it can become corrupting." If that's your thesis, state it explicitly in the introduction, and make sure every paragraph clearly supports it.

Unsupported generalizations: Several claims need evidence or at least a concrete example to be persuasive:
"If one has to work hard for their salary, they most likely will spend it more wisely..." — Is this your observation, a commonly cited belief, or research-based? Consider adding an example (a person you know, a common scenario, or a statistic).
"Money can be either a slave for us or it can make us slaves." This is a compelling metaphor, but it needs to be illustrated with a specific case — what does a person who is "enslaved" by money actually do or believe?

Structure and Organization

The paragraphs currently function almost independently. Consider these connections:

Paragraph 1 establishes that money is necessary — a good starting point, but it needs to connect to your thesis about corruption/misuse of money, not just its necessity.
Paragraph 2 shifts to a different quotation about power. The transition here is abrupt. If power is central to your argument, explain explicitly how power and money are linked (eg, "Because money is a primary source of power in society, the corrupting effects often attributed to power apply equally to money").
Paragraphs 3 and 4 introduce new distinctions (earned vs. unearned money; money as master vs. slave) without clear transitions from the power discussion.
Paragraph 5 offers a strong concluding statement, but it should echo language and ideas from your introduction and thesis so the essay feels unified.

Suggestion: After drafting your thesis, outline 3 main points that support it, and give each its own paragraph with a clear topic sentence stating how that paragraph supports the thesis.

Language and Mechanics

Several spelling and grammar issues need correction — these are common areas where German sentence patterns influence English writing:

"currupts absolute" → "corrupts absolutely" (this is misquoted; the original is "Power corrupts, and absolute power corrupts absolutely")
"begats" → "begets"
"cicle" → "circle"
"sence" → "sense"
"sucess" → "success"
"indespencable" → "indispensable"
"one heir more substantive" → this phrase is unclear; I believe you mean "on their more substantive accomplishments" — please revise for clarity
"somewhere" is two words in this context: "have some where to sleep" → "have somewhere to sleep"

Capitalization/possessives: "ones basic," "a person's," "ones sense" — be consistent with the possessive apostrophe: "one's basic needs," "one's sense."

Run-on sentences and comma splices: Several sentences string together independent clauses without proper punctuation (eg, in Paragraph 4, "Money can be either a slave for us or it can make us slaves" works, but the surrounding sentences need clearer separation with periods or semicolons rather than commas).

Word choice: "the almighty dollar is a must" is a nice colloquial phrase, but make sure your register stays consistent — mixing informal expressions with more formal argumentative writing can feel uneven. Decide on your target tone and revise for consistency.

Summary of Next Steps

State the quotation you're responding to and your clear thesis in the introduction.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one visibly supports that thesis, with clear transitions between them.
Add at least one concrete example or piece of evidence per major claim.
Proofread carefully for spelling, possessives, and sentence boundaries — reading your essay aloud can help catch these errors.

You have a genuinely interesting and nuanced perspective here. With clearer organization and more precise language, this essay will be much more persuasive.

Feedback on Your Essay: "Is Money the Root of All Evil?"

Thank you for tackling this classic ethical question. Your essay shows genuine engagement with the topic and reaches a thoughtful, nuanced conclusion. Below are suggestions to help you strengthen the argument, structure, and language in your next draft.

Content and Argumentation

Missing quotation: The placeholder  at the start suggests you intended to include the actual saying you're discussing (eg, "money is the root of all evil"). Make sure this is filled in—your reader needs to see the exact claim you're examining.

State your thesis early: Right now, your position only becomes fully clear in the final paragraph. Academic essays in English typically benefit from a clear thesis statement in the introduction. Consider revising your opening to something like: "While many people believe money itself is evil, I will argue that money is morally neutral—it is human choices about money that determine whether its effects are good or evil." This gives your reader a roadmap for the rest of the essay.

Resolve the apparent contradiction: Your third and fourth paragraphs seem to argue that pursuing money *leads* to evil (gambling, neglecting family), which sounds like you're blaming money itself. Then your fifth paragraph reverses course, insisting money is "incapable of being evil." This is actually a sophisticated move—you're distinguishing between money and the human desire for money—but you need to signal this shift explicitly. Try adding a transition sentence such as: "However, upon closer examination, it becomes clear that money itself is not the true source of the problem." This will help readers follow your reasoning rather than feeling the argument contradicts itself.

Address the religious framing: You build your argument around Christian values (subservience, humility, generosity). This is a valid approach, but not all readers share this framework. Consider briefly acknowledging that your argument draws on a Christian ethical perspective, or broaden your examples to include secular reasoning as well (eg, psychological research on materialism, or philosophical arguments about virtue).

Add concrete examples: Your final paragraph about money helping the homeless and funding education is compelling, but it remains abstract. Consider adding one specific example—a person, an organization, or a statistic—to make this point more persuasive and vivid.

Organization

Your paragraphs each cover a single idea, which is good, but they read as somewhat disconnected blocks. Add transitional sentences at the start of each paragraph to guide the reader through your logic. For example, before paragraph 4, you might write: "This pursuit of wealth does not stop at material desires; it can escalate into destructive behavior such as gambling."

Language and Grammar

Here are specific corrections to watch for:

"more that you have" → "more than you have"
"christian" → capitalize as "Christian" (and "Christ" is always capitalized in English)
"subservant" → "subservient"
"God centered life" → "God-centered life" (hyphenate compound adjectives before a noun)
"mear" → "mere"
"can not" → "cannot" (one word in this context)
"lead into gambling" → "led into gambling" (past tense needed)
"educated the uneducated" → awkward repetition; try "educate those without access to schooling"
"how it is used and how you allow it to use you" — this is a nice, memorable closing line! Keep this rhetorical flourish.

General tip: German sentence structure sometimes allows more flexibility with word order than English does. Read your sentences aloud—if they sound clipped or the logic is hard to follow, try breaking them into shorter units or adding connecting words like "however," "therefore," "in contrast," or "as a result."

Strengths to Keep

Your final line is genuinely effective and quotable—it captures your nuanced thesis in one memorable sentence.
You handle a complex, abstract topic with a clear personal voice.
Your movement from "money causes evil behavior" to "money is neutral; people are the moral agents" shows real philosophical sophistication, even though it needs clearer signaling.

Next Steps

Insert the missing quotation and craft a clear thesis statement in your introduction.
Add transition sentences between paragraphs to clarify your logical progression.
Proofread carefully for the grammar points listed above.
Consider adding one or two concrete examples to strengthen your final argument about money's positive uses.

With these revisions, your essay will move from a good first draft to a polished, persuasive piece of academic writing. Well done on a strong start—I look forward to seeing your revision!

Feedback on "Crime Does Pay"

Thank you for a lively and provocative essay. You've chosen a genuinely interesting angle for an argumentative piece, and your use of concrete numbers to support your position is a good instinct. However, the essay would benefit significantly from revision in several areas: argumentation, structure, and language accuracy. Below are my main observations.

Argumentation: One-Sidedness Weakens Your Case

The most significant issue is that your essay presents only one side of the argument. A strong argumentative essay anticipates and addresses counterarguments — this is what makes a thesis persuasive rather than naïve. Right now, a skeptical reader will immediately think: *What about the risk of arrest, imprisonment, violence, or death? What about the psychological toll, damaged relationships, or the fact that most criminals do NOT retire wealthy?* Ignoring these obvious counterpoints makes your argument seem underdeveloped rather than confident.

Suggestion: Add at least one paragraph acknowledging the risks and costs of criminal life (imprisonment, lack of legal protections, violence, inability to build credit or a stable retirement fund through legitimate means), and then explain *why*, despite these risks, you still believe crime "pays" for at least some criminals. This will make your argument far more convincing.

Logical/Numerical Consistency

Your financial comparisons are the heart of your argument, so they need to be airtight. Currently there are inconsistencies:

You state the criminal "can work one hour a day and make $300.00" — this implies a rate of $300/hour.
Later, you say the criminal works "5 hours per week" and earns "$300.00 per hour," which is consistent with the first claim but creates a huge jump in total earnings that isn't clearly explained or justified with a source.
You never explain *where* these numbers come from. Are they averages? Estimates? Hypothetical? Without any indication of source or plausibility, readers may dismiss the figures as invented for convenience.

Suggestion: Either cite a source (even a general one, like "studies estimate...") or explicitly frame these as illustrative hypothetical figures ("Suppose, for example..."). Also, double-check your arithmetic is presented consistently between paragraphs so readers can follow the logic without confusion.

Structure and Paragraphing

Your essay follows a reasonable overall structure (intro → job security → wages → retirement → conclusion), but a few things could be tightened:

The sentence "Then if we think about the work day of most working people." is a sentence fragment — it lacks a main clause. Consider: "Next, let us consider the work day of most working people."
Transitions like "Then," "And finally" are quite informal for academic writing. Try more analytical connectors: "A second consideration is...", "Finally, one must also examine..."
The conclusion is very brief. Given the complexity of the topic, a stronger concluding paragraph should briefly restate your three main points and, ideally, address the counterargument you (hopefully) added.

Language and Grammar

As a German-speaking writer, a few recurring issues are worth flagging:

"it's" vs. "its": "Being a criminal has it's advantages" → should be its (possessive, no apostrophe). This is a very common confusion in English because German doesn't have this distinction.
"rather or not" → "whether or not": This looks like an error carried over from mixing "whether" and "rather." Whether is correct here.
"There is never a lack of work are a means for continuing employment." — this sentence is unclear; likely a typo where "are" should be "or." Consider revising to: "There is never a lack of work or a shortage of means for continued employment."
Comma splices: eg, "If he/she invest half this money and earns interest they could potentially live on the interest income alone" — the subject-verb agreement is off ("he/she invest" should be "invests," "earns" should also match). Watch subject-verb agreement with singular subjects like "he/she" or "one," which can be tricky since German verb conjugation patterns differ.
Number formatting: In English, it's more common to write "$300 per week" rather than "$300.00 per week" in running prose (the decimal is usually reserved for actual invoices/tables). This is a minor style point, but consistent, cleaner formatting improves readability.

Tone

Phrases like "I beg to differ" and "Let's take time to compare" are conversational. This can work in a personal essay, but for an academic argumentative piece, consider a more formal opening, eg, "Contrary to the common assumption, this essay argues that crime can indeed be financially advantageous."

Summary of Priority Revisions

Add a counterargument/rebuttal paragraph.
Clarify and verify your numerical examples.
Fix the sentence fragment and comma-splice issues.
Correct "it's/its" and "rather/whether" errors.
Strengthen the conclusion.
Consider a more formal tone throughout.

Overall, you have an interesting and provocative thesis with real potential — strengthening the logical structure and addressing counterarguments will make it much more persuasive. Good work so far!

Feedback on Your Essay: "Crime Pays"

Overall Impression
Your essay tackles a provocative and complex social issue with a clear, confident voice. You have a genuine flair for vivid description (the opening image of luxury cars and designer clothes is memorable), and your writing has real rhetorical energy. However, the essay currently reads more like a persuasive opinion piece than an academic argument. To strengthen it for university-level work, you need to address issues of evidence, logical structure, and language precision.

Argumentation and Evidence
This is the most important area for revision. Your central claim—that crime pays and that this explains youth involvement in crime—is asserted repeatedly but never actually demonstrated with evidence.

Anecdotal generalization: The essay opens with a personal observation ("drive down any street...you will see") and treats this as proof of a widespread pattern. In academic writing, you need statistics, studies, or cited sources (eg, criminology research on recidivism rates, incarceration data, or economic studies of illegal markets) to support claims like "the shockingly low percentage of criminals who actually end up in jail."
Unsupported causal claims: You assert that police are "far outnumbered" and that citizens "refuse to pay taxes" for more police, but provide no data. These are testable claims—find sources.
Overgeneralization: Phrases like "these criminals are merely working a job" and "it is obvious that crime pays" present a single explanation as though it were the *only* explanation. Consider: not all inner-city youth turn to crime; not all crime is non-violent or profitable; some go to prison for long periods. Acknowledging exceptions and complicating factors (a counterargument paragraph) would make your argument more credible, not weaker.
False dichotomy: The essay implies only two options exist—crime or working at McDonald's for $5/hour. This ignores other paths (education, trade skills, family support, social programs) that a rigorous essay should at least mention and refute.

Suggestion: Add at least two or three cited sources (sociological or criminological) to support your claims about incarceration rates, police funding, and recidivism. This will transform assertions into an argument.

Structure
Your paragraphs move logically from image → cause → consequence → conclusion, which is a reasonable shape. However:
The essay lacks a clear thesis statement early on that frames your argument analytically (right now the "answer" is revealed almost as a punchline: "the answer is simple: crime").
There is no paragraph addressing counterarguments (eg, "One might argue that most crime does not pay because most criminals are eventually caught and punished severely..."). Including and rebutting an opposing view is expected in argumentative essays and will make your reasoning look more balanced and persuasive.
The conclusion mostly repeats the introduction rather than synthesizing your argument or suggesting implications (eg, policy consequences, what should change).

Language and Style
Because you are writing in your second language, a few patterns are worth flagging:

Spelling/typos: "wether" → *whether*; "en in sight" → *end in sight*. Proofread carefully—these are easy to catch on a careful re-read.
Punctuation: There's a stray double period after "hire them. ." — check for formatting errors from editing.
Subject–verb agreement: "The only danger that exists for a criminal come from..." → should be *comes*.
Word choice/register: Words like "rides" (for cars) and phrases like "living larger" are colloquial. In academic writing, use more formal equivalents ("vehicles," "living more affluently").
Rhetorical questions: You use several ("How do these young people acquire the funds?" "What alternative does an inner-city youth have?"). These work well in speeches but can feel manipulative in essays if overused—rely more on direct assertions supported by evidence.
Absolute language: Words like "obvious," "no threat," and "only danger" overstate certainty. Academic writing favors hedged claims ("this suggests," "evidence indicates") unless you can back up the absolute with strong data.

Suggested Revisions
Write a clear thesis sentence stating your specific claim and its scope (eg, "For many low-income urban youth, involvement in non-violent crime represents a rational economic choice given systemic underfunding of policing and limited legal alternatives.")
Add 2–3 credible sources to support key statistical claims.
Insert a paragraph addressing a counterargument.
Proofread carefully for spelling, agreement, and punctuation.
Revise the conclusion to add a forward-looking insight or policy implication rather than restating the introduction.

Your instincts for vivid, persuasive writing are strong — now channel that energy into evidence-based argumentation. This will make your essay far more convincing at the university level.

===== ICLE-US-MICH-0001.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Essay: The Impact of Computers on Society

Overall Impression

You've chosen a genuinely interesting and debatable thesis—that computers simultaneously ease physical burdens while creating mental ones—and this "catch-22" framing gives your essay a promising structure. However, the essay would benefit from tighter organization, more rigorous argumentation, and closer attention to some recurring language issues. Below are specific suggestions organized by area.

Structure and Organization

Your physical/mental division is a good organizing principle, but it isn't consistently applied. In your introduction, you cite job elimination through automation as an effect of computers—yet you then discuss this issue *twice*: first as a "physical effect" (automation of factories) and again as a "mental effect" (worker anxiety about reliability). Job loss itself is arguably a physical/economic phenomenon, while the *emotional response* to it (anger, fear) is mental. Consider separating these more clearly: use the "physical effects" section strictly for changes to labor and daily tasks, and reserve "mental effects" for psychological and cognitive consequences (over-reliance, loss of understanding, anxiety).

Your conclusion introduces solutions (better math education, curtailing "theoretical programming") somewhat abruptly. These are good ideas, but they need to connect explicitly back to the specific problems you raised. For instance, if the problem is that people don't understand *why* their calculations might be wrong, how exactly would more classroom time in philosophy and psychology fix this? Spell out the causal link.

Argumentation and Logic

Some of your claims need clearer reasoning or evidence:

The link between "computers make math easy" and "computers produce errors" is not fully established. You seem to be arguing that ease of use leads to overconfidence, which leads to undetected mistakes—but this chain isn't stated explicitly. Walk the reader through each step.
The claim that mathematical models are "absolutely riddled with errors" is a strong, sweeping statement. Academic writing generally requires either evidence (a specific example, a statistic, a cited source) or more cautious phrasing ("can contain significant errors" rather than "riddled with").
The parenthetical remark about people in "2nd & 3rd world countries" not knowing how abundant computers are feels like a missed opportunity. The digital divide is a substantive issue relevant to your topic—either develop it as a real point or remove it, since as an aside it reads as dismissive.

Language and Style

A few patterns recur that are worth revising:

Comma splices and run-on structures: eg, "The computer has evolved at an incredibly fast pace: from the very first modern computer... there have been many technological advances and also there has been a tremendous impact on society." Consider breaking this into two sentences for clarity.

Overuse of "etc" and vague quantifiers ("many," "much," "numerous"): Academic prose benefits from precision. Instead of "accounting, engineering, and other scientific programs, etc," name specific examples and stop the list there—"etc" often signals to the reader that you haven't thought through the full scope.

Scare quotes: You place quotation marks around ordinary words like "told," "needless," and "variables." In English academic writing, quotation marks usually signal irony, a direct quotation, or a technical/contested term—not just an informal or approximate usage. Ask yourself whether the word truly needs distancing, or whether you can simply commit to it.

Word choice: "if programmed 100% correctly (another perhaps impossible computation)"—here "computation" is the wrong word; you likely mean "task" or "feat." This may be interference from German *Berechnung*, which can extend more broadly than English "computation."

Preposition errors: "ramifications about what they are doing" should be "ramifications *of* what they are doing." This is a common German-English transfer point, since German prepositions often don't map directly onto English ones—worth double-checking preposition choices throughout.

Register: "catch-22" is a vivid, idiomatic phrase, appropriate for informal essays but slightly casual for strict academic register. Consider whether a more formal equivalent ("paradox," "double-edged phenomenon") suits your context—though this is a minor stylistic choice, not an error.

Suggestions for Revision

Reorganize the physical/mental sections so job-related content appears in only one place.
Add at least one concrete example or source to support your claim about computational errors.
Replace vague lists ending in "etc" with two or three well-chosen, fully explained examples.
Revise scare-quoted words—either commit to the term or replace it.
Strengthen the conclusion by explicitly linking each proposed solution to a specific problem raised earlier.

Your argument has real substance—keep refining the connections between your claims and the structure will follow naturally.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Computer's Impact on Society

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting and relevant topic, and you clearly have ideas about how computers affect work and finance. However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of loosely related observations than a unified argument. To strengthen it, focus on: (1) clarifying your thesis, (2) developing your examples more fully, (3) improving transitions between paragraphs, and (4) polishing language issues that likely stem from direct translation from German.

Structure and Argument Development

Thesis Statement: Your introduction claims that "having knowledge of how the computer operates is considered a necessary component of leading a successful life." This is a reasonable thesis, but it's quite broad. Consider narrowing it—are you arguing specifically that computer literacy is essential *for employment*? Your examples (the manager losing his job, banking security) suggest you're really focused on the workplace and financial risks, not "success" in general. A more precise thesis would help guide your reader (and you) through the essay.

Paragraph 2 (The Manager): This is your strongest paragraph because it has a concrete narrative. However, notice the phrase "his inadvertent refusal to learn" — this is logically contradictory. A refusal is a deliberate choice; you cannot "inadvertently" refuse something. You likely mean that he didn't foresee the consequences of his choice. Revise for clarity: *"His decision not to learn computer skills—made without knowing the company would switch to computerized inventory—ultimately cost him his job."*

Also, this paragraph would benefit from more analysis. Right now, you tell the story but don't explicitly connect it back to your thesis. Add a sentence explaining *why* this case proves your point: eg, "This case demonstrates that even long-term, loyal employees are vulnerable if they lack basic computer skills."

Paragraph 3 (Banking): This paragraph feels disconnected from the previous one. The transition "The banking field is another area computers have come to dominate" works structurally, but the content shifts from *individual job security* to *systemic risks like hacking and financial errors*. This is a different kind of argument—is your essay about how computers threaten jobs, or about how computers create new risks/responsibilities for everyone? You need to clarify how these two ideas relate to your central thesis. Perhaps reframe the thesis to encompass both: computer literacy is necessary both to *keep your job* and to *protect your own financial security*.

This paragraph is also underdeveloped—only three sentences. Consider adding a specific example (a hacking case, a personal account error) to match the level of detail in Paragraph 2.

Conclusion: Your final paragraph is only one sentence and doesn't sufficiently synthesize your argument. A strong conclusion should briefly restate your thesis, summarize the key examples, and perhaps offer a broader implication (eg, what should individuals or society do?). Expand this significantly.

Language and Style Notes

A few specific corrections and suggestions:

"twenthieth" → *twentieth* (spelling)
"auto-pasts company" → *auto-parts company*
"succesful" → *successful*
"defecit" → This should likely be *liability* or *detriment*, not *deficit* (a deficit refers to a shortage of money, not a person being a burden).
"persuasive discoveries" → Perhaps you mean *influential* or *significant* discoveries; "persuasive" usually applies to arguments, not inventions.
"internet working of large corporations" → This phrasing is unclear. Do you mean *the networking systems of large corporations*?
"A vivid illustration of this can be found by examining the work" → "the work" is missing context (whose work? what kind of work?). Try: *"A vivid illustration of this can be found in the modern workplace."*

These are the kinds of small but noticeable errors that often occur when translating idiomatic phrases directly from German. I'd recommend reading your sentences aloud and asking whether each noun phrase would make sense to someone with no context.

Suggestions for Revision

Sharpen your thesis to explicitly cover both employment and personal financial risk (or choose one focus).
Expand each paragraph with more specific details, especially the banking paragraph.
Add analytical sentences after each example explaining how it supports your thesis—don't leave the reader to infer the connection.
Strengthen the conclusion by summarizing your argument and suggesting practical implications.
Proofread carefully for spelling and word-choice errors, ideally using a dictionary to check words like "deficit" that may be "false friends" with German vocabulary.

With these revisions, your essay will move from a series of examples toward a cohesive, well-supported argument. You have good instincts for using real-world illustrations—now it's a matter of tying them tightly to a clear central claim.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Impact of Computers on Modern Life

Overall Impression

You've chosen an engaging, relevant topic and have clear personal opinions about it. Your essay shows a genuine argumentative stance—you're not just listing facts, but evaluating them. This is a strong foundation. However, the essay needs work in three main areas: structure/organization, grammar and word choice, and argument development. Below, I'll address each area with specific suggestions.

Structure and Organization

Your introduction currently repeats the same idea three times (computers are new, computers change lives, computers have good/bad effects) without adding new information each time. Consider tightening this to 2-3 sentences that clearly state your thesis: computers have transformed life in both positive and negative ways.

Your two body paragraphs work well as a "positive vs. negative" structure, but each one tries to do too much. In your negative paragraph, you touch on *four* separate concerns (job loss, dehumanization, over-reliance/safety, emotional limitations) without fully developing any single one. Suggestion: Choose your two or three strongest points and dedicate a full paragraph (or clear sub-section) to each, with a concrete example. For instance, expand the "driver relying on computer" example—give a specific scenario, then explain the consequence.

Your conclusion introduces a new idea (medical technology saving lives) that isn't discussed anywhere else in the essay. Save new points for the body, and use your conclusion to synthesize what you've already argued.

Grammar, Spelling, and Word Choice

Since you're a native German speaker, some of these patterns are recognizable (eg, loanword spelling, article usage). Here are the recurring issues:

Spelling:
"Interraction" → *Interaction* (single "r")
"obstical" → *obstacle*
"live" → *life* (typo, likely from typing speed)

Subject-verb agreement:
"people's life styles" — later you write "changes... in the lifestyle" (singular) vs. "people's lives" (plural). Be consistent: are you discussing one collective "lifestyle" or many individual "lives"?
"Computers have also made live transpire much quicker" — this sentence is unclear. Do you mean "life moves more quickly"? "Transpire" usually means "to become known" or "to happen," not "to progress." Consider: *"Computers have also made daily life move much faster."*

Article usage (a known challenge from German, which uses articles differently):
"We now have ATM" → "We now have the ATM" or better, "We now have ATMs"
"a person who wanted money from a bank had to wait until it was open" — this is correct, but check similar constructions throughout for missing articles before nouns.

Word choice / false friends:
"proceed with the same transactions" — *proceed* means "to continue an action already started." You likely mean "process" transactions.
"the invention of the computer" (used twice in paragraph 1) — try varying this phrase to avoid repetition: "this technology," "its emergence," etc

Punctuation:
You have inconsistent spacing before punctuation (eg, "improved people's lives drastically.  No longer") — check for double spaces or missing spaces uniformly.
Overuse of quotation marks for emphasis ("fast enough," "impersonalized," "slow") — in academic English, italics or simply strong word choice is preferred over scare quotes.

Argument Development and Critical Thinking

Your ideas are promising but often stated rather than *argued*. For example:
"Interaction with humans in some places is extinct" — this is a strong claim. Can you give a specific example (eg, self-checkout machines, automated customer service) to support it?
"Computers are not able to express and truly feel emotions, and therefore are incapable, in some instances to function" — this sentence is logically unclear. Do you mean computers *fail* in situations requiring emotional judgment (eg, healthcare, counseling)? Clarify the connection between "lacking emotion" and "incapable to function."

Suggestion: For each claim, ask yourself "Can I give one concrete example?" This will also help you meet academic writing conventions, which value evidence over general assertions.

Priority Revisions

Rewrite the introduction — cut repetition, state a clear thesis.
Choose 2-3 focused arguments for the negative paragraph and develop each with an example.
Proofread for the specific errors listed above (spelling, "transaction/process," article use).
Remove the new idea in the conclusion or introduce it earlier and develop it properly.

A Note of Encouragement

Your English is quite fluent, and your ideas are thoughtful and timely—especially your point about over-reliance on technology reducing critical thinking. With some structural tightening and attention to the grammar points above, this essay will be much stronger. Keep writing with this level of personal engagement; it's a real strength in argumentative writing.

Feedback Report on Your Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an important and timely topic—cultural awareness and the fight against prejudice—and it's clear you feel passionately about it. This passion comes through in your writing. However, the essay would benefit from clearer structure, more concrete evidence, and careful proofreading. Below are suggestions organized by area.

Content and Argument Development

The "discovery" framing needs strengthening. Your introduction sets up an essay about "discoveries" from the 20th century, but the body paragraphs discuss cultural awareness as an ongoing *social process* rather than a discrete discovery. You acknowledge this tension yourself in the final paragraph ("Unlearning prejudice may not seem like a traditional discovery..."). Consider addressing this directly and earlier: explain *why* you consider increased cultural awareness a "discovery" comparable to, say, a scientific breakthrough. This would make your unconventional choice of topic feel intentional rather than like an afterthought.

Claims need evidence. Right now, the essay makes broad, abstract assertions—racism is "inherent," media is full of "discriminative language," organizations are "presently being held" to address these issues—without specific examples. Ask yourself:
*Which* media examples illustrate racist or sexist representation?
*Which* historical figures or movements challenged stereotypes courageously? (You could mention specific civil rights leaders, suffragists, etc)
*What* organizations or initiatives are you referring to in your conclusion?

Adding even two or three concrete examples would transform your essay from a series of general statements into a persuasive argument.

The opening paragraph is too general for an argumentative essay. The first few sentences ("the most significant discovery will depend on the individual...") read as a preamble that could apply to any topic. Consider cutting this and moving directly into your specific argument, perhaps with a strong opening claim like: "Among the many discoveries of the 20th century, the growing awareness of cultural difference stands out as one of the most transformative—not because it changed technology, but because it changed how humans see one another."

Structure

Your essay currently has three paragraphs: introduction, one long body paragraph, and a brief conclusion. I recommend expanding to 4–5 paragraphs:
Introduction with a clear, specific thesis
Body paragraph 1: The problem (historical/ongoing discrimination) with examples
Body paragraph 2: The shift/progress (how awareness is changing) with examples
Body paragraph 3 (optional): Why this matters for the future
Conclusion that circles back to your thesis and the "discovery" framing

This would let each idea breathe and give you space to develop evidence.

Language and Grammar

As a German speaker, you may notice some patterns worth watching for. Here are specific corrections:

"peoples lives" → people's lives (possessive apostrophe)
"more then" → more than (this is a common English spelling confusion, unrelated to German)
"discriminitive" → discriminatory
"has been excepted" → has been accepted (different word entirely—"except" vs. "accept")
"enought" → enough
"deeply breed sterotypes" → deeply bred stereotypes (or perhaps you mean "deeply ingrained")
"burried" → buried
"forms are presently being held" → did you mean forums? "Forms" and "forums" are easy to confuse.
"avalibility" → availability
"existance" → existence

A note on sentence-level German interference: Some of your sentences show German syntax patterns, such as placing extra clauses before the main verb ("Cultural and racial equality is a new term that as a society we are striving for"). In English, it often reads more naturally to keep the subject and verb closer together: "As a society, we are striving toward cultural and racial equality." Watch for this pattern throughout—try reading sentences aloud to hear whether the word order sounds natural in English.

Also check: the recurring use of "we" and "society" as vague collective subjects. Sometimes specifying *who* ("scholars," "activists," "younger generations") will strengthen your claims.

Revision Priorities

If you revise this essay, I suggest focusing on:
Sharpening your thesis to justify why cultural awareness counts as a "discovery."
Adding 2–3 concrete examples (historical figures, specific media, named organizations) to support your claims.
Restructuring into more, shorter paragraphs for clarity.
Proofreading carefully for the spelling and word-choice errors listed above—reading your essay aloud can help catch these.

You clearly have strong convictions about this topic—now the goal is to give your reader the concrete evidence and structure needed to be persuaded by them. I'd be happy to look at a revised draft.
