Feedback on Your Essay

Overall Impression

You are tackling an ambitious and genuinely interesting argument: that the American ideal of equal opportunity is a myth reinforced by media, and that this myth obscures real inequality in an increasingly fast-paced, individualistic society. This is a strong, debatable thesis with real intellectual substance. However, the essay currently reads more like a series of loosely connected observations than a structured argument. Below are suggestions for revision, organized from big-picture issues to smaller ones.

Clarify Your Thesis

Your opening sentence tries to pack in too many ideas at once (the "ideology," equal opportunity, personal failure, and media all at once), which makes it hard for a reader to know exactly what you will argue. Try breaking this into two moves:
First, state the ideology clearly and concisely: eg, "American culture promotes the belief that success results from individual effort and that opportunity is equally distributed."
Second, state your claim about this belief: eg, "This belief is a myth that mass media perpetuates, masking structural inequalities that a faster-paced, more individualistic society makes easier to ignore."

This second sentence is close to your real thesis — but right now it only appears as a fragment at the very end of the essay ("the parts of the new machine that need oiled and regulated"). Bring this idea forward and state it explicitly early on.

Strengthen the Connection Between Paragraphs

Right now, paragraph 1 discusses ideology/media and paragraph 2 shifts to technology/pace of life without a clear transition. Ask yourself: how exactly does technology relate to the myth of equal opportunity? Your intuition — that faster living makes inequality easier to overlook — is compelling, but you need a transition sentence that makes this link explicit for the reader, rather than assuming they will infer it.

Add Concrete Evidence

You mention "T.V. commercials & programs" and "popular media" but never give a specific example. Academic argument is strengthened by concrete illustrations: Which commercial? Which kind of program? What image of success does it show? Even one specific, described example (a commercial, a show, a slogan) would make your claim far more persuasive than a general statement.

Similarly, the claim that "nothing was ever equal" regarding America's founding values is a big historical claim dropped in without support. Either develop it with a specific example (eg, property qualifications for voting, exclusion of certain groups) or remove it, since as written it feels like an assertion rather than an argument.

Fix the Fragment at the End

"The parts of the new machine that need oiled and regulated" is not a complete sentence — it has no verb performing an action, and it isn't clear whom or what it modifies. This seems to be your most important image (a machine metaphor for society), so it deserves a full, developed sentence, perhaps even its own concluding paragraph, eg: "The disadvantaged become the parts of this new machine that most need oiling and regulation, yet their voices are drowned out by the louder, faster voices of mass media."

Language: The Pronoun "One"

I notice you use "one" very frequently and in ways that feel influenced by the German impersonal pronoun *man*. In English, "one" is grammatically correct but sounds very formal and becomes repetitive and stiff when overused. Vary your phrasing: use "people," "individuals," "we," passive constructions, or restructure sentences with a specific subject. For example:
Instead of "one can see these ideas in popular media" → "These ideas appear throughout popular media."
Instead of "one no longer sees the relative humanness between people" → "People no longer perceive their shared humanity."

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

A few things to fix in your next draft:
Spelling: comercials → commercials; substitued → substituted; nolonger → no longer; realitive → relative; unsuccessfull → unsuccessful; thier → their
Abbreviations: avoid "T.V." and "20th cen" in formal writing — write "television" and "the twentieth century."
Editing marks left in text: "the values of/that" — this looks like an unresolved revision; choose one option.
Run-on/comma splices: Several sentences string together independent clauses with commas only (eg, "one sees the differences, the unlucky, the unsuccessful, and attribute their inadequacy..."). Break these into shorter, clearer sentences, or use semicolons/conjunctions correctly.
Verb form: "need oiled" should be "need to be oiled."

Suggested Next Steps

Rewrite your thesis as two clear, connected sentences.
Add a transition sentence linking media/ideology to technology/pace of life.
Insert at least one concrete, described example (a specific commercial or show).
Turn your final fragment into a full concluding sentence or paragraph.
Do a "search and revise" pass specifically for the word "one" and vary your phrasing.
Proofread for the spelling and mechanical issues listed above.

You have a genuinely compelling argument here — the revision work now is mostly about making the structure and evidence match the sophistication of your idea. Well done for tackling such a complex topic.

Feedback on Your Essay: Technology as Friend and Enemy

Overall Impression

You write with real passion and a strong personal voice, and your opening metaphor of technology as both "closest friend" and "greatest Nemesis" is a compelling hook. Your personal anecdotes (your grandmother's cancer treatment, the children asking about Spain and Michigan) are vivid and memorable. However, the essay currently reads as two loosely connected essays rather than one unified argument. The revision should focus on clarifying your central claim and making sure every paragraph visibly supports it.

Argument and Structure

The core problem: Your introduction promises an essay about technology's double-edged nature. Your second paragraph delivers on this promise beautifully. But from the third paragraph onward, you shift to a different argument—American educational isolationism (lack of geography and foreign-language instruction)—without ever clearly explaining *how this connects to technology*. Right now, a reader has to guess at the link. Is your argument:

That technology has made Americans complacent, believing they don't need to learn about the wider world because information is always available at their fingertips?
That technological convenience has replaced curiosity?
Something else entirely?

You need one explicit sentence—probably at the start of paragraph three—that states this causal link directly. For example: "One consequence of this technological self-sufficiency is a growing insularity in American education." Without this bridge, the essay feels like it changes topics rather than deepening one argument.

Missing conclusion: The essay ends abruptly on the sentence about your country "lagging behind." Consider adding a final paragraph that returns to your opening metaphor (friend/enemy) and explains what this specific example (education, isolation) teaches us about that duality.

Evidence and reasoning: Your personal experience is compelling evidence, but you assert the technology-isolationism connection more than you argue it. Consider adding at least one sentence of reasoning or a source that explains *why* technological convenience might reduce curiosity about other cultures—otherwise, the claim risks feeling like a personal opinion rather than a supported argument.

Language and Style

Several sentences would benefit from revision for clarity:

Pronoun ambiguity: "Alone, they do not threaten us, but when they become welded with certain aspects of and certain people in our societies..." — "they" refers back to "the means humans have developed," but this is several clauses away. Consider restating: "Alone, these tools do not threaten us..."

Unclear/garbled sentence: "The effort of the United States has caused our minds to be heard, and a world that is growing while my country lags behind in its selfish self-satisfaction." This sentence is difficult to parse—I suspect "heard" may be a slip for "closed" or "narrowed"? Also, the grammatical structure breaks down ("a world that is growing" doesn't connect logically to what precedes it). Please rewrite this sentence entirely, as it currently obscures rather than delivers your concluding point.

Capitalization (likely German interference): In German, all nouns are capitalized, and this pattern appears here: "Nemesis" and "Geography" are capitalized in English where they shouldn't be (unless "Nemesis" is being used as a personification/proper name, which could work if done consistently and deliberately—but then it should be signaled, eg, "technology has been our Nemesis" without "the greatest"). Do a careful pass checking every capitalized noun.

Punctuation with dashes: "the same 'I am a self-sufficient- I don't need any help' type of mentality" — the dash placement is inconsistent. Try: "the same 'I am self-sufficient—I don't need any help' mentality" (note also: "self-sufficient" doesn't take an article "a" before the adjective in this construction).

Sentence fragments/run-ons: "This thing, technology has been and will always be..." — consider a comma or colon: "This thing, technology, has been..."

Concrete Suggestions for Revision

Add a transitional/thesis sentence at the start of paragraph 3 that explicitly links technology to the educational isolationism you describe.
Rewrite the final sentence completely for grammatical clarity.
Add a concluding paragraph that returns to the "friend/enemy" metaphor from your introduction.
Proofread for capitalization of common nouns (a common transfer error from German).
Consider whether you want to keep both the "American self-sufficiency" argument and the "lack of geography/language education" argument, or focus more tightly on one and use the other as a single supporting example rather than a second major point.

Final Note

Your voice and personal investment in this topic are real strengths—don't lose them in revision. The main task now is architectural: build a clearer bridge between your beautiful opening metaphor and the American education material, and give the essay a conclusion that closes the circle you opened.

Feedback on Your Essay: "How Could People Live Without the Invention of the Computer?"

Thank you for sharing this essay. You've chosen a relevant and interesting topic, and your English is generally clear and readable. Below is feedback organized by key areas, along with suggestions for revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your introduction poses a question and states that "computers have significantly changed people's lives." This is a reasonable starting point, but it is quite general. A strong argumentative essay needs a specific, debatable claim that the rest of the essay works to prove. Currently, your essay reads more like a descriptive report on computer usage than an argument.

Suggestion: Sharpen your thesis. For example: *"While computers have made daily tasks more efficient, they have also created a problematic dependency that leaves people vulnerable when technology fails."* This would give your essay a clearer argumentative edge and connect your body paragraphs to your interesting conclusion about breakdowns and dependency.

Structure and Paragraph Coherence

Your paragraphs each address a sub-topic (presence of computers, efficiency, job skills, dependency), but they read as a list of separate points rather than a connected argument. Each paragraph tends to repeat the same idea (computers are useful/efficient) without building on the previous one.

Suggestion: 
Consider organizing around a clearer progression: (1) computers are everywhere, (2) they bring major benefits, (3) but this creates dependency, (4) which has real consequences when computers fail. 
Your final paragraph actually contains your most original and interesting idea (dependency and vulnerability). Consider moving this idea earlier and letting it guide the whole essay, rather than introducing it only at the end.

Development and Evidence

The essay currently relies on general statements ("computers are useful," "computers make things more efficient") without specific examples, evidence, or elaboration. For an argumentative essay, you need concrete support.

Suggestions:
Instead of "Schools use computers to teach children because it is fun and exciting," give a specific example: what kind of learning software? What skills do students gain?
Instead of "Businesses use computers because they can be more productive," could you name a specific task (eg, spreadsheets, communication, inventory tracking) and explain *how* this changed compared to before computers?
Your final paragraph raises a great point about vulnerability when computers break down — but this claim needs an example (eg, a business losing data, a hospital unable to access patient records, a store unable to process payments). This would make your argument much more persuasive.

Repetition

There is considerable repetition of the same idea using slightly different words — especially the claim that computers make things faster/easier/more efficient. This appears in paragraphs 2, 3, and 4. Try to make sure each paragraph adds *new* information or a *new angle* rather than restating the same point.

Suggestion: After drafting, go through and ask: "What new idea does this sentence contribute?" If a sentence only repeats a previous point, either cut it or replace it with a specific example or a counterpoint.

Language and Style Notes (for a German-speaking writer)

A few small language issues to watch for:

"laboreous" → should be "laborious"
"speed-up" → in English, this is usually not hyphenated as a verb: "speed up" (verb) vs. "speed-up" (noun). Use "computers speed up tasks."
Comma splices/run-ons: eg, "This is when it becomes evident that people are very dependent on computers when computers do break down." Consider breaking this into two clearer sentences.
Article usage: "In people's homes calculators as usually found" seems to have a typo — did you mean "are usually found"? Also check article usage before "calculators" and "personal computers" (German does not always require articles where English does, so this is a common area of difficulty for German speakers).
Closing quotation mark missing at the very end of the essay after "computer?"

Introduction and Conclusion

Your introduction and conclusion both end with the same rhetorical question, which creates nice symmetry. However, since your essay doesn't strongly build toward this question throughout, the ending feels somewhat disconnected from the middle. If you sharpen your thesis around dependency (as suggested above), this closing question will feel like a natural, powerful conclusion rather than a repeated rhetorical flourish.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Develop a clearer, more specific thesis about computers and dependency.
Reduce repetition — make sure each paragraph adds something new.
Add concrete examples and evidence to support general claims.
Reorganize so your strongest, most original idea (dependency/vulnerability) shapes the whole essay, not just the ending.
Proofread for the small grammar and spelling issues noted above.

You have a genuinely interesting core idea here — the tension between the *benefits* of computers and the *dependency/vulnerability* they create. Focusing your revision on developing that idea with specific examples will make this a much stronger argumentative essay. Good work — keep refining!

Feedback Report

Thank you for sharing this ambitious and thought-provoking essay. You are clearly grappling with big ideas—the philosophical implications of relativity, the connection between scientific paradigms and human psychology, and the political consequences of nuclear weapons. This intellectual ambition is a real strength. However, the essay currently struggles to communicate these ideas clearly, and several revisions would significantly strengthen your argument. Below I offer feedback organized by category.

Argument Structure and Clarity

Your central claim seems to be that relativity has profound but underappreciated philosophical implications, and that humanity has so far only applied Einstein's ideas destructively (atomic weapons) rather than transformatively (a shift from a fear-based to a peace-based worldview). This is a compelling thesis, but it needs to be stated explicitly, ideally in your opening paragraph, so the reader knows where the essay is heading.

Currently, the essay moves associatively—from the abstract nature of relativity, to Socratic philosophy, to your personal experience of time, to the atomic bomb, to global politics—without clear transitional logic connecting these sections. Each paragraph raises interesting points, but the *connections between them* need to be made explicit. For example: How exactly does the shattering of "the philosopher's stone" (a striking image, by the way) lead to your discussion of atomic bombs? You need a bridge sentence that shows this causal or logical link.

Suggestion: After drafting, try writing a one-sentence summary of each paragraph's function. If you cannot connect these summaries into a logical chain, the essay's structure needs more work.

Precision of Language and Claims

Some claims are stated too briefly to be persuasive. For instance:
"It set up a whole network of global systems" is a very large claim compressed into one sentence. Which systems? How did the bomb specifically cause them?
"Namely fear!" is a strong rhetorical move, but you need at least a sentence explaining *how* fear became "the main point of departure for global community construction."

Academic argumentation requires you to slow down and unpack big claims rather than asserting them and moving on. Choose two or three of your most important claims and develop them with more explanation or evidence.

Use of Sources and Names

You mention Einstein's wife in a bracketed note but don't develop this point—if you want to credit Mileva Marić's possible contributions to the theory, this deserves a sentence of its own, properly explained, not a parenthetical aside. Similarly, "sidphantha and his rock" is unclear to the reader (I assume you mean Sisyphus, or perhaps Siddhartha?). Please clarify this reference, as currently it may confuse rather than illuminate your point.

Also, check the spelling of proper nouns throughout: Einstein (not "Einstien"), Stephen Hawking (not "Hawkings").

Language and Mechanics

Since you're writing in a second language, a few patterns are worth flagging:

Article use: German and English handle articles differently. Watch phrases like "a topic which has volumes, volumes a literature" (I think you mean "volumes of literature" or "many volumes of literature").

False friends / word choice: "Personally, I find" is fine, but phrases like "indwell the topic" are unusual in English—did you mean "immerse myself in" or "dwell on"?

Punctuation: You use semicolons somewhat unconventionally (eg, "USA-USSR; with Japan..."). In English, semicolons typically join two independent clauses. Consider using colons, dashes, or simply restructuring these into full sentences.

Capitalization: "socratic" should be "Socratic" (proper adjective from a name).

Sentence fragments: Several sentences are fragments (eg, "The socratic idea that all was known.") While fragments can be used for stylistic emphasis in some contexts, in academic writing they should be used sparingly and deliberately, not as a default sentence structure.

Voice and Style

Your writing has a distinctive, almost poetic energy—phrases like "pulverized into utopic and nostalgic dust" are vivid and memorable. Don't lose this voice in revision! But balance this poetic quality with clearer argumentative scaffolding, especially in an academic context where readers expect explicit signposting.

Concrete Next Steps

Write an explicit thesis statement for your introduction.
Add transitional sentences between paragraphs that explain the logical connections.
Expand your discussion of the atomic bomb's political consequences with more specific detail.
Clarify the Sisyphus/Siddhartha reference.
Proofread for proper noun spelling and article usage.
Consider having a native speaker or writing center tutor review sentence-level grammar after you've revised the structure.

You have genuinely interesting ideas here—the next draft should focus on making them accessible and persuasive to your reader.

Feedback on "The Impact of Shopping Malls on Kalamazoo"

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting and locally grounded topic, and your personal connection to Kalamazoo gives the argument authenticity. You show a genuine understanding of urban economic history — the shift from centralized cities to car-dependent suburbs. However, the essay needs stronger organization, a clearer thesis, and more careful editing to fully convey your argument to an academic reader. Below are suggestions organized by area.

Thesis and Argument

Your thesis — that malls are "more of a detriment than a blessing" — is a good starting point, but it appears only briefly in the introduction and isn't sharply defined. What exactly is the harm you want to prove? Right now, the essay drifts between describing *how* malls developed and stating *why* this is harmful. Consider revising your thesis to make the causal claim explicit, eg: "The rise of suburban shopping malls has drained economic resources from downtown Kalamazoo, disproportionately harming low-income residents who cannot follow businesses to the suburbs."

Your central argument — that wealthier residents left downtown, took businesses with them, and left the poor without access to goods, services, or investment — is compelling, but it needs more support. Right now, it relies heavily on assertion ("I believe," "Everyone was near the central city") rather than evidence. Try to include specific examples: What businesses left downtown Kalamazoo? What data exists on downtown vacancy rates or poverty concentration? Even brief, concrete references would strengthen your credibility.

Structure and Paragraphing

The essay would benefit from clearer paragraph divisions tied to distinct ideas. Currently, paragraph two contains the historical background, the causal mechanism (wealth flight), a rhetorical question, and a claim about business owners — all crowded together. Consider breaking this into three shorter paragraphs:
Historical development of Kalamazoo's downtown
The mechanism of "wealth flight" to the suburbs
The consequences for those left behind

Also, avoid inserting isolated exclamatory fragments like "and still could be!" as separate lines — these break the flow and feel more like an emotional aside than part of the argument. If you want to make this point, integrate it into a full sentence within the paragraph.

Tone and Voice

The rhetorical question ("Why should we, say those with cars, go downtown...") is a nice attempt to bring the reader into the mindset of suburban shoppers, but it shifts the essay's voice from analytical to conversational quite abruptly. In academic writing, it's often more effective to *describe* this reasoning rather than voice it directly: "Suburban residents with cars had little incentive to shop downtown when malls offered greater convenience under one roof."

Similarly, watch your use of "I believe" — while appropriate for an argumentative essay, overusing it can make your claims feel more like opinions than reasoned conclusions. Try phrasing some claims more assertively: "This shift left downtown businesses without a stable customer base," rather than "I believe it is only the storeowners who realize..."

Language and Grammar

A few recurring issues to watch for (these are common patterns for German speakers writing in English):

False friends / word choice: "clientel" → *clientele*; "the flu from the inner cities" → likely you mean *the flight from the inner cities* (a false friend with German "Flucht"?). Check this carefully, as "flu" changes the meaning significantly.
Article usage: "the invention of the shopping mall" is correct, but watch phrases like "There neighborhoods" — this should be *Their neighborhoods* (a homophone error, not really a German-interference issue, but worth double-checking).
Sentence fragments and run-ons: Several sentences start with "And" or "While" and lack clear main clauses (eg, "And, the executives and business people rely on commuting..."). Combine or restructure these for smoother academic prose.
Subject-verb agreement: "the could keep a distance" → *they could keep a distance*.
Comma splices: eg, "Downtown Kalamazoo was once a thriving and successful district. Near the train station were the larger industries, and other essential specialized stores..." Some of these could be combined more smoothly with subordinating conjunctions.

I recommend reading your essay aloud, or having someone else read it aloud to you, to catch these fragments and unclear referents.

Conclusion

Your conclusion effectively returns to the human cost of the mall's rise, which is good. However, it introduces new evaluative language ("majority would") without clear support. Consider ending with a clearer call to action or a restated thesis that ties together your causal argument and its social consequences.

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Sharpen and consistently maintain your thesis.
Add concrete evidence or examples specific to Kalamazoo.
Reorganize paragraphs around single, clear ideas.
Revise tone to maintain academic consistency (avoid rhetorical asides).
Proofread carefully for word choice, agreement, and fragment issues.

You have a strong foundation and a genuinely interesting argument — with revision, this essay could be both clearer and more persuasive.

Feedback on Your Essay: Television and Society

Overall Impression

You have chosen an engaging topic and taken a clear stance on it. Your English is generally strong, and you communicate your ideas with confidence. The essay would benefit most from tightening the argument, developing your points with more concrete evidence, and correcting some recurring language issues.

Thesis and Argument

Your central claim—that television has become an "escape route" rather than an educational tool—is clear and debatable, which is good for an argumentative essay. However, there is a slight tension in your argument that needs addressing: in your body paragraphs you argue that television makes people passive and disconnects families, but in your conclusion you also claim it has made society "more informed." These two ideas are not necessarily contradictory, but you need to explicitly reconcile them. Right now it reads as if you are contradicting your own thesis. Consider clarifying: Is television both informative *and* harmful? Or has its potential for good been overshadowed by its harmful use? Make this relationship explicit early in the essay (in your introduction) so the reader knows exactly what you will argue.

Structure and Development

The essay follows a logical order (potential of TV → actual use of TV → consequences → conclusion), but each paragraph would be stronger with more specific, concrete support. Currently, your claims remain quite general and abstract:

"Millions of Americans can be found staring at the T.V." — Do you have any statistics, studies, or even illustrative examples (a specific study, a well-known statistic about average viewing hours, a famous example of a show or event) to support this?
"T.V. is a major cause of lack of togetherness" — This is a strong causal claim. Can you explain the mechanism (eg, family members watching separately in different rooms, or watching instead of talking) or cite a source?

Academic argumentative writing is more persuasive when general claims are followed by specific evidence—an example, a statistic, a quotation, or a clearly reasoned illustration. Right now, your essay relies mainly on assertions. I'd recommend adding at least one concrete example or piece of evidence per body paragraph.

Additionally, your second paragraph lists television's *potential* benefits in some detail, but then dismisses them quickly ("However, this is not the way..."). Since your argument depends on contrasting potential vs. reality, spend a little more time showing *why* the potential remains unrealized (eg, is it a market/ratings problem? A network incentive to entertain rather than educate?). This would deepen your analysis beyond simple assertion.

Conclusion

Your conclusion largely restates the introduction and body without adding new insight. This is a common structure, but try to move beyond summary—perhaps end with a brief reflection on what this means for the future, or a suggestion (eg, how society might use television differently). Also, note the typo "00 years ago"—this should presumably read "a hundred years ago" or "one hundred years ago."

Language and Style Notes

Your English is impressively fluent, but here are recurring issues to watch for (common patterns for German speakers writing in English):

Spelling/false friends: "integrety" → *integrity*; "dificulties" → *difficulties*; "becomed" → *became* (irregular verb).
Pronoun agreement: "The more time one spends watching TV, the less motivated he becomes" — since you started with "one," stay consistent: "...the less motivated one becomes" or shift to "people... they."
Comparative structures: "a closer look at current events than cannot be given by newspaper" — this sentence is grammatically tangled. Likely you mean: "a closer look at current events than newspapers can provide."
Article use: English articles are often tricky for German speakers since German uses them differently. Watch phrases like "the television" vs. "television" as a general concept—you use both, so pick one consistent approach when speaking generally (usually no article: "television has changed society," not "the television").
Word choice/register: "stare at a box" is a vivid, informal phrase—effective for emphasis, but make sure your essay maintains a consistent academic register elsewhere. A few more formal, analytical sentences would balance this out.
Comma splices: Some sentences run two independent clauses together with only a comma (eg, in your final sentence). Check each sentence with a comma to see if it should instead be a semicolon or a new sentence.

Priorities for Revision

Clarify the relationship between "informed" and "lazy/disconnected" in your thesis.
Add concrete evidence/examples to strengthen each body paragraph.
Proofread for the specific errors listed above (spelling, pronoun agreement, comparative structures).
Expand your conclusion beyond summary.

You have a strong foundation here—these revisions will make your argument sharper and more convincing.

Feedback on Your Essay on the Polio Vaccine

Thank you for sharing this draft. Your topic is compelling, and I can see genuine engagement with the subject. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas: argumentative clarity, use of evidence, and language precision. Below is detailed feedback organized by category.

Thesis and Argument

Your central claim—that the polio vaccine was one of the most significant inventions of the 20th century—is a strong, arguable thesis. However, it gets lost. The introduction ends with a rhetorical question ("Why was it so great?") followed by two vague claims ("It changed the population and acted as a foundation for further medical advances"). These need to be stated as a clear thesis sentence that previews your actual argument, and each body paragraph should demonstrably support one of these claims.

More importantly, your reasoning about the Baby Boom is contradictory. You write that "the vaccination did not CAUSE the boom," but then argue it may have "contributed" to it, and later assert that disease normally functions as population control that the vaccine disrupted. This is an interesting idea, but you present it as speculation ("I'm sure," "for each individual to decide") rather than an argued claim. Academic writing requires you to commit to a position and support it—not leave the conclusion up to the reader. Either research this connection (there is historical demography on this) or reframe the paragraph as a more modest claim about survival rates rather than population growth in general.

Evidence

This is the essay's biggest weakness: there is no historical evidence—no dates, statistics, names of sources, or quotations. For an argumentative essay on a historical/medical topic, you need concrete support:
When exactly was the vaccine developed and released (1955)?
What were mortality/paralysis rates before and after?
What is the actual scholarly consensus on vaccines and population growth?
What did the vaccine's success enable in later medical research (your "foundation" claim)?

Right now the essay relies on general impressions ("many people have been rescued") and personal anecdote (your generation's experience with AIDS vs. polio). This anecdote is a nice rhetorical device for showing generational contrast, but it cannot substitute for historical evidence and should be used briefly, not as a main body of a paragraph.

Structure

The introduction tries to do too much at once (disease history, praise of Salk, thesis) without a clear logical sequence. Consider: one sentence on the historical problem of disease, one on Salk's breakthrough, then a clear thesis with two supporting claims.
The second paragraph shifts between personal reflection, population theory, and vaccine mechanics. Split this into separate paragraphs, each with a clear topic sentence.
The conclusion is only three sentences and does not synthesize your argument; it simply restates that Salk was helpful. Expand this to review your two main claims and explain their broader significance (this is also where you could gesture toward the "foundation for further medical advances" claim, which is never actually developed in the body).

Language and Style

Since you are writing in your second language, focus especially on the following recurring issues:

Spelling/typos: "mamed" → *maimed*; "never never" → *never*.
Punctuation: You have several comma splices and inconsistent use of dashes (eg, "For 20 year olds polio has never never been an issue, it is one of the many vaccines..."). Consider breaking into two sentences.
Register: Phrases like "I'm sure," "for us," and rhetorical questions ("Why was it so great?") are conversational and better suited to spoken argument than academic prose. Replace with more formal transitions ("This raises the question of why...").
Capitalization: "AIDS" is correctly capitalized, but "Baby Boom" appears both capitalized and in quotation marks inconsistently—choose one convention and apply it throughout.
Word choice: "inequivalent" is unusual phrasing; consider "unmatched" or "unparalleled." "Torment of disease" is a nice phrase but slightly overwrought for an argumentative essay—watch your balance between vivid language and academic tone.
Sentence fragments: "The process of vaccination also opened new doors" is vague; specify *what* doors (eg, attenuated/killed-virus vaccine research, later vaccines for measles, mumps, rubella, etc).

Next Steps

For your revision, I recommend:
Write a clear thesis statement with two or three specific, defensible claims.
Find 3–4 concrete historical facts or statistics (with citations) about polio incidence before/after 1955.
Research (briefly) the actual demographic literature on vaccines and population growth, rather than speculating.
Reorganize paragraphs so each has one clear focus with a topic sentence.
Proofread carefully for spelling, comma splices, and register consistency.

You have a genuinely interesting argument here—the revision should focus on grounding it in evidence and giving it a clearer, more confident structure. I'd be happy to look at a revised draft.

Feedback on "The Invention of the Airplane" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay has a clear, logical structure and addresses an interesting topic with a good range of examples. You organize your body paragraphs effectively around four distinct domains (travel, business, entertainment, politics), which shows strong planning. However, the essay would benefit from a stronger thesis, more developed argumentation, and careful proofreading. Below are specific suggestions organized by category.

Thesis Statement and Argument

Your introduction states that the airplane has "drastically changed" many lives, but this claim is quite general. For a more compelling argumentative essay, consider sharpening your thesis to make a specific claim you will defend — for example, arguing that the airplane's benefits *outweigh* its costs, or that it has fundamentally *redefined* how humans conceive of distance and time. Right now, your essay reads more as a descriptive survey of effects rather than an argument. Ask yourself: what is the one claim I want my reader to accept by the end?

Paragraph Development

Each body paragraph introduces a good topic but often stops at the example stage without deeper analysis. For instance, in your business paragraph, you state that international business has increased, and mention you have "seen this," but you don't explain *how* or *why* faster air travel causes this growth (eg, enabling face-to-face negotiations, just-in-time supply chains, executive travel). Try adding one or two sentences of analysis after each example — explain the *significance* of the fact, not just the fact itself.

Similarly, the politics paragraph ends abruptly after mentioning Pearl Harbor and the atomic bombings. This is your most complex point (weighing costs against benefits), so it deserves more development, perhaps a full paragraph on its own with a clearer evaluative conclusion: do you think the negative uses of aviation outweigh the positive diplomatic benefits, or not?

Evidence and Precision

Some of your examples are excellent and concrete (Michigan to California driving vs. flying time; Michael Jackson's concert). However, be careful with unsupported personal claims like "I have seen this in the increase in International Business during the past few decades" — this is an assertion, not evidence. Where possible, try to reference a statistic or general knowledge fact rather than personal impression, since this strengthens academic credibility.

Grammar and Language Issues (Common Patterns)

Since English and German differ in some structural ways, here are recurring issues to watch for:

Subject-verb agreement: "these would not take place as often as the do" → should be "as often as *they* do."
Verb tense/form errors: "has been drastically effected" → *affected* (verb) vs. *effect* (noun) — this confusion appears several times ("effected," "Effected" in paragraph 4). Remember: *affect* = verb (to influence); *effect* = noun (a result).
Comparative/superlative and passive constructions: "much easier than previously possibly" → likely meant "than was previously possible."
Article and preposition use: "in their perspective countries" → should be "*respective* countries" (a word confusion, not just an article issue).
Run-on sentences and comma splices: Several sentences join independent clauses only with a comma, eg, "Air transportation allows people to travel from place to place more quickly & efficiently & overseas travel has been drastically effected." Consider breaking this into two sentences or using a semicolon/conjunction properly.
Ampersands (&): Avoid using "&" in formal academic writing; always write "and."
Spelling: "efficeintly" → *efficiently*; "shorten" → *shortened*; "choosen" → *chosen*; "musicans" → *musicians*; "Habor" → *Harbor*; "perspective" → *respective*.
Awkward phrasing: "The invention of the airplane has had many positive effects-, unfortunately, however, it was brought about some negative changes, also." This final sentence needs revision for clarity — perhaps: "The invention of the airplane has had many positive effects; however, it has also brought about some unfortunate negative changes."

Style Suggestions

Avoid starting paragraphs with "As I've already said" — this is informal. Try a transitional phrase like "Building on this point" or simply restate the theme freshly.
Numbers under 100 are typically spelled out in formal writing (eg, "thirty to thirty-five hours" instead of "30-35 hours"), though this rule varies by style guide — check what your instructor prefers.
Be consistent with formality: contractions like "would've" and "I've" are common in speech but often avoided in academic essays.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Sharpen your thesis into a clear, defensible claim.
Add analytical sentences after each example to explain *significance*.
Expand the politics paragraph with a clear evaluative stance.
Proofread carefully for affect/effect confusion, subject-verb agreement, and spelling.
Replace ampersands and contractions with formal equivalents.

You have strong raw material here — with revision focused on deepening analysis and polishing language, this essay can become much more persuasive and academically polished. Keep up the good structural instincts!

Feedback Report: Essay on AIDS

Overall Impression

Your essay addresses an important and complex topic, and you clearly have some genuine engagement with the subject. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas: developing a clearer argument, supporting your claims with evidence, and correcting language errors. Below is feedback organized by category, followed by concrete suggestions.

Argument and Thesis

At present, the essay lacks a clear central claim. Your introduction mentions that AIDS has "changed many people's lives and their ways of thinking," but it isn't clear what exactly you want to argue: Is the essay about *stigma*, about *behavioral change*, or about *both*? These are related but distinct ideas, and each deserves its own clear treatment.

Suggestion: Write a one-sentence thesis stating your specific argument, eg, "The AIDS epidemic has transformed American society in two significant ways: it has fueled dangerous prejudice against marginalized groups, while simultaneously prompting more cautious and responsible sexual behavior." Then organize your paragraphs to support this thesis directly.

Use of Evidence

Several claims in your essay are stated as fact without support:
"AIDS...always leads to death" – This is factually questionable, especially with treatment advances. Even in earlier decades, this claim would need qualification.
"'casual sex'...now...rarely occurs" – This is a strong claim with no evidence. How do you know this? Surveys? Statistics?
The Ryan White example is good, but you need to cite where this information comes from (a documentary, article, book?).

Suggestion: For an argumentative essay, every generalization should be backed by a source, statistic, or concrete example. Avoid absolute words like "always," "definitely," and "rarely" unless you can prove them. Replace personal impressions ("I feel," "I'm sure") with evidence-based statements, which are more persuasive in academic writing.

Structure and Paragraphing

Your paragraphs jump between ideas rather than developing them systematically:
Paragraph 2 introduces both general prejudice *and* the Ryan White example, but doesn't explain the connection between them clearly.
Paragraph 3 shifts to a new topic (undetectable AIDS strains) without a transition explaining how it relates to your main argument.

Suggestion: Use topic sentences at the start of each paragraph that clearly state the paragraph's main point, and use transition phrases ("In addition to fostering stigma, the epidemic has also..." ) to guide your reader logically from one idea to the next.

Language and Style

A few stylistic points to improve academic tone:
Avoid first-person opinion markers like "I feel" and "I'm sure." Instead, use more objective academic phrasing: "This suggests that..." or "Evidence indicates that..."
The phrase "This kind of ignorant thinking is breaking us apart" is emotionally strong but vague — try to be more specific and analytical rather than editorializing.
"There is more of stigma attached to..." should be "There is more stigma attached to..." (no "of").

Grammar and Spelling

Since English spelling doesn't always follow German phonetic patterns, watch for these specific errors:

| Your spelling | Correct spelling |
|---|---|
| peoples lives | people's lives |
| awhile | a while |
| untill | until |
| devistating | devastating |
| definately | definitely |
| strands (of AIDS) | strains (of AIDS) |

Also check apostrophe use: "it's presence" should be "its presence" (possessive "its" has no apostrophe; "it's" = "it is").

Concrete Revision Steps

Write a clear thesis statement and place it at the end of your introduction.
Add citations or specific sources for the Ryan White case and any statistical claims (eg, about changes in sexual behavior).
Reorganize paragraphs so each one has a single, clear focus with a topic sentence.
Replace subjective phrases ("I feel," "I'm sure") with more formal, evidence-based language.
Proofread carefully for the spelling and apostrophe errors listed above — reading your essay aloud can help you catch these.
Qualify absolute statements ("always," "definitely," "rarely") — academic writing usually requires more nuance and precision.

Final Note

Your topic choice is strong, and the Ryan White example shows real potential as compelling evidence. With a sharper thesis, more supporting evidence, and careful proofreading, this essay could become a much more persuasive piece of writing. I encourage you to revise with these points in mind and feel free to visit office hours if you'd like to discuss your thesis further.

Feedback Report: Argumentative Essay on Nuclear Weapons and the Cold War

Overall Impression

This essay tackles an ambitious and historically significant topic, and your passion for the subject comes through clearly. You've chosen a compelling thesis—that nuclear weapons development is the most significant factor of change in modern history—and you support it with personal experience and historical observation. However, the essay needs revision in several areas: grammatical precision, argumentative structure, and clarity of evidence. Below are specific suggestions organized by category.

Grammar and Language Issues

As a German speaker, you may be transferring certain German sentence structures into English, which occasionally creates awkward phrasing. Some specific issues:

"Though advances" should be "Through advances" — this is a common confusion between these two words, but the meaning changes significantly.

"the invention of nuclear weapons have been" — subject-verb agreement error. The subject is "the invention," which is singular, so it should be "has been."

"harness of atom and its energy" should be "the harnessing of the atom and its energy" — you need the article "the" before "atom" and the gerund form "harnessing" works better as a parallel noun to "discovery."

"chief Executive commandpost" — this should be capitalized consistently and possibly hyphenated: "Chief Executive command post" (two words).

"has virtually arrived a standstill" should be "has virtually come to a standstill" — this is an idiomatic expression that doesn't translate directly.

"Although the old habits die hard, they are indeed dying" — while this is a nice rhetorical touch, check subject agreement: "old habits die hard" is itself an idiom (singular concept), so consider "Although old habits die hard, they are indeed dying" (remove "the").

I recommend reading your essay aloud or having a native speaker review it, as several other minor prepositional and article errors (a hallmark of German-English transfer) appear throughout.

Structural and Organizational Issues

Your essay follows a roughly chronological structure (WWII → Cold War → present), which is logical, but the connection to your thesis could be strengthened:

Thesis clarity: Your thesis claims nuclear weapons are "the most significant factor of change in our lifetime." This is a strong claim, but the essay spends more time *describing* the Cold War atmosphere than *arguing* why nuclear weapons specifically caused this change more than other factors (eg, ideological conflict, economic systems, technological advancement in general). Consider explicitly comparing nuclear weapons' impact to other candidates for "most significant factor" to strengthen your argument.

Paragraph 2 shifts between personal anecdote, broad claims about fear replacing nationalism, and a critique of military spending. Each of these could be its own supporting point—consider separating them or making clearer transitions between them.

Missing counter-evidence or complexity: A strong argumentative essay acknowledges complications. For instance, did nuclear weapons only cause fear and division, or did the doctrine of "mutually assured destruction" also *prevent* larger-scale conventional wars? Addressing this complexity would strengthen your argument's sophistication.

Conclusion (paragraph 3): This paragraph shifts to a hopeful tone about the end of the Cold War, which is a nice thematic close, but it doesn't fully return to your original thesis. Consider explicitly restating how these recent changes (fall of the Berlin Wall, reduced troop numbers) *confirm* or *complicate* your original claim about nuclear weapons being the most significant factor of change.

Evidence and Specificity

Your essay relies heavily on generalized claims ("uncountable trillions of dollars," "hundreds of smaller symbols of freedom") and personal impressions. To strengthen your argument:

Consider adding at least one or two concrete statistics or historical facts (eg, actual spending figures, specific treaties like SALT or START, or specific dates).
Your personal anecdote about associating "Soviet citizens with anti-Christ" is vivid and effective—consider incorporating more sensory or specific details like this throughout, as it makes your argument feel grounded rather than abstract.

Stylistic Notes

The phrase "our frigid planet and cold neighbors may once again be a warm and friendly people" is a nice metaphorical close, but "planet...may be a warm and friendly people" has a slight logical mismatch (a planet cannot "be a people"). Consider revising for clarity: "our frigid world and its estranged nations may once again become warm and friendly neighbors."

Summary of Priority Revisions

Proofread carefully for article usage, prepositions, and subject-verb agreement (consider using a grammar-checking tool or native-speaker reader).
Strengthen the thesis by directly comparing nuclear weapons' impact to other potential "most significant factors."
Add specific historical evidence (dates, figures, treaties) to support generalized claims.
Acknowledge complexity or counter-arguments (eg, deterrence theory) to deepen your argument.
Ensure your conclusion explicitly returns to and confirms your thesis.

With these revisions, this essay has strong potential—your ideas are original and your personal perspective adds authenticity to a well-worn historical topic.

Feedback on Your Essay: "The Discovery of Penicillin"

Overall Impression

You've chosen an interesting topic, and your English is generally fluent and readable. However, as it stands, this piece reads more like a historical summary than an argumentative essay. To meet the requirements of the assignment, you need to sharpen your central claim and build a case that could reasonably be debated or supported with evidence, rather than simply narrating a sequence of events.

Thesis and Argument

Right now, there is no clear arguable claim. Sentences like "The discovery of penicillin... was one of the greatest advances in medicinal chemistry" state an opinion but never develop it into a thesis that the rest of the essay defends with reasoning and evidence. Ask yourself: What is the *specific* claim I want to prove? For example:
Did penicillin's discovery fundamentally change the *structure* of the pharmaceutical industry?
Did it shift medical practice in a way that had both benefits and drawbacks?
Was its impact primarily scientific, economic, or social?

Choose one angle and make it explicit in your introduction, then use each paragraph to build support for that specific claim rather than moving chronologically through general history.

Evidence and Specificity

The essay relies on vague, unsupported generalizations: "many uses," "a variety of new drugs," "millions of people," "huge enterprises." An argumentative essay needs concrete, cited evidence — names, dates, statistics, or specific case studies. For instance:
*When* was penicillin discovered, and by whom?
*Which* other antibiotics followed, and in what years?
*What* is the source for the claim about Upjohn's growth?

Without citations, claims like these will not be considered credible in academic writing. Look for at least 3–4 scholarly or historical sources to anchor your argument in verifiable facts.

Structure

Your three paragraphs move chronologically (discovery → industrial expansion → conclusion), which works for a narrative but not for an argument. Consider restructuring around points of argument instead of time periods. For example:
Paragraph 1: Introduce your thesis and give brief necessary background.
Paragraph 2: Present your first line of evidence/argument (eg, economic impact).
Paragraph 3: Present a second line of argument (eg, scientific/methodological impact).
Paragraph 4: Address a counterargument or limitation (eg, overuse, resistance, cost).
Conclusion: Synthesize how the evidence supports your thesis.

Adding a counterargument (even briefly) would strengthen the "argumentative" nature of the essay significantly.

Language and Style

Your English is strong, but I noticed a few patterns that may stem from German sentence structure:

a) Overuse of passive voice. Many sentences begin with "It was discovered..." or "It was also attempted..." This is a common transfer from German academic style (*"Es wurde entdeckt..."*), but in English, overusing passive constructions makes the writing feel impersonal and repetitive. Try giving agency to the subjects:
Instead of: *"It was also attempted to increase the production..."*
→ Try: *"Chemists also attempted to increase production..."*
Instead of: *"It was discovered that the older drugs could be synthesized..."*
→ Try: *"Researchers discovered that older drugs could be synthesized..."*

b) Word choice/terminology consistency. You switch between "medicinal chemistry" and "medical chemists." These are not interchangeable in English — "medicinal chemistry" is the field; a person working in it is typically called a "medicinal chemist" (not "medical chemist," which usually refers to something else, like a chemist working in a hospital lab). Choose one term and use it consistently.

c) Small grammar points to fix:
"attemped" → "attempted" (typo)
"a small bacteria" → "a small bacterium" (bacteria is the plural form)
"discovered to have many uses" is awkward; consider: "penicillin proved useful in stopping and preventing infections."

d) Sentence variety. Several sentences follow the same subject-verb pattern ("It was discovered that...", "It was also attempted..."). Varying your sentence openings will make the prose more dynamic and less repetitive.

Conclusion

Your conclusion currently just restates the body paragraphs rather than reinforcing an argument. Once you've established a clear thesis, revise the conclusion to explicitly return to that claim and explain its broader significance (eg, what this means for the pharmaceutical industry today, or what lessons can be drawn from it).

Summary of Priority Revisions

Develop a specific, arguable thesis — not just "penicillin was important," but *how* or *why* in a way that can be debated.
Add citations and concrete evidence (dates, names, statistics).
Restructure paragraphs around arguments, not chronology.
Reduce passive voice; give your sentences active subjects.
Fix minor grammar/vocabulary issues (bacterium, medicinal vs. medical chemist).

With a clearer thesis and more specific evidence, this essay has good potential — the writing itself is clear and grammatically solid overall. Focus your revision on argument and evidence rather than sentence-level polishing at this stage.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Invention of Television

Your essay addresses an interesting and relevant topic, and you've organized it around three clear themes: entertainment, communication, and family life. This structure gives your essay a logical backbone. Below are suggestions to help you strengthen both the content and the language.

Content and Structure

Thesis and Focus: Your introduction promises to discuss entertainment, communication, and family values—and you do address all three. However, your thesis statement could be more specific. Instead of simply listing what television "has" done, consider stating your actual argument: Is television's overall impact positive, negative, or mixed? Right now, your essay presents both sides of each issue without committing to a clear stance, which makes it read more like a balanced report than an *argumentative* essay. Since this is an argumentative piece, your reader will expect you to take a position and defend it with evidence.

Development of Ideas: Each paragraph raises interesting points but doesn't develop them fully. For example, you mention that television helps "warn people of dangers, both natural and social," but you don't provide a specific example (eg, weather emergencies, breaking news alerts). Concrete examples—historical or contemporary—would make your argument more persuasive and less abstract. Similarly, your discussion of family life presents two opposing views (television unites vs. divides families) but doesn't weigh in on which you find more convincing or explain why.

Conclusion: Your conclusion largely repeats the introduction rather than synthesizing your argument or offering a final insight. Consider ending with a more decisive statement about television's legacy, or a forward-looking comment about its role today (streaming, screens beyond the TV set, etc), which would give your essay a stronger sense of closure.

Language and Grammar

Since English is your second language, I want to highlight some recurring error patterns rather than fixing every instance individually, so you can watch for these in your own revision.

their/there confusion: "Of course their have been..." should be "there have been." Similarly, "As television programing improves their is an increasing number" should be "there is." This is a common homophone confusion—it may help to remember that "their" always shows possession (their house), while "there" indicates place or existence (there is/are).

Article usage: German and English handle articles differently. Check phrases like "changed our way of live" (should be "way of *life*"—note also the noun/verb confusion here) and "creates news forms" (should be "new forms"—likely a typo, but worth double-checking).

Subject-verb agreement: "The types of programs is another issue" should be "*are* another issue," since "types" is plural.

Word choice/spelling: "yonkies" should be "junkies"—a colorful word choice, but make sure it's spelled correctly, and consider whether informal slang fits the academic register of your essay.

Punctuation with ampersands: You use "&" twice (eg, "entertainment & communication"). In formal academic writing, always spell out "and."

Comma splices and run-ons: Sentences like "This invention not only introduced a new form of entertainment, it has enhanced communication..." need a stronger connector. Try: "This invention not only introduced a new form of entertainment, but it also enhanced communication..."

Verb tense consistency: You shift between past and present tense somewhat inconsistently (eg, "the become desensitized" should be "they become," and check whether you want present tense throughout for general truths, or past tense when discussing historical development).

Suggestions for Revision

Sharpen your thesis to state a clear position on television's overall impact.
Add specific examples (real programs, historical events, statistics) to support your claims about entertainment, communication, and family life.
Take a stance in each body paragraph rather than only presenting both sides—acknowledge counterarguments, but tell the reader which view you find more persuasive and why.
Proofread carefully for the homophone and agreement errors noted above; reading your essay aloud can help you catch these.
Revise your conclusion to synthesize your argument rather than repeat the introduction.

Final Thought

You have a solid foundation with relevant, thoughtful content. The main task now is to transform this from a balanced overview into a persuasive argument by taking a clearer position and supporting it with specific evidence. Keep working on the grammar patterns identified above, as they will become more automatic with practice. Well done on tackling a topic with genuine complexity!

Feedback on Your Essay: The Washing Machine and Modern Convenience

Overall Impression

Your essay has an engaging, lively voice, and your use of humor and vivid imagery (the person clutching the washing machine while fleeing a fire) shows real creative flair. However, for an *argumentative* essay, the piece currently reads more like a humorous reflection. To strengthen it academically, you need a clearer thesis, more logical development, and more consistent formal register. Below are specific suggestions.

Clarify Your Thesis and Argument

Your actual claim only emerges in the final paragraph: modern conveniences like washing machines reduce physical activity, which may contribute to weight and health issues. This is an interesting and debatable claim—exactly what an argumentative essay needs. But you should state it clearly in your introduction, not save it for the end. Right now, readers spend three paragraphs on a different point (that people are anxious about losing their gadgets) before you pivot to your real argument. Decide early which claim you want to make and build every paragraph toward it.

Restructure Around Your Argument

Currently, paragraphs 2 and 3 both illustrate the same idea (people panic at the thought of hand-washing) using similar exaggerated examples. Consider merging them into one, tighter paragraph, which frees up space to develop your actual argument—the link between convenience and inactivity—more fully. For example, you could add a paragraph explaining *how* washing machines specifically eliminate physical labor (scrubbing, wringing, carrying water) and *why* this matters for health, rather than just asserting it in one sentence at the end.

Support Your Claims with Evidence

Right now, your argument rests entirely on rhetorical questions and hypothetical scenarios ("What would a college student do without a washing machine?"). For an academic essay, you need actual support:
Statistics on time saved by washing machines historically (studies exist on this).
Data on obesity rates or physical activity levels over the decades.
A source connecting labor-saving devices to reduced caloric expenditure.

Even one or two concrete facts would transform your washing-machine-to-obesity connection from a clever aside into a persuasive claim. Without evidence, the leap from "gadgets save time" to "therefore we need diet programs" feels unearned, even if it's plausible.

Reconsider Tone and Register

Your humor is entertaining, but overused hyperbole (people "furiously running" with washing machines, "panic in most teenagers") undercuts the credibility of your argument. Academic argumentative writing can use wit, but sparingly—usually to open or close, not throughout. Consider keeping one strong humorous image (perhaps the fire scenario, since it's memorable) and cutting the rest, replacing them with more analytical sentences.

Similarly, rhetorical questions ("But is that all that bad?") and trailing ellipses ("....") feel more like spoken or informal writing. Try converting some of these into direct statements. For instance, instead of "But is that all that bad?" you might write: "This raises the question of whether convenience is worth its hidden costs."

Strengthen the Conclusion

Ending on a question ("but was Weight Watchers ever an issue?") is a nice rhetorical touch, but the essay needs a real concluding move first: restate your thesis and briefly summarize why it matters. Right now, the essay simply stops. Add a sentence or two synthesizing your argument (eg, "The washing machine, like other modern conveniences, has freed our time but at a physical cost we are only beginning to reckon with") before ending on your rhetorical question.

Language and Mechanics

A few smaller issues to address in revision:
Generic "man": Using "man" to mean "humanity" is outdated in academic English; consider "humans" or "people."
Spelling: "continously" → *continuously*; "necesity" → *necessity*; "with out" → *without* (this is one word).
Symbols: Avoid "&" in formal writing—write out "and."
Punctuation: Multiple question marks ("?....") and excessive ellipses should be reduced to standard single punctuation marks in academic prose.
Word choice: "aid us in our modern process of rushing around" is a bit awkward; consider "contribute to our fast-paced, rushed lifestyles."

Summary

You have a genuinely interesting argument buried in this essay—convenience technology's link to reduced physical activity. Bring that thesis to the front, cut redundant paragraphs, add real evidence, and tone down the exaggeration to let your argument, rather than your humor, carry the essay. With these revisions, this could become a sharp, persuasive piece.

Feedback on Your Essay: "The Zip-Lock Bag"

Overall Impression

Your essay is lively, personal, and clearly written with enthusiasm — the anecdotes about the smelly locker and the shampoo disaster are vivid and memorable. However, since this is meant to be an argumentative essay, it currently reads more like an informal, conversational piece (a diary entry or a blog post) than an academic argument. Below are the main areas to work on.

Thesis and Argumentative Structure

An argumentative essay needs a clear, explicit thesis stated early on — a sentence that tells the reader exactly what you are claiming and why. Right now, your "thesis" is buried in the introduction ("I'm going to have to agree that that is an invention that I cannot live without") and is not really argued, just asserted. Consider opening with something like:

> *"Although it seems like a simple household object, the zip-lock bag deserves recognition as one of the most useful inventions of everyday life because of its versatility in preserving freshness, controlling odor, and simplifying travel."*

This gives your reader a roadmap for the rest of the essay and signals that each paragraph will support this claim.

Paragraph Organization and Topic Sentences

Your paragraphs are organized around examples, but the topic sentences ("Another thing that zip-lock bags are good for is…", "But, there are other uses for zip-lock bags…") sound like spoken transitions rather than academic ones. Try to make each topic sentence state the *point* the paragraph will prove, not just introduce a new topic. For example, instead of "There are also times when foods tend to take on the smells of other foods," you might write: "Zip-lock bags also solve the common kitchen problem of food odors transferring between items." This subtly reminds the reader how the paragraph connects to your thesis.

Register and Tone

Since this is an academic essay, avoid:
Personal storytelling as the main evidence ("It is funny that we are writing on this subject…," "my father suggested…"). You can keep *one* brief anecdote to illustrate a point, but don't let the essay open and close on personal narrative.
Rhetorical questions, especially at the end ("What more could you ask for?" "Where else can you put ice…?"). These work in speeches or blogs but weaken the seriousness of an argument. Replace them with direct statements.
Exclamation marks ("A third solution would be to bag everything!"). These read as casual/emotional rather than analytical.

Development of Ideas

Some paragraphs list examples without fully explaining *why* they matter. For instance, the "bag everything" solution is mentioned but not developed — is this actually a good solution, or a joke? If argumentative, clarify your reasoning. Similarly, the final paragraph lists many small uses (Q-tips, pantyhose, earrings) in one long sentence; consider whether all of these deserve equal weight, or whether you should group them thematically (eg, "protecting delicate items while traveling") and choose the strongest 2–3 examples rather than an exhaustive list.

Language and Mechanics

A few recurring issues, likely influenced by German sentence patterns and punctuation conventions:

Comma splices and run-on sentences: "Anything can be put in zip-lock bag, and not get freezer burn" — needs an article ("a zip-lock bag") and smoother phrasing.
Extra/incorrect punctuation: "…I never had a smelly locker again. ." (double period); "Gallon size zip-lock bags, solve this problem" (unnecessary comma before the verb).
Article usage: German does not require articles in the same contexts as English, so watch for missing articles: "put in zip-lock bag" → "put in a zip-lock bag."
Word choice/spelling: "earing" → "earring."
Parenthetical asides like "(onlies???)" should be removed in formal writing — express uncertainty about word choice by simply choosing a clearer word instead (eg, "clothes labeled 'dry-clean only'").
Repetition: "zip-lock bag(s)" appears very often. Vary with synonyms ("these bags," "this product," "the invention," "it") to improve flow.

Conclusion

Your conclusion should restate your thesis with a fresh perspective and perhaps suggest broader significance (eg, how such a small, inexpensive object has quietly changed daily household routines). Ending with "The zip-lock bag was definitely a good invention" is too casual and vague for a strong closing argument — aim for a sentence that feels conclusive and confident.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Add a clear, argumentative thesis in the introduction.
Rewrite topic sentences to explicitly connect to that thesis.
Remove rhetorical questions and exclamations; adopt a more analytical tone.
Tighten examples — choose the strongest ones rather than listing everything.
Proofread for punctuation, articles, and word choice.

You have great material and genuine enthusiasm for your topic — with a firmer structure and more formal tone, this essay will become a much stronger piece of argumentative writing.

Feedback Report: "The Impact of the Computer"

Overall Impression

Your essay demonstrates real enthusiasm for the topic and a clear writing voice, but as an *argumentative* essay, it currently reads more like a description or a prediction than an argument. To meet the assignment's goals, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, strengthen your evidence, and address the genre conventions of academic argumentation more explicitly. Below are specific areas to work on.

Thesis and Argumentative Stance

Right now, your essay asserts that the computer is "the most influential" invention of the 20th century, but this claim is never really *argued*—it's simply stated and then illustrated with general observations. An argumentative essay needs:

A clear, debatable claim (Why is the computer more influential than, say, antibiotics, the airplane, or the internet as a separate invention?)
Acknowledgment of counterarguments (Why might someone disagree? What other inventions could compete for this title, and why does yours win?)
Reasoned support, not just assertion

Consider revising your thesis to something more specific and defensible, such as: "The personal computer has had a more transformative effect on daily life than any other 20th-century invention because it has restructured how we work, communicate, and access information." This gives you something concrete to prove.

Structure and Development

The essay has three paragraphs, but the organization doesn't yet serve an argumentative purpose:

Paragraph 1 (history of computers) is largely descriptive. Ask yourself: does this paragraph support your thesis, or is it background information that could be condensed into a sentence or two in your introduction?
Paragraph 2 (impact) contains your strongest material but tries to do too much—business, productivity, information overload, and future predictions all appear here without clear separation. Consider splitting this into two paragraphs: one on documented, present-day impacts (with evidence), and one on future implications (clearly framed as speculation).
There is no real conclusion. The essay ends on a speculative, almost promotional note ("the future...looks 'bright' indeed") rather than returning to and reinforcing your central argument. Add a concluding paragraph that restates your thesis in light of the evidence you've presented and perhaps addresses a counterargument.

Evidence and Specificity

Many claims are unsupported generalizations:
"productivity of people has increased ten-fold" — Where does this figure come from? You need a citation or at least a more cautious phrasing ("has increased significantly, with some studies suggesting...").
"every human being in the developed world will have their own PC" — This is a prediction, not evidence. Predictions can be part of an argument, but they should be clearly framed as such and ideally supported by trends you can point to (eg, rising rates of PC ownership, mobile technology adoption).

A stronger essay would include at least one or two concrete examples (a specific industry, a specific statistic, a named development) rather than relying on broad, unverifiable claims.

Language and Style Notes

A few recurring issues to watch for, some of which may reflect German sentence patterns transferring into English:

Spelling/typos: "occured" → "occurred"; "2st century" → "21st century."
Subject-verb agreement: "the way data and information is dealt with" — in academic English, "data" is often treated as plural ("data...are dealt with"), though usage varies; "information" is singular. Consider separating these or adjusting the verb.
Redundancy: The first two sentences of your introduction essentially repeat the same idea ("changed the way people live" appears twice within three sentences). Consider combining them for a tighter opening.
Hedging vs. overstatement: Phrases like "forever changed," "20th century miracle," and "limited only by our imaginations" are quite emphatic for academic prose. Academic argumentation generally favors measured, precise claims over rhetorical flourish—save the enthusiasm for your evidence, not your adjectives.
Spacing: I noticed some extra spaces before punctuation (eg, "predecessors did," "imaginations
 and"). Proofread carefully for these formatting slips.
Quotation marks around "bright": This scare-quote usage feels informal; either commit to the word directly or replace it with more precise, formal language.

Suggested Next Steps

Rewrite your thesis to make a specific, arguable claim.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one clearly supports that claim.
Add at least two concrete pieces of evidence (statistics, examples, or references to credible sources).
Write a real concluding paragraph that reinforces your argument.
Proofread for spelling, spacing, and agreement errors.

You have good instincts for an engaging opening and closing tone—now the task is to build the argumentative "muscle" in between. I'd be happy to look at a revised draft.
