Feedback Report on "AIDS Discovery" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an important and emotionally resonant topic, and your passion for the subject is evident. However, the essay would benefit from more precise conceptual framing, a clearer thesis, and more rigorous argumentation. Below are specific areas to address in revision.

Content and Argumentation

Conceptual clarity: Your opening claim—that "AIDS" itself was a "discovery"—is imprecise. AIDS is a *disease* or *syndrome*; what was "discovered" was the HIV virus and its mechanisms of transmission. This distinction matters for academic writing, since your entire argument hinges on this concept. Consider reframing your thesis around the *discovery of HIV/AIDS* and its social consequences, rather than treating the disease as an invention.

Thesis and focus: Your essay moves between several claims: (1) society's discriminatory response to AIDS, (2) the shift in public concern once children were infected, (3) the debate over sex education vs. abstinence, and (4) the need for compassion toward AIDS victims. Each of these could be a strong essay on its own, but combined without clear signposting, they weaken your argument. Ask yourself: what is the *one* claim you want to prove? A possible thesis: "The discovery of HIV/AIDS forced American society to confront both its prejudices and its mortality, but public sympathy only grew once the disease could no longer be dismissed as affecting only marginalized groups." This would give you a unifying thread.

Evidence and support: Many of your claims are asserted rather than demonstrated. For example: "no one really cared that gays would die" and "Children are becoming sexually active at the age of eleven" are strong, controversial statements that need sources or at least some acknowledgment of complexity. Academic argumentation requires you to support such claims with evidence (statistics, historical accounts, or scholarly sources) rather than presenting them as self-evident truths.

Logical gaps: The transition from discussing Ryan White to the sex-education debate feels abrupt. You need a bridging sentence explaining *why* his case led to this particular controversy. Similarly, the final paragraph introduces Magic Johnson very late, almost as an afterthought; if his case is important to your argument, it deserves development earlier in the essay, not a single sentence near the end.

Structure

Your essay lacks a clear introduction that states your thesis and previews your argument's structure. Currently, the first paragraph reads more like a general reflection than an academic introduction. Consider adding a sentence at the end of paragraph one that explicitly states your argument.

Your conclusion is very short and introduces a new idea (Magic Johnson) rather than synthesizing your argument. A stronger conclusion would return to your thesis, summarize your key points, and end with a broader reflection on what this history teaches us.

Language and Style

Register: This essay's tone is closer to informal commentary than academic prose. Phrases like "How wrong they were," "How likely is it that an eleven year old is going to go to a pharmacy," and "It's too bad that it takes a superstar" are rhetorically effective but too colloquial for academic writing. Consider revising these into more formal constructions, eg, "This assumption proved to be mistaken" or "It is unfortunate that public attention often requires a celebrity figure such as Magic Johnson."

Sentence fragments: You have several fragments that need correction:
"Even if a cure is never found." — this is not a complete sentence; attach it to the following sentence.
"And once again no one really cared, simply because they thought AIDS would never effect them." — starting with "And" is stylistically weak in formal writing; also note *effect* should be *affect* (a common confusion, but importantly a verb/noun distinction: "affect" = verb, "effect" = noun).

Punctuation: You use ampersands ("&") throughout, which is inappropriate in formal essays—always spell out "and." Also check your comma usage before conjunctions like "however" and "and."

German interference: As a native German speaker, watch for:
Capitalization: "20th Century" should be lowercase ("20th century") in English, unlike German nouns.
Word order and article use: generally your English is strong, but double-check idiomatic verb collocations (eg, "contract AIDS" is correct, well done).

Recommendations for Revision

Clarify your thesis and restructure paragraphs around it.
Distinguish clearly between "HIV" and "AIDS" where relevant.
Support key claims with historical evidence or citations.
Eliminate sentence fragments and informal phrasing.
Replace all ampersands with "and."
Expand your conclusion to synthesize your argument rather than introducing new material.

With more precise conceptual grounding and tighter argumentative structure, this essay has strong potential to make a compelling, well-supported case about society's evolving response to AIDS.

Feedback on Your Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a thought-provoking and philosophically ambitious angle on this topic, and your passion for the subject is evident. However, the essay would benefit from more rigorous argumentation, concrete evidence, and closer attention to logical consistency. Below are suggestions organized by area.

Argument and Logic

Your central claim—that only medical inventions have improved life because they don't seek to "dominate" nature or people—is compelling but needs to be tested more carefully against counterexamples. Consider:

Do medical inventions really avoid domination of nature? Vaccines, antibiotics, and genetic engineering are arguably *quintessential* examples of humans manipulating natural processes (viruses, bacteria, genetics) to serve human ends. You might need to either narrow your definition of "domination" or acknowledge this complexity rather than presenting medicine as uniquely innocent.
The "dominate nature vs. dominate people" dichotomy is asserted rather than demonstrated. You state that most inventions fall into these two negative categories, but you don't examine any specific inventions in depth. What about inventions like the printing press's descendants (radio, television), sanitation systems, or agricultural technology that fed billions? These complicate a purely negative reading of 20th-century innovation.
Consider the counterargument more seriously. A strong argumentative essay anticipates objections. Someone might argue that industrial technology, despite environmental costs, also lifted billions out of poverty. Even briefly acknowledging this and explaining why you still find your position more persuasive would strengthen your credibility.

Development and Evidence

This is the area needing the most attention. Right now, the essay relies almost entirely on assertion:

"We continue to destroy the plants, animals, and the earth itself" — *which inventions specifically? What evidence supports this?*
"We invent better weapons and better economic systems geared for killing" — *name a weapon, a war, a specific economic policy.*
"Medical inventions...are designed to help people of all colors" — *give an example: penicillin, the polio vaccine, insulin, etc*

Academic argumentative writing needs specific, verifiable evidence (historical examples, statistics, or quoted sources) to move from personal conviction to persuasive argument. Right now, the essay reads more like a personal reflection or manifesto than an evidence-based argument. Try adding at least one concrete example per paragraph.

Structure and Paragraphing

Your paragraphs are currently very short and underdeveloped—especially paragraphs 2 and 3, which are only a few sentences long. Each paragraph should:
State a clear topic sentence,
Provide evidence/examples,
Explain how that evidence supports your thesis,
Connect back to your overall argument.

Consider merging or expanding your body paragraphs so each fully explores one aspect of your argument (eg, one paragraph on weapons/warfare, one on environmental destruction, one on medical inventions as a counterexample).

Language and Mechanics

Since English is your second language, a few specific issues to watch for:

Typos/proofreading: "occured" → occurred; "0the century" → 20th century (likely a typo); "truely" → truly; "affect" → effect (as a noun, "effect" is correct: "a negative *effect* on our lives").
Spacing/formatting: There's an extra space after "Interestingly enough," and inconsistent paragraph indentation.
Word choice: "petty insecurities" is a strong, somewhat dismissive phrase—make sure it fits your intended tone.
Repetition: The word "dominate" appears many times. Consider varying your vocabulary (eg, control, subjugate, exploit, overpower) to avoid monotony.
Absolute claims: Phrases like "the *only* inventions," "cannot be used," and "one of the *only* truly 'kind'" are very strong. Academic writing often benefits from slightly more hedged language ("I would argue that most..." or "with few exceptions...") unless you can fully defend the absolute claim.

Suggestions for Revision

Add a concrete example in every paragraph—a real invention, event, or statistic.
Address the strongest counterargument to your thesis (eg, medical technology's own "unnatural" interventions, or a positive non-medical invention).
Expand paragraphs 2 and 3 so each argument gets fuller treatment.
Proofread carefully for typos and word choice, ideally reading the essay aloud.
Reconsider absolute language ("only," "cannot") for more precise, defensible claims.

Your ideas are original and worth developing—strengthening the evidentiary support and complicating your own argument slightly will make the essay considerably more persuasive.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Satellite Dish and Global Communication

Thank you for sharing this draft. You've chosen an interesting and relevant topic, and I can see you have real enthusiasm for it. However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of loosely connected observations than a focused argumentative essay. Below are the main areas to work on.

Thesis and Argumentative Position

An argumentative essay needs a clear, debatable thesis that you defend with evidence. Right now, your essay doesn't take a position — it lists effects (some positive, some negative) without arguing *for* anything. Your conclusion even states "The only observation which can be made... it is up to the average person to decide." This is a summary, not an argument.

Revise this by asking yourself: What do *I* claim about the satellite dish? For example: "The satellite dish has been a net positive for global communication despite its costs" or "The satellite dish has widened inequality between information-rich and information-poor societies." Choose one clear claim and organize every paragraph to support it.

Structure and Paragraph Unity

Each paragraph currently introduces a new topic (news, business, entertainment, cable TV, military) without transitions or a unifying argument connecting them back to your thesis. This makes the essay feel like a list. 

Add topic sentences that explicitly link each paragraph to your central claim.
Add transitions between paragraphs ("Beyond its impact on journalism, the satellite dish has also transformed...").
Consider whether all these topics are equally important — you may need to cut some (eg, the military/STARWARS paragraph feels disconnected and underdeveloped) to keep focus.

Evidence and Support

Several claims need sources or at least more explanation:
"40% of U.S. millionaires are entertainers" — where does this come from? Unsupported statistics undermine your credibility.
"The intelligence of an average teenager is lowering itself due to cable viewing" — this is a strong claim that needs evidence or should be softened/removed.
"Naval programs have wasted exceeding amounts of money" — vague; give specifics or omit.

Academic writing requires you to support claims with credible sources (studies, statistics with citations, expert opinions). Unsupported generalizations weaken your argument.

Precision and Clarity of Language

Some phrases are imprecise or confusing for an academic audience:
The opening ("a large, 1/2 egg... as large as a small FIAT") is a creative image but too informal and unclear for an academic essay. Consider opening with a clear, direct statement of the topic and its significance instead.
"created in the last 0 years" — likely a typo; check this.
"Not certain about the date of its invention" — if you're unsure of a fact, look it up rather than including uncertainty in the text itself.
Brand names should be capitalized correctly (Panasonic, not "panasonic"), and "CORE" should be clarified — is this a real company?

Grammar and Mechanics (Common Issues to Review)

Since English is your second language, here are patterns to watch for:

Apostrophes: "it's center" should be "its center" (possessive "its" has no apostrophe). Similarly, "pro's and con's" should be "pros and cons," and "millionares" should be "millionaires."
Comma splices/unnecessary commas: eg, "The satellite dish, created in the last [X] years, has changed..." — check whether commas are needed grammatically or just used for pauses.
Verb agreement and tense: "recieve" → "receive" (spelling); "affect" vs. "effect" — you want "effect" (noun) in "strong affect on the world economy."
Sentence fragments: "Stocks are an integral part of their livelihood" — this sentence needs to be connected more clearly to the previous one; right now it feels disconnected.
Capitalization: "century" not "ctry"; avoid abbreviations in formal writing.

I recommend reading your essay aloud or using a grammar-checking tool (like Grammarly) after revising content, to catch these smaller errors systematically.

Suggested Revision Plan

Write a one-sentence thesis stating your specific argument.
Outline 3-4 paragraphs, each with a clear topic sentence supporting the thesis.
Find 2-3 credible sources (statistics, expert quotes) to support key claims.
Cut tangential material (eg, STARWARS, naval programs) unless you can connect it directly to your argument.
Rewrite the conclusion to reinforce your thesis, not just summarize neutrally.

Final Note

You clearly have good ideas and knowledge about this topic — the challenge now is shaping that knowledge into a focused, evidence-based argument. I'd be happy to discuss your revised thesis before you rewrite the full draft. Good luck!

Feedback on Your Essay: The Fall of the Berlin Wall

Overall Impression

Your essay has a compelling personal voice and captures the emotional intensity of a historic moment. The imagery in your opening is vivid, and your instinct to ground a large historical claim in personal experience is a strong one. However, as an *argumentative* essay, the piece needs a clearer central claim and more consistent logical development. Right now it reads more as a personal reflection interspersed with a historical argument that emerges only gradually. Below are suggestions to sharpen both structure and language.

Argument and Structure

Identify your thesis early. Your actual argument — that the fall of the Berlin Wall was the *catalyst* for the end of the Cold War — doesn't appear clearly until the final paragraph. Consider stating this claim near the beginning, perhaps right after your opening narrative, so readers know what you're building toward.

Watch for a self-contradiction near the end. You write: "Without the 'events of Berlin Wall' history probably would not have taken a very different course. Eventually, I think that things would have turned out as they did." These two sentences seem to cancel each other out — the first suggests the Wall's fall *did* change history's course, the second suggests history would have unfolded similarly anyway. Clarify which claim you actually want to defend. If your real argument is that events *would eventually* have happened regardless, but the Wall's fall *accelerated and inspired* them, say so explicitly — that is actually a nuanced and interesting claim, but it needs to be stated clearly rather than left as an apparent contradiction.

Connect the classmate anecdote more explicitly. The story about your classmate's father is moving, but its relevance to your argument needs to be spelled out. Are you using it as evidence of the suffering under communism that makes the Wall's fall significant? If so, tell the reader that directly rather than letting them infer it.

Fix the pronoun inconsistency. You introduce "a classmate whose father had escaped," then refer to the father as "him" in the dialogue, but later write "her father's words" — suggesting the classmate is female. Double-check and make this consistent throughout.

Paragraph Organization

Your three paragraphs roughly correspond to: (1) the emotional experience of the event, (2) personal/historical context (the classmate's story, the suffering under communism), and (3) the broader argument about historical consequence. This is a reasonable structure, but the transitions between them are abrupt. Consider adding a sentence at the end of paragraph one that signals the direction of your argument (eg, "To understand why this moment mattered so much, it helps to remember what came before it").

Language and Mechanics

Punctuation: You use dashes (--) very frequently where a period or comma would be more appropriate in English. This may reflect German punctuation conventions, but in English academic writing, the em-dash is used sparingly, typically to set off a parenthetical remark or create emphasis — not as a general-purpose separator between independent clauses. Please revise sentence boundaries using full stops and commas instead.

Spelling corrections needed:
"mesmirized" → *mesmerized*
"Tchekoslovaquia" → *Czechoslovakia*
"Roumania" → *Romania*

Ambiguous phrasing: "our now as-President Bush" is unclear — did you mean "then-President Bush" (referring to his presidency at the time of the events) or "our now former President Bush" (writing after he left office)? Given that you're writing a few years after 1989, one of these is likely intended; choose the clearer option.

Repetition: The phrase "in vain" appears twice in close proximity ("their efforts were not in vain" and "their struggles and hopes... will not have been in vain"). Consider varying your phrasing to avoid redundancy, or use the repetition deliberately as a rhetorical echo — but if the latter, make sure it reads as intentional rather than accidental.

Sentence fragments: Several sentences are quite short and standalone in a way that fragments your argument's flow (eg, "Many people had tried to escape. Some had been executed."). This can be effective for emphasis, but check that this technique is used purposefully rather than as a default style throughout.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

State your central argument explicitly and early.
Resolve the apparent contradiction about historical causation in your conclusion.
Clarify the connection between the classmate's story and your thesis.
Fix pronoun consistency for the classmate.
Replace many of the dashes with appropriate punctuation.
Correct the spelling errors noted above.

You have real narrative skill and a genuine emotional investment in this topic — with a tighter argumentative frame, this essay could be very effective.

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling topic and your enthusiasm for the subject comes through clearly. Your central claim—that television represents the most significant invention of the twentieth century because of its unique power to connect people visually across distances—is interesting and defensible. However, the essay would benefit from revision in several areas: argumentative structure, sentence-level clarity, and proofreading. Below I address these in turn.

Argumentative Structure

Paragraph development: Your body paragraphs raise excellent points (radio vs. television comparison, the "global village" idea, television's persuasive power) but each idea is only briefly illustrated before you move on. For an argumentative essay, you need to develop your reasoning further:
*Why* does seeing an event create more "absorption" than hearing about it? What is the psychological or social mechanism?
Your examples (the president, the store owner's son) are effective, but you introduce them and then immediately say "the examples could be endless" rather than analyzing *why* they matter for your argument.
The final paragraph shifts into a discussion of television's dangers (persuasion, commercialization) — this is an important complication, but it feels abrupt. Consider signaling this shift explicitly: *"Yet this very power that makes television so significant also makes it potentially dangerous."* This would turn a sudden pivot into a deliberate concession that strengthens your argument.

Conclusion: The essay ends on the persuasive power/danger of television, but doesn't return to reaffirm why television is *the most important* invention. A brief concluding paragraph tying back to your central claim would give the essay a stronger sense of closure.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

Since you mentioned German is your first language, here are patterns to watch for:

Article and possessive errors: "to make one lives easier" → "to make one's life easier." English requires the possessive apostrophe-s here, unlike German constructions.

Subject-verb agreement: "The television strength must be realized" → "Television's strength must be realized" (missing possessive).

Spelling errors to correct:
   - "vertually" → virtually
   - "boarders" → borders
   - "peak" → peek (you want "a peek at," not "a peak," which means a mountaintop)
   - "absorbtion" → absorption
   - "spening" → spending
   - "necesary" → necessary
   - "olympic" → Olympic (capitalize proper nouns)

Comma splices and run-on sentences: Several sentences string together independent clauses without proper punctuation. For example: *"A system that seemed to work, many households throughout the nation owned or had access to a radio..."* This sentence fragment attached to the previous one needs restructuring—perhaps: *"This system seemed to work well; many households throughout the nation owned or had access to a radio..."*

Word choice: "literal explosion" — explosions are always literal in a physical sense; you likely mean "figurative" here, or simply "surge" or "boom."

Punctuation with quotation marks: Your use of quotation marks around "peak," "understand," and "peek" is inconsistent in purpose—sometimes for irony, sometimes for a play on words. Use them sparingly and make sure the reader understands why the word is marked.

Style Suggestions

Avoid absolute claims ("all was almost immediate," "the examples could be endless") without support. Academic writing benefits from more measured, precise language.
The phrase "keep the vision in television" is a clever play on words to end the essay, but it appears without enough groundwork. If you want a memorable closing line, make sure the concept of "vision" (as in foresight, control, ethical use) has been developed earlier in the essay.

Revision Priorities

Sharpen and foreground your thesis statement.
Develop each supporting point with more analysis, not just examples.
Proofread carefully for spelling and possessive forms—reading the essay aloud may help you catch errors your eye skips over.
Add a concluding paragraph that reinforces your argument.

Your ideas are strong and your topic is engaging—with revision focused on structure and precision, this essay has real potential.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

You've chosen a strong, relevant topic, and your enthusiasm for the subject comes through clearly. Your English is generally fluent and your ideas are logically sequenced. However, the essay currently reads more as a descriptive history of the computer than an argumentative essay. Since your assignment calls for argumentation, this is the most important issue to address in revision. Below I address structural, content, and language issues.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your introduction states that the computer "has had a great impact" on how society and business handle information — this is a claim, but it's quite general and not very contestable. A strong argumentative thesis should stake out a position that a reasonable person could disagree with. Consider sharpening your claim: Are you arguing that the computer is *the single most transformative* invention of the century? That it has fundamentally changed *the structure* of business and society, not just made tasks easier? Right now, the body paragraphs mostly narrate *what happened* (IBM released the PC, computers got faster, modems appeared) rather than *arguing why this matters* or *defending a debatable position*. Revise your thesis to make a clear, specific, arguable claim, and then use each paragraph to build evidence toward that claim rather than simply listing historical developments.

Structure and Paragraph Development

The essay lacks a clear counterargument or acknowledgment of complexity. Argumentative essays are strengthened by addressing potential objections (eg, "Some might argue that the internet, not the personal computer, was the truly revolutionary technology" or "Some critics argue that increased information access has created overload rather than clarity"). Consider adding a paragraph that raises and then responds to an opposing view.
Your third paragraph is very long and tries to cover too much (the 1980 PC, subsequent technical advances, modems, portable computers). Consider breaking this into two or three paragraphs, each with its own focused claim (eg, one paragraph on the PC's initial impact, one on networking/connectivity, one on portability), each explicitly tied back to your thesis.
Your conclusion introduces a new idea (future predictions) without connecting it back to your main argument. Strengthen the conclusion by restating your thesis in light of the evidence presented and explaining its broader significance.

Factual Accuracy

Please double-check your claim that "IBM Corporation started a revolution... in 1980." IBM released its PC in 1981, and earlier personal computers (Apple II, Commodore PET, etc) existed before IBM entered the market in the late 1970s. For an argumentative essay, factual precision matters for credibility — inaccurate dates or oversimplified history can undermine your argument. Consider researching this more carefully or qualifying your claim (eg, "IBM's entry into the market in 1981 helped popularize...").

Language and Style Issues

Since German is your first language, here are some patterns to watch for:

Subject-verb agreement: "There has been many inventions" → should be "There have been many inventions" (plural subject "inventions" requires plural verb).
Article use: English articles work differently from German; watch phrases like "with the use of Modem" → should be "with the use of a modem" (count noun needs an article).
Spelling: "recieve" should be "receive" (i before e except after c).
Verb tense consistency: You shift between past tense (describing history) and present/future tense (describing today and predictions) somewhat abruptly. Make sure transitions between time frames are signaled clearly, eg, "Since the 1980s..." or "Today, by contrast..."
Sentence-final prepositions/word order: Some sentences show German syntactic influence, eg, "help one sort out, save, delete or add and receive information in one's own home" — the list of verbs is a bit cluttered; consider simplifying: "help users store, edit, and retrieve information at home."
Repetition: Words like "information" and "process" appear very frequently. Consider using synonyms (data, content; handle, manage, analyze) to add variety.
Comma splices: eg, "Some have had a great impact, while others have not." — this one is fine, but check other long sentences for run-ons, such as the third paragraph's sentence "What this meant was that you now had a faster tool that could do more, receive more, and analyze more information than ever before" — good, but nearby sentences run long without clear breaks.

Suggestions for Revision

Rewrite your thesis to state a specific, debatable claim.
Reorganize body paragraphs around distinct sub-claims that support the thesis, each with a topic sentence stating an argument (not just a historical fact).
Add a counterargument paragraph to strengthen your argumentative stance.
Verify historical facts (dates, IBM's role) through a quick source check.
Proofread for article use, subject-verb agreement, and spelling errors.
Revise the conclusion to tie back explicitly to your thesis rather than introducing a new, unsupported prediction.

Final Note

You have good command of vocabulary and a genuine interest in your topic — the next step is to transform this descriptive narrative into a persuasive argument with a clear, defensible claim. I'd be happy to discuss thesis options with you in office hours if that would help.

Feedback on Your Essay: "The Century of Democracy"

Overall Impression

Your essay has an engaging, personal voice and a clear emotional investment in your topic. The idea of calling the 20th century "The Century of Democracy" is provocative and could make for a strong argumentative essay. However, in its current form, the essay reads more like a personal reflection or diary entry than an academic argument. To strengthen it, you need to sharpen your thesis, provide more substantial evidence, and address the complexity of the historical record you are simplifying. Below are suggestions organized by area.

Thesis and Argument

Your central claim—that the 20th century should be remembered primarily as "The Century of Democracy"—is bold, but it needs to be argued, not simply asserted. Right now, the essay moves from a decade-by-decade list (flappers, the Depression, hippies) to a personal, present-tense reflection ("I'm feeling very patriotic") without clearly explaining *why* democracy outweighs these other phenomena as the century's defining feature.

A stronger essay would acknowledge the difficulty of the claim. The 20th century also saw two World Wars, the Holocaust, numerous authoritarian regimes, and ongoing conflicts even after the Cold War. If you want to argue that democracy is nevertheless the century's defining legacy, you need to directly address this tension—perhaps by arguing that the *struggle toward* democracy, even through immense violence, is what defines the era. As it stands, the argument feels one-sided because it omits counter-evidence.

Structure

The essay's organization would benefit from a clearer logical progression:

The opening paragraph introduces multiple decades and their symbols, but these examples (flapper skirts, automobiles) are never tied back to your democracy thesis. Consider cutting this list or explicitly connecting each decade to a step in democracy's development.
The personal reflection on "feeling patriotic" is engaging but shifts the essay's register from analytical to autobiographical. If you keep this material, frame it explicitly as a personal anecdote that *illustrates* a broader historical point, rather than letting it become the essay's main content.
The body paragraphs on Germany and the Soviet Union are your strongest material, but they are underdeveloped. Each is only 2-4 sentences and relies on personal memory rather than concrete facts (dates, figures, causes).
The conclusion introduces a new idea—"democracy as a unifying bond for world peace"—that deserves more development earlier in the essay rather than appearing only at the end.

Suggestion: Restructure with a clear thesis paragraph, 2-3 body paragraphs each developing one piece of evidence in depth (Germany, USSR, end of Cold War), and a conclusion that returns to and complicates your opening claim.

Evidence and Support

Several claims are asserted without support:
"Arms sales have gone down" — by how much? According to what source?
"The idea of nuclear war is practically non-existent today" — this is a strong claim; is it accurate, especially considering ongoing nuclear tensions?
The Soviet Union's "transition to democracy" is presented as complete and straightforward, but this glosses over significant political instability, economic hardship, and the fact that not all former Soviet states became democracies.

For an academic essay, aim to support major claims with specific facts, dates, or (if permitted) sources. Personal anecdotes (like buying a piece of the Berlin Wall) can add color, but should supplement, not replace, factual evidence.

Language and Style

Your English is generally fluent and expressive, but a few patterns need attention:

Sentence fragments/run-ons: "For change is inevitable, and as difficult as it may be to pinpoint any one accomplishment... there is one aspect..." This sentence is overloaded. Consider breaking it into two.
Register shifts: Phrases like "How could we ever forget" and "I'm feeling very patriotic these days" are conversational; academic essays typically maintain a more formal, analytical tone throughout.
Word choice: "Capricious" to describe a decade is unusual — perhaps you mean "unpredictable" or "turbulent"? Also, "superpower" is used to describe reunified Germany, which is historically inaccurate; Germany became a major European power, not a global superpower.
Punctuation: Watch comma usage before "for" and "as" when used as conjunctions—English comma rules differ from German here. For example: "Perhaps, it is because today is November 4th" doesn't need a comma after "Perhaps."
Transitions: Paragraphs often begin with repeated "Perhaps" or move abruptly; consider clearer transitional phrases ("This shift is also visible in...", "A further example is...").

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Sharpen and complicate your thesis to acknowledge counter-evidence.
Reorganize paragraphs so each develops one piece of evidence with facts, not just personal impressions.
Shift the tone from personal reflection toward analytical argument.
Proofread for run-on sentences and comma placement.

You have a compelling idea here—now it needs the structural and evidentiary support to make it persuasive as an academic argument. I look forward to seeing your revision!

Feedback on Your Essay: The Dangers of Atomic Energy

Thank you for sharing this passionate and clearly heartfelt piece. Your commitment to the topic comes through strongly, and you've chosen a subject with real stakes. However, there are several areas—both structural and linguistic—where revision would strengthen your argument considerably. Let's go through them.

Factual Accuracy (Important!)

There is a significant historical error that undermines your credibility: J. Robert Oppenheimer did not "invent" the atomic bomb in the sense of unveiling it alone in 1945. He was the scientific director of the Manhattan Project, a massive collaborative effort involving thousands of scientists. Also, atomic energy was not "invented" as such—nuclear fission was *discovered* (by scientists like Otto Hahn, Lise Meitner, and Fritz Strassmann in 1938), and its destructive application was *developed* through years of research. Since your entire essay opens with this claim, it's worth revising carefully to ensure historical precision. This matters especially in academic writing, where credibility depends on accuracy.

Argumentative Structure and Logic

Your essay currently reads more as an emotional appeal than a structured argument. Consider the following:

Thesis clarity: What exactly are you arguing? Is it that nuclear energy should be banned entirely? That world leaders are negligent? That civic action is needed? Right now, the essay touches on all three but commits fully to none. Choose one central claim and build your paragraphs around supporting it with evidence.

The "many alternatives" claim: You state that we have "many other power source alternatives," but you don't specify what these are or explain why they are preferable. This is a crucial argumentative gap—readers will want evidence here (eg, solar, wind, hydroelectric—and why these are safer or more viable).

Unsupported claims: Statements like "the lack of sight from our leaders is almost appalling" and claims about ozone holes being linked to nuclear energy use need evidence. Nuclear *power plants* do not primarily damage the ozone layer—this is more associated with CFCs and other pollutants. Be careful not to conflate different environmental issues (nuclear waste, nuclear weapons, general pollution, ozone depletion) as though they are the same problem with the same cause.

Conclusion: The final call to action ("Write your local congressman") is abrupt given that nothing earlier in the essay has built toward political advocacy as a solution. If this is your intended message, you need to develop it earlier—perhaps by discussing what citizen action has achieved historically, or what specific policies you want changed.

Language and Style

Since English is your second language, here are some specific points to address:

Typos and repetition: "the the most awesome" (repeated word); "routed" should be "rooted"; "irreversable" should be "irreversible."
Capitalization: "Atomic energy" and "Atomic bomb" don't need capital letters unless used as proper nouns in specific contexts. Also, "It is considered" mid-paragraph shouldn't have a capital "I" unless starting a sentence.
Word choice: "Augments" is a bit unusual in this context—"grows" or "increases" would sound more natural. Similarly, "routed" (rooted) and "capacity" are used somewhat repetitively; try varying your vocabulary.
Tone: Phrases like "the saddest part of this tragedy" and "what is to become of our children" are highly emotive. In academic writing, especially in English-language argumentative essays, it's often more persuasive to let evidence and logical reasoning carry the emotional weight rather than relying on dramatic phrasing. This is a common difference between German and English academic styles—German essays sometimes allow for more overt rhetorical flourish, while English academic convention tends to favor restraint and evidence-based persuasion.

Suggestions for Revision

Fact-check your historical claims about Oppenheimer and the discovery of nuclear fission.
Narrow your thesis: Decide if this essay is about nuclear weapons, nuclear power, general environmental policy, or civic responsibility—then focus.
Add evidence: Cite specific examples (Chernobyl, Fukushima, or statistics on nuclear energy vs. renewable energy) to strengthen your claims.
Separate distinct issues: Nuclear weapons, nuclear power plants, and general pollution are related but distinct problems—address them separately for clarity.
Tone down emotional language slightly and let your evidence do more persuasive work.
Proofread carefully for typos, repeated words, and capitalization errors.

Final Note

Your writing shows genuine engagement with an important issue, and your English is generally quite strong—your sentence structures are ambitious and mostly grammatically sound. With more precise historical grounding, tighter argumentative focus, and a bit more evidence, this essay could become a compelling and rigorous piece of persuasive writing. I encourage you to revise with these points in mind and feel free to bring a draft to office hours if you'd like to discuss further.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting and debatable topic—the impact of television as a communication technology—and you clearly have good ideas about its historical significance (Vietnam War coverage, humanitarian awareness). However, the essay currently reads more as a description of television's importance than as an *argumentative* essay. To strengthen this piece, you need clearer structure, a more explicit thesis, and more developed argumentation. Below are specific areas to focus on in your revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Stance

Your introduction states that television "has to do with" speed of communication and is "the most frequently used and influential invention." But an argumentative essay needs a clear, debatable claim that you will defend throughout. Right now, your thesis is somewhat buried and hedged ("If I were to choose..."). Consider stating directly: *"Television is the most significant communication invention of the 20th century because it has fundamentally transformed how humans perceive and respond to global events."* This gives your reader a clear position to track.

Additionally, you mention "downsides" in your introduction but then only briefly address them in one sentence near the end ("There are many who feel that it can be a harmful thing"). If you're going to raise counterarguments, they need real engagement—either integrate them more substantially as a counterargument you address, or remove this thread from your introduction to maintain focus on your main claim.

Structure and Paragraph Organization

Each paragraph should have a clear controlling idea sentence (topic sentence) that connects back to your thesis. Currently:

Paragraph 2 opens with an awkward negation ("Not even truly dealing with...") that confuses rather than clarifies your point. Try something like: "Beyond simple viewing habits, television has reshaped human life in several profound ways."
Paragraph 3 contains multiple ideas (shrinking the globe, ease of communication, desensitization, harm) that could each be developed separately or more tightly connected. Right now they feel like a list rather than a sustained argument.

Consider using clearer transitions between paragraphs and ideas within paragraphs (eg, "Furthermore," "In addition to shaping public opinion during wartime, television also...").

Development of Evidence

Your examples (Vietnam War, Somalia) are strong choices, but they need more elaboration. Ask yourself:
*What specifically* did people see during the Vietnam War that led to protest? A brief concrete detail (eg, footage of casualties, the My Lai massacre coverage) would strengthen your point.
*How exactly* did television coverage of Somalia lead to public concern? Naming a specific event or effect (eg, humanitarian aid campaigns, U.S. intervention debates) would make this more persuasive.

Right now, these examples are asserted rather than demonstrated. Expanding them with 2-3 additional sentences each would significantly strengthen your argument.

Language and Grammar (specific to German-English transfer issues)

A few patterns to watch for:

Word order transfer: "Not even truly dealing with the amount..." reflects German syntax patterns and doesn't work in English. English typically wants Subject-Verb-Object order maintained even in complex sentences.
Article usage: "the vietnam war" should be "the Vietnam War" (capitalize proper nouns).
Redundant phrasing: "has changed in many ways how we live, and in a way it has, and continues to shrink" — this repetition of "in a way" and "has" creates a cluttered sentence. Simplify: "has changed how we live and continues to shrink the planet."
"made smaller the dimensions of the planet" — this is a direct translation pattern (adjective-object inversion common in German) that sounds unnatural in English. Try: "made the planet smaller" or "shrunk the world."
Comma splices: "like all inventions can be used in a useless way" needs a comma or conjunction: "like all inventions, it can be used in an unhelpful way" (also, "useless" is likely not quite the word you want—perhaps "harmful" or "counterproductive"?).

Conclusion

Your final paragraph reintroduces the "harmful" counterargument only to dismiss it quickly ("Either way, however, that it is used..."). This weakens your argument rather than strengthening it. A strong conclusion should return to your thesis with new insight, not raise unresolved tensions. Consider ending with a clear restatement of *why* television's benefits outweigh its drawbacks, based on the evidence you've provided.

Priority Revisions

Sharpen your thesis into one clear, arguable sentence.
Expand your two main examples (Vietnam, Somalia) with concrete details.
Either develop or remove the counterargument about television's harms—don't leave it underdeveloped.
Proofread for word order and article usage, which are the most noticeable grammar issues stemming from German syntax patterns.

With these revisions, you'll have a much more persuasive and polished essay. Your ideas are genuinely interesting—now let's make sure the structure and language do them justice!

Feedback on "The Invention of the Airplane" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a strong, arguable topic, and your enthusiasm for the subject comes through clearly. The essay has a logical progression from travel to cultural awareness to international relations. However, the essay needs work in three main areas: structural completeness, argument development, and grammatical precision. Below are specific suggestions for revision.

Structure and Organization

Missing conclusion: The essay ends abruptly after discussing postal communication. You need a concluding paragraph that synthesizes your argument and perhaps reflects on the broader significance of air travel (eg, its role in globalization, its future implications, or a return to your opening idea about "impossible concepts").

Thesis clarity: Your introduction touches on three ideas—travel, perception of the world, and the concept of flying itself. The "flying" theme is intriguing but is never developed in the body paragraphs. Either integrate it into your argument or remove it from the introduction so your thesis matches what you actually discuss.

Uneven paragraph development: Your second paragraph (on cultural awareness) is well-developed with a personal example. However, your third paragraph (international relations) feels rushed—it makes several claims (political systems, business, postal communication) without developing any of them with specific examples or evidence, the way you did with your Spain anecdote.

Suggestion: Consider dedicating a full paragraph to international relations and another to global business, using historical examples (eg, diplomatic summits, the growth of multinational corporations, disaster relief efforts) to give this section the same depth as your travel paragraph.

Argument Development

Your personal anecdote about visiting Spain is effective—it grounds an abstract claim in real experience. However, one personal example is not sufficient to support a claim about global cultural awareness. Consider adding:
A historical fact (eg, how long transatlantic travel took before commercial aviation)
A statistic (eg, growth in international tourism since the mid-20th century)
A concrete historical example of an event enabled by air travel (eg, international summits, humanitarian aid flights)

This will make your argument more persuasive to a reader who doesn't share your personal experience.

Language and Grammar

Since German is your first language, watch for the following patterns:

Spelling: "extend" → extent (a common confusion since German uses similar-sounding words differently)

Word order (verb-adverb placement): German often places adverbs differently than English.
   - *Original*: "has improved drastically the establishment of international trade"
   - *Revised*: "has drastically improved the establishment of international trade"

Relative clause construction: 
   - *Original*: "The frequency that which foreign countries are able to communicate"
   - *Revised*: "The frequency with which foreign countries are able to communicate"

Redundant emphasis: "very ethnocentric and limited concept" and "very difficult, time consuming task"—try varying your intensifiers instead of repeating "very" and "extremely" throughout the essay. Consider synonyms like *considerably*, *remarkably*, or restructuring sentences for stronger verbs instead of adverb + adjective combinations.

Run-on/comma splice: 
   - *Original*: "One could travel by boat but this was inconvenient and often dangerous because of various things like disease, hunger, and threatening weather; also as noted above, it was extremely time consuming."
   - This sentence tries to do too much. Consider splitting it: "One could travel by boat, but this option was inconvenient and often dangerous due to disease, hunger, and severe weather. As noted above, it was also extremely time-consuming."

Awkward phrasing: 
   - *Original*: "It also introduced what seemed a completely impossible concept which no human is capable of doing without a machine: the concept of flying."
   - This is wordy and slightly redundant (impossible + incapable). Try: "It also introduced something once thought impossible: unaided human flight."

Colloquial phrasing: "basically speeded up" — In academic writing, avoid filler words like "basically." Simply: "has also accelerated postal communication."

Final Suggestions

Add topic sentences to each paragraph that explicitly connect back to your thesis.
Expand the third paragraph with concrete historical examples.
Write a conclusion (aim for 3-5 sentences) that restates your thesis in new words and offers a final insight or reflection.
Proofread for word order and relative pronoun usage—these are the most frequent grammar issues in your draft.

Overall: You have the ingredients for a strong essay—an interesting topic, a personal connection, and a logical structure. With more balanced development between paragraphs, a conclusion, and careful attention to sentence-level grammar, this essay will be significantly stronger. Well done on tackling a genuinely interesting historical claim!

Feedback on Your AIDS Essay

Thank you for tackling such an important and still-relevant topic. Your essay shows genuine concern for public health and a desire to argue that education is a powerful tool against AIDS. That is a strong, defensible thesis. However, the essay needs significant revision in structure, argumentation, and language before it meets academic standards. Below are my main observations, organized by category.

Thesis and Structure

Your central claim — that education can help prevent AIDS where medicine has failed — is a good one, but it gets lost. The introduction buries it in a subordinate clause, and the essay never returns explicitly to develop or defend it systematically. Each paragraph introduces a new idea (support centers, school assemblies, misconceptions about who is at risk, risky behavior) without clear transitions showing how these ideas build toward your thesis.

Suggestion: State your thesis clearly and prominently at the end of the introduction, eg, "This essay argues that public education—through school programs, community outreach, and awareness campaigns—is currently a more effective tool against AIDS than medical treatment." Then organize body paragraphs so each one supports one clear sub-point (eg, paragraph 1: school education; paragraph 2: public misconceptions about risk groups; paragraph 3: behavioral change). Right now, several paragraphs are only one or two sentences long (eg, "Many schools have begun..."), which suggests underdeveloped ideas. Combine and expand these into fuller paragraphs with evidence and explanation.

Argument Development and Evidence

Your essay makes several claims without supporting evidence: How many schools have started these assembly programs? Which studies show that education reduces infection rates? What are your sources for the claim about dentists? Academic essays require you to back up assertions with data, statistics, or citations, not just anecdote or assumption.

Also, some logical connections are unclear or need more explanation:
The sentence about drug use "as a possible suicide" is confusing — do you mean the risk of accidental infection is comparable to a slow form of self-harm? This needs to be explained more directly.
The concluding paragraph—"Obviously our culture has begun to educate its population or I would be unable to write about this particular topic"—is not a strong or logical closing argument. It doesn't follow that your ability to write about AIDS proves education is effective. Consider replacing this with a summary of your strongest points and a forward-looking statement about what more needs to be done.

Register and Style

This is an academic essay, so it should avoid the informal abbreviations you've used throughout: "&", "w/", "w/ his" — these need to be written out fully ("and," "with"). Academic writing in English avoids these shorthand forms even though they may be more common in informal German writing contexts (eg, quick notes).

Also, watch your tone: phrases like "selling their lives short" and "our population" are somewhat casual/rhetorical. Consider a more measured, analytical tone appropriate for argumentative essays, eg, "individuals underestimate the risks associated with unprotected sexual contact with unfamiliar partners."

Grammar and Mechanics (Common German-to-English Transfer Issues)

A few recurring errors:
"it's" vs. "its": You wrote "it's killing our population" (correct) but should double check throughout — "its" is possessive, "it's" is a contraction of "it is."
"viruses" vs. "virus": "The AIDS viruses was discovered" should be "The AIDS virus was discovered" (singular).
"peoples" vs. "people's": "changed many peoples lives" should be "many people's lives" (possessive apostrophe needed).
"interact sexual" should be "interact sexually" (adverb form needed to modify the verb "interact").
Long, run-on sentences: The sentence beginning "Upon realizing that homosexuals are not the only possible carriers..." is very long and packs too many ideas together, making it hard to follow. Consider breaking this into two or three shorter sentences.

Content Accuracy

Since this essay seems to present AIDS as essentially untreatable ("due to the ineffectiveness of medicines"), please double-check the currency of your medical information. If this essay is meant to reflect a specific historical period (eg, the 1980s–90s, before antiretroviral therapy), make that explicit. If it's meant to be current, you'll need to update your claims, since medical treatment for HIV/AIDS has advanced enormously.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Clarify and foreground your thesis.
Expand underdeveloped paragraphs and cut informal abbreviations.
Add concrete evidence/citations for your claims.
Revise the conclusion to logically summarize your argument.
Proofread for the grammar patterns noted above (apostrophes, singular/plural, adverb formation).

You have a promising topic and a clear moral stance — with more structure, evidence, and precision, this essay can become a much stronger piece of argumentative writing. I'd be happy to look at a revised draft.

Feedback on Your Essay: Contact Lenses

Overall Impression

Your writing has energy and personal voice—qualities that make a text enjoyable to read. However, this assignment asks for an argumentative essay, and right now your text reads more like a personal narrative or reflective blog post. To move it closer to the genre you need, I'd like you to focus on three things: strengthening your argument, adding balance, and tightening the language and punctuation.

Argument and Structure

Your thesis claims that contact lenses are "one of the most important inventions of the 20th century." This is a strong, debatable claim—exactly what an argumentative essay needs. The problem is that your evidence never really supports this bold claim; it only supports the smaller point that *contact lenses have been useful for you personally*. These are two different arguments:

Claim A: Contact lenses are one of the most important 20th-century inventions.
Claim B: Contact lenses have significantly improved my personal quality of life.

Right now, you argue B while claiming A. To fix this, either narrow your thesis to match your evidence (eg, "Contact lenses have transformed the way people with poor eyesight can live active lives") or broaden your evidence to match your bigger claim (eg, mention how contact lenses have impacted athletes, workers in certain professions, people in developing countries, or the medical/optical industry more broadly).

Additionally, an argumentative essay should acknowledge a counterargument. You mention that hard lenses were uncomfortable, but you dismiss this quickly. Consider addressing: Are there disadvantages to contacts (cost, risk of infection, difficulty for some users)? Even briefly acknowledging and then countering an opposing view would make your argument feel more persuasive and less one-sided.

Evidence

Your personal anecdotes (basketball, swimming, disposables) are vivid and effective *as illustrations*, but an argumentative essay usually needs more than personal experience—especially to support a superlative claim like "most important invention." Consider adding one or two outside facts: eg, how many people worldwide wear contact lenses, how they've impacted sports/military/medicine, or a brief historical note on their invention. This would elevate the essay from anecdote to argument.

Style and Register

The essay currently uses many features of spoken/informal English: exclamation marks, rhetorical questions ("How would I be able to see...?"), and scare quotes around words like "lense craze," "sting," "blind," and "feel my way." In academic argumentative writing, these devices should be used sparingly. I recommend:

Removing most exclamation marks; let your word choice convey enthusiasm instead.
Reducing quotation marks used for emphasis—use them only for actual quoted terms (eg, the technical term *hard lenses* the first time you introduce it, not every time).
Turning your opening rhetorical questions into a direct, declarative thesis statement.

Language and Mechanics

A few recurring issues, which may relate to German writing conventions, need attention:

Ampersands (&): Avoid using "&" in academic writing; always write "and."
Semicolons: You use semicolons where a comma or period would be more natural (eg, "if I wore my glasses, they would end up on the floor; and if I didn't wear them..."). In English, semicolons usually join two complete independent clauses, not clauses beginning with "and" or "if."
Subject-verb agreement: "another type of lenses were made" → should be "was made" (type is singular). Similarly, "a style called 'hard lenses' were" → "was."
Spelling: "lense" should be "lens" (plural: "lenses").
Dashes and punctuation before quotation marks: Some of your dash usage is inconsistent (eg, "-- But, people buy them"). In English, a comma or period, not a dash, typically follows a parenthetical remark like the one about colored lenses.
Comma splices: Sentences like "I got my first pair of contact lenses when I was fourteen years old. I had been wearing glasses..." are fine, but elsewhere you have run-ons that would benefit from being split into two sentences (eg, the swimming sentence).

Suggested Next Steps

Rewrite your thesis to align with the evidence you actually provide, or add broader evidence to support the bigger claim.
Add a short paragraph addressing a counterargument or limitation of contact lenses.
Revise punctuation: replace "&" with "and," check semicolon usage, and reduce scare quotes.
Tone down exclamation marks and rhetorical questions to sound more analytical and less conversational.
Proofread for subject-verb agreement and spelling ("lens/lenses").

You have a genuinely engaging personal story here—now it's a matter of shaping it into a more balanced, evidence-supported argument. I look forward to seeing your revision!

Feedback on "Feminism as a Discovery"

Overall impression

You have chosen an original and thought-provoking angle: comparing feminism to a "discovery" alongside technological inventions. This is a creative rhetorical move, and your concluding metaphor of a "discovery in progress" is genuinely elegant. However, the essay in its current form is too short and underdeveloped to fully support this ambitious framing. Below are suggestions organized by argument, structure, content, and language.

---

The Central Analogy

The comparison between feminism and technological discoveries (cars, telephones, nuclear energy) is intriguing, but you need to justify it more explicitly. Why is feminism like a discovery rather than, say, a social movement, an ideology, or a slow cultural shift? Discoveries are typically understood as revealing something that already existed (like a scientific law), while inventions create something new. Which do you mean? Right now, the analogy is asserted at the beginning and end but not really argued for in the body paragraphs. Consider adding a sentence or two explaining *why* you think "discovery" is the right word—perhaps because society is discovering injustices that were always there but unrecognized. This would strengthen your unusual and compelling thesis.

Structure and Development

At under 400 words, this essay reads more like an outline than a fully developed argument. Each body paragraph introduces an idea (legal/economic equality; representation and image) but doesn't develop it with sufficient evidence, examples, or analysis. For a stronger essay, consider:

Expanding paragraph 2 with more historical detail and perhaps one concrete example (a law, a court case, a statistic with a source).
Developing paragraph 3 with a specific example of how "pornography or language" shapes women's image—right now this claim is abstract. A brief example (eg, a specific word, a specific media trend) would make it more persuasive and vivid.
Adding a paragraph that addresses a counterargument—for instance, someone might argue that gains in feminism are already "mainstream" today. Engaging with this would strengthen your claim that it is still "in progress."

Accuracy of Claims

A few factual points need checking:
Women's suffrage in the U.S. came in 1920 (19th Amendment), not "the 1800s"—though you're right that a few individual states granted suffrage earlier (Wyoming, 1869). Since this is presented as background fact, precision matters here.
The "60 cents for every dollar" statistic is a well-known figure, but you should cite where it comes from (and specify time period/country), since data varies over decades and by country.

Since this is an argumentative essay, unsupported statistics can weaken your credibility. Even one source citation would help.

Language and Style

Your English is generally strong and fluent, and only a few things need adjustment:

Typo: "0th century" should be "20th century."
Awkward phrasing: "it was still the most typical as well as the ideal to have the woman in the home" — consider rephrasing: "it was still both typical and considered ideal for women to remain at home."
Self-correction mid-sentence: "It's only recently that we've 'discovered', or begun to think about rather, how much..." This reads as unfinished editing. Choose one verb and commit to it: "It is only recently that we have begun to recognize how much..."
Article usage: "the woman is still only earning" should be "women are still only earning" (avoid the generic definite article + singular, which is a common transfer from German "die Frau").
Comma splices: "Feminism is better described as a 'discovery in progress'; it's a movement, an attitude that has not yet become mainstream." This works well with the semicolon, but check other sentences for similar constructions that may need periods or conjunctions instead of commas.

Tone and Register

The essay uses contractions ("it's," "we've") which can be appropriate for a more informal argumentative piece, but check your assignment guidelines—many academic contexts expect formal register without contractions. If this is meant to be more academic, consider revising these throughout.

---

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Expand and justify your central analogy—explain more explicitly *why* feminism functions as a "discovery."
Develop each paragraph with more evidence, examples, and specific historical or statistical support (with citations).
Add a counterargument paragraph to strengthen the claim that feminism remains "in progress."
Fix the factual/historical details (suffrage dates).
Polish language: fix the typo, avoid self-corrections mid-sentence, watch article usage with generic nouns, and decide on a consistent register (formal vs. informal).

You have a strong conceptual core here—now it needs more "flesh on the bones" to become a fully convincing academic essay. I look forward to seeing the expanded version!

Feedback on Your Essay: "Caring for the Environment"

Overall Impression

You have chosen a compelling and relevant topic, and your enthusiasm for the subject comes through clearly. However, the essay currently reads more like a list of related topics than a developed argument. To strengthen it, you need to focus on three main areas: sharpening your thesis and argumentative structure, deepening your analysis with evidence, and revising for academic register and grammar.

Thesis and Argumentation

Your introduction states that environmental awareness has "most significantly changed people's lives," but the essay doesn't really argue *why* or *how* this is true—it mostly describes a series of environmental sub-topics (nuclear weapons/waste, deforestation, recycling, energy conservation, animal rights) without connecting them analytically. Ask yourself: What is the *claim* you want to defend? Possible options:

Environmental awareness represents a fundamental shift in how humans see their relationship to nature (from domination to stewardship).
This shift has produced concrete changes in law, economy, and daily behavior.

Once you choose a clear thesis, each paragraph should *argue* a point that supports it, not simply list a phenomenon. For example, instead of just describing recycling programs, explain how recycling reflects a changed mindset and what evidence shows this change has actually altered behavior or outcomes.

Also reconsider the nuclear weapons/waste paragraph—nuclear weapons themselves are a security issue, not directly an environmental one. If you want to keep this content, connect it more explicitly to environmental consequences (eg, radioactive contamination, testing sites) rather than treating weapons and waste as one undifferentiated topic.

Development and Evidence

Right now, most paragraphs consist of general assertions ("Many countries realize that recycling is...beneficial") without supporting evidence—no statistics, historical dates, examples of specific policies, or references to real events (eg, the Clean Water Act, the Kyoto Protocol, specific reforestation campaigns). For an argumentative essay, even brief, concrete examples will make your claims far more persuasive. Consider adding at least one specific fact or example per paragraph.

Structure and Paragraph Unity

Several paragraphs repeat the same pattern ("We used to assume X was unlimited; now we realize Y"), which becomes repetitive. Try varying your sentence structures and topic sentences. Additionally, some paragraphs contain multiple loosely connected ideas (eg, the recycling paragraph shifts from packaging to homeless individuals collecting cans to national economies). Each paragraph should have one clear focus, introduced by a strong topic sentence and closed with a sentence that ties back to your thesis.

Register and Style

This is an academic essay, so avoid text-message abbreviations and symbols: "&", "w/o", "b/c", "2" should all be spelled out ("and," "without," "because," "two"). Academic writing also avoids exclamation marks (as in your final sentence) and overly casual phrasing like "hopeful by the 21st century we will have fixed everything." Consider a more measured, analytical closing statement.

Grammar and Word Choice (Language Transfer Issues)

A few specific corrections:

"countries that which have many weapons" → "countries that have many weapons" (only one relative pronoun needed).
"we are more considerable of our animal life" → "we are more considerate of animal life" (false friend: *considerable* ≠ *rücksichtsvoll*; you want "considerate").
"conserving is not beneficial to our environment but it is also saves money" → likely meant: "conserving is not only beneficial to our environment but also saves money." Watch for the correlative structure "not only...but also," which seems influenced by German "nicht nur...sondern auch"—the English structure needs parallel verb forms.
"One realizes that animals have rights too" — this sentence suddenly shifts to a generic "one," while the rest of the essay uses "we." Keep pronoun use consistent.
Watch subject-verb agreement and article usage throughout (eg, "the idea...that of caring for the environment" is awkward; consider "the idea of environmental awareness" instead).

Suggested Revision Steps

Write a one-sentence thesis that makes a specific, arguable claim.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one supports that thesis with one clear idea plus evidence.
Cut or transform the nuclear weapons paragraph so it directly relates to environmental impact.
Do a full read-through solely to catch abbreviations, symbols, and informal phrasing.
Proofread for the specific grammar points noted above, and check for consistent pronoun use.

You have a solid foundation and clear passion for this topic—your revision should focus on transforming description into argument, and casual phrasing into academic register. I'd be happy to review a revised draft once you've reworked the thesis and paragraph structure.

Feedback on Your Essay: Computers and Their Impact on Life

Thank you for sharing this draft. You raise an interesting and relatable topic, and your personal examples make the essay easy to follow. However, to bring this piece up to the standard of academic argumentative writing, there are several areas you should revise: the strength of your argument, the organization of your ideas, and some recurring language issues that stem from informal writing habits and, in a few cases, German sentence patterns. Below I address each area with concrete suggestions.

Argument and Content

Right now, your essay reads more like a personal narrative than an argumentative essay. You *describe* how computers have changed your life, but you don't build a clear, analytical claim that you then support with reasoning and evidence. An argumentative essay needs:

A clear thesis statement early on (eg, "Computers have fundamentally transformed how individuals manage information and interact with institutions, making everyday tasks faster and more reliable.")
Distinct supporting points, each with its own paragraph
At least a brief acknowledgment of counterarguments (eg, downsides of computers, such as dependency, security risks, or job loss) — even a strong personal-experience essay benefits from this, as it shows critical thinking rather than one-sided reporting.
A conclusion that returns to your thesis and explains its broader significance, rather than ending abruptly with a general statement.

The second paragraph, about applying to the Madrid program, is a good example but feels disconnected from the first. Right now it reads as "here's another story," not "here's my next piece of evidence for my claim." Add a transition sentence that explicitly links this example back to your thesis (eg, "Institutional processes reveal an equally important dimension of computers' influence: efficiency in large-scale administration.")

Organization

Your paragraphs are very long and combine multiple ideas (storage, calculations, word processing, online purchasing) without clear breaks. Consider:

Breaking each idea into its own paragraph with a topic sentence.
Using clear transitions between examples ("In addition to storage capacity..." / "Beyond personal use, computers also...").
Adding a short concluding paragraph that synthesizes your points rather than ending on a single summarizing sentence.

Style and Register

This is the biggest issue for an academic essay. You use text-message abbreviations throughout: "w/," "&," "info." These are inappropriate for formal writing and must be replaced with full words: *with*, *and*, *information*. Academic English requires full, formal language even when the topic is personal.

Also try to reduce your reliance on "I" as the constant subject. While personal experience is valid evidence, phrasing like "I'm able to," "I have," "I realize" repeated many times makes the essay sound like a diary entry. Try alternating with more general or analytical phrasing: "This capability allows users to..." or "Such tools significantly reduce the time needed to..."

Grammar and Mechanics

A few recurring errors to fix:

"effected" → "affected." *Affect* is the verb meaning "to influence"; *effect* is usually the noun (or means "to bring about," rarely used here).
"my life has change" → "my life has changed" (past participle needed after *has*).
Comma splices: eg, "I have a computer and my life has changed because of it. I'm able to store..." — many of your sentences run together independent clauses with only commas. Use periods, semicolons, or coordinating conjunctions correctly.
"the this program" — remove the extra article ("this program").
"would inevitably inundated" — missing verb: "would inevitably be inundated."
"flew here form the US" — typo: "from."
Subjunctive/conditional consistency: "But, w/out the use of computers I would still be struggling..." is correct in form, but check throughout that your conditional structures (would/would have) are used consistently — German conditional constructions sometimes carry over awkwardly into English tense choices.

Suggested Next Steps

Write a one-sentence thesis statement and place it at the end of your introduction.
Reorganize your paragraphs so each addresses one clear point (eg, storage/organization; calculation and editing; institutional/administrative efficiency).
Replace all abbreviations with full words.
Add a paragraph acknowledging a potential drawback or limitation of computers, then explain why your main argument still holds.
Proofread specifically for verb tense agreement and comma splices — reading your sentences aloud can help you hear where clauses need separating.

Overall, your ideas are promising and your examples are vivid and concrete — you just need to sharpen the argumentative structure and formalize the language. I'd be happy to look at a revised draft once you've addressed these points.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Satellite and Global Awareness

Thank you for sharing this passionate and thought-provoking piece. Your enthusiasm for the topic and your use of a concrete, powerful example (Tiananmen Square) show real argumentative instinct. Below is feedback organized by area, with specific suggestions for revision.

Argument and Structure

Your central claim — that satellites created a new kind of "nowness" that changes political accountability — is compelling, but it needs to be stated more clearly and *early*. Right now, the thesis is buried inside several short, fragmented sentences. Consider opening with one clear sentence that states your full argument, for example: "The satellite is the most significant invention of the century because it has created global political accountability through instantaneous, unfiltered witness of events."

The essay would also benefit from:
A second example. One case study (Tiananmen Square) is vivid, but a single example makes the argument feel narrow. Even a brief second example (eg, a natural disaster, a war, or another political event) would strengthen your claim that this is a broader phenomenon, not an isolated case.
Clearer logical transitions. Sentences like "As it happens— the most profound byproduct of the satellite" are grammatically incomplete and interrupt the flow. Each new idea should be linked explicitly to the previous one (eg, "This immediacy, in turn, means that...").
A stronger conclusion. The final sentence ("when China is placed down the block, who wouldn't want to help his neighbor?") is a nice image, but it appears too suddenly and isn't explained. Consider developing this "global neighborhood" metaphor as a proper concluding idea — perhaps as your final paragraph's main point, not a single throwaway line.

Sentence-Level Clarity

Several sentences are fragments or lack clear subjects and verbs, which is likely due to translating rhythm/emphasis patterns from German (where sentence fragments and dashes for emphasis are more common in rhetorical writing). In English academic writing, sentences generally need a complete subject-verb structure. For example:

*Original:* "As it happens- the most profound byproduct of the satellite."
 *Revised:* "This immediacy is the most profound byproduct of the satellite."

*Original:* "And, to a certain extent, it has influenced now the world functions."
 *Revised:* "It has also influenced how the world functions." (note: "now" → "how")

*Original:* "Freedom US. the tank of injustice made its way into the lives of most of the world's people."
 *Revised:* "The image of freedom versus the tank of injustice reached the lives of people around the world."

I'd recommend reading your essay aloud — this often reveals where a sentence is missing a verb or feels incomplete.

Word Choice and Register

"Nowness" is a creative coinage, but in academic writing it's better to use an established term like *immediacy* or *instantaneity*, unless you explicitly define your invented term for the reader.
"Geo-centric satellite" is not a standard term — you likely mean *geostationary* or simply *satellite technology*. Double-check technical vocabulary before submitting.
Avoid absolute claims like "Without question" and "With certainty" repeated multiple times. Academic writing values measured claims; overusing intensifiers can weaken credibility rather than strengthen it. Try varying with "It seems clear that..." or simply presenting the evidence and letting it speak.

Spelling and Mechanics

There are several spelling errors that a careful proofreading pass would catch:
"evention" → invention
"preceed" → precede
"litterally" → literally
"Teniman Square" → Tiananmen Square
"openned" → opened
"sensorship" → censorship
"mor" → more
"he young hero" → the young hero
"a like" → alike

Also check capitalization: "T.V." is fine, but be consistent (you also write "US." where you likely mean "vs." — versus — not the abbreviation for United States, which caused some confusion in that sentence).

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Write a clear, complete thesis sentence near the beginning.
Add a second example to broaden your evidence base.
Fix sentence fragments — ensure each sentence has a subject and verb.
Proofread carefully for spelling (perhaps read the essay backward, sentence by sentence, to catch errors your eye skips over when reading normally).
Develop your closing metaphor about the "global neighborhood" into a full concluding paragraph.

You have a genuinely interesting argument here — the political and ethical implications of "watched" governments is a rich topic. With attention to sentence structure and a bit more evidence, this could become a strong, persuasive essay. I'd be happy to look at a revised draft.
