Feedback Report: Argumentative Essay on Technology and Computers

Overall Impression

Your essay addresses a clear and relevant topic—the impact of computer technology on society, particularly in education and business. You have a logical structure with distinct paragraphs for different areas of influence (companies, students, teaching tools). However, the essay would benefit significantly from revision in several key areas: developing a stronger thesis, providing more specific evidence, refining sentence structure, and correcting grammatical and spelling errors. Below, I outline the main areas for improvement.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your introduction states that technology "has greatly influenced the lives and companies of the world," but this claim is quite broad and descriptive rather than argumentative. An argumentative essay should take a clear position or make a claim that could be debated. Consider revising your thesis to make a more specific, arguable claim—for example, "Despite concerns about screen time and technology dependence, computers have been overwhelmingly beneficial to society by streamlining data management and enhancing educational opportunities." This gives your essay a clearer purpose and something to defend throughout.

Development and Evidence

Your paragraphs would be much stronger with specific examples, statistics, or even brief anecdotes rather than general statements. For instance:
Instead of "Computers have been a god send for large companies," consider citing a specific type of company or a statistic about efficiency gains.
In the paragraph about hospitals and law firms, you could mention specific systems (eg, electronic health records) to add credibility.
The paragraph about "cosm graph in Mathematics" is unclear—did you mean "cosmology" or "graphing calculators"? This section needs clarification and a concrete example (eg, simulation software, online tutorials, specific programs like GeoGebra).

Adding "for example" or "for instance" followed by concrete details will strengthen your argument considerably.

Grammar and Spelling Issues

There are several errors that need correction. I recommend using a spell-checker and reading your essay aloud to catch these:

"definantly" → "definitely"
"god send" → "godsend" (one word)
"accesible" → "accessible"
"w/o" → avoid abbreviations in formal writing; write "without"
"previosly" → "previously"
"cosm graph" → unclear; please clarify what you mean here
"The extend" → "The extent"
"but they is clearly imp" → "but they are clearly important" (avoid abbreviations like "imp" in academic writing)

Sentence Structure (Note for German L1 Speakers)

I notice a few structural patterns that may reflect German sentence construction:

"Computers have enabled to students to learn" – In English, we don't use "to" before the object in this construction. Correct version: "Computers have enabled students to learn."
Word order in complex sentences: English typically prefers Subject-Verb-Object order, and German-influenced sentences sometimes place verbs differently. Reread your sentences to ensure the verb follows the subject directly, especially in subordinate clauses.
Article usage: Watch for missing or unnecessary articles (a/an/the), as German and English differ in when articles are required. For example, "the use of a card catalog is no longer used" is also redundant (see below).

Redundancy and Clarity

Some sentences contain redundant phrasing:
"The use of a card catalog is no longer used" – This is repetitive. Try: "Card catalogs are no longer used" or "Libraries no longer rely on card catalogs."
"students now have access to a whole new way of learning" is vague—specify what this "new way" entails.

Conclusion

Your conclusion is quite short and doesn't fully synthesize your arguments. Rather than ending with a rhetorical question ("who knows where these machines will take us"), consider summarizing your main points and restating your thesis with greater conviction. You might also briefly acknowledge a counterargument (eg, concerns about over-reliance on technology) and explain why your position still holds—this is common in strong argumentative essays.

Paragraph Transitions

Your paragraphs jump between topics (companies → students → teaching tools) without clear transitional sentences. Adding transitions like "In addition to benefiting companies, computers have also transformed..." would help the essay flow more smoothly.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Sharpen your thesis into an arguable claim.
Add specific examples and evidence to support each paragraph.
Proofread carefully for spelling errors and avoid informal abbreviations (w/o, imp).
Review sentence structures, particularly verb placement and article usage, which may be influenced by German syntax.
Strengthen your conclusion by summarizing arguments and addressing a counterpoint.

With these revisions, your essay will have a much stronger argumentative foundation and greater clarity. I encourage you to revise a draft and, if possible, read it aloud or have a peer review it before submission—this often helps catch both grammatical errors and gaps in logical flow. Keep up the good work on structuring your ideas; the content itself is engaging and relevant.

Feedback on "The Impact of Television" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an engaging topic and shows genuine enthusiasm for your subject. You have interesting ideas about how television connects people globally and shapes understanding of the world. However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of related thoughts than a tightly structured argument. Below are suggestions to strengthen both the content and the language.

Argument and Structure

Thesis and scope: Your opening claims that "every person in the world" can watch events as they happen is an overstatement — many people lack access to television or the internet. Consider qualifying this claim (eg, "in much of the developed world" or "for those with access to television") to make your argument more credible and precise.

Paragraph unity: Your first two paragraphs work well together, building toward the "global village" idea. However, the third paragraph shifts to entertainment and introduces a counterargument (TV glorifies violence, distorts reality) that you dismiss quite quickly with "this sense of reality is not so distorted." This deserves more development — why isn't it distorted? What evidence supports this? Right now, it feels like an assertion rather than a reasoned conclusion.

Missing conclusion: The essay ends abruptly after discussing entertainment. Add a concluding paragraph that returns to your main claim (TV's transformative role) and synthesizes the two functions you've discussed (news/information and entertainment).

Attribution: You mention "one journalist" who coined "global village" — this is actually the media theorist Marshall McLuhan, not a journalist. Since this is a well-known concept, name him directly and, ideally, cite the source.

Language and Style

Several patterns in your writing suggest interference from German sentence structure, which is very common and easy to fix with practice.

Overly long sentences with multiple joined clauses.
German permits long sentences with several subordinate clauses connected by commas; English readers expect shorter, more clearly separated sentences. For example:

> "While previously people had to wait days, or even weeks for news (which consisted of second-or third-hand accounts, which always involved much interpretation & distortion) now they can see it happening, for themselves, as it is happening, this allows them to make their own, informed judgment about the event; and if a reaction is necessary- such as the gathering of opposition to a certain law -- it can be made immediately, while the emotions are still strong, and before the action is finalized."

This one sentence contains at least four separate ideas. Try breaking it into 3–4 shorter sentences:

> "Previously, people had to wait days or even weeks for news, which often consisted of second- or third-hand accounts full of interpretation and distortion. Now, they can witness events as they happen and form their own informed judgments. This also allows for faster reactions—for example, organizing opposition to a new law—while public emotion is still strong and before the situation is resolved."

Comma splices.
You frequently join independent clauses with only a comma, where English requires a period, semicolon, or conjunction:
"...now they can see it happening, for themselves, as it is happening, this allows them..." → Add "which" or start a new sentence: "...as it is happening. This allows them..."

Punctuation choices.
You use dashes and semicolons very frequently, sometimes inconsistently (mixing single and double hyphens as dashes). In academic English, commas and periods are generally preferred for clarity. Reserve dashes for genuine emphasis or interruption, and use semicolons only to join two complete, closely related sentences.

Ampersands (&).
Avoid "&" in formal academic writing; always write "and."

Colloquial asides.
Phrases like "(well, at least everybody who watches)" are conversational and undercut the formal register expected in academic essays. Either remove such asides or integrate the qualification more formally: "at least among those who own a television."

Grammar slip.
"making life seem the good guy always wins" should be "making it seem as though the good guy always wins" or "making life seem like a story in which the good guy always wins."

Suggestions for Revision

Add a clear thesis statement at the end of your introduction stating both the benefits and potential drawbacks you'll discuss.
Break long sentences into shorter, clearer units — aim for one main idea per sentence.
Replace comma splices with periods, semicolons, or subordinating conjunctions ("which," "because," "while").
Develop your counterargument about TV's distortion of reality more fully — give an example and explain your reasoning.
Write a concluding paragraph that ties together your points about information and entertainment.
Proofread for punctuation consistency (dashes, ampersands) and register (avoid casual asides).

You have strong ideas here — with attention to sentence structure and paragraph organization, this essay could become a compelling, polished argument. Keep up the good work!

Feedback on Your Essay: The Microwave Revolution

Thank you for sharing this creative and enthusiastic piece! Your extended metaphor comparing culinary history to the sea is imaginative and shows real stylistic ambition. Below is feedback organized by category, with specific suggestions for revision.

Content and Argument Development

Your central claim—that the microwave represents a revolutionary break from tradition—is clear and defensible. However, the essay would benefit from more developed evidence in a few areas:

The science needs more precision. You explain that microwaves "excite water particles" causing friction and heat—this is good, but could you explain *why* this is revolutionary compared to fire-based heating? What makes this mechanism fundamentally different, not just novel?

The claim about "revolutionizing a science" in your second paragraph is strong language. Consider: are you arguing the microwave revolutionized cooking as a *practice*, or as a *science*? These are different claims requiring different evidence.

Consider addressing a counterargument. Skilled argumentative essays often acknowledge opposing views. For instance, some critics argue microwaves don't cook food as evenly or that they've changed eating habits negatively (less family cooking time, nutritional concerns). Briefly addressing this would strengthen your argument's credibility.

The historical survey in paragraph 2 (cavemen → spits → wood stoves → gas/electric) is effective, but feels rushed. Consider dwelling on one or two of these transitions instead of listing all of them, or explain *why* each shift happened before the microwave's "complete break."

Structure

Your five-paragraph structure works well: introduction, historical break, time-saving benefits, conclusion. One suggestion:

Your third paragraph covers several distinct benefits (speed, safety for children, reheating). Consider whether these deserve separate paragraphs, or whether you need clearer topic sentences to organize them within one paragraph.

Language and Grammar (Common Patterns)

Since German is your first language, here are some patterns to watch for:

Spelling errors (proofread carefully—these look like typos rather than grammar issues):
"culunary" → culinary
"consistant" → consistent
"rythm" → rhythm
"twentyth" → twentieth
"comodity" → commodity
"differents" → different
"dizzing" → dizzying
"revelations" → likely meant "innovations" (revelation usually implies divine/sudden insight, not the right connotation here)

Verb tense/form issues:
"Progress has move slow" → "Progress has moved slowly"
"have showed little changed" → "have shown little change"
"cave men cooked" — this is correct, but check consistency: you switch between present and past tense in your historical survey. Decide: are you narrating history (past tense) or making general statements (present tense)?
"the fool of mankind" → likely "the food of mankind" (typo)
"a mother who works, can come home can whip up" → needs restructuring: "A mother who works can come home and whip up..."

Article usage (a known challenge for German speakers, since German and English article systems differ):
"the source of life" — check if you need "a source"
"As History progressed" — "History" doesn't need capitalization or an article here in most contexts

Sentence fragments/run-ons:
"For As I have said before, there is no fire!" — this is grammatically confusing. Did you mean "For, as I have said before, there is no fire"? Remove "For" or restructure.
Watch for comma splices, eg, "A mother who works, can come home can whip up something in minutes in the microwave, whereas before she would have been slaving in the kitchen" — this needs punctuation/conjunction adjustments.

Style

Your voice is enthusiastic and the extended metaphor (sea/waves/storm) is a strong stylistic choice—but ensure it doesn't overshadow clarity. In academic writing, especially argumentative essays, examiners will want to see the metaphor purposefully tied back to your thesis, not just as decorative language.

Also consider: "FIRE" in all-caps and multiple italics/emphasis markers ("Again time is conserved") are more common in informal writing. In academic essays, let your word choice and sentence structure create emphasis rather than typographical tricks.

Priority Revisions

Proofread for spelling (many errors are simple typos)
Fix verb tense consistency throughout
Clarify the "revolutionized a science" claim
Add at least one counterargument or complicating perspective
Revise run-on/fragment sentences for clarity

Overall, this is a lively, original take on a topic that could easily become mundane. With careful editing for grammar and a bit more argumentative depth, this could be a strong essay. Keep the creative energy—just tighten the technical execution!

Feedback on Your Essay on Radio's Impact on the 20th Century

Overall Impression

You have chosen an interesting topic, and your personal examples (the weather forecast ritual, your great-grandmother's relationship with television) show real engagement with the subject. However, the essay currently reads more like a set of loosely connected observations than a tightly argued essay. My main suggestions concern the argumentative structure; I also point out some language issues common for German speakers writing in English.

Clarify Your Thesis and Follow Through on It

Your introduction makes a striking claim: the machine gun and the radio made repressive government possible. This is a strong, specific thesis with real argumentative potential — but you never return to it. After the second paragraph, "repression" and "propaganda" disappear entirely, and the essay becomes a general survey of radio (and then television) as sources of information and entertainment.

You essentially have two different essays here:
Essay A: Radio as a tool of political control and propaganda.
Essay B: Radio/TV as everyday sources of information and entertainment.

Decide which one you want to write, or explicitly connect the two. If you keep the propaganda angle, you need a paragraph showing *how* radio enables control (eg, one voice reaching millions instantly, no possibility of dialogue or fact-checking, state monopoly on transmitters). If you drop it, rewrite your introduction so it doesn't promise an argument the essay doesn't deliver — otherwise the reader feels misled by paragraph three.

Watch for Internal Contradictions

You write that radio "keeps people informed" and is a "source of information," but then say it turns people into "passive observers rather than active participants." These two claims are in tension: being well-informed is usually associated with active citizenship, not passivity. If you want to make the "passive observer" point, you need to explain *why* listening/watching is passive (eg, no interaction, no verification, one-directional communication) rather than simply asserting it.

Distinguish Radio from Television

Your fourth paragraph shifts from radio to television without much justification, and by the fifth paragraph you are discussing television and video games exclusively. If your essay's stated topic is "radio," you should either (a) explicitly frame television as radio's technological descendant and justify treating them together, or (b) keep the focus on radio and use television only briefly, as a comparison. Right now, the essay's title promises "radio," but half of it argues about television.

Strengthen the Conclusion

Ending with "I don't know what life was like before radio" undercuts your authority as an arguer. This may be true personally, but an argumentative essay should end by reinforcing your thesis, not admitting uncertainty. Consider ending by returning explicitly to your opening claim (repression/propaganda) or by summarizing the balance between radio's benefits (information, entertainment) and its risks (passivity, manipulation).

Paragraph-Level Suggestions

The anecdote about your great-grandmother is charming but needs a clearer analytical point: what does her example *prove*? That television provides comfort to isolated elderly people? That it replaces human interaction? Make the point explicit rather than letting the anecdote stand alone.
"Newspapers had been around for many years but it was not until the invention of radio that the power of the media became evident" — this is a good comparative point; expand it. What specifically changed with radio that newspapers couldn't do?

Language and Mechanics

A few recurring issues to fix in revision:

Apostrophes: You consistently write "don' t," "it' s" with a space before the apostrophe — this is likely a typing/autocorrect issue, but check carefully, as it appears throughout.
"Marshall Law" should be "martial law" (a common confusion, since "Marshall" is a name).
Garbled sentence: "I don't think any people of my generation can even comprehend now our lives would be different" — this sentence is missing a word; you likely mean "can even comprehend how our lives would be different." Also, "any people" should probably be "anyone."
Encoding error: "se�ora" should be "señora" — but also consider whether a Spanish word is appropriate here in an English/German-context essay; if you mean "great-grandmother," it may be clearer to use the English term unless the Spanish word is doing specific cultural work you want to signal.
Comma splice: "it sends its signals through the air, waves, with potential for millions of receivers" is unclear — do you mean "through the airwaves"? Consider rewriting as: "it sends its signal through the airwaves, with the potential to reach millions of receivers."

Summary

Your strongest move for revision is to decide on one clear argumentative thread — either radio-as-control or radio-as-everyday-life-changer — and restructure the essay so every paragraph supports that single claim. Tighten the radio/television distinction, resolve the informed/passive contradiction, and proofread carefully for apostrophes and missing words. The ideas are promising; they now need a single, consistent argument to hold them together.

Feedback on Your Essay: Advances in Science and Technology

Thank you for sharing this essay. You've chosen an engaging, relatable topic and used a personal narrative approach that makes your writing feel genuine and easy to read. However, to strengthen this into a more effective piece of argumentative academic writing, I'd like to focus your revision on three main areas: overall argument and structure, development of ideas, and language and style.

Argument and Structure

Your introduction promises a broad discussion of "advances in science and modern technology" that have impacted people's lives, but the essay narrows almost immediately to a single example—the microwave—without explaining *why* this example represents the larger topic, or returning to that larger claim at the end. Right now, the essay reads more like a personal anecdote than an argument.

To fix this, consider:
Adding a clear thesis statement at the end of your introduction that states your actual position. Are you arguing that technology generally improves lives despite some resistance? That the value of technology depends on personal lifestyle and values? Right now, this position is implied but never stated directly.
Using topic sentences at the start of each paragraph that connect back to this thesis, rather than paragraphs that simply narrate events.
Revisiting your thesis in the conclusion. Currently, the conclusion only resolves the anecdote (your uncle gave away the microwave) but doesn't tell the reader what this means for your broader argument about technology's impact on society.

Also, many of your paragraphs are only one or two sentences long. In academic writing, paragraphs typically group several related ideas together, with a topic sentence and supporting details. Try combining related short paragraphs (eg, the paragraphs about your family's cooking schedule) into a single, more developed paragraph.

Development of Ideas

Your strongest material is the personal example of your great-uncle, which effectively introduces a counterargument (some people see microwaves as unnecessary or even harmful to tradition). This is good instinct for argumentative writing! However, the counterargument is underdeveloped—you state your uncle's view but don't really engage with it. Ask yourself:
Is your uncle's concern about a loss of tradition or care in food preparation a valid criticism of technology in general, not just microwaves?
How would you respond to this concern with reasoning, not just the fact that your family kept the microwave?

Right now, the resolution ("it has been a wonderful appliance for all of us") doesn't actually address your uncle's argument about appreciating the work in cooking—it just asserts convenience won. Strengthening this response, or acknowledging the validity of his view before countering it, would make your argument more persuasive and balanced.

Additionally, since this is meant to be an argumentative essay, consider incorporating at least one piece of outside evidence—a statistic, expert opinion, or reference to how technology has changed daily routines more broadly. This would elevate the essay beyond a personal narrative into a more substantiated argument.

Language, Grammar, and Style

Your writing is fluent and natural, which is a real strength. A few recurring issues to address in revision:

Apostrophes for possession: "peoples lives" → "people's lives"; "They' re" → "they're" (watch the placement of the apostrophe).
Verb forms after "take turns": "we each take turns cook" → "we each take turns cooking."
Spelling: "recieved" → "received."
Redundant phrasing: "the point is, is that" — remove one "is." Similarly, watch for repeated words when revising sentences (read your work aloud to catch these).
Capitalization: "great Uncle" and "great Aunt" should not be capitalized unless used as part of a name (eg, "Uncle Hans"), so write "great-uncle" and "great-aunt" (also note the hyphen).
Register: Phrases like "In fact I have a little story to tell about that very thing," or "The long and short of the story," are conversational and better suited to spoken English or informal writing. In an academic essay, aim for more formal transitions, such as "This tension between convenience and tradition is illustrated by an experience in my own family."
Comma splices and run-on sentences: Some sentences link independent clauses only with a comma (eg, "But the point is, is that a microwave isn't right for everyone"). Review your sentences for correct punctuation between independent clauses.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Write a clear thesis statement and revisit it in your conclusion.
Combine short paragraphs into fuller ones with topic sentences.
Develop your counterargument (your uncle's view) more thoughtfully, and respond to it with reasoning.
Consider adding at least one outside source or broader observation about technology.
Proofread for the specific grammar and style issues listed above.

This essay has a strong personal core—now it needs a clearer argumentative frame around it. I look forward to reading your revision!

Feedback on Your Essay: "ATMs as a 20th-Century Invention"

Overall Impression

You've chosen a relatable, concrete topic, and your enthusiasm for the subject comes through clearly. The essay has a recognizable structure (introduction, body, conclusion) and includes personal examples that make your argument feel authentic. However, the essay would benefit from more developed argumentation, clearer organization, and some language polishing. Below are suggestions organized by area.

Content and Argumentation

Your central claim—that ATMs have improved everyday life—is reasonable, but it needs to be argued more rigorously for an academic essay. Right now, the essay relies heavily on personal anecdotes ("I have either forgotten my wallet...") rather than broader reasoning or evidence. Consider these points:

Develop your counterargument. You raise an important objection—that ATMs eliminate bank jobs—but dismiss it in a single sentence ("we must also focus on the positive aspects"). This is the weakest point in your argument because it doesn't actually *refute* the objection; it just changes the subject. A stronger paragraph would acknowledge the job-loss concern specifically, then explain *why* the benefits outweigh this cost (eg, new jobs created in ATM maintenance/banking technology, or how tellers were freed up for more complex customer service tasks). This is where your essay most needs revision.

Reduce reliance on "I feel" and personal experience. Phrases like "I feel ATM cards are an excellent invention" and "I do not know what I would do without my card" are appropriate for a personal reflection but weaken an *argumentative* essay, which should persuade through reasoning and evidence rather than personal preference. Try converting some of these into more objective claims: instead of "I feel ATM cards are an excellent invention," you might write "ATM cards represent one of the most practical technological advances of the century because..."

The conclusion introduces a new, broader idea (computerized technology reducing life's stress) that isn't prepared for in the body of the essay. A strong conclusion should synthesize the points you've already made, not introduce a new claim. Consider revising the final sentence to tie back explicitly to the three benefits you discussed: convenience, time-saving, and security.

Structure and Organization

The essay currently has only three paragraphs, and the body paragraph tries to cover several ideas at once (global availability, personal anecdotes, and safety). I'd suggest breaking this into two clearer paragraphs, each with its own topic sentence:

Paragraph 1 (intro): State your thesis clearly and preview your main points (time-saving, flexibility, safety).
Paragraph 2: Focus on convenience/flexibility (24-hour access, global presence).
Paragraph 3: Focus on safety (avoiding large sums of cash) — this idea currently feels like an afterthought at the end of the anecdote paragraph, but it's a strong point that deserves its own space.
Paragraph 4: Address the counterargument (job loss) and refute it substantively.
Paragraph 5 (conclusion): Summarize and restate the significance of your argument.

Language and Style

Since you're writing in your second language, here are some specific patterns to watch for:

Prepositions: "access *at* money" should be "access *to* money." German prepositional structures often don't map directly onto English; when in doubt, check a learner's dictionary for the preposition that follows a specific noun (eg, "access to," "danger of").
Capitalization: In English, only proper nouns are capitalized. "World" should be lowercase ("all areas of the world"), and "ATM machines" doesn't need capital letters mid-sentence.
Redundancy: "ATM machines" is redundant since the "M" in ATM already stands for "machine." Just use "ATMs."
Sentence fragments/short choppy sentences: "This entices robbers and can promote danger" is grammatically fine but feels underdeveloped. Consider combining it with the previous sentence: "Keeping large amounts of cash at home or in a wallet increases the risk of robbery, whereas an ATM card removes this danger."
Word choice: "Machinery" is a slightly odd word for a single ATM (it usually refers to a collection of machines/equipment). Simply "This technology" or "This device" would work better.
Dates: "1900's" is generally written as "1900s" (no apostrophe) when referring to a decade or era in modern English usage.

Priority for Revision

If you only have time to revise a few things, focus on:
Expanding and strengthening your rebuttal to the job-loss counterargument.
Reorganizing into clearer, more focused paragraphs.
Reducing "I feel" statements in favor of more objective claims.
Revising the conclusion so it summarizes existing points rather than introducing new ones.

You have a solid foundation here—these revisions will help sharpen the argument and make the essay feel more persuasive and academically rigorous.

Feedback on "The Cellular Telephone" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen an interesting and relatable topic, and your English is generally fluent and easy to follow. However, this essay currently reads more like a descriptive list of observations about cell phones than an *argumentative* essay. To strengthen it, you need a clear, consistent thesis and a tighter structure that supports that thesis throughout.

Thesis and Argumentative Stance

The biggest issue is that your position on cell phones shifts several times without resolution:

Paragraph 1: cell phones are "incredible" but also "a complete luxury and not a necessity" (implying a negative judgment)
Paragraph 2: convenience is presented positively, though you call it "a bit ridiculous"
Paragraph 3: you list negatives (accidents, cost)
Paragraph 4: you return to admiration ("truly amazing," "great expansion in technology")
Conclusion: you pivot to robots, a different topic altogether

An argumentative essay needs one clear claim that you defend from beginning to end. Ask yourself: *Are cell phones, on balance, a positive or negative development?* Or perhaps a more specific claim: *Cell phones have improved communication but come with costs that society should be more critical of.* Whatever you decide, state it explicitly in your introduction, and make sure every paragraph supports it rather than simply listing pros and cons side by side.

Organization

Right now the essay moves: benefits → drawbacks → personal anecdote → general reflection on technology. This structure doesn't build toward a conclusion; it meanders. Consider this framework instead:

Introduction: Present your thesis clearly.
Body paragraphs: Each should have a topic sentence connecting back to your thesis, followed by evidence and analysis. If your thesis is that benefits outweigh drawbacks, you might still discuss drawbacks, but frame them as "even though X is a concern, it is outweighed by Y."
Conclusion: Return explicitly to your thesis and summarize how your evidence supports it. Avoid introducing a completely new topic (robots) in the final paragraph—this leaves the reader with a different question than the one you started with.

The anecdote about your family's conference call (paragraph 4) is a nice concrete example, but it currently functions as a standalone story. Integrate it into a body paragraph as *evidence* for a specific point (eg, "cell phones have made long-distance communication easier"), and explain *why* it matters for your argument.

Development and Evidence

Several claims need more support:
"Cellular telephones are also very expensive" — compared to what? Give approximate figures or a comparison to justify this.
"the accident rate of people who are talking on their car phone" — this is a strong claim; even a general statistic or reference would make it more convincing than personal impression alone.

Academic argumentative writing benefits from concrete evidence (statistics, expert opinion, documented examples) rather than only personal impressions ("To me, it is a bit ridiculous").

Language and Style Notes

A few patterns likely reflect German writing conventions that don't transfer well to English academic style:

Ampersands (&): Avoid "&" in formal writing; write "and."
Exclamation marks: You use several ("!!", "can afford to pay the bills!"). Academic English rarely uses exclamation points—they weaken your authority rather than adding emphasis.
Dashes for asides: "from Madrid - On his 'party line'..." — this reads like spoken narration. Consider restructuring as a complete, more formal sentence.
Comma splices/fragments: "if they are stranded or broken down" is a fragment; "Although most people who can afford to buy a cellular phone, can afford to pay the bills!" has a comma splice—remove "Although" or restructure: "Most people who can afford to buy a cellular phone can also afford the bills."
Informal phrasing: "It is truly a mark of great expansion in technology" and "just around the corner" are closer to conversational English. Academic writing favors more measured, precise phrasing.

Concrete Next Steps

Write one clear thesis sentence and place it at the end of your introduction.
Reorganize body paragraphs so each one explicitly supports that thesis.
Turn your anecdote into evidence within a paragraph, not a separate narrative.
Cut the robot conclusion; instead, restate your thesis and summarize your argument.
Proofread for informal punctuation (&, !!, dashes) and fragments/comma splices.

You have good raw material here—an interesting topic, a concrete personal example, and awareness of both sides of the issue. With a clearer argumentative focus and tighter structure, this essay will be much stronger.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Creation/Evolution Debate in the Classroom

Your essay tackles a genuinely difficult and interesting topic, and it's clear you have done real research (Bergman, Bliss, Darwin quotes, the Michigan State example). However, the essay needs work in several areas before it will meet the standards of academic argumentative writing. Below are my main observations, organized by category.

Argumentative Balance

The biggest structural problem is that this essay presents itself as weighing two sides, but it is actually a one-sided advocacy piece dressed as balanced analysis. Notice how every time you introduce an opposing point ("Some argue yes... Others claim it is against the law... What about attempts to indoctrinate students?"), you immediately answer it with the two-model advocates' rebuttal — often in the very next sentence, without seriously examining whether the counterargument has merit. A rigorous argumentative essay should represent the opposing view fairly enough that a reader could recognize its strongest form, *before* you dismantle it. Right now, the evolutionist position is summarized in a single sentence early on ("creationism is religion and does not belong in a science class") and then essentially disappears. Consider devoting a full paragraph to the strongest version of the opposing argument — eg, the scientific-community consensus on evolution as empirically tested theory, the Establishment Clause rulings (*Edwards v. Aguillard*, *Epperson v. Arkansas*), and the definitional problem of calling creationism "science" — before responding to it.

Logical Weaknesses

Some analogies and factual claims need re-examination:

The Einstein/Newton example is scientifically inaccurate. Einstein's relativity did not "refute" or overturn Newton's laws; it extended them to conditions (very high speeds, strong gravity) Newton hadn't addressed. Newtonian mechanics still works perfectly for everyday physics. Using this as proof that "even solid laws get overturned" misrepresents how scientific theories build on and refine each other — this weakens rather than strengthens your argument, since a careful reader will notice the error.
The Mississippi law claim ("in some states... it is against the law to teach evolution") needs a source and a date. Such laws (eg, the Butler Act) were struck down decades ago (*Epperson v. Arkansas*, 1968); presenting this as current fact without evidence damages your credibility.
The Darwin quote is used as an appeal to authority ("this is a major advantage... because Darwin is the founder of evolution"). Being the founder of a theory does not automatically validate a claim about how it should be taught — this is a logical shortcut. Explain *why* the content of the quote matters, not just *who* said it.

Missing Citations

You have many bracketed placeholders () where quotations should be — these need to be filled in with the actual sources before submission, along with proper in-text citations (author, year, page) and a reference list. As it stands, a reader cannot verify a single claim, which is a serious problem in an argumentative essay that relies heavily on authority and evidence.

Structure and Signposting

Your paragraphs often begin well with a clear topic sentence ("The first argument is..." / "The second argument is...") but then drift into extended narrative examples (especially the Einstein paragraph) that lose sight of the topic sentence. Try to close each paragraph by explicitly returning to your thesis: *how does this example support the claim that the two-model approach is more effective?* Also, your introduction promises to show that "the opposition's argument becomes increasingly weak," but the essay doesn't build this weakening cumulatively — each rebuttal section feels self-contained rather than part of a developing case.

Language Notes (English/German Interference)

A few patterns to watch for, since these are common transfer errors from German:
Comma splices and long compound sentences: several sentences (eg, "Since many of the world's population...isn't the classroom...") pack multiple clauses without clear connectors. Break these into shorter units.
Article use: "in the hands of evolutionists" and similar phrases are fine, but double-check definite/indefinite articles before abstract nouns (English often omits the article where German would use one, eg, "religion" vs. "die Religion").
Typos to fix: "fro ma" → "from a," "E=MC²" formatting, "where as" → "whereas."
Word choice: "genuine places of inquiry" and "infamous equation" are slightly off in register — "infamous" implies notoriety/negative connotation, which isn't intended here; consider "famous" or "celebrated."

Overall Recommendation

Rework the essay with a clearer, more balanced treatment of the opposing (evolutionist) position, verify and correctly cite all quotations, and reconsider the Einstein analogy, which currently undermines your credibility. Strengthening these areas will make your genuinely thoughtful research much more persuasive.

Feedback on Your Essay on Euthanasia

Thank you for sharing this draft. You are clearly engaging seriously with a difficult ethical issue, and you have gathered several sources to support your discussion. Below are suggestions to help you strengthen the essay's argument, structure, and language before your next revision.

Clarify and Sustain Your Thesis

Your introduction states a personal position: individuals should be free to choose euthanasia because God gave them the "gift of life itself" (implying freedom over how to use that life). However, this position becomes muddled later when you argue that God "allotted" a specific span of life and that "it is not society's choice to decide when he or she has had enough of life." This is a direct contradiction of your opening claim. Before revising further, decide clearly: are you arguing for individual autonomy, or for the primacy of divine/moral authority over life-and-death decisions? You can acknowledge both perspectives, but the essay needs to make clear which one you ultimately endorse, and why the other view does not undermine your position. Right now, a reader finishes the essay unsure of your final stance.

Fill in the Missing Citations

There are several places marked with  where a quotation or specific example was clearly intended but is missing (eg, "Some say that in cases of euthanasia they ," or "prolife activists argue that these cases "). These gaps make it impossible to evaluate your evidence and interrupt the flow of your argument. Please insert the actual quotations or paraphrases from your sources, with proper in-text citation (author, page number, and a Works Cited list).

Strengthen the Use of Sources

You refer often to "many articles," "some," "others," and "advocates" without naming who is being cited. Academic writing requires you to attribute claims to specific, identifiable sources (eg, "According to Siegler..." — which you do well in the Kevorkian paragraph — try to do this consistently throughout). This will make your synthesis of the "values vs. consequences" framing much more persuasive, since right now it reads as a generalization rather than an analysis of specific texts.

Reconsider the Structure

The essay currently moves through: (1) definition, (2) values vs. consequences framing, (3) religious/family values, (4) the Kevorkian case, (5) tone of articles, (6) the Cruzan case, (7) the "gift of God" argument, (8) a brief conclusion. This is a lot of ground, and the organization feels like a list of observations about your sources rather than a build toward your own argument. Consider restructuring around your own claim, using the sources as supporting or opposing evidence:

Paragraph 1: Thesis (your position, clearly stated)
Paragraphs 2–3: The "value" framing in your sources, with the Kevorkian and Cruzan cases as evidence
Paragraph 4: The religious counter-argument, addressed and responded to
Paragraph 5: Your qualified conclusion (eg, "euthanasia is acceptable in X circumstances, but not Y")

The final paragraph is a good start toward this qualified position ("if the person has a chance of being cured... euthanasia should be a restricted process") — consider moving this nuance earlier and developing it as your actual thesis.

Language and Style Notes

Since English is your second language, here are some recurring patterns to watch for:

Articles: "an complex issue" → "a complex issue." German does not distinguish "a" vs. "an" by sound, so double-check before vowel sounds.
Spelling consistency: "Kervokian" vs. "Kevorkian" — check the correct spelling and use it consistently.
Word choice/typos: "en a painful life" should likely be "end a painful life." "ethic" should be "ethics" (as a field of study, this noun is usually plural in form).
Pronoun agreement: You alternate between "his or her," "he or she," and occasionally lapse into "himself" or awkward constructions like "to his or herself" (should be "to himself or herself"). Consider using "they/their" as a gender-neutral option throughout for smoother reading, as this is now widely accepted in academic English.
Capitalization: "Euthanasia" is capitalized inconsistently (only capitalize if starting a sentence or in a title).
Sentence length: Some sentences run long and combine multiple ideas with commas rather than conjunctions (eg, "Freedom itself is his greatest gift. Life has been given and its allotted span goes with the gift."). Consider breaking these into clearer, shorter units and using transitions like "however," "therefore," or "in contrast" to guide the reader through your logic.

Conclusion

Your final paragraph raises an important point — that outsiders cannot fully know another person's suffering — but it ends somewhat abruptly with another unfinished quotation (). Use your conclusion to explicitly restate your (now clarified and consistent) thesis and briefly summarize how the evidence you've presented supports it.

Overall: You have strong instincts for using real cases (Kevorkian, Cruzan) to ground an abstract ethical debate. Your next revision should focus on (1) resolving the tension between your autonomy argument and your religious argument, (2) completing all missing citations, and (3) tightening sentence-level grammar. I'd be happy to look at a revised draft once these changes are made.

Feedback on Your Essay: Capital Punishment

Your essay tackles a complex and controversial topic with a clear thesis and a logical structure. You've organized your argument well by presenting both sides before making your case, and your use of outside sources (Hoyler, Nygaard, Conway, Reiman) shows good research effort. Below is feedback to help you strengthen the essay further.

Structure and Argumentation

Strengths: Your essay follows a clear pattern: introduction with thesis, arguments for capital punishment, then arguments against it, followed by a conclusion. This organization helps readers follow your reasoning.

Areas to improve:

Balance of treatment: You give the "pro" arguments relatively brief treatment before moving to a much longer refutation. While your thesis states the anti-death penalty argument is stronger, make sure you're not simply asserting this stronger position without giving the opposing view a fair, substantive presentation first. Consider developing the "eye for an eye" and deterrence arguments a bit more before dismantling them—this makes your rebuttal more persuasive.

The "two wrongs don't make a right" section is your strongest part—you develop the "lowering the value of human life" argument with real depth and originality. This is your most sophisticated point; consider moving it earlier or giving it even more prominence, since it's more compelling than simply restating that deterrence is unproven.

Missing counter-evidence for deterrence: You claim deterrence "does not have much evidence to support it," but you should also mention *counter-evidence*—studies comparing murder rates in death penalty vs. non-death penalty states, for instance. Right now, your refutation relies on logical reasoning ("we can't know a criminal's mind") rather than empirical data, which weakens the rebuttal.

Conclusion overreaches: The final paragraph makes a strong claim—"society simply craves violence"—that feels like a new argument rather than a synthesis of what came before. This paragraph should tie together your existing points, not introduce a fresh, somewhat inflammatory claim without support.

Language and Style (German-English Considerations)

Article usage: Watch for missing or unnecessary articles, which is common for German speakers. For example: "the highest sentence that can be enforced" is fine, but check phrases like "capital punishment has been carried out on criminals for quite some time"—this is natural, but review similar constructions throughout for consistency.

False friends/word choice:
   - "heinous act that is morally incorrect" → "morally wrong" is more natural English than "incorrect" (which sounds more like a math error).
   - "relative strengths" (used to describe the pro-arguments) is vague—do you mean "significant strengths" or "certain strengths"?
   - "plummets to a shameful level" is a nice image, but "shameful" may not be the most precise word—consider "deplorable" or "disturbing."

Repetition: The phrase "value of human life" appears many times. While this is your central concept (which is good!), try varying your phrasing: "the worth we assign to life," "how we measure the sanctity of life," etc

Sentence-level clarity: Some sentences are quite long and could be split for clarity, eg, "When society takes the life of a criminal, they set the value of his life equal to that of the victim, and, therefore unconsciously lower the value of their own lives." This is a good idea, but consider breaking it into two sentences for readability.

Pronoun agreement: "A criminal's life is generally considered... they set the value of his life equal..." — here "society" (singular) is paired with "they" (plural). This is common in English due to gender-neutral language conventions, but be consistent—either use "it" for society or explicitly plural constructions.

Content Suggestions

Define "value of human life" more precisely: This is your central analytical lens, but you never explicitly define what you mean by it. Is it inherent moral worth? Social utility? Legal status? A brief definition early on would sharpen your entire argument.

Address wrongful convictions: A powerful argument against capital punishment—especially given your focus on the value of human life—is the risk of executing innocent people. This seems like a natural fit for your essay and is currently missing.

Citations: You have placeholder  marks where quotes should go—make sure these are filled in with properly formatted citations (author, source, page number) before submission.

Overall

This is a solid draft with a clear argumentative stance and an interesting central concept (the "value of human life" as a shared casualty of capital punishment). Focus your revision on: (1) balancing your treatment of both sides, (2) adding empirical evidence for the deterrence rebuttal, (3) tightening word choice, and (4) ensuring your conclusion synthesizes rather than introduces new claims. Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay tackles a genuinely difficult and important topic, and I can see you've done real research (the Kennedy executive order, Shaw, Gillespie, Beer). You also have a clear thesis and a stated plan for your essay, which is excellent practice. Below are suggestions organized by area, to help you revise.

Structure and Argument Development

Your introduction promises a specific four-part structure (define affirmative action → show legality → use both sides to show necessity → suggest improvements for proponents), and you do largely follow this. However, the connection between the opening anecdote about campus riots and cultural centers and your actual argument about quotas is unclear. Consider either integrating this example more explicitly later in the essay (eg, as evidence of ongoing racial tension) or trimming it, since currently it functions as a detached hook rather than a building block of your argument.

Your strongest sections are the ones where you directly engage with counterarguments—particularly the discussion of "reverse discrimination" and the paragraph addressing quotas and "black failure." Continue developing this comparative structure throughout.

Missing citations: You have several  placeholders where quotations should appear. Before submitting, make sure every source is properly quoted, cited, and *integrated*—don't just drop in a quote; explain what it shows and how it supports your point.

Argumentation Weaknesses

Circular reasoning risk: Your claim that "opponents are really defending the need for such programs" by pointing out affirmative action hasn't worked is a clever move, but you need to justify it more carefully. Simply asserting it isn't enough—show the logical steps.

Unsupported generalizations: In the paragraph about opponents' views on black morality, you write about opponents demanding blacks "stope using and selling drugs" — this is a strong claim attributed to "opponents" as a whole. Make sure you're representing this position fairly and with citation, not caricaturing it, even though you clearly disagree with it.

Conclusion transition: Your final paragraph shifts to a comparison between slavery's duration and affirmative action's short existence. This is a powerful rhetorical move, but it appears suddenly. Consider foreshadowing this point earlier so it doesn't feel like a last-minute addition.

Language and Style (for a non-native English writer)

Since German is your first language, here are patterns to watch for:

Article usage: English requires more attention to definite/indefinite articles than German. eg, "the tents of affirmative action" (likely meant "tenets") should be checked carefully—also watch for missing "the" before nouns like "the necessity" vs. "necessity."

Spelling errors: "tents" → "tenets"; "achie ved" → "achieved" (also remove the stray space); "stope" → "stop"; "Gillepsie" → "Gillespie" (be consistent with author names); "morals" → likely "morale" (different meaning—check which you intend).

Preposition choices: "achieve of their own merit" should be "achieve on their own merit" or "through their own merit."

Word order and register: Some sentences use slightly formal/archaic constructions translated from German patterns, eg, "let us assess the necessity" is fine, but check phrases like "They demand blacks to get out of jail"—in English, we'd say "demand that blacks get out of jail" (subjunctive without "to").

Verb agreement: "writer's need to examine" should be "writers need to examine" (apostrophe error, not a grammar issue, but proofread for these).

Citation and Source Integration

You mention specific authors (Shaw, Gillespie, Beer) but never give full names or clear signal phrases explaining their stance before quoting. Introduce each source with enough context: What is their position? Why are they credible? This will also help you avoid the choppy feel of "Author Bill Shaw states that, ."

Overall Recommendation

Your ideas are sophisticated, and your instinct to synthesize both sides before offering your own view is a strong rhetorical strategy. Focus your revision on:
Filling in and properly integrating all quotations,
Tightening the connection between your opening example and your thesis,
Careful proofreading for spelling/grammar (a reading-aloud pass often catches many of these), and
Making sure counterarguments are represented with nuance and fairness.

With these revisions, this essay will be both clearer and more persuasive. Well done on tackling a complex topic with genuine intellectual engagement.

Feedback on Your Essay: "New Age Teaching" Argument

Your essay tackles an interesting and timely topic, and you clearly did real research. However, there are several issues with argumentation, objectivity, and structure that weaken the essay's persuasive power. Below is feedback organized by area.

Argumentative Objectivity

This is the most serious issue. Your thesis claims to evaluate which *argument* is stronger based on rhetorical criteria (values, consequences, evidence), but partway through, you abandon that analytical stance and reveal your own religious conviction: "I feel that the Bible is very real and true." This is a critical problem for an *academic* argument essay. You are no longer analyzing rhetoric—you are endorsing one side because you personally agree with its values. A reader who does not share your faith will see this as bias undermining your entire analysis, not as evidence that the argument is objectively "stronger." Try to keep your evaluative criteria consistent and applied evenhandedly to both sides, without declaring your own belief as fact.

Logical Consistency

Several of your supporting claims contain reasoning gaps:

Appeal to popularity: You argue that the anti-New-Age case is stronger because it is published in more "mainstream" magazines (Christianity Today) that "reach more people." Reaching more readers does not make an argument more *true* or *logically sound*—only more *widely read*. This conflates persuasive reach with argumentative quality, which is a fallacy you should avoid or explicitly acknowledge as a limitation.
Anecdotal overreach: The story about running to ask your neighbors whether tobacco is "the most destructive drug" is a fun narrative moment, but it functions as your *sole* evidence for dismissing an entire author's credibility. One informal poll among neighbors is not a "hard fact" either—ironically, this undermines your own standard for what counts as reliable evidence.
Single anecdote as proof of consequence: Similarly, the New Age children's behavior described in *Moody* magazine is one family's testimony, yet you treat it as demonstrating a general negative consequence of an entire ideology. This is the same weakness you criticize in the pro-New-Age sources (single authors, personal opinion). Be consistent: if anecdote-as-proof is a weakness on one side, acknowledge it as a weakness on both.

Structure and Focus

The Yoda/yoga opening is engaging, but it goes on a bit long and delays your thesis. Consider trimming it to 3-4 sentences and moving to your argument sooner.
The placeholder markers () suggest missing quotations that never got inserted into the final draft—make sure these are filled in before submission, as currently the essay references content the reader cannot see (the "four main themes," the Andrew Weil quote, the *Moody* testimony).
Your key terms—"question of value" and "question of consequence"—are used as if the reader already knows them (likely from class material), but they are never clearly defined in the essay itself. Add a brief explanation early on so the essay stands on its own.
The conclusion largely restates the introduction without synthesizing *why* the criteria matter. Consider ending with a reflection on what this comparison reveals about argumentation in general, or about the challenge of evaluating value-based debates.

Language and Style Notes

Since you're writing in a second language, here are some recurring patterns to watch for:

Apostrophes for possession: "authors opinion" → "author's opinion"; "that families values" → "that family's values." German doesn't use apostrophes for possession the way English does, so this is worth double-checking throughout.
Comma splices: Sentences like "Everyone can have their own values, but when an author is attempting..." are fine, but some other places join full independent clauses only with commas—read these aloud to check if a period or semicolon fits better.
Capitalization inconsistency: "New Age" vs. "new age" appears both ways; pick one and use it consistently (capitalized, since it functions as a proper noun/movement name).
Colloquial phrasing in an academic essay: Phrases like "I honestly ran out of my room" or "stupendous job" are vivid but too informal for academic register. Consider more measured phrasing: "This claim seemed so implausible that I sought a second opinion" rather than the informal narrative.
Word choice: "harvest the energy need as" should likely be "harvest the energy needed as"—watch for missing -ed endings, a common transfer issue from German past participles.

Summary

Your strongest move would be to (1) remove your personal declaration of religious belief and instead analyze *how* the Bible functions rhetorically as a "book of values" without asserting its truth, (2) apply your evaluative criteria evenly to both sides, flagging weaknesses in each, and (3) tighten the introduction and fill in the missing quotations. This will make your argument more persuasive to *all* readers, not just those who already share your starting assumptions.

Feedback on Your Essay: Nuclear Power Argumentation Analysis

Your essay tackles an interesting rhetorical question—how proponents and opponents of nuclear power construct their arguments differently. You've done solid research and organized your body paragraphs logically around the proponents' four main points. However, there are several areas where revision would strengthen your essay significantly.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your thesis states that opponents rely on "values and beliefs" while proponents use "facts and figures." This is a promising analytical framework, but your essay doesn't fully deliver on it. After the second paragraph, you almost entirely abandon discussion of the opponents' arguments and spend the rest of the essay simply *summarizing* the proponents' claims rather than *analyzing* the rhetorical strategies both sides use. Ask yourself: is this essay meant to be a rhetorical analysis (how do these groups argue?) or an argumentative essay taking a side (nuclear power is good)? Right now it does both awkwardly, and the concluding paragraph reveals your own pro-nuclear stance, which undercuts the analytical framing established in your introduction. Decide on your purpose and revise the thesis and conclusion to match consistently.

Structural Issue: Missing Analysis of Opponents

Since your thesis promises an examination of *both* sides' rhetorical strategies, you need a body paragraph (or several) that analyzes *how* opponents use values-based appeals with the same depth you give the proponents' fact-based appeals. Currently, opponents are only briefly introduced and then dropped. Consider adding a paragraph that examines specific emotional/values-based language opponents use—perhaps analyzing actual rhetoric about safety fears, mushroom clouds, or children's health—parallel to how you analyze the proponents' statistics.

Content and Logic Concerns

Factual error: A nuclear bomb's yield is measured in kilotons or megatons, not "three hundred tons of TNT"—this is likely a mistranslation or research error. Please verify this figure.
Missing citations: You cite specific statistics (50% cheaper, 20% cheaper, lives saved per megawatt) but provide no sources. Since this appears to be a research-based essay, you must cite where these figures come from. Without citations, your reliability as a source is questionable—ironic, given that your essay argues proponents win by being more "factual."
Incomplete sentence: The passage beginning "Though simple, easy-to-understand facts such as: " appears to be missing content—perhaps a placeholder you forgot to fill in. Please complete this.

Language and Style (German-to-English patterns)

I noticed several recurring issues common among German-speaking English learners:

"do to" → "due to": A homophone confusion; be careful with spell-check, which won't catch this.
"then" vs. "than": You consistently write "then" for comparisons (eg, "cheaper then coal," "more cost-efficient then"). These are different words in English—"than" is for comparison, "then" is for time/sequence.
Article usage: Phrases like "the common An American" (paragraph 1) suggest editing confusion—clean this up. Also watch definite/indefinite articles, eg, "a large group of opponents to nuclear power are formed" reads awkwardly; consider "opponents to nuclear power form a large group."
Word choice: "meggawatts" should be "megawatts"; "took" should be "taken" ("amount of precaution took" → "taken").
Comma splices: Several sentences incorrectly join independent clauses with commas alone (eg, "The fact is, there is waste..."; "they fear this new entity, seeing it as..."). Review comma splice rules—these need semicolons, periods, or conjunctions.
Analyzation: This word doesn't exist in standard English—use "analysis."

Suggestions for Revision

Clarify your purpose: Rhetorical analysis or persuasive essay? Adjust thesis and conclusion accordingly.
Balance your analysis: Add substantive discussion of *how* opponents argue, not just *what* they argue.
Add citations: Any statistics need sources—this is essential for academic credibility.
Proofread systematically: Create a personal checklist for "then/than," comma splices, and article usage, since these recur throughout.
Complete the essay: Fill in the incomplete sentence in the waste paragraph.
Reconsider the conclusion: Right now it reads as personal opinion rather than analytical synthesis—make sure it matches your thesis's analytical framing.

Overall, your content knowledge is strong, and your paragraph organization is clear. Focus your revision on structural balance (both sides), rhetorical consistency (analysis vs. persuasion), and language precision. This has good bones—it just needs sharpening.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay on School Integration

Overall Impression

You have chosen a rich and important topic, and your essay show a genuine effort to weigh both benefits and drawbacks of school integration. The personal narrative about the Chapter 220 program is a real strength — it gives your argument authenticity and a perspective that statistics alone cannot provide. However, the essay needs work in three main areas: argumentative structure, use of sources, and language precision. Below I offer specific, actionable suggestions.

Sharpen Your Thesis and Structure

In your introduction, you promise to examine integration through the lens of "questions over value and consequence." This is a sophisticated framework, but you never return to it explicitly. Readers need to see this analytical lens applied consistently — for instance, you could label sections as addressing the *ethical/value* dimension versus the *practical/consequentialist* dimension, or explicitly signal at each transition which type of argument you are making.

Currently, the essay reads as three loosely connected parts: (1) academic achievement, (2) race relations (via your personal experience), and (3) community resistance. Each part is interesting, but the connections between them are implicit rather than argued. Add topic sentences that explicitly link back to your thesis, and end each section with a sentence that shows *how* this evidence supports or complicates your overall claim.

Your conclusion currently just restates that there are "values and consequences" without synthesizing what you've shown. Consider ending with a clearer, more specific claim: what conditions, based on your evidence, make integration more likely to succeed or fail?

Fix and Strengthen Your Use of Sources

Several sentences contain placeholder marks () where a citation or quotation should be — for example, after "the Supreme Court decided that" and "According to a thesis written by Howard Fuller." These gaps make it impossible for a reader to evaluate your evidence. Before submitting, make sure every source is:

Fully quoted or paraphrased (not left blank),
Properly cited with author, title, and page/date according to your assigned citation style (MLA or APA),
Introduced with context — eg, not just "the book *Desegregation and Beyond*" but "In *Desegregation and Beyond*, [Author's name] argues that..."

Also be careful with vague attributions like "a study done by two researchers from New York" — name the researchers if possible, or at least cite the source properly so the claim is verifiable.

Your personal anecdote about Mequon-Thiensville is valuable, but it should be explicitly framed as a *case study* or *illustrative example*, not simply inserted as a new paragraph. A sentence like "My own experience with the Chapter 220 program illustrates both the successes and failures described by researchers above" would help integrate it into your argument rather than presenting it as a separate narrative.

Language and Style Issues

Since English is your second language, here are some specific points to review (these are common German-English interference patterns):

Word choice: "conductive" → *conducive*; "hinderance" → *hindrance*. Watch for near-misses in vocabulary — a spellchecker won't catch these because they are real words, just the wrong ones.
Verb choice with people: "more students were implemented into the program" — "implemented" is used for policies or systems, not people. Use *enrolled*, *placed*, or *integrated* instead.
Apostrophes for possession: "the white communities reluctance" should be "the white community's reluctance." This is a recurring issue — check every plural/possessive noun.
Comma splices and run-ons: eg, "One of the biggest downfalls of school integration, I feel comes from the community" needs restructuring — perhaps: "One of the biggest downfalls of school integration, I feel, comes from the community" (with a second comma) or rephrase as "I feel that one of the biggest downfalls comes from the community."
Sentence fragments/incomplete constructions: "As can seen" should be "As can be seen."
Article usage: German does not always require articles where English does (and vice versa) — read your sentences aloud checking for missing "a/the," eg, "increased slowly but surely" is fine, but check phrases like "put a front" (should be "put up a front" — actually you have this right) and similar idiomatic expressions throughout.

I recommend reading your essay aloud, sentence by sentence, or using a tool like Grammarly specifically to flag article and preposition issues, which are the most common transfer errors from German.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Fill in all missing citations and quotations.
Make your "values vs. consequences" framework explicit throughout, not just in the introduction.
Add clearer transitions and topic sentences linking each section to your thesis.
Frame your personal narrative explicitly as evidence/case study.
Proofread carefully for word choice, possessives, and comma splices.

You have a strong foundation and a compelling personal angle — with more explicit structure and cleaner sourcing, this essay could be very persuasive. Good work so far, and I look forward to seeing your revision.

Overall Impression

Your claim that black and white communities in poverty share similar crime rates needs a citation.

Logical Gaps

You assert that white communities have "caused" black poverty and higher crime rates, but you move quickly past this claim. Even if you believe it, spell out the causal mechanism (eg, historical exclusion from housing, employment, education) rather than asserting it as self-evident.

Briefly restate your thesis in fresh words
Synthesize your two counterargument rebuttals
End with a forward-looking or call-to-action sentence

Capitalization: "University" should only be capitalized when part of a proper name (eg, "Marquette University" but "a university education," "attending a university").
Article use: Check phrases like "the racism in our Universities" vs. simply "racism at our universities"—English often omits "the" before abstract or general nouns where German would use an article.

Word order: *"As Steele mentions is her essay"* — likely a typo for "in," but also check verb placement; English is stricter about subject-verb-object order than German.
Repeated words/typos: "the problems can the problems begin to be solved" — clearly a drafting error; proofread final sentences carefully.
Pronoun agreement: "a person of color must deal with the same stereotypes... on a college level" followed by generic "they" — make sure singular/plural references stay consistent.

Vary sentence openings—you rely frequently on "As," "With," and "In" to begin sentences, which becomes repetitive.
"One might ask/argue" is used often; vary this with phrases like "A critic could contend..." or "Some readers may object that..."

Next Steps

Feedback on "Surrogate Motherhood" Essay

Overall Impression
You have a clear, defensible position (surrogacy is ethically problematic) and you attempt to support it with a recognizable structure — background, opposing views, rebuttal, conclusion. However, the essay is undermined by several structural and language issues that need attention before this can be considered a finished argumentative essay.

Missing Content
The most pressing problem is the number of blank placeholders () throughout the text — including in the very first sentence, where the definition of surrogate motherhood itself is missing. These gaps appear where you likely intended to insert a definition, a citation, a summary of an opposing argument, or a transition. As it stands, a reader cannot follow your argument because key logical links are literally absent. Before revising style or grammar, fill in every blank with the content you intended, and make sure each one is a *complete sentence* that connects logically to what comes before and after.

Thesis and Organization
Your thesis ("I agree with those individuals who find surrogacy unethical") does not appear until paragraph five. In academic argumentative writing, the thesis should appear in the introduction, so the reader knows your position from the start and can evaluate how each paragraph supports it. Revise your opening paragraph to state clearly: what surrogate motherhood is, why it is ethically contested, and what your position is.

Your paragraphs also tend to repeat the same rhetorical pattern ("Many people feel... Others feel...") without building toward a new point each time. Consider outlining your argument first: (1) definition and background, (2) the bodily-autonomy argument and your response, (3) the "baby-selling" argument and your response, (4) the emotional-harm argument (your strongest point), (5) conclusion. This would reduce redundancy and give the essay clearer forward motion.

Argument Consistency and Terminology
There is a recurring terminological confusion between "adoptive mother," "intended mother," and "biological mother." You use "adoptive mother" to mean the woman who intends to raise the child but is not the surrogate — this is confusing, since "adoptive" usually implies formal adoption. Choose consistent terms (eg, *intended mother*, *surrogate mother*, *genetic father*) and use them uniformly throughout.

There are also some logical tensions worth resolving: early on you argue there are "only two [biological] parents," but later you argue the intended mother's needs are unfairly neglected because she has no biological tie — this seems to support the opposing view unless you clarify how it reinforces your own thesis. Make sure each supporting paragraph explicitly ties back to your central claim (emotional harm to the surrogate makes surrogacy unethical), rather than raising points that could just as easily support the other side.

Evidence and Citation
You refer to "Annas" and "the article above" without formal citation. Since this is a case-based argument (Baby M, 1987), you should cite your sources properly (author, year, page) according to the citation style required by your course (APA or MLA). This also strengthens your credibility: right now, claims like "the surrogate mother experiences hormonal changes that are preparing her... for motherhood" sound assertive but unsupported. Add a source or example wherever you make an empirical claim.

Language and Style
Since German is your first language, watch for these recurring patterns:
Subject–verb agreement: "Others feels that..." → "Others feel that..."; "who's lives" → "whose lives."
Article and preposition use: "for another couple back" → reorder as "asked the judge to give her back the child she had borne for another couple."
Calques from German connectors: phrases like "Due to the fact that" and "By saying that" are overused; vary with "Because," "Since," or "This suggests that."
Repetition of phrases: "walking womb," "reproductive structure," and "fill their void" appear multiple times — vary vocabulary or cut repeated ideas.
Spelling: "substancial" → "substantial"; "mother hood" → "motherhood."

A careful proofreading pass focused on these patterns will make the prose read more fluently.

Conclusion
Your final paragraph largely repeats the introduction and body claims rather than synthesizing them into a final, forceful statement. Strengthen it by directly answering the "so what" question: why does the emotional harm to the surrogate outweigh the benefits to the infertile couple, and what should follow from this (eg, stricter regulation, banning commercial surrogacy)?

Next Steps
Fill in all blanks with complete, cited content.
Move your thesis to the introduction.
Standardize terminology (surrogate/intended/genetic parent).
Add citations for all factual or case-based claims.
Proofread specifically for subject–verb agreement and repeated phrasing.

With these revisions, your argument — which is thoughtful and has real potential — will be much clearer and more persuasive.

Run-on/comma splices: The sentence beginning "Yet another example of price blackmail is Sandoz corp. Requiring healthcare providers..." is a sentence fragment—it needs a finite verb structure: "Sandoz Corp. required healthcare providers to buy..."

Pronoun/antecedent clarity: "This price-gouging shows in high hospital bills, which " — once filled in, make sure "which" clearly refers back to "hospital bills," not "price-gouging."

Suggestions for Revision Priority

Fill in the two missing  sections—these are essential to your argument's coherence.
Clarify the logic in the Clozapril paragraph.
Realign the Depo-Provera example with its topic sentence, or adjust the topic sentence.

You have excellent command of complex argumentation and technical content—the ideas here are genuinely persuasive.
