Similarly, the Roger Keith Coleman example contains a factual inconsistency: you state he was convicted in 1982 and executed "on May 20, 1982," which would mean trial, appeals, and execution all occurred within the same year. Please verify these dates; errors like this undermine your credibility on a topic where accuracy is essential.

Structure and Argumentation

Your three-part structure (deterrence, constitutionality, ethics) is a sound way to organize the essay, but the transitions between claims within paragraphs need tightening. In the deterrence paragraph, you move through four different studies without clearly stating what unifies them—consider adding a sentence after each study explaining how it supports your specific claim, rather than letting the reader infer the connection.

The concession paragraph on ethics ("Before dealing with the ethical argument against the use of capital punishment, the ethical argument supporting it must be examined") is a good instinct—strong argumentative essays acknowledge the opposing view—but your rebuttal is thin. You state that the pro-death-penalty arguments "do not stand up" without explaining *why* the "fair is fair" argument fails philosophically. Since this is your strongest rhetorical opportunity to show critical thinking, consider expanding this rebuttal with a specific philosophical or logical objection (eg, addressing retributive justice theory directly).

Language and Style

A few patterns typical of German-to-English transfer appear:

Passive voice overuse: "It is reported that," "Courts have been accused of being," "Errors have been found to occur." English academic prose favors more direct, active constructions where possible. Consider: "Newsweek reported that..." or "Critics accuse courts of..."
Vague quantifiers: Phrases like "a number of," "many people believe," and "Perhaps the most obvious statistic" weaken your argument by avoiding specificity. Replace with concrete numbers or named sources once your evidence is filled in.
Redundant phrasing in the thesis: The sentence "it is clear that capital punishment should be abolished based on the stronger opposing argument" is circular—you're essentially saying the opposing argument is stronger because it's stronger. Rewrite your thesis to state your three reasons directly and let the essay demonstrate their strength, rather than asserting it upfront.
Repetition across paragraphs: The constitutional section begins with "Proponents of capital punishment often stand by its use based on the fact that it is provided for in the constitution," and the paragraph ends with a nearly identical restatement. Vary your phrasing to avoid this loop.

Specific Suggestions for Revision

Fill in all placeholder citations with accurate data and proper in-text citation format (author, year, page).
Verify all dates and facts, especially in case studies.
Rewrite the thesis statement to be more direct and less circular.
Strengthen the rebuttal of the "fair is fair" argument with explicit philosophical reasoning.
Reduce passive constructions where an active voice would be clearer and more persuasive.
Add a concluding paragraph that goes beyond summary—consider addressing what abolition would require practically, or why this issue remains urgent.

Final Note

The bones of a well-organized, multi-pronged argumentative essay are here. Your priority for the next draft should be evidentiary completeness and precision—once your statistics and quotations are properly sourced and verified, focus on tightening the logical connections between evidence and claims, and on making your prose more direct. I'm happy to review a revised draft once the citations are complete.

Feedback on Your Prozac Essay

Overall Impression

You have chosen a genuinely controversial and interesting topic, and your position—that Prozac's benefits outweigh the concerns raised against it—is clear from the introduction. You also do a good job structuring the essay around distinct sub-arguments (how the drug works, its safety, its social benefits, and its critics). However, the essay needs significant revision in three main areas: completing and properly integrating your sources, strengthening the logical depth of your argument, and cleaning up language issues typical of translation from German. Below are specific suggestions.

Missing Quotations

This is the most urgent issue: several sentences contain a placeholder  where a quotation should be. A reader cannot evaluate your argument without this evidence—right now, sentences like "One doctor commented that Prozac " are simply incomplete. Before you revise anything else, go back to your sources and insert the actual quotations, each properly introduced with a signal phrase (eg, "Dr. Auerbach argues that...") and followed by a citation. Quotations are the backbone of your evidence here, so this must be fixed first.

Strengthening the Argument

The mechanism of Prozac is described inaccurately. You write that the drug "causes serotonin to slow in spaces between the nerve cells." In fact, SSRIs like Prozac *block the reabsorption* (reuptake) of serotonin, which *increases* its concentration in the synapse. Since this is a factual/scientific claim, please verify it and correct it—an error here could undermine your credibility on the rest of the argument.

Your rebuttal to the suicide concern is a good instinct (comparing prescription numbers to confirmed deaths), but the reasoning needs to be spelled out more explicitly. Simply stating the numbers doesn't yet prove that Prozac isn't causing some suicides in vulnerable patients; you need a sentence explaining *why* a low percentage supports your claim (eg, "This suggests that suicide in these cases is more likely linked to untreated depression than to the drug itself.") Also, cite where these numbers come from.

The conclusion currently ends on a rhetorical question ("what is wrong with making yourself better?"), which oversimplifies a genuinely complex ethical debate that Kramer himself raises (the idea of "cosmetic pharmacology"—using a medical drug not just to treat illness but to enhance personality). Since you quote Kramer earlier, it would strengthen your credibility to briefly acknowledge this deeper philosophical concern before dismissing it, rather than brushing past it with a rhetorical flourish. A strong conclusion should synthesize your evidence and restate your thesis with more nuance, not end on an unanswered question.

Organization and Transitions

Your paragraph structure is logical (mechanism → safety → social benefit → counterarguments → conclusion), and you use decent topic sentences ("Not only is the drug medically reliable..."). To improve flow:
Add a brief transition sentence at the start of the suicide-counterargument paragraph explaining *why* you are now turning to criticism (you do this reasonably well, but make the logical link to your thesis more explicit each time).
The final paragraph introduces two new doctors' opinions but doesn't explain how they differ from or add to the doctors already cited—consider clarifying their specific contribution.

Language and Mechanics

Because English is not your first language, here are some recurring issues to watch for:

Spacing/spelling errors: "banish ment" should be "banishment"; "name Sam" should be "named Sam"; "pas social lives" should be "past social lives." Proofread carefully for such slips—reading the essay aloud can help catch them.
Idiomatic phrasing: "ask a physician 'for something'" is a bit colloquial for academic writing; consider "ask a physician for a prescription" or similar.
Article usage: German does not have articles in the same distribution as English, so watch for missing/extra "a/the" (eg, "controversial drug is the anti-depressant Prozac" is fine, but check other noun phrases throughout for consistency).
Sentence variety: Several sentences begin the same way ("Prozac...", "The drug..."). Try varying sentence openings to avoid repetitiveness.

Summary of Priorities

Insert and properly cite all missing quotations.
Correct the scientific explanation of how Prozac works.
Strengthen the logical reasoning in your rebuttals, especially regarding suicide risk.
Revise the conclusion to synthesize your argument rather than end on a rhetorical question, and briefly acknowledge the "cosmetic pharmacology" debate for added credibility.
Proofread carefully for spelling, spacing, and idiomatic phrasing.

You have a solid foundation and a clear stance—focus your revision on deepening the argument's logic and polishing the language, and this will become a much stronger essay.

Feedback on Draft Essay: "Homosexuals in the Military"

Thank you for sharing this draft. Your topic is compelling, and you have clearly worked with both source articles. Below is feedback organized around argumentation, structure, source use, and language, followed by suggestions for revision.

Argumentation and Thesis

Your introduction is currently confusing because it sends mixed signals about your position. You compare the military ban to the civil rights and women's movements—both associated with expanding rights—but then argue *against* extending rights to gay soldiers. If you intend this comparison ironically (ie, "unlike those movements, this issue should be resolved differently"), you need to state that explicitly. Otherwise, readers will expect you to support inclusion.

Additionally, phrases like "the obvious answer may be" and "it will be ultimately decided" sound evasive. State your thesis directly and early: *"This essay argues that Pattullo's reasoning is more convincing than Wall's, and therefore homosexuals should not be permitted to serve in the military."* Academic writing values directness over suspense.

Treatment of Wall's Argument

A significant weakness is that you frequently caricature Wall's position rather than engaging with it seriously. For example:

> "Wall attacks the issue of homosexuals in the barracks with a lighthearted attitude."

This is an assertion, not an argument. Without knowing Wall's actual words (marked here as ), readers cannot judge whether his tone is truly "lighthearted" or whether you are oversimplifying a more serious point for rhetorical effect. Similarly:

> "the comparison of music taste to concerns of being viewed as a sex object in the showers cannot be made"

You dismiss Wall's analogy without explaining *why* it fails logically. Is it a false equivalence? Does it ignore power dynamics or physical vulnerability? Spell out the reasoning rather than simply asserting dismissal—this will make your critique far stronger and more persuasive to a skeptical reader.

Reliance on Assertion Rather than Evidence

Several claims are stated as self-evident truths without support:

> "There would be a real concern of homosexuals interacting sexually with each other."

> "This would offend many heterosexuals and result in the disintegration of camaraderie."

These are Pattullo's *claims*, but you present them as established fact. In an argumentative essay, you must distinguish between reporting an author's claim and endorsing it as true. Use attributive language consistently: "Pattullo *claims* that..." or "According to Pattullo..." and then explain *why* you find this reasoning convincing (evidence, logic, real-world examples), rather than simply repeating the claim in your own voice.

Missing Quotations

The placeholders  indicate missing textual evidence. This is the most urgent structural issue: without direct quotations, your reader cannot verify your interpretation of either source, and your analysis currently reads as assertion-heavy. Please insert the actual quotations and, in each case, follow them with your own analysis—not just a restatement of what the quote says.

Rhetorical Questions

You use rhetorical questions somewhat frequently ("Why worsen it with the presence of homosexuals?", "How can this be if..."). In academic writing, rhetorical questions can feel like a substitute for argument. Consider converting these into direct statements: *"This creates a contradiction: if barracks tensions already exist, Wall's proposal for equal treatment ignores this problem."*

Conclusion

Your conclusion effectively shows how the sources changed your mind—this is a nice touch. However, revise the phrasing: "it was decided by the writer of this essay" is awkward and impersonal. Simply write: *"Before reading Pattullo's article, I believed there was no reason to prohibit homosexuals from serving."* First-person use is generally acceptable in this kind of reflective/argumentative essay unless your instructor specifies otherwise.

Language and Grammar (Typical German-English Interference)

A few recurring issues:

Article/preposition errors: "there should be a ban gays serving" → "there should be a ban *on* gays serving."
Word order: "James M. Wall's article*s* does not give" → subject-verb agreement (singular "article" needs "does," but you wrote "articles").
False friends/awkward phrasing: "will make on change their mind" → likely "will make many change their mind" or "will make readers change their mind."
Redundant demonstratives: "The point that Pattullo makes is very sound" — consider more precise phrasing: "Pattullo's reasoning here is logically sound because..."

I recommend reading your sentences aloud or using a grammar-checking tool (eg, Grammarly) for subject-verb agreement and article usage, as these are common trouble spots for German speakers due to differing article systems.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Clarify thesis in the introduction—remove ambiguity.
Insert missing quotations and analyze them explicitly.
Engage fairly with Wall's argument instead of dismissing it via tone.
Distinguish between reporting a claim and asserting it as truth.
Reduce rhetorical questions; replace with direct argumentative statements.
Proofread for article usage and subject-verb agreement.

You have a clear structure (introduction → Pattullo → Wall → rebuttal → conclusion) and genuine engagement with both texts—strengthening the evidentiary and logical foundation will make this a much more persuasive essay. I'd be happy to look at a revised draft!

Overall Impression

This three-part structure is a real strength — each body paragraph corresponds to a part of your thesis. However, the essay needs work in three areas: evidence and citation, argument development, and language precision. Below are specific suggestions.

Content and Argumentation

Right now, your argument rests on assertions like "it has been proven" without any proof actually shown. For a persuasive essay, concrete, properly cited evidence is not decoration — it *is* the argument. Please insert the actual quotations, and use a consistent citation format (eg, "Forst argued that capital punishment produced no measurable deterrent effect (Forst, 1983, p. XX)"). Vague phrases like "a scientist named Forst" should become "criminologist Erik Forst."

Check your facts. You state that "Liberal Democrats" feel the death penalty is essential to America's future well-being. In US politics, support for capital punishment is generally associated with conservatives, not liberals. Separate your ideas more clearly. Your third body paragraph ("full-proof") actually contains three distinct arguments: unequal access to good lawyers, court backlogs, and wrongful convictions. Consider splitting this into two paragraphs, or at least using clearer transitions ("A second flaw... A third, more troubling flaw...") so each point gets room to develop rather than being rushed through.

Strengthen your moral argument. Currently, your central moral claim is essentially: "No, to me no murder is right, not even the death penalty." This is a strong personal conviction, but as an argument it is circular — you are asserting your conclusion rather than defending it. Consider explaining *why* state-sanctioned killing is morally different from what you claim it merely mirrors, or explain more fully why "an eye for an eye" fails as a justice principle. Either develop this idea briefly in the body (perhaps in your first argument, where you discuss deterrence) or remove it from the conclusion — a conclusion should synthesize existing points, not introduce new ones.

Language and Style

Your English is generally strong, but here are recurring issues (some likely influenced by German sentence structure):

Run-on sentences and comma splices: eg, "Well it has been proven through several studies that the death penalty has not reduced the rate of homicides by any significant number at all." Break long sentences into two; avoid starting sentences with "Well" in formal writing.
Word choice: "full-proof" should be "foolproof." "Swaying governments into their own direction" should be "swaying governments to their side" or "in their favor." "Increases crime" should be "increasing crime."
Passive voice overuse: Phrases like "it has been proven" and "it has been proven that many criminals..." appear often without a clear agent. Try: "Studies show..." or name the source directly.
Article usage: Watch for missing or extra definite articles (a common German-English difference), eg, "the death penalty" vs. "a death penalty" — you switch between these inconsistently within the same argument; decide whether you're discussing the concept generally (no article, or "the death penalty" as an institution) and stay consistent.
Rhetorical questions: "is it moral for the government to turn right around and murder them" needs a question mark, and as an argumentative technique, rhetorical questions should be used sparingly — one or two per essay is more effective than several.

Summary of Priorities

Insert and properly cite all missing quotations.
Verify factual/political claims.
Split overloaded paragraphs and strengthen the moral argument with more explanation, less assertion.
Either integrate or remove the "education" point in the conclusion.

These are different claims, and mixing them can confuse readers about what you are actually defending.

Evaluate sources, don't just report them
Right now, much of the essay summarizes what supporters say, then summarizes what opponents say, and treats the opponents' rebuttal as automatically more convincing. You need to show your own critical reasoning. For example, when you write "This claim is quite weak and is refuted by the opposing side," explain *why* it is weak — walk the reader through your reasoning rather than asserting it and moving to the next quotation. Right now the essay sometimes reads as "opponents say X, therefore proponents are wrong" without showing the logical link.

Since your thesis takes a side, remember that a strong argumentative essay still represents the opposing view fairly and completely before critiquing it. In a few places, the pro-prayer position is stated briefly and then dismissed quickly (eg, the Ralph Reed point). Consider whether you're giving the strongest possible version of the opposing argument, or a version that's easy to knock down (a "straw man"). Strengthening your opponent's case will make your rebuttal more persuasive.

Language and Style Issues (for a German-speaking writer)

Article usage: Check definite/indefinite articles carefully, as German and English differ in usage (eg, "the prayer that has been proposed" vs. "a prayer that has been proposed" — decide which is intended).
Word choice/spelling errors: "writes" should be "writers"; "intrest" should be "interest"; "parishoners" should be "parishioners"; "oppotunity" should be "opportunity." Please proofread carefully — these are likely typos rather than comprehension issues.
Sentence length: Some sentences are very long and contain multiple embedded clauses (common in academic German prose but less standard in English academic writing). Consider breaking these into two shorter sentences for clarity, eg, in your first paragraph and in the thesis paragraph.
Transitional phrasing: You rely heavily on formulas like "A second claim that is made by..." and "This claim is quite weak." Vary your transitions to avoid a mechanical, repetitive rhythm (eg, "Yet this argument overlooks...", "A closer look at this evidence reveals...").
Verb tense: Watch for consistency — you shift between present and past tense when describing what authors "claim" or "claimed." Academic convention is usually present tense for discussing arguments in texts you're analyzing ("Quindlen argues," not "Quindlen argued," unless referring to a past historical event).

Suggestions for Revision

Fill in all quotations and citations immediately.
Write a one-sentence thesis statement and place it clearly at the end of your introduction.
For each rebuttal, add 2–3 sentences of your own analysis explaining *why* the evidence undermines the opposing claim.
Proofread for spelling and word-choice using a tool like Grammarly, but also read the essay aloud to catch awkward phrasing.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay: School Prayer Debate

Overall Impression
You have chosen a genuinely controversial and interesting topic, and your position—that state-mandated prayer in public schools is inappropriate—is defensible and consistently argued. You also do a good job incorporating a counterargument and attempting to refute it. However, the essay needs revision in several areas: source integration, argument consistency, paragraph structure, and sentence-level grammar.

Missing Quotations
The most pressing issue is technical: nearly every paragraph contains a  placeholder where a quotation should appear. As it stands, a reader cannot evaluate your evidence at all. Before revising anything else, insert the actual quotations, properly punctuated and attributed (eg, "As Anna Quindlen writes, '...'"). Each quote should be followed by 1–2 sentences of your own analysis explaining *why* it supports your point—you already do this in places (eg, after the Quindlen quote), but make sure every borrowed quote gets this treatment.

Logical Consistency
There is a tension between two positions in your essay that needs to be resolved explicitly. In your counterargument section, you propose a compromise: prayer should be allowed *after* school hours, and you argue this would not be controversial. Yet in your final two paragraphs, you return to a more absolute claim—that "the role of government is not to bring religion into public schools" at all, and that any government-written prayer violates freedom of religion. Readers may wonder: does your objection apply only to *mandatory, in-class* prayer, or to *any* government involvement with prayer, including your own after-school proposal? Clarify this in your thesis and remind readers of this distinction each time you shift between "in-class prayer" and "after-school prayer" as topics, so your position reads as one coherent argument rather than two competing ones.

Thesis Statement
Your thesis—"Time for prayer in public schools should not be instituted in the classrooms during school hours, because religion is imposed on the children who will recite the prayer with little interest to actually pray"—is serviceable but a bit awkward and narrow. Consider revising it to more clearly preview your two main lines of argument: (1) mandated prayer is religiously and pedagogically empty, and (2) it violates the constitutional principle of religious freedom. This will help readers anticipate your essay's structure.

Structure and Transitions
Your paragraphs are organized by sub-argument, which is good, but transitions between them are weak—many paragraphs begin with a new claim rather than a sentence linking back to the previous point. Consider adding brief transitional phrases ("Beyond the issue of academic stress, there is also the deeper problem of...") to guide the reader. Also, your conclusion largely restates earlier points without synthesizing them into a final, memorable statement. Try ending with a forward-looking or evaluative sentence rather than simply repeating "the government has no right to do this."

Rhetorical Questions
You use several rhetorical questions ("Does this mean...?" "How could there possibly be any thoughtfulness...?"). Used occasionally, these are effective, but overuse weakens the essay's assertiveness. Consider converting one or two into direct statements to strengthen your argumentative voice.

Language and Grammar
As a native German speaker, you will want to watch for the following recurring patterns:

Subject–verb agreement in questions/inversions: "Does this means..." should be "Does this mean..."; "Even is there is a silent time" should be "Even if there is a silent time." German word order habits may be causing some of these slips—read these sentences aloud to check verb form.
Preposition + gerund vs. infinitive: "with little interest to actually pray" should be "with little interest in actually praying."
Countable/uncountable confusion: "there is a little effect" should be "there is little effect" (no article needed with "little" in this sense).
Word repetition: "professed" appears four times; vary your verbs (eg, *claimed, argued, stated, asserted*) to avoid monotony that can read as a translation artifact.
Comma splices and run-ons: Some sentences string together several clauses with commas where a period or semicolon would improve clarity (eg, in the second paragraph, the long sentence about Jewish children could be split).

Citation Style
Rather than "According to the author of the December 7, 1994 issue of the New York Times, she states," name the author directly: "In her *New York Times* article, Anna Quindlen argues that..." This is more concise and more academically conventional.

Priority Revisions
Insert and properly integrate all missing quotations.
Resolve the tension between opposing *all* government prayer initiatives versus endorsing an after-school compromise.
Sharpen the thesis to preview your two-part argument.
Proofread for subject–verb agreement and preposition use, especially in questions and conditional clauses.
Strengthen the conclusion with a synthesis rather than repetition.

You have strong instincts for counterargument and evidence-based reasoning—tightening the logical thread and cleaning up the grammar will make this a genuinely persuasive essay.

Overall Impression

You have chosen a genuinely difficult and important topic, and it's clear you have done real research (the Traystman/epinephrine case, the Draize test, thalidomide, the depression experiments). The essay also shows an attempt to present both sides before reaching a conclusion, which is the right structural instinct for an argumentative essay. However, the piece needs significant revision in four areas: missing citations, argument balance and logic, paragraph organization, and language precision. Below I address each.

Missing Citations (Urgent)

Throughout the essay there are placeholders marked  where quotations or paraphrased claims should be. For example: "in the Bible, it says that humans were to have " and " states Herbert Waddams." These gaps make the essay currently unreadable in places and, more importantly, unverifiable. Before resubmission, every claim attributed to a source (the Bible, Waddams, Traystman, Bromey) needs the actual quotation or paraphrase, plus a proper citation (author, year, page if applicable). Right now a reader cannot tell what these authorities actually said, which seriously weakens your credibility.

Argument Logic and Balance

Your introduction promises to explore a "dilemma," which implies genuine tension between two defensible positions. Your conclusion, however, closes down that tension with a rhetorical question ("who's life is more sacred, the life of a three year old boy, or a pig?") that assumes what it should prove. This is a false dichotomy: not all animal testing involves choosing between a child's life and an animal's life, and framing it this way oversimplifies a complex issue.

A few other logical weaknesses to address:
The Timmy Baker story is powerful, but is it a real, documented case or a composite/hypothetical illustration? If invented for effect, say so explicitly — using it as unmarked "evidence" is misleading. If real, it needs a source.
The claim that testing for depression research "does not really classify as human pain and discomfort" is asserted, not argued. Depression is widely recognized as causing serious suffering; you need to either support this claim with evidence or drop it, as currently it undermines your credibility rather than your argument.
The appeal to the Bible as an authority ("What better of an authority figure than God") will not persuade readers who do not share your religious framework. In an academic argumentative essay, it's safer to present this as *one* ethical perspective among several (eg, utilitarian, religious, rights-based) rather than asserting it as settled truth.
The comparison to hunting, bullfighting, and cockfighting at the end is a good point but underdeveloped — it could either be cut for space or expanded into a real argument (eg, about inconsistency in how society regulates animal suffering).

Structure and Paragraphing

The essay would benefit from a clearer roadmap. Right now, topics appear in this order: general dilemmas → biblical justification → Timmy story → statistics → animal rights argument → researcher rebuttal → thalidomide → medical training → the dog/depression case → living conditions → slippery slope → other animal deaths → conclusion. This is a lot of ground, and some paragraphs contain two or three separate ideas (eg, the paragraph mixing ethics definitions with Waddams's quote and the Draize test). Consider:

Writing a clearer thesis at the end of the introduction stating your specific position (not just "there are dilemmas").
Grouping your "against testing" arguments together, then your "for testing" arguments together, before your conclusion — rather than alternating point/counterpoint in a way that can feel scattered.
Cutting or condensing the "slippery slope" (bacteria/viruses) paragraph, which is a good idea but currently just one sentence deep.

Language and Style Notes

Since English is your second language, here are patterns to watch for:
Articles: watch missing/incorrect articles, eg, "Animal testing accounts for a substantial part" (good) vs. "One animal experiment, labeled as evil..." (you wrote "On animal experiment").
Word confusion: "aides" should be "aids" (thalidomide "aids in the healing of," not "off"); "party activity involved" should likely be "party actively involved."
Run-on/comma splices: several long sentences link independent clauses with commas only (eg, in the Timmy Baker paragraph). Consider breaking these into shorter sentences.
Register: phrases like "Thank God for little Timmy" are emotionally effective but too informal/editorializing for academic argumentative writing — consider more neutral phrasing.

Summary of Priority Fixes
Fill in all  citations with real, sourced content.
Clarify whether Timmy Baker is real or illustrative.
Rebalance the conclusion to avoid the false dichotomy.
Reorganize paragraphs into clearer for/against blocks.
Proofread for articles, word choice, and sentence boundaries.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

This is a well-organized, ambitious essay that tackles a genuinely controversial topic. You have a clear thesis, you anticipate and respond to counterarguments, and you attempt to support your claims with research. This is exactly the structure a strong argumentative essay needs. However, there are several issues—some structural, some related to language transfer from German—that you should address in revision.

Structure and Argumentation

Strengths: Your essay follows a recognizable and effective pattern: state the controversy, address each major objection (promiscuity, abstinence, condom reliability), and rebut it with evidence. This "objection-response" structure is a hallmark of strong argumentative writing.

Areas to improve:

Placeholder citations (<\*>): Your essay currently has numerous gaps where evidence should be. This makes it impossible to fully evaluate your argument's strength. When you fill these in, make sure the quotations and statistics are smoothly integrated with signal phrases (eg, "As the CDC reports..." rather than just "according to the Centers for Disease Control, <\*>"). Right now some sentences read as bare frames waiting for content—remember to paraphrase where a full quotation isn't necessary, and quote directly only when the original wording is especially powerful.

Ending with a new element: Your conclusion introduces Robert Rygor's quote as if it were a final rebuttal, but this is the *first* time this essay uses a personal testimony from someone with AIDS. Introducing a new source in your conclusion weakens your ending, because conclusions should synthesize existing arguments, not introduce new evidence. Consider either moving Rygor's quote earlier (perhaps into your emotional appeal in the introduction or into the section on condom reliability) or explicitly framing it in your conclusion as a summarizing/emotional close rather than a new argument.

Logical leaps: Some conclusions drawn from evidence are too quick. For example: "This leads one to believe that if condoms were more readily available, students would be more apt to practice safer sex" and "This shows that the idea of abstinence is a mere fantasy" are strong claims that need more explicit reasoning connecting the cited statistic to the conclusion. Try adding a sentence that explains *why* the evidence supports your claim, rather than letting the reader infer the connection.

Repetitive phrasing: You use "This shows/proves" as a transition several times. Vary your analytical language: "This suggests," "This indicates," "These findings imply," etc

Language and Style (Common German-to-English Transfer Issues)

Spelling/word confusion: "form" → "from"; "than" → "then" ("If condoms are our only means... than anyone..."); "refutals" → "rebuttals"; "John Hopkins" → "Johns Hopkins" (proper noun); "adolescent male" should be "adolescent males" (plural needed after "a national survey of").

Article usage: German does not always require articles where English does, and vice versa. Check phrases like "according to Time" (should this be "*The New York Times*" or "*Time* magazine"? Make sure proper nouns for publications are italicized or capitalized correctly and consistent).

Verb tense consistency: You shift between present and past tense when discussing research findings ("Researchers... conducted," but "These educators have found"). Since you are describing completed studies, past tense is often more appropriate: "found," not "have found," unless you are emphasizing ongoing relevance.

Sentence-final prepositions and calques: Watch for constructions that feel like direct translations from German syntax, e stated in Time" reads oddly—normally in English we'd say "As *Time* magazine reported" or "According to *Time*."

Overly long, list-like sentences: Some sentences pack in multiple ideas without clear connectors (eg, "Many parents believe that condom distribution undercuts schools' unstated purpose of teaching values"—this is fine, but consider breaking up denser sentences elsewhere for clarity).

Citation and Source Integration

Vary your reporting verbs. You rely heavily on "states" and "found." Try "argues," "contends," "reports," "concludes," "emphasizes."
Make sure every source is properly introduced with full name and credentials on first mention (you do this well with Dr. Imellizzeri and Epstein, but be consistent).
Double-check spelling of names (eg, "Imellizzeri" should be verified against the source).

Suggested Revision Priorities

Fill in all placeholder citations with properly integrated evidence.
Revise the conclusion so it doesn't introduce new material.
Proofread carefully for the specific spelling/grammar issues noted above.
Strengthen the logical connections between evidence and claims—don't let the reader do all the interpretive work.
Vary sentence structure and reporting verbs for a more sophisticated academic register.

Overall, you have a strong argumentative skeleton. With careful revision of language details and tighter logical connections, this could be a very persuasive essay.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

This is a well-organized essay with a clear thesis and logical structure. You effectively use analogy (the "thimble of water" comparison is memorable) and address counterarguments systematically. However, several issues with clarity, word choice, and argumentation need attention before this essay is ready for submission.

Structural Strengths

Your essay follows a clear pattern: each paragraph presents an opposing argument, then refutes it. This is a strong strategy for argumentative writing. Your introduction effectively establishes context and states your position clearly.

Areas for Improvement

Word Choice and Idiomatic Expressions

Several phrases suggest interference from German that native English readers may find unusual:

"receive such a negative reputation" — consider "has such a negative reputation" or "is viewed so negatively"
"compares to trying to extinguish" — should be "is comparable to" or "is like"
"lead to moral virtue" (should be "leads")
"The concept of God is not discourage" — verb form error; should be "discouraged"
"religion diversity" — should be "religious diversity"
"encounter any determent" — "determent" is not standard English; you likely mean "deterrent" or "obstacle"
"face apostasy" — apostasy means abandoning one's own faith; you likely mean students would feel pressured or their beliefs would be challenged

Suggestion: Have a native speaker or writing center tutor read through for these small but noticeable errors, or read your essay aloud to catch awkward phrasing.

Logical Clarity

Some sentences conflate cause and effect in ways that weaken your argument:

In paragraph 2, you argue correctly that correlation isn't causation regarding prayer and moral behavior — this is a strong point. But then you list moral failures *during* the prayer era (segregation, lynching) as if this disproves prayer's effectiveness. Be careful: a critic could argue you're using the same flawed causal logic you just criticized. Clarify that you're using this as an *illustration* of why correlation arguments are unreliable, not as proof that prayer caused these problems.

The final paragraph's closing sentence is grammatically tangled: "Because America's vast religious diversity and the lack of apparent good resulting from discouraging religion diversity..." This sentence doesn't parse correctly — it's unclear what is being discouraged and why. Please rewrite this sentence entirely; consider breaking it into two clauses with a clearer causal link.

Paragraph on Senator Gunn

This paragraph would benefit from more precision. You write "By blanketing all students in America as Christians, a large part of the religious population evaporates" — the metaphor "evaporates" is unclear here. Do you mean these students are excluded, ignored, or erased from consideration? Choose a clearer verb.

Additionally, since you're quoting a specific senator, briefly explain the quote's context after the placeholder so readers unfamiliar with this debate can follow your rebuttal without needing outside knowledge.

Redundancy Across Paragraphs

Paragraphs 4 and 5 (on projecting the ban as a "religious straitjacket" and on "individual beliefs") make very similar points: that public prayer privileges one religious view over others. Consider merging these paragraphs or clearly differentiating their focus — perhaps one addresses the *rights* argument and the other addresses the *practical consequences* for students with different beliefs.

Conclusion

Your final paragraph does a good job returning to your thesis, but the essay would be stronger with one added element: a brief acknowledgment of what supporters of school prayer genuinely care about (eg, moral guidance for youth) before restating why your solution better serves everyone's rights. This makes your conclusion feel less like simple repetition and more like genuine synthesis.

Specific Sentence-Level Corrections

"The concept of God is not discourage" → "The concept of God is not discouraged"
"does not encounter any determent" → "should not face any obstacle"
"religion diversity" → "religious diversity" (appears twice)

Suggested Next Steps

Read aloud each paragraph to catch grammatical inconsistencies (verb tense, subject-verb agreement).
Revise the final sentence of the conclusion for grammatical clarity.
Clarify the historical-comparison paragraph (paragraph 2) so it doesn't inadvertently commit the same causal fallacy you're critiquing.
Consider consolidating paragraphs 4 and 5, which currently overlap significantly.
Add context for the Senator Gunn quote so readers unfamiliar with the debate can follow your counterargument.

Overall, your argument is compelling and your use of analogy is effective. With attention to grammar, precise word choice, and slightly tighter logical transitions, this essay will be significantly stronger.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay on AIDS Discrimination

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an important and emotionally resonant topic, and your passion for the subject comes through clearly. You've done research and found compelling case studies (Woodley, Lentz, the Valhalla pharmacist) that could serve as strong evidence. However, the essay needs significant revision in three areas: logical consistency, structural organization, and use of sources. Below are the main points to address.

Critical Logical Error

The most serious problem is in your paragraph on health risk (para. 6). You write: "This is more than enough proof that HIV... is not, and cannot be transmitted through casual contact. Therefore, co-workers, patients, friends, family, or anyone else in close contact with the victim is in danger of contracting the disease." This is a direct contradiction—you prove the opposite of what you conclude. This is likely a slip while translating your thoughts quickly, but it undermines your entire argument at its most crucial point. Please rewrite this sentence carefully: your evidence should support the conclusion that people *are not* at risk through casual contact.

Unsupported and Overstated Claims

Your opening sentence, "Aids is the most common disease of our world today," is factually incorrect and will damage your credibility immediately. Diseases like the common cold, influenza, or malaria are far more prevalent. Consider replacing this with a more defensible claim, such as "AIDS remains one of the most stigmatized diseases of our time" or citing actual prevalence statistics.

Similarly, phrases like "hundreds, perhaps even thousands, have been denied" need a source. Without one, this reads as speculation rather than evidence-based argument.

Missing Citations

Your essay contains numerous placeholder brackets  where quotations should appear. In the final draft, every one of these needs to be filled in with the actual quotation, properly introduced, and cited (author, publication, date, page number if available). Right now, the essay's evidentiary backbone is literally missing. When you insert these quotes, make sure each one is followed by a sentence of analysis explaining *why* this quote supports your argument—don't just let the quotes speak for themselves.

Structural Suggestions

Introduction: Your thesis ("It is morally and legally wrong...") is good, but it arrives late, after several other claims. Consider stating your thesis in your first or second sentence, then use the rest of the introduction to preview your structure (workplace discrimination, insurance discrimination, legal/moral arguments, rebuttal of opposition).

Counterargument: You gesture toward the opposing view (employer/insurer risk-avoidance) but don't explore it with the depth or fairness that strengthens an argumentative essay. Consider dedicating a full paragraph to presenting the opposition's *strongest* version of their argument before you refute it. Right now you call it "a weak argument" before you've even shown why it's weak—save the evaluative judgment for after your rebuttal.

Conclusion: Your conclusion introduces new material (the Americans with Disabilities Act) rather than synthesizing what you've already argued. Consider moving the ADA reference earlier, perhaps into your rebuttal section as legal evidence, and use your conclusion to return to your central claims and their broader implications.

Language and Style Notes

Since English is your second language, here are some patterns to watch for:

Consistency: You spell the subject's name both "Woodley" and "Wookley." Proofread names carefully.
Article usage: "he had A AIDS" — remove the article; also, "AIDS" typically takes no article ("he had AIDS," not "the AIDS" or "a AIDS").
Spelling: "fays" should be "days"; "hospice car" should be "hospice care." These look like typos rather than grammar errors—a careful proofreading pass will catch them.
Repetitive phrasing: You use "One can clearly conclude..." and similar constructions multiple times. Vary your transitions to keep the reader engaged.
Tone: Phrases like "society would just as soon bury someone with AIDS alive" are powerful rhetorically, but in an academic argumentative essay, strive for a balance between persuasive language and measured, evidence-based analysis. Reserve your strongest rhetorical moments for after you've established your evidence.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Fix the logical contradiction in the "casual contact" paragraph—this is essential.
Insert and properly cite all missing quotations.
Verify/soften factual claims (especially the opening sentence).
Strengthen your treatment of the counterargument before rebutting it.
Revise the conclusion to synthesize rather than introduce new points.
Proofread for spelling, article usage, and repetitive phrasing.

You have a strong emotional and ethical core to this essay—now it needs a tighter logical structure and rigorous sourcing to match. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Your essay tackles a genuinely interesting and debatable topic, and you've clearly done research to support your position. However, the essay needs revision in several areas: overall structure, argumentation, and language accuracy. Below are the main points to address.

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis—that orphanages should be reinstituted—is buried at the very end of a long, meandering introduction. Consider stating your position more directly and earlier, perhaps in the second or third sentence, so readers know immediately what you're arguing. The essay also lacks a clear logical progression. This is the most serious issue: many of your quotations are missing (marked with <\*>). As it stands, the essay makes claims but doesn't substantiate them—for example, you assert that Zmora's study shows orphanages "provided a well-balanced lifestyle," but the actual evidence isn't there. Similarly, your closing paragraph promises "three things" from Cooper but doesn't deliver them. Counterargument Handling

You mention that opponents worry about (1) family break-up and (2) cost, which is a good rhetorical strategy. However, the cost argument is dropped almost entirely after the introduction—you never really return to refute it in depth (your funding paragraph addresses future costs, not the opposition's claim that orphanages are too expensive). Make sure every counterargument you raise gets a genuine rebuttal later in the essay.

Overgeneralization and Unsupported Claims

Be cautious with absolute language ("no where near," "many children"); consider more precise, hedged phrasing such as "a significant number" or "several studies suggest."

Language and Grammar

Since English is your second language, here are recurring patterns to watch for (some show German-influenced structures):

Missing possessive apostrophes: "publics attention" → "public's attention"; "it's own" → "its own" (note: "it's" = "it is," so "its" is correct here, but you reversed it elsewhere).
Spelling/word confusion: "no where" → "nowhere"; "content with" → "contend with" (different meanings!); "he use" → "the use."
Article usage: "provide and alternative" → "an alternative." German doesn't require articles as consistently as English, so double-check every noun phrase.
Word order/verb agreement: "many such as Republican Newt Gingrich feel" is fine, but check subject-verb agreement throughout, eg, "a chairman... states" should perhaps be "a chairman... who states."
Capitalization: You capitalize "Republican," which is correct, but be consistent with title capitalization for works like *Children's Institutions* (italicize or underline titles).
Run-on/comma splices: eg, "Orphanages, like many other institutions, have been around for hundreds of years. Stated by..." — the second sentence is a fragment; you need a proper subject before "stated," eg, "As Nicholas Scoppetta... states, ..."

Style Suggestions

Avoid starting sentences with "Stated by [Name]"—this is passive and awkward in English. Instead: "Nicholas Scoppetta argues that..." or "According to Scoppetta, ..."
Vary sentence openings—many paragraphs begin with "While" or "In," which becomes repetitive.
The conclusion is quite short and doesn't fully restate your thesis or synthesize your argument. Consider expanding it to briefly recap your three main points (history, family issues, foster care problems) before ending with your call to action.

Next Steps

Fill in all missing quotations and analyze them explicitly.
Strengthen topic sentences so each paragraph's purpose is clear.
Return to the "cost" counterargument and refute it directly.
Proofread carefully for the grammar patterns listed above—reading your essay aloud can help catch missing articles and apostrophes.

You have a solid foundation and an interesting argument—now it needs tightening, evidence, and polishing.

Feedback on Your Essay: Business Ethics and Relationships

Thank you for sharing this draft. You clearly have a strong personal conviction—that business succeeds through trust and relationships rather than profit alone—and this conviction gives your essay energy. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas before the argument will be persuasive to an academic reader. Below are my main concerns, organized by category.

Thesis and Argument

Your central claim (relationships matter more than profit in business ethics) is a good starting point, but it appears buried in the middle of paragraph one rather than stated clearly as a thesis. Consider opening with a direct thesis statement rather than the rhetorical question ("When someone thinks of business what do they think of?"). Academic essays in English generally prefer a more direct entry point.

More importantly, your "contrast" paragraph about B.F. Goodrich does not actually contrast with your thesis—it supports it (the company sacrifices short-term profit for a long-term relationship). If you intend this as a counterargument, you need to explain *why* someone might see it as opposing your view before showing how you refute that view. Right now it reads as simply another supporting example, which weakens your essay's engagement with opposing perspectives. A strong argumentative essay needs at least one genuine counterargument, addressed and answered.

Use of Sources

This is the most urgent issue: your essay has numerous placeholders () where quotations or paraphrased evidence should appear. Before submitting, every one of these needs to be filled with an actual, correctly cited quotation or paraphrase, followed by your own analysis of *how* that evidence supports your point. Currently, sentences like "According to Fortune, . This supports my claim because..." show good structure (source → explanation), but without the content, a reader cannot evaluate your argument. Make sure to:
Use consistent citation format (eg, APA or MLA, whichever your course requires).
Introduce each source with enough context (author's argument, not just "the article states").
Follow every quotation with your own analysis—don't let the quotation do the work for you.

Structure and Repetition

Several paragraphs repeat the same point (that profit-focused businesses harm relationships) without adding new reasoning. For example, the advertising paragraph and the Norton paragraph make almost identical claims. Consider combining these or making sure each paragraph contributes a *distinct* piece of evidence or a new angle (eg, one paragraph on advertising, one on sales tactics, each with a different ethical concept—deception vs. manipulation, for instance).

Also revisit your conclusion: it introduces a new, unattributed quotation () and then moves quickly to a general statement. Try restating your thesis in new words, briefly synthesizing your three main examples (Sam Walton, Goodrich, advertising), and ending with a forward-looking statement rather than a proverb-like generalization ("Money can not buy you friends...").

Register and Tone

Some phrasing is too informal for academic writing:
"This is what irritates business because many people..." → Consider: "This tendency is ethically problematic because..."
"they are just greedy and out for self satisfaction" → This is an assumption about motive that needs support or softer phrasing, eg, "critics argue that some businesses prioritize self-interest over consumer welfare."
Avoid absolute claims like "many people in business are just out for themselves." Academic writing prefers qualified claims ("some businesses," "critics argue that...").

Grammar and Sentence-Level Issues (Common German-English Transfer Points)

Subject-verb agreement: "Ethics are the goals..." — *Ethics*, when referring to the field of study, is usually singular ("Ethics is..."). When referring to specific moral principles, it can be plural, but be consistent.
Run-on sentences: Several sentences string together multiple clauses with "and" or semicolons where a period or subordinating conjunction would create clearer logic. Example: "Business is not just a matter of economic exchange, money, commodities, and profits; it involves human interactions, is basic to human society, and is intertwined with..." — consider splitting into two sentences for readability.
Article usage: Watch for missing or added articles, a common German-English transfer issue (eg, "the book Business Ethics and Common Sense" vs. "the book *Business Ethics and Common Sense*"—italicize titles).
Word choice: "correct guidelines" → consider "appropriate" or "ethical guidelines"; "the correct way of doing business" → "an effective/ethical way."

Next Steps

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You have constructed a clear, well-organized comparative analysis with a strong central thesis: that Shriver's argument is more persuasive than Methvin's because of his use of evidence and avoidance of fallacies. This is a good foundation. However, the essay needs revision in three main areas: integration of evidence, argumentative precision, and language/grammar.

Evidence and Quotations

This is the most urgent issue: your essay contains numerous  placeholders where quotations should appear. Without the actual quotations, your reader cannot evaluate whether your analysis is accurate, and the essay currently cannot stand on its own. Before doing anything else, insert the exact quotations from Shriver and Methvin, and cite them properly (author, page/paragraph number, according to your assignment's citation style).

Once the quotations are in place, work on *integrating* them more smoothly. Right now, many paragraphs follow a repetitive pattern: "He states, ." Vary your sentence structure — introduce quotations with signal phrases that indicate *why* you are including them ("Shriver strengthens this claim by citing scripture directly: ...") rather than simply announcing that a quotation is coming.

Argumentative Precision

Your analysis is strongest when you explain *how* a rhetorical move functions, not just *that* it occurs. For example, you correctly identify Methvin's appeal to unqualified authority, but you should define this fallacy explicitly for the reader (a brief definition: "an appeal to unqualified authority occurs when a writer cites a source without establishing that source's credentials") before applying it. This makes your critique more rigorous and shows command of argumentation theory, which seems to be the analytical framework for this assignment.

Similarly, some claims are asserted rather than demonstrated. You write that Methvin "does not use his information effectively," but you should specify *what* would have made it effective (eg, statistics, expert testimony with credentials, historical documentation). The same applies to your claim that Shriver's second reason is "hard to argue with" — explain precisely *why* readers would find it persuasive, rather than asserting that they will.

Also reconsider a few sentences where your logic slips: for instance, "it is hard to argue with, although it supports his claim more than his opponent's claim" is unclear — hard to argue with *and* one-sided are somewhat in tension, so clarify what you mean.

Finally, your conclusion largely restates the introduction. Use this space instead to synthesize your findings — for example, reflect on what this comparison suggests more broadly about what makes religious/political arguments persuasive in public discourse.

Language and Grammar

Since English is your second language, here are patterns to watch for (all are common transfer errors from German):

Missing articles: "it continues to be influential factor" → "an influential factor." German does not always require articles where English does.
Verb tense/typos: "people and learned through war" → "people have learned"; "This ruling ha become" → "has become"; "praying has should not be allowed" → remove "has."
Subject-verb agreement: "He defines the two most prominent voices in the debate are those of piety" — restructure: "He identifies the two most prominent voices in the debate as those of piety..."
Word choice/collocations: "corroborates with his claim" → "corroborates his claim" (no preposition needed); "the reader is lead to believe" → "led"; "any where else" → "anywhere else."
Awkward phrasing: "A research for his argument he conducted a survey" is a garbled sentence — likely two ideas merged. Consider: "As research for his argument, he conducted a survey..."
Typo: "momen tum" → "momentum."

I recommend reading your essay aloud or using a grammar-checking tool (eg, Grammarly) after revision, since many of these errors are easy to miss when you know what you *meant* to write.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Insert all missing quotations and cite them properly — this is essential before anything else.
Define key terms (like "appeal to unqualified authority") before applying them.
Deepen your analysis — explain *why* something is persuasive or weak, not just that it is.
Revise the conclusion to synthesize insights rather than repeat the introduction.
Proofread carefully for articles, verb forms, and word choice, paying particular attention to the patterns noted above.

Your comparative structure and thesis are solid — with revision focused on evidence, depth of analysis, and language precision, this essay will be considerably stronger.

Feedback Report: The Media's Right to Report on Executions

Thank you for this engaging and well-researched essay. Your topic is compelling, and you clearly did substantial research into the Robert Alton Harris case. Below is feedback organized by category to help you revise.

The most pressing issue is the number of empty placeholders () throughout the essay. Before submitting a final draft, you must fill these in with properly cited quotations (with page numbers or article dates) or paraphrase them clearly in your own words. This is not a minor formatting issue—your argument's persuasiveness depends on this evidence being visible to the reader.

Thesis and Argument Development

Your opening thesis is somewhat circular: "the public has a right to be informed about anything... and people want to be informed... therefore, media should have access." This reasoning conflates *desire* with *right*—wanting information does not, by itself, establish a legal or ethical entitlement to it. Consider grounding your thesis more explicitly in the First Amendment argument you develop later, and perhaps distinguish between the *public's right to know* and the *press's right to access*, since these are related but distinct legal concepts.

Additionally, much of the essay summarizes the Harris case narratively rather than analyzing it. Ask yourself, for each paragraph: "What claim does this paragraph support, and how?" For example, the paragraph beginning "Robert Alton Harris was sentenced..." is purely factual background; it would benefit from a sentence explicitly linking these facts to your argument (eg, why does the *length* of the appeals process or his *prior record* matter to the media-access question?).

Structure

Your essay follows a logical chronological structure (ban → lawsuit → ruling → later restrictions → counterarguments → conclusion), which works well for a case study. To strengthen it:
Add a brief paragraph early on addressing the *legal* stakes (First Amendment vs. prison security), so readers understand the framework before the narrative begins.
The counterargument section (media "seeking high ratings") is good practice—you acknowledge the opposing view before refuting it. Continue this pattern by more directly addressing *why* security concerns don't extend to reporters with pens/notebooks, using Schnacke's reasoning as evidence.
Your conclusion is strong in tone but a bit abrupt in register ("If you find the event grisly and gruesome, turn it off; it is that simple"). This works rhetorically but feels casual for an academic essay—consider tempering it slightly or following it with a more formal closing sentence.

Grammar and Language (common patterns to watch for)

As a German-speaking writer, you're navigating some typical interference errors. Here are the main patterns:

there/their confusion: "However, there access should be restricted" → *their access*.
Subject-verb agreement: "One such case which receive national attention" → *received*; "inmate are more tense" → *inmates are*.
Missing/incorrect negation or word choice: "the warden... had made to effort to justify" — this sentence is unclear; likely you mean "had made *no* effort" or "had made *an* effort." Please revise for clarity.
Article usage: German lacks the English distinction between definite/indefinite articles in some contexts, which may explain occasional omissions (eg, "attorney for the San Francisco Examiner and author of the brief" is fine, but check other noun phrases for missing "a/the").
Comma splices/run-ons: Several sentences chain multiple independent clauses with commas alone (eg, the paragraph explaining Schnacke's ruling). Break these into shorter sentences or use semicolons/conjunctions.

I recommend reading your draft aloud—this often catches agreement and word-order issues that are easy to miss when writing in a second language.

Citation Style

You mention sources informally ("Sherwood told Editor & Publisher...") but don't provide full citations. Since this is an argumentative essay likely requiring academic sourcing, add a Works Cited/References list and use consistent in-text citation (APA, MLA, or Chicago, depending on your course requirements).

Summary of Priority Revisions
Fill in all  placeholders with real, cited quotations.
Strengthen the thesis to clarify *right* vs. *desire*.
Add analytical sentences linking factual narrative to your argument.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Thank you for sharing such a personal and courageous piece of writing. Your willingness to draw on your own experience gives this essay real emotional power, and your central argument—that schools should teach sexual morals and values because parents and other institutions are failing to do so—is a clear and debatable claim worth making. Below are suggestions organized by area to help you strengthen the essay in revision.

Structure and Argument Development

Your personal narrative in the opening paragraph is compelling, but the connection between your experience and your thesis needs to be made more explicit. Right now, the essay jumps from your story to a general claim about schools without fully explaining *why* your specific experience proves that schools (rather than parents, for instance) are the right solution. Consider adding a sentence or two that bridges this gap—something like: "My father's failure to have this conversation, and my own lack of preparation, illustrate a pattern repeated in households across the country."

The essay would also benefit from a clearer roadmap. After your thesis, tell the reader what points you will cover (eg, "This essay will show that parents lack the tools to teach sexual education, that schools are well-positioned to fill this gap, and that the consequences of inaction are severe").

Evidence and Support

This is the area needing the most attention. Several places in your draft have placeholder markers () where statistics or evidence should be. These gaps are noticeable and undermine your credibility, since claims like "over two million couples" or references to "studies" are left unsupported. Before your next draft, please:
Locate the actual sources and statistics you intended to cite
Introduce them properly (author, publication, date)
Explain what the data means for your argument, rather than just stating it

Unsupported claims—such as "Education of sexual activity for youth is no longer taught by parents"—are broad generalizations. Consider qualifying these ("Many parents..." or "Studies suggest that a majority of parents...") and backing them with evidence.

Counterargument

Your paragraph addressing those who think sex education is "unethical" is a good instinct—strong essays acknowledge opposing views. However, your response doesn't fully engage with *why* some parents object (often for religious or values-based reasons, not because they're unaware teens are sexually active). Consider researching this counterargument more carefully and responding to its strongest version, not a simplified one.

Clarity of Values Being Advocated

You list several values you want schools to teach: abstinence until marriage, lifelong commitment, and condom use for "safe sex." These are somewhat in tension with each other (abstinence-only vs. safe-sex education represent different philosophies in this debate), and you don't acknowledge this tension. Clarifying your own position—are you advocating abstinence-based education, comprehensive sex education, or both?—will make your argument more coherent.

Language and Grammar (German-to-English patterns)

A few recurring issues to watch for:

Apostrophes for plurals: "the bird's and the bee's," "youths'," "parent's" — in English, plural nouns do not take an apostrophe (birds, bees, youths). Apostrophes are only for possession or contractions.
Article use: English articles work differently than German. For example, "Educations of sex in school" should likely be "Sex education in schools" (no article needed before general/plural nouns in many cases).
Word order and connectors: Some sentences show German syntax patterns, eg, "Results of these studies show" followed directly by a claim—consider more natural English transitions like "These studies reveal that..."
Capitalization of pronouns: "Him" is capitalized mid-sentence ("critical for Him")—this should be lowercase unless referring to a deity.
Comma splices: Several sentences run two independent clauses together with just a comma (eg, "We felt the right decision was to get married, have the child, and live happily ever after. We were wrong, after a few difficult years..."). Consider using a period, semicolon, or conjunction.

Tone Consistency

The essay shifts between vulnerable personal narrative and somewhat moralizing generalizations ("Casual sex is unacceptable, its degrading, animalistic"). These strong value judgments may alienate readers who don't share your perspective. Consider whether softening some of this language, or explaining your reasoning more fully, might make your argument more persuasive to a skeptical audience.

Next Steps

Fill in all placeholder statistics with real, cited sources
Clarify and resolve the tension between different sexual education philosophies you're advocating
Strengthen the counterargument section
Proofread carefully for apostrophe use and comma splices
Add a clearer bridge between your personal story and your general thesis

Your voice and personal investment in this topic are real strengths—with revision, this essay's argument can become as compelling as its narrative opening.

Overall Impression

Additionally, several paragraphs (especially the second, on defining life) drift toward description rather than argument. Ask yourself after each paragraph: "How does this support my thesis?" If the answer isn't clear, either make the connection explicit or revise the paragraph.

This is the single most important thing to fix before resubmitting—these missing pieces make it hard to evaluate your argument's coherence.

The physician paragraph ends with a question ("How do they face it?") that is never really answered, then pivots to a claim ("most physicians are very sensitive...") without support. Either develop this paragraph further with research or an interview, or consider cutting it if it doesn't serve your central argument.

Cite real cases (eg, well-known right-to-die cases such as Karen Ann Quinlan, Terri Schiavo, or contemporary debates on assisted dying).
Reference the historical shift in medical/legal definitions of death (heartbeat → brain activity) with a source, since you mention this but don't cite anything.
Consider bringing in a philosophical or ethical framework (eg, autonomy, quality of life) to give your personal opinions more analytical weight.

Style and Language

Short, stacked simple sentences: In the first two paragraphs especially, you have many short sentences in a row ("Our memories of childhood define how we look upon events. Our personality plays a part..."). Consider combining some with subordinating conjunctions or transitions to create more varied, sophisticated sentence structures—English academic style tends to favor more complex sentences than a string of short ones.
"to use" instead of "to us" – proofread carefully for small typos like this.
Comma splices: eg, "It is a personal decision; one that must be taken very seriously, for there's no turning back" — this is fine, but watch for similar constructions elsewhere where independent clauses are joined without proper punctuation.
Rhetorical questions: You use many ("Where do you draw the line...?" "How do they face it?"). These can be effective, but overuse weakens argumentative force. Use them selectively at key turning points, then answer them clearly.
Redundancy: Phrases like "our own decision," "our very own decision," "our own choice" repeat throughout. Vary your phrasing.

This essay has real emotional and intellectual substance—the revision process should focus on giving that substance a firmer argumentative and evidentiary backbone.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Thank you for sharing this deeply personal and passionately argued essay. Your firsthand experience with the welfare system gives your argument real credibility and emotional power, and your critique of the "Contract with America" proposals is clear and well-organized. Below are suggestions to help you strengthen the essay further.

Content and Argument Structure

Strengths: Your personal anecdote is the most compelling part of the essay—it grounds an otherwise abstract policy debate in lived experience. Your three-point critique of Gingrich's proposals is logically structured and effectively shows the gaps in his plan.

Areas to develop:

Historical claims need verification. You state that Hoover began the welfare system in the "early 1930s." This is historically inaccurate—Hoover largely resisted federal relief programs, and it was Roosevelt's New Deal (1933 onward) that established federal welfare programs. Since your entire historical argument rests on this foundation, please verify your sources before revising. This is a significant factual issue that undermines your credibility.

The bracketed placeholders  appear twice in your draft where you intended to insert direct quotations (from Hoover, and from the social worker). Remember to complete these before submitting—an essay with visible gaps looks unfinished to your reader.

Strengthen your causal claims. You assert that welfare "eroded" family values and even "led to violence and crime." These are strong claims that need supporting evidence (statistics, studies, expert opinions) rather than assertion alone. Currently, the argument relies heavily on your own experience generalized to "many other people out there"—consider acknowledging this is anecdotal and supplementing it with research.

Consider counterarguments. Your essay would be more persuasive if you acknowledged *why* someone might support stricter welfare limits (eg, concerns about dependency, cost) before showing why your alternative is better. This shows analytical depth rather than dismissal.

Language and Style (for a German-speaking writer)

"It's" vs. "its" — This is a recurring error throughout: "It's purpose," "and it's values," "lose it's position." In English, *its* (no apostrophe) is possessive; *it's* (with apostrophe) means "it is." This is likely influenced by German's different apostrophe conventions. Please do a search-and-replace check for every instance of "it's" in your essay.

Capitalization patterns — You capitalize many common nouns that should be lowercase in English: "Reform the Welfare System," "air programs" (likely a typo for "aid programs"), "Government," "Society," "Country." German capitalizes all nouns; English only capitalizes proper nouns and titles. Watch for this throughout.

"food rent" — This appears to be a typo; you likely mean "food, rent."

Comma splices and run-on sentences — For example: "My reply, was, 'Thanks, but no thanks'." The extra commas here disrupt the sentence. Read sentences aloud to check natural pause points.

False friends/direct translation risk — "Touted" is used correctly, but check words like "concentrated plan" (do you mean "concerted" or "focused"?) and "solely support" (perhaps "fully support" is more natural).

Word choice: "indolent" — This is quite formal/literary; "lazy" might fit your otherwise conversational tone better, or keep "indolent" if you want a more elevated register consistently throughout.

Structural/Rhetorical Suggestions

Your introduction is strong and engaging with the rhetorical question opening. Consider using a similarly vivid rhetorical device in your conclusion to bookend the essay.
The essay is somewhat long in the middle sections (historical background, then family values, then critique of Gingrich). Consider adding subheadings or clearer transitional sentences between sections to guide the reader.
Your conclusion introduces a big claim about the U.S. losing its "position as the greatest Country in the World"—this feels like a stretch and slightly undermines the specific, concrete argument you've built. Consider ending instead by returning to your personal story or the concrete policy solutions you proposed.

Priority Revisions

Fill in the  placeholders with actual quotations and citations.
Verify the Hoover/Roosevelt historical claim and correct if needed.
Do a full pass correcting "it's/its" and capitalization.
Add at least one piece of external evidence (statistic or study) to support your causal claims about welfare and family breakdown.

Your voice and conviction come through clearly—with these revisions, the essay will be both more polished and more persuasive.

Overall Impression

Paragraph focus: Most paragraphs are well-organized around a single idea (the Jessica/Richard comparison, Erikson's stages, effects on the Does, adoption as an institution). However, some paragraphs try to do too much. The paragraph on adoption as an institution, for example, shifts between describing why adoption is valuable and how public attitudes are changing—these could be split for clarity.

Counterargument: You raise one counterargument (the Jessica case) and respond to it effectively. Consider also addressing Kirchner's perspective more directly—you dismiss his position quickly in your conclusion ("if he knew what was best for his son...") without engaging with his legal or moral claim to biological parenthood. Use of Evidence

The bracketed placeholders () suggest you intended to insert quotations, citations, or dates that are missing from this draft. This is the most urgent issue to fix before submission: readers cannot evaluate your use of the Newsweek article, the Erikson reference, or the ABA Journal quotation without the actual content. When you insert these:
Introduce each source before quoting (eg, "Psychoanalyst Erik Erikson identifies four-year-olds as being in the stage of...")
Follow quotations with your own analysis explaining their relevance
Include full citation information according to your assigned style guide (MLA, APA, etc)
