This rhetorical question is engaging, but it's a stylistic choice better suited to persuasive/opinion writing than formal argumentative essays. If your assignment calls for academic register, consider replacing it with a more analytical sentence about early childhood attachment research instead.

Similarly, phrases like "There is so much at stake" and "Nobody likes to be hurt, so why go through with it?" are conversational. These aren't wrong, but used repeatedly they can make the essay feel less rigorous. Aim for a balance between passionate argument and scholarly tone.

Language Notes (Common German-to-English Issues)

A few recurring patterns worth watching:

Relative pronouns: "the adoptive parents, whom are referred to..." → use *who* (subject pronoun), not *whom* (object pronoun). This is a common confusion since German *wer/wen/wem* doesn't map directly onto English who/whom.

Vary vs. very: "These laws will no longer very from state to state" → you mean *vary*. Easy typo, but worth a careful proofread pass.

Capitalization after full stops: "when Daniela Janikova gave birth" begins with a lowercase letter after a period—likely a typo, but check throughout.

"Ripping him away form" → *from* (typo).

False friends: German learners sometimes import sentence rhythms that feel slightly formal or indirect in English (eg, "It is a decision which shall greatly affect..."). In English academic prose, *will* is more natural than *shall* in this context.

Comma splices: "One might argue that since things are turning out well for Jessica (now named Anna), the same will happen to Richard. However..." — this sentence is fine, but elsewhere you have some run-on constructions joined only by commas. Reading your essay aloud can help you catch these.

Specific Suggestions for Revision

Write a clear thesis statement and place it at the end of your introduction.
Fill in all missing citations and integrate them properly.
Add a paragraph engaging seriously with Kirchner's rights as a biological father before rebutting his position.
Do a proofreading pass focused specifically on relative pronouns (who/whom), homophones (very/vary, form/from), and capitalization.
Reconsider the direct-address rhetorical question; if you keep it, make sure it aligns with your assignment's tone requirements.

Final Note

Your argument has real emotional and intellectual substance—the Erikson framework in particular gives your claims analytical weight beyond pure sentiment. Focus your revision on tightening the structure and finishing the source integration, and this will be a compelling piece of writing.

There is no single U.S. Supreme Court ruling on euthanasia comparable to *Roe v. Wade*; euthanasia/assisted-suicide law varies by state, and you should specify *which* law or state you mean (eg, Oregon's Death with Dignity Act, which passed in 1994, not 1988).
The monkey example is unclear—if this refers to a real case (possibly the "Silver Spring monkeys," which was about experimentation, not euthanasia), you need to research and cite it accurately, or remove it.
Double-check the Paul Hill/abortion timeline and Roe v. Wade dates for consistency.

Unverified or vague historical claims seriously weaken academic credibility. Before revising further, confirm every factual claim with a real, citable source.

The abortion analogy: This comparison is your strongest rhetorical move, but you need to explicitly acknowledge its limits (you gesture at this once, then drop it). Euthanasia is chosen by the person whose life ends; abortion is not the fetus's choice. This is a major disanalogy that opponents will immediately raise—address it directly to strengthen your credibility.

Missing citations: You have four  placeholders where quotations should appear. These must be filled in with actual, accurately cited material before this essay can be evaluated. Right now, key pieces of your evidence are literally missing.

Logical gaps: The move from "protestors turned violent after abortion was legalized" to "therefore euthanasia should be restricted" needs an explicit bridging step. You're implying that restricting the *practice* would prevent violent *protest*, but protest often targets the *legality* itself, not the safeguards around practice. Clarify whether your solution (more safeguards) would actually reduce protest, or whether you're making a separate argument.

Language and Grammar (German-influenced patterns)

"there own lives" → their own lives (this appears twice; watch there/their/they're)
"few people who are thinking... and are also of sane mind" → the logic here is unclear; do you mean "few people who consider ending their lives are of sane mind"? Revise for clarity.
"a live is worth living" → a life is worth living
"my America" → by America
"twenty years later" consistency: check your math on the Hill case relative to Roe v. Wade.
Article omission: German doesn't always require articles where English does. Check phrases like "with questions such as these, there is no black or white answers" → "there are no black-or-white answers."
Word order with adverbs: "it is difficult to please everyone" is fine, but watch constructions like "the law... needs to be changed to fit the needs" — good, but nearby sentences often bury the verb. Read sentences aloud to catch this.
Comma splices: Several long sentences run independent clauses together (eg, in the introduction). Break these into shorter sentences for clarity.

Priority Revisions (in order)

Fill in all citations with real, verifiable sources.
Verify all factual/historical claims—dates, court cases, the monkey example.
Tighten your thesis into one clear sentence.
Address the disanalogy between abortion and euthanasia directly.
Proofread systematically for there/their, subject-verb agreement, and article usage.

Overall

You have the bones of a solid three-part argument (public resistance, comparison to abortion, legal vagueness), and your final rhetorical question is effective. The main work now is fact-checking, filling evidentiary gaps, and tightening logical connections between paragraphs. I'd be happy to discuss sourcing strategies or grammar patterns in office hours if that would help.

Feedback on Your Essay: Televised Executions

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling, ethically complex topic, and the opening anecdote about Amy is genuinely effective at drawing the reader in. Your instinct to categorize the debate as one of "value" versus "consequence" shows real analytical ambition. However, the essay in its current form reads as an early draft: several quotations are missing (marked with ), the central argument becomes repetitive rather than developed, and there are recurring grammar issues typical of German-to-English transfer. Below are concrete areas to address.

Missing Citations
This is the most urgent issue. You reference Sherman, Smolowe, Pat Clark, and KQED's own statement, but the actual quotations are placeholders. Without them, I cannot assess whether your evidence supports your claims, and the essay currently looks unfinished. Please insert the quotes and cite them properly (author, source, page/paragraph). Strong argumentative essays live or die by the quality of evidence integration—right now the argument is asserted, not demonstrated.

Thesis and Central Claim
Your thesis appears at the end of paragraph one, but it's dense and could be sharpened. Consider stating explicitly, early on: *"KQED frames this as a matter of value (a question of rights), while opponents frame it as a matter of consequence (predicting real-world harm)—and the consequence-based argument is stronger because it is supported by historical evidence."* This framework, once stated clearly, should organize every paragraph that follows. Right now, you introduce the value/consequence distinction in paragraph two but don't return to it consistently—paragraph four drifts into "some see it as good, others as bad" without linking back to your value/consequence framework.

Repetition and Circular Reasoning
Watch for circular statements like: *"only one aspect is being considered and is therefore not a valid argument."* This restates the conclusion rather than proving it. Similarly, "not a valid argument" appears twice in one sentence. Try to advance the reasoning with each sentence rather than restating the claim in slightly different words.

Paragraph Transitions
Paragraphs 3 and 4 begin abruptly ("A specific instance..." / "Watching executions on television will be both well-received and rejected...") without signaling how they relate to the previous paragraph or to your thesis. Add transitional phrases such as "In contrast to KQED's rights-based claim, opponents support their position with a historical case study..." This will help the reader follow your logical chain.

Language and Grammar (selected examples)

Word choice/false friends: "usual routing" → *usual routine*; "will effect their ratings" → *affect* (effect is a noun; affect is the verb you want here).
Capitalization: "first amendment right" → *First Amendment right* (proper noun); "pat Clark" → *Pat Clark*.
Sentence fragments/typos: "her mother was in the be drooms gathering laundry" — likely a formatting error from copy-pasting; proofread carefully before submission.
Preposition errors: "presented by the opponents to this idea" → *opponents of this idea*.
Sentence beginning with lowercase: "their argument is more thoroughly defined" → capitalize "Their."
Awkward passive constructions: "This point is what their whole case is being based on" is grammatically correct but stylistically heavy for academic English. Consider: *"Their entire case rests on this single point."*
Long, loosely connected sentences: eg, "They do not want to share any possible bad consequences that could result because the only concern that they have at this time is how televising this execution will effect their ratings." Consider splitting into two sentences for clarity: *"They do not address the possible negative consequences. Their only stated concern is how the broadcast will affect ratings."*

German speakers often carry over subordinate-clause-final verb placement or comma usage from German syntax; reading your sentences aloud can help you catch places where English requires more separation between clauses (shorter sentences, more periods) than German would.

Structural Suggestion for Revision

Introduction: Keep the Amy anecdote, but tighten it, then state your thesis clearly using the value/consequence terminology.
Body Paragraph 1: Define "issue of value" vs. "issue of consequence" explicitly before applying them.
Body Paragraph 2: Present KQED's argument and explain why it remains undeveloped (add the missing quote here).
Body Paragraph 3: Present the opponents' Rainey Bethea case and Sherman's quote as concrete consequence-based evidence.
Body Paragraph 4: Address counterarguments (Pat Clark's "educational" argument) and explain why they don't overturn your thesis.
Conclusion: Return to Amy, but also explicitly restate why the consequence-based argument outweighs the value-based one.

Final Note

Your critical thinking is strong—you clearly understand the shape of the debate. Focus your revision on (1) inserting real quotations, (2) tightening the value/consequence framework throughout, and (3) proofreading for the grammar patterns noted above. This will elevate the essay considerably.

Feedback on Your Abortion Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled a highly contentious topic with clear conviction, and your five-paragraph structure (introduction, two biological "proofs," medical ethics, conclusion) shows a logical plan. However, the essay currently reads more as advocacy than academic argumentation. Below are the main areas to revise.

Argumentative Balance and Tone

Your introduction promises to present *both* the pro-choice and pro-life positions, but the essay never seriously engages the pro-choice argument beyond a brief, dismissive summary. For a persuasive academic essay, you need to represent the opposing view accurately and then explain *why* your evidence outweighs it — not simply assert your conclusion is "resounding."

Watch for loaded language that undermines your credibility with a skeptical reader: "senseless killing," "innocent child," "turn their backs on that oath." These phrases signal bias rather than argument. Academic persuasion works best when the evidence does the emotional work, not the adjectives.

Also reconsider your rhetorical questions ("Is it moral for doctors...?" "Just what is the moral decision?"). You use several in a row; used sparingly, they can be effective, but overuse makes the essay feel like a sermon rather than an analysis.

Logical Structure

Your causal chain — (1) the fetus is alive and human, (2) it is a separate entity, (3) therefore abortion is murder, (4) therefore it is immoral — is clear and worth keeping. But each step needs the *opposing* position acknowledged and refuted, not just supporting quotations stacked up. For example: pro-choice advocates typically do not dispute that a fetus is biologically alive: they dispute whether it has the *moral status* of a person with rights equal to the woman's. Your essay conflates "alive/human" with "possessing full rights," which is the actual point of contention. Address that gap directly.

Citations

The bracketed placeholders () suggest you intend to insert direct quotations, but in the current draft they create serious gaps — a reader cannot follow your argument without the missing content. When you insert the quotations:

Introduce each source with context (credentials, publication, date) *before* the quotation, not scattered mid-sentence.
Be consistent with names: you write "Daniel Calahan" and later "Daniel Callahan" — check the correct spelling and use it uniformly.
Cite according to your required style (MLA/APA), including page numbers.
Avoid quoting so heavily. Right now entire paragraphs are built from quotations with minimal analysis. Paraphrase where possible and spend more sentences explaining *why* the quotation supports your claim.

Language and Grammar (typical L1-German interference points)

A few recurring issues to watch for as you revise:

Missing "than": "more ninety-eight percent" → "more than ninety-eight percent."
Preposition confusion: "A direct quote taken form the oath" → "from the oath." Also check "endure the above procedure" — since this is the opening sentence, the reader has no referent yet for "the above procedure." Introduce abortion by name in the first sentence rather than relying on a placeholder to define it.
Article use: German does not always require articles where English does, so watch for missing "a/the" (eg, "abortion...is being performed at a rate").
Subject-verb agreement and sentence fragments: eg, "Or 'endure' the right word?" is missing "is" ("Or is 'endure' the right word?").
Overly long, comma-spliced sentences: Break some of your longer sentences into two for clarity, especially in paragraph 2, which contains several dense, quotation-heavy sentences.

These are minor and fixable in a careful proofreading pass — read your essay aloud, which often catches missing small words.

Conclusion

The conclusion currently ends on an aphorism ("You can't legislate morality") that is asserted rather than argued. Since your entire essay has argued that morality *should* be legislated (ie, abortion should be illegal because it is immoral), this final line seems to contradict your thesis. Reconsider whether you want to end with a call for legal change, a call for ongoing debate, or an acknowledgment of the impasse — but make sure your final sentence aligns with the argument you have built.

Suggested Next Steps

Fill in and properly cite all quotations.
Add a paragraph (or integrate throughout) that states the strongest pro-choice counterargument and directly responds to it.
Revise emotionally loaded language into more neutral, evidence-based phrasing.
Proofread for the grammar patterns noted above.
Rewrite the conclusion so it logically follows from your thesis.

You have a solid argumentative skeleton — the revision work now is about deepening the fairness of your treatment of the opposing side and tightening the language.

Feedback on Your Essay: Medical Technology, Genetic Disease, and the Value of Life

Thank you for sharing this thought-provoking and ambitious essay. You tackle a genuinely difficult bioethical question—whether medicine's success at prolonging life comes at long-term costs—and you bring in a range of material (constitutional rights, genetics, antibiotic resistance, genetic counseling) to support your position. Below are suggestions to help you strengthen the essay's argument, structure, and use of evidence.

Clarify and Narrow Your Thesis

Right now, your central claim emerges only gradually and shifts in scope. At times you argue that treating symptoms rather than causes is medically inefficient; at other points you suggest that modern medicine is *dysgenic* (allowing "weaker" genes to persist in the population); and in the conclusion you argue that prolonging life devalues life itself. These are three distinct claims, and each requires different kinds of evidence. Choose the strongest one—I would suggest the argument about resource allocation and symptom- vs. cause-focused treatment—and make it your explicit thesis in the introduction. Then organize each paragraph so it visibly supports that one claim.

Handle the Darwinian/Genetic Argument with More Care

The argument that medicine allows "weaker" genes (PKD, asthma, poor eyesight) to spread and thereby weakens the species is the essay's most controversial claim, and it will raise red flags for most academic readers because it echoes discredited social Darwinist and eugenic reasoning. This doesn't mean you cannot make a version of this argument, but you must:
Acknowledge directly that this reasoning has a fraught history, and explain how your point differs.
Address the ethical stakes: are you saying it would be *better* if people with PKD had died before reproducing? If not, clarify what alternative you're proposing (better prevention? different resource allocation?).
Bring in the counterargument that a society's ethical value lies precisely in caring for the vulnerable, not in "selecting" for fitness. Engaging this counterargument will make your position more persuasive and defensible, not weaker.

Reconsider the Tone Toward Individual Choices

The line "I see no honor to life in this parent's selfish act" is a strong moral judgment made without knowing the full context of the missing quotation (marked ""). Passing harsh judgment on a parent's private reproductive decision—especially without giving readers the actual quotation to evaluate—will alienate readers rather than persuade them. Consider presenting the dilemma more analytically: what competing values are at stake for parents receiving genetic information? This will make your critique of genetic counseling practices (which is a reasonable point) land more effectively.

Fix the Missing Citations

Several placeholders () appear where quotations or data should be. These need to be filled in before submission—as it stands, readers cannot evaluate your evidence (eg, what Bosk's parent actually said, what Heimer observed, the Hantavirus example). Also make sure you're using consistent citation format (MLA, APA, etc) for the Heimer review and Bosk quotation.

Trim or Refocus the Antibiotics Section

The paragraph on antibiotic resistance is interesting but drifts from your main argument. It's about *patient noncompliance* causing resistance, not about the medical community choosing symptom treatment over cures. If you keep it, explicitly connect it back to your thesis (eg, "This illustrates a broader pattern: even successful treatments create long-term biological costs that we ignore in the short term").

Strengthen Paragraph Transitions and the Conclusion

Paragraphs currently read as separate mini-essays (PKD, genetic counseling, antibiotics, viruses, cost) rather than a connected argument. Add transition sentences that explicitly link each example back to your thesis. Your final paragraph introduces a new and important idea—"life is precious only when it can be lost"—but this deserves its own developed paragraph rather than a rushed closing line, since it is close to your real thesis.

Language Notes

Your English is strong and largely fluent; here are a few recurring points to watch:
"The medical community derives its existence solely on the preservation of life" → *from* the preservation of life.
Watch article usage: "a viral infection" should likely be "a bacterial infection" (a small but important content error, not just a language one, since antibiotics don't treat viruses—this actually undermines your later point).
Some sentences run very long with multiple subordinate clauses (eg, the opening paragraph); breaking these up will improve clarity for readers.
Capitalization: "Doctors," "Cancer," "Aids" should not be capitalized as common nouns.

Overall

You have an intellectually adventurous essay with real philosophical stakes. The next revision should focus on: (1) one clear, well-scoped thesis; (2) more careful, self-aware handling of the genetics/eugenics argument; (3) completed citations; and (4) tighter paragraph-to-thesis connections. I look forward to seeing the next draft.

Feedback on Your Essay: Assisted Suicide and Dr. Kevorkian

Thank you for tackling this complex and ethically challenging topic. You clearly have researched multiple perspectives and want to engage seriously with the debate. Below are suggestions to strengthen the essay, organized by area.

Argument and Purpose

Your essay reads as a rhetorical analysis (evaluating the strengths and weaknesses of others' arguments) rather than an argumentative essay (defending your own position). This creates tension: your introduction promises to weigh both sides' "strengths and weaknesses," and you do this consistently—but the essay never arrives at your own claim. The conclusion states we "need to come to some sort of decision," but doesn't tell readers what *you* think that decision should be.

Revision suggestion: Decide which type of essay this should be. If argumentative, each body paragraph should build toward a thesis you state early and defend throughout, using opposing views mainly to refute them. If analytical, make this explicit in your introduction ("This essay will evaluate the strengths and weaknesses of key arguments on both sides") rather than implying you'll settle the debate.

Structure

The introduction is doing a lot of work—posing questions, summarizing all three anti-Kevorkian arguments, and previewing your evaluative approach. Consider trimming it so the thesis is clearer and more prominent.
Each body paragraph tends to follow the same formula: state the opposing argument → quote a source → assert it's "significant" → note one weakness. This becomes repetitive. Try varying how you introduce and respond to evidence.
The evaluations of "strengths" and "weaknesses" are often asserted rather than demonstrated. For example, you say the pro-Kevorkian argument's weakness is "neglection to name the consequences"—but you don't explain *why* that omission is a flaw in the *logic* of the argument, only that it leaves the reader uninformed. Push your analysis further: what specifically makes an argument logically strong or weak (eg, use of evidence, emotional appeal without substance, slippery-slope reasoning, etc)?

Use of Quotations

Several quotations appear as placeholders (), so I can't assess how you're using them, but a few reminders:
Always introduce a quotation (who is speaking, what authority/context they bring) and analyze it afterward—don't just state that it's "significant" or "strong."
Avoid quoting so heavily that the paragraph becomes a summary of sources rather than your own reasoning.
Make sure all quotations are properly cited according to your assignment's required format (MLA, APA, etc).

Language and Grammar

Your English is generally strong, but here are recurring issues to watch for (common patterns for German speakers):

Fragments used as full sentences: "While opponents believe that Kevorkian is trying to play God..." is a subordinate clause, not a sentence. Combine it with the previous sentence using a semicolon or comma: "...acts of mercy...; opponents, however, believe..."
Article use: English requires articles more consistently than German in generic statements. eg, "faith i doctors" (typo for "in") should be "the faith in doctors" — check throughout for missing/misplaced articles.
Word choice: "neglection" is not a standard English word; use "neglect" or "failure to address." "Af raid" should be "afraid" (spacing error—proofread carefully).
Comma splices: Several sentences join independent clauses with only a comma, eg, "This is important because this would let people be presented with other options, which could allow them..." Consider breaking these into shorter sentences or using conjunctions.
Repetition of stock phrases: "This is significant because..." and "This is a great strength/weakness" appear many times. Vary your transitions and analytical vocabulary (eg, "This claim gains force because...", "The argument falters here since...").
Typos: "i" for "in" (twice), "af raid" — a careful proofreading pass will catch these.

Conclusion

Your conclusion restates that both sides have merit but doesn't synthesize your analysis into a clear takeaway. Consider ending with either (a) your own reasoned position, or (b) a sharper synthesis of what criteria society should use to decide these cases (eg, terminal diagnosis, consultation requirements, psychological evaluation) rather than a general call for "some sort of decision."

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Clarify whether this is an argumentative or analytical essay, and adjust your thesis and conclusion accordingly.
Deepen your analysis of *why* each argument is strong or weak—don't just assert it.
Properly introduce, cite, and analyze all quotations.
Proofread carefully for articles, fragments, comma splices, and typos.
Vary your analytical language to avoid repetitive phrasing.

You have a thoughtful grasp of the debate's complexity—now the goal is to make your own analytical voice more visible and precise. I'm happy to discuss any of these points further before your revision.

Feedback on "Corporal Punishment" Essay

Your essay tackles an interesting rhetorical angle—critiquing not the practice of corporal punishment itself, but the *quality of argumentation* used by its opponents. This is an ambitious and analytically sophisticated approach. Below are suggestions organized by priority.

Missing Evidence (Most Urgent)

The placeholders  appear four times where direct quotations should be. Since your entire argument rests on demonstrating inconsistent definitions and contradictory claims, these quotations are not optional—they *are* the evidence. Right now, a reader must simply trust your characterization of Jambor, Johns/MacNaughton, and Diamantes without seeing their actual words. Fill these in before doing anything else; the rest of your revision will depend on what the quotations actually say.

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis—"The opponents' argument is ineffective because of ambiguous terminology and a wavering position"—is clear and appropriately narrow. The two-part structure (definitional confusion, then inconsistent position) is logical and well-signposted with topic sentences. This is a strength; keep this architecture.

However, the essay would benefit from a stronger acknowledgment early on of *why* this critique matters. You gesture toward this in the conclusion (children currently being paddled), but consider moving a version of that stake-setting closer to the introduction, so readers understand from the start why definitional precision has real consequences, not just rhetorical ones.

The Opening Narrative and Direct-Address Questions

The Tommy narrative is vivid and functions well as a hook, but the transition into the thesis is abrupt and the tone shifts sharply from storytelling to academic argument. Consider adding one or two sentences that explicitly bridge the anecdote to your analytical claim—something like: "Stories like Tommy's shape strong personal opinions, but they do little to help a genuinely undecided reader evaluate the *arguments* on either side."

The rhetorical questions ("Are you from the old school?", "So, were you a tommy?") are effective in the introduction and conclusion for framing, but used this frequently they risk feeling more like a persuasive speech than an academic essay. Keep one or two; trim the rest, or reserve this device exclusively for the introduction/conclusion frame.

Logical Gaps to Strengthen

You assert that authors "confuse readers by failing to define... with any decisiveness," but you'll need the actual definitions to show *how* they differ (contradictory scope? Different acts included? Vague versus precise language?). Naming the *type* of inconsistency will make your critique sharper than simply saying they're inconsistent.
Similarly, for Diamantes/Heitzman, specify *what kind* of contradiction occurs (eg, does one sentence say punishment reduces misbehavior and the next say it has no effect?).
The paragraph on "vacillating on consequences" is currently the weakest—it makes a claim ("Some authors make reference to behavioral reactions... but none is decisive") without any supporting example. Add a citation here, or cut the paragraph.

Word Choice and Grammar (Common L1-German Interference Patterns)

A few recurring issues, likely related to English/German differences:

Missing/incorrect articles: "took he seat" → "took his seat" (also a typo: *he* → *his*). "list alternatives to its use" is fine, but check other noun phrases for missing articles, a common gap since German article usage differs from English in generic contexts.
Missing prepositions: "subjected the paddle" → "subjected to the paddle."
Non-standard word formation: "unteeteringly" is not an English word; consider "unwaveringly" or "consistently."
Word choice precision: "incorrigible" means *incapable of being corrected/reformed*—likely not what you mean when describing corporal punishment itself. Consider "wrong," "harmful," or "unjustifiable."
Comma splices: "The authors who define the term do not apply the same definition, several of the authors fail..." — this needs a semicolon, period, or conjunction between the two independent clauses.

These are minor and easily fixed in a proofreading pass—your syntax is otherwise fluent and often quite polished (eg, "his teacher met him... 'Thomas, you are late,' he bellowed" reads naturally).

The Statistics Paragraph

The 1988 statistic (twelve states, etc) is useful but oddly placed—it appears only in the conclusion, disconnected from your main argument about ineffective rhetoric. Consider integrating it earlier, perhaps in paragraph two, to establish the practice's continued prevalence *before* you critique the opposition's failure to end it.

Summary of Priorities

Insert and analyze the actual quotations.
Sharpen each contradiction with specific description, not just assertion.
Add support to the "consequences" paragraph or remove it.
Proofread for articles, prepositions, and word choice.
Smooth the narrative-to-thesis transition.

Your analytical instinct here is strong—critiquing argumentative technique rather than the underlying policy is a genuinely engaging thesis. With the evidence filled in and the prose tightened, this could be a compelling essay.

Feedback on Your Essay on Television Censorship

Thank you for sharing this draft. You've clearly done real research (Huston's study, Berns' article, the Parents survey) and you care about the topic. Below are the main areas to focus on as you revise, moving from the biggest structural issue down to smaller language points.

Clarify Your Authorial Voice (Most Important Issue)

Throughout the essay, you refer to "the advocates for censorship" in the third person, as if you are analyzing someone else's argument rather than making your own. Phrases like "the advocates for censorship prove," "the proponents effectively demonstrate," and "the advocates for censorship presented a strong argument" make it sound like you are writing a rhetorical analysis of another text, not an argumentative essay expressing your own position.

This creates real confusion for the reader: Is this your argument, or are you summarizing/evaluating someone else's argument *about* censorship? If you support censorship, say so directly and argue in your own voice: "Television needs stronger censorship because..." Reserve "advocates argue that..." only for moments where you are explicitly citing a source's claim before adding your own analysis. Right now the third-person distancing weakens your credibility as an arguer.

Fix: Decide on your purpose. If this is your argument, revise so that *you* are making claims, and sources (Huston, Berns, Groller) are cited to *support* your claims, not treated as the ones "proving" things on their own.

Strengthen Structure and Paragraph Unity

Several paragraphs try to do too much at once (introduce a study, editorialize about it, and pivot to a new example within the same paragraph). Consider organizing each body paragraph around one claim, using this pattern:
Topic sentence stating your claim
Evidence (study, example, quotation)
Your analysis explaining *why* this evidence supports your claim
Transition to the next point

Also, your counterargument section (the "opponents" paragraph) feels underdeveloped compared to the rest of the essay. Since this is a strong potential objection (parental responsibility), give it its own fully developed paragraph with a clear rebuttal, rather than folding it into a single dense paragraph.

Missing Citations and Quotations

You have several placeholder brackets () where specific show titles, quotations, or evidence should appear. Before submitting, make sure every one of these is filled in with the actual content and properly cited (author, title, date). Quotations that are simply dropped into a sentence — eg, "one of her influential, rather clean comment... would be: " — need to be integrated with a signal phrase and followed by your own explanation of its significance.

Language and Grammar

A few recurring issues to watch for, since they appear multiple times:

effect vs. affect: You consistently use "effect" as a verb where you mean "affect" (eg, "effects their behavior," "how these programs effect the minds," "the decline is effecting America's youth"). Remember: *affect* is usually the verb ("television affects children"); *effect* is usually the noun ("the effect of television").

Spelling/word choice: "seventy's" → *seventies*; "bout" → *about*; "deminish" → *diminish*; "caniving" → *conniving*; "peak" → *pique* ("pique his interest"); "fore shadowing" → *foreshadowing* (and reconsider whether this is the term you mean — you seem to be describing *prediction* or *warning*, not literary foreshadowing).

Typo: "The advocates for censorship realize that is censorship does not increase" → should be *if*.

Run-on sentences and comma splices: Many sentences chain several independent clauses together with commas (eg, the long sentence beginning "Grace Under Fire is one of the latest hit sitcoms..."). Break these into shorter sentences for clarity — this will also make your arguments easier to follow.

Sentence fragments used as transitions: "if it were only that simple." and similar fragments read as stylistic flourishes but can feel abrupt in formal academic writing. Consider integrating them into complete sentences.

Word order: A few sentences show German-influenced word order, eg, "By showing how the networks have so little regard for that status that the government won't even stand up to the networks..." — try restructuring as: "This shows how little regard the networks have for that status, since the government won't even stand up to them."

Conclusion

Your conclusion currently summarizes "the advocates'" argument rather than restating *your* thesis and its broader significance. Revise it to clearly state your own position and its implications, echoing the specific evidence you found most persuasive.

Overall

You have gathered strong, specific evidence and clearly feel passionately about this issue — that's a great foundation. Your revision priority should be: (1) establish a consistent, confident authorial voice; (2) tighten paragraph structure around single claims; (3) fill in missing citations; (4) proofread for the affect/effect confusion and run-on sentences. Good luck with the revision!

Feedback on Your Essay

Overall Impression

You clearly understand the ethical debates surrounding genetic research and the human genome project, and you have a good sense of the kinds of arguments (eugenics history, privacy, gene manipulation, lack of oversight) that could be used to build a case. However, there is one fundamental problem that needs to be addressed before we can talk about smaller issues: this essay describes how to write an argument rather than actually making one.

Major Structural Issue: Argument vs. Meta-Argument

Look at your own sentences: "One possible way of presenting the most effective argument opposing genetic research and its application is by doing three doings." Later: "This would make a very strong case..." "Citing his example... would be excellent for this argument." "This consequence *could* convince a reader..."

You are consistently writing *about* a hypothetical argument someone else might make, rather than committing to the argument yourself. This creates distance between you and your claims and makes the essay read like an instruction manual or a rhetorical analysis, not a persuasive essay. Ask yourself: is your assignment to argue against genetic research, or to explain how one *would* argue against it? If it's the former, revise every conditional/hypothetical sentence into a direct claim:

Instead of: "This reference to the Nazis would be effective because..."
Write: "The Nazi eugenics program demonstrates that humanity cannot be trusted with control over its own genetic future."

This single change—shifting from meta-commentary to direct assertion—will transform the essay's persuasive power.

Missing Quotations

There are several placeholder brackets  throughout the essay where quotations from Sinsheimer, Maddox, and Annas should appear. These are essential to your argument—right now, readers cannot evaluate your reasoning because the evidence you're building on is invisible. Before further revision, insert the actual quotations and make sure your surrounding sentences accurately introduce and explain them.

Use of Sources

Once the quotations are in place, work on *integrating* them more smoothly. Currently, many source references are dropped in abruptly ("Robert Sinsheimer says this subject "). Try a pattern like:

> Robert Sinsheimer directly invokes this history when he argues that [quote]. By reminding readers of the Nazi eugenics program, Sinsheimer forces us to ask whether humanity can be trusted with genetic power today.

Also, be careful with Sinsheimer's claim about "distortions" never happening again—you present this concession without fully explaining why you find it unconvincing. Spell out your reasoning rather than leaving it implicit.

Paragraph Focus

Some paragraphs try to do two things at once. For example, the eugenics paragraph both narrates history *and* evaluates whether readers would find it persuasive. Separate the historical claim from your evaluative commentary, or better yet, remove the evaluative commentary altogether (see the meta-argument issue above) and let the historical example speak for itself.

Language Notes (with your German background in mind)

Word choice/calques: "by doing three doings" is a literal calque and doesn't work in English. Use "by taking three steps" or "in three stages."
Article usage: "One consequences deals with privacy" – note the subject-verb mismatch (consequences/deals). Also check singular/plural agreement throughout (a common trouble spot since German articles work differently): "One consequence deals with privacy."
Spelling: "Mendal" should be "Mendel."
Preposition/phrasing: "if the argument be kept private in " — this looks like a fragment; the sentence lost its logical connector. Revise to something like: "The question is whether genetic information can be kept private in a world where employers and insurers have strong incentives to access it."
False friends/register: "the layman" is fine but slightly dated—consider "the general public" for a more neutral academic register.
Sentence-final conjunctions: You sometimes use "for" as a coordinating conjunction meaning "because" (eg, "for genetic research is leading to this"). This is grammatically correct but very formal/literary in English—use sparingly, and make sure it's not a direct translation of German "denn," which is used more freely in German than "for" is in English.

Concluding Paragraph

Your final paragraph promises that the argument outlined "would be very strong," again treating the argument as hypothetical. Rewrite this as a genuine conclusion that restates your actual position and its stakes—not a summary of the essay's rhetorical strategy.

Priority for Revision

Convert meta-argumentative language into direct claims (most urgent).
Insert and properly integrate all missing quotations.
Tighten paragraph focus (one claim per paragraph).
Proofread for subject-verb agreement and idiomatic phrasing.

You have strong raw material here—the sources and issues you've chosen are well-suited to a compelling essay. The main task now is to inhabit the argument as your own rather than describing it from the outside.

Feedback on Your Essay: Binge Drinking in JAMA vs. Newsweek

Overall Impression

You have chosen a strong analytical framework—comparing audience, context, and attitude—and you apply it consistently throughout the essay. Your grasp of rhetorical concepts (physical vs. social context, audience assumptions) is solid, and you clearly understand the two articles well. However, the essay would benefit from tightening the argument, reducing repetition, and refining language and citation practices.

Content and Argument

Your central claim—that audience shapes how the same facts are interpreted—is a good, arguable thesis. However, a few points need more support:

The "exploitation" claim. In your introduction you assert that popular magazines "exploit a topic to increase their readership." This is a strong accusation, but you never fully substantiate it with evidence of *intent*. Consider either softening this claim or providing clearer evidence (eg, specific rhetorical strategies) that supports it.

The "good writer" claim. The statement "making those assumptions is what makes a good writer" appears without justification. Explain *why* this is true, or remove it—right now it reads as an unsupported generalization dropped into the middle of an analytical paragraph.

Repetition between sections. Your paragraphs on "audience," "social context," and "attitude" cover overlapping ground—each returns to the same point that Newsweek is emotional/negative and JAMA is neutral/comprehensive. Try to make each section build on the previous one rather than restating it. For instance, the "social context" paragraph could focus specifically on *word choice and framing* (eg, specific adjectives, metaphors), while "attitude" could focus on tone markers like exclamations, rhetorical questions, or narrative anecdotes. Right now they read as three versions of the same argument.

Structure and Organization

The overall structure (intro → content comparison → audience assumptions → physical context → social context → attitude → conclusion) is logical, but the boundaries between categories are blurry. A clearer signal at the start of each paragraph—stating explicitly what new angle you are adding—would help readers follow your progression. Your conclusion, in particular, mostly restates the thesis; consider ending with a more pointed reflection: What does this comparison reveal about how readers should approach *any* science reporting in the media?

Use of Sources

You refer to quotations and statistics with placeholders like —remember to insert the actual quotations and provide proper in-text citations (author, year, page) before submitting. Right now, a reader cannot verify your claims. Also, when you cite statistics ("56% or men and 34% of women"), double check the accuracy of transcription—this looks like a typo for "of men."

Language and Style

As a German-speaking writer, you're doing well with complex sentence structures, but a few recurring issues need attention:

Apostrophes for possessives: "todays college campuses" → *today's*; "it audience" → *its audience*.
Prepositions/word choice: "have an affect" → *have an effect*; "take in to account" → *take into account* (one word: *into*).
Article usage: English requires articles more consistently than German in generic statements—check phrases like "the existence of a problem with drinking on todays college campuses" (fine) versus places where articles are dropped or added unnecessarily.
Run-on/comma-spliced sentences: eg, "This scenario could probably fit the activities outside any dorm at any college on a Friday or Saturday night." is fine, but elsewhere sentences like "Studies reported on in JAMA said that while the majority of those surveyed, 56% or men and 34% of women, were classified as binge drinkers, there were also a percentage, 9% of men and 14% of women, that had not consumed alcohol" are overloaded with embedded clauses—consider breaking these into two sentences for clarity.
Redundant phrasing: "not just a small amount, but drinking to get drunk" is vivid but slightly repetitive; trust the reader to infer intensity from "binge."

Suggestions for Revision

Tighten your thesis to explicitly state the *three* categories of analysis (context, audience assumption, attitude) so readers know the roadmap.
Eliminate overlap between the "audience," "social context," and "attitude" sections by assigning each a distinct rhetorical lens.
Insert real quotations and citations in place of placeholders.
Proofread for possessive apostrophes and preposition errors—these are minor but frequent, and cleaning them up will make the essay read more fluently.
Strengthen the conclusion by adding a broader implication—what should readers take away about interpreting media coverage of scientific/social issues?

You have a clear analytical mind and a good eye for rhetorical detail. With tighter organization, more precise language, and proper citations, this essay could be very strong. Good work overall—keep refining!

Feedback Report: Sex Education Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You have tackled a genuinely controversial topic and gathered substantial evidence from named sources (Flores, Socia, Dawson, Webb-Wood, Brick, Roffman, Gibson). This shows real research effort. However, the essay currently reads more as a *summary of a debate* than as an *argumentative essay* with your own clear position. Below are the main areas to revise.

Missing Quotations/Citations

The essay contains numerous  placeholders where quotations should appear. Since these are the actual evidence your argument depends on, the essay is currently unreadable as a finished piece — a reader cannot evaluate your reasoning without seeing what Flores, Socia, or Sullivan actually said. Before revising further, insert the full quotations and proper citations (with page numbers or dates), since your analysis of "misleading" or "illogical" claims only makes sense once we see the original wording.

Unclear Thesis and Authorial Voice

Your introduction states two opposing views but does not tell the reader which position *you* are arguing for. The conclusion suggests you favor comprehensive education ("it is crucial to give correct and comprehensive views"), but this stance should be stated explicitly and early — ideally as a clear thesis sentence at the end of the introduction (eg, "This essay argues that abstinence-only programs rely on misleading or unverified claims, and that comprehensive sex education offers a more defensible approach").

Related to this: throughout the essay you write "opponents argue," "proponents claim," etc, which distances you from the argument. As the writer, you need to occasionally step in with your own analytical voice — not just report both sides' claims but tell the reader *why* one side's reasoning is stronger. Right now the essay sounds like a neutral survey of a controversy rather than your own argument, even though your evidence selection clearly favors one side.

Confusing Terminology

"Opponents" and "proponents" are used without being anchored — the reader must constantly recalculate who is being referred to (opponents *of what*?). Since your whole essay depends on this vocabulary, define your terms once, clearly, in the introduction: eg, "I will refer to defenders of abstinence-only education as 'proponents' and defenders of comprehensive sex education as 'opponents' throughout this essay." Also consider whether "opponents" is the best label at all — it might be clearer to use "comprehensive-education advocates" vs. "abstinence-only advocates."

Paragraph Structure

Each body paragraph does follow a topic (illogical reasoning → inconclusive studies → fear-based reasoning), which is a good organizational principle. However:
Topic sentences could be sharper. eg, the second paragraph's opening sentence ("Many of the suggestions... can be easily refuted with common sense") is a strong claim — but then the paragraph drifts into multiple separate examples without linking them back to that claim explicitly.
End each paragraph with a sentence that ties the evidence back to your thesis, not just a restatement of the opposing side's frustration (eg, "Hence, if teenagers do not listen, the goals... will not be accomplished" doesn't clearly serve your argument).

Language and Sentence-Level Issues

As a German-English writer, watch for these recurring patterns:

Article omission/misuse: "given misinformation" should likely be "given misinformation" → check whether a definite/indefinite article is needed elsewhere, eg, "the misinformation" if referring to something specific.
Verb forms after auxiliary "will": "teens will not being informed" → should be "will not be informed." Similarly, check gerund vs. infinitive constructions throughout.
Tense consistency: You shift between present tense ("Socia demonstrates") and past tense ("Socia demonstrated") when discussing the same ongoing debate. Decide on a consistent tense for reporting arguments (present tense is conventional for discussing ongoing scholarly/social debates) and a separate tense for historical events/studies (past tense, eg, "a 1986 study showed").
Run-on/comma-spliced sentences: eg, "since teens know neither of those ideas is true and since, why should teens even listen" — this sentence loses grammatical control. Break long sentences into two shorter, clearer ones.
Possessive apostrophes: "opponents misinformation" → "opponents' misinformation"; "proponent's case" is fine, but check "Proponents' support has turned into their own fallacies" — this is a confusing image; clarify what you mean (their evidence became their weakness?).
Word choice: "facile reasoning" is used twice — good vocabulary, but vary it ("simplistic," "reductive") to avoid repetition. "More than recent" is unclear — do you mean "not a recent phenomenon" or "long-standing"? Revise for precision.

Conclusion

The conclusion introduces new rhetorical questions ("Should we teach prevention methods in addition to abstinence?") rather than reinforcing your argument. Revise the conclusion to restate your thesis and briefly synthesize your strongest pieces of evidence, ending with a firm final statement rather than an open question.

Priority for Revision

Fill in all missing quotations.
Write an explicit thesis statement.
Clarify and define "opponents/proponents" terminology.
Revise topic and concluding sentences in each paragraph to connect to your thesis.
Proofread for verb tense, articles, and sentence boundaries.

You have strong source material — the next draft should focus on making your own argumentative voice as visible as the sources you cite.

Feedback on Your Essay: Biological vs. Adoptive Parents' Rights

Overall Impression

You've taken on a genuinely difficult analytical task: evaluating the *quality* of arguments on both sides of an adoption rights debate, rather than simply arguing for one side. This is a sophisticated approach, and your systematic method—working through values, consequences, and rhetorical strategies for each side—shows real analytical ambition. However, the essay needs work in several areas: clarifying its central claim, tightening terminology, filling in missing evidence, and polishing sentence-level English.

Clarify Your Thesis and Purpose

Your actual argument is that *neither side argues well* because both rely on weak values, weak consequences, and rare/extreme examples instead of solid, representative evidence. This is a strong, specific claim—but it doesn't emerge clearly until late in the introduction, and it gets somewhat lost in the body paragraphs. Consider stating explicitly, near the start, what standards you're using to judge "strong" versus "weak" arguments (eg, "An argument is strong when it uses representative evidence, addresses counterarguments, and considers real consequences rather than hypothetical ones"). This gives readers a rubric to apply as they read your analysis, and it will make your evaluations feel less like personal opinion and more like reasoned judgment.

Terminology Consistency

You use several different labels for the same two sides—"supporters of biological parents," "proponents of biological parents," "opponents to biological parents" (meaning adoptive-parent supporters), "supporters of adoptive parents," "proponents for adoptive parents." This inconsistency, especially the phrase "opponents to biological parents," is genuinely confusing, because grammatically it could mean people opposed *to* biological parents having any rights, or people who are biological parents' opponents. Choose two fixed terms—for example, "biological-parent advocates" and "adoptive-parent advocates"—and use them consistently throughout.

Missing Evidence

Several key sentences contain placeholder brackets () where a statistic, quotation, or article title should be. These are central to your argument (eg, the National Council for Adoption statistic, the title of the Ingrassia/Springen article), so they must be filled in with the actual source information and properly cited before this essay is complete. Right now, a reader cannot evaluate whether your claims about these sources are accurate.

Paragraph Development

Your paragraphs generally follow a good pattern: state the argument, give an example, then evaluate its strength. To make this more effective:

Start each paragraph with a clear topic sentence that names the *type* of weakness you're about to disculss (eg, "Adoptive-parent advocates often rely on hypothetical rather than actual consequences").
Explain your reasoning more fully. For example, you write that citing the 18-year-old mother's case "shows the inconsistency of our court system," but you don't explain *why* one case demonstrates a systemic pattern rather than an anomaly. Push your analysis one step further each time.
Avoid vague evaluative phrases like "somewhat strong" or "somewhat reasonable"—these hedge without telling the reader why. Replace with a specific reason: "This argument is strong because..." / "This argument is weak because it generalizes from a single case."

Sentence-Level and Language Issues

As a German-English writer, watch for these recurring patterns:

Word choice/false friends: "righteousness of this point is debated well" — likely you mean "the validity of this point is debated" or "this point is effectively challenged."
Article use: English requires articles more consistently than German in generic statements—check phrases like "burden of guilt that is felt by the parents" (fine) versus places where "the" or "a" is missing or added unnecessarily.
Sentence fragments/run-ons: eg, "This is somewhat reasonable when you look at the fact that the adoptive parents are the only parents the child has ever known. Yet, young children are known for their resilience..." — these are fine, but elsewhere sentences get long and lose clarity (eg, the opening paragraph's second sentence packs in a rhetorical question and an emotional appeal that don't yet connect to your analytical thesis).
Typos: "on could argue" should be "one could argue"; "discuilss" — double check all such slips in your final proofread.
Repeated sentence patterns: Many paragraphs end with a nearly identical structure ("This makes their argument weaker because…"). Vary your syntax to keep the reader engaged.

Structural Suggestion

Consider adding one short paragraph early on that explicitly defines "values-based" and "consequences-based" argumentation (your key analytical categories), since these terms drive your whole essay. Right now readers must infer your framework from context.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

State your thesis and evaluative criteria explicitly and early.
Fix and standardize terminology for the two sides.
Replace all bracketed placeholders with real, cited evidence.
Strengthen topic sentences and push analysis further in each paragraph.
Proofread carefully for article usage, word choice, and run-on sentences.

You have a strong analytical instinct here—the raw material for a sharp, original essay is present. The revision work now is mostly about clarity, consistency, and precision.

Feedback on Your Essay on Academic Cheating

Overall Impression

You have chosen a genuinely engaging topic and clearly did research (library sources, examples like the University of Virginia and the "XF" grade). Your personal investment—drawing on your own experience with cheating classmates—gives the essay authenticity. However, the essay currently reads as a set of loosely connected observations rather than a tightly argued position paper. Below are the main areas to work on.

---

Missing Citations / Source Integration

The placeholders () throughout the text suggest that citations or source references have been dropped. This is a serious problem: a paper that relies heavily on outside research (Levine, Ward, university statistics) *must* clearly attribute claims to their sources. Right now, sentences like "Mr Levine of Harvard believes that " are incomplete and leave the reader unable to evaluate your evidence. Before you revise anything else, restore every citation and make sure each source is introduced with a proper signal phrase (eg, "According to Levine, a Harvard dean, peer pressure undermines honor codes because…") and cited in your required format (MLA/APA). Un-cited or incomplete claims will seriously weaken your credibility.

Thesis and Overall Argument

Your introduction states that honor codes *and* revamped academic codes are "the two strongest methods," but your conclusion argues that honor codes are clearly superior. This shift is confusing because it isn't set up early on. Decide now what your actual claim is, and preview it clearly in the introduction. For example:

> "While several strategies—honor codes, revised grading penalties, and faculty involvement—aim to reduce cheating, this essay argues that honor codes, despite their imperfections, offer the most sustainable long-term solution because they address students' internal motivation rather than merely punishing behavior."

This kind of thesis tells your reader exactly what to expect and gives your body paragraphs a clear purpose: evaluating each solution *against* this claim, not just describing it.

Paragraph Organization and Logical Consistency

The honor-code paragraph is strong in structure (claim → evidence → counterargument), but the counterargument (peer pressure) appears only briefly and isn't resolved. If honor codes are your preferred solution, you need to address *how* the peer-pressure weakness might be overcome—not just mention it and move on.
The paragraph on revamping academic codes ("XF" grades) is well-supported with statistics, but its relationship to your thesis is unclear: are you presenting it as a competing solution, or as evidence that supports your final claim that academic-code loopholes are "too strong"? Make this explicit.
The faculty-involvement paragraph is underdeveloped compared to the others—it lacks your own analysis or a counterargument, making it feel like a summary of sources rather than an argued section.
The paragraph on judiciary/corporal punishment appears abruptly near the "conclusion" and disrupts the flow. Consider moving it earlier, as a third comparative example, and give a clearer transition ("Other, more punitive measures have proven even less effective...").

Language and Grammar

Since English is your second language, here are recurring patterns worth reviewing:

Article errors (common for German speakers, since German articles work differently): "ensure and honest academic environment" → should be *"an* honest academic environment." Watch for missing/incorrect *a/an/the* throughout.
Verb forms: "to eliminated this problem" → *"to eliminate this problem."* Check for infinitive vs. past participle confusion.
Subject–verb agreement: "the range of punishments are focused" → *"the range... is focused."*
Logical/lexical precision: "students tend to be less reluctant to cheat" actually means the opposite of what you intend—check whether you mean *more likely to cheat* or *less willing to report cheating*.
Preposition errors: "when taken in account" → *"taken into account."*
Vague pronouns: Sentences like "This shows that their effectiveness can be increased" leave "their" and "this" unclear. Always make sure pronouns have an obvious, single antecedent.

A close proofreading pass—perhaps reading the essay aloud—will help you catch many of these.

---

Suggestions for Revision

Restore all citations and properly integrate quotations.
Rewrite the introduction to state a clear, arguable thesis.
Reorganize body paragraphs so each openly ties back to your thesis (honor codes as best solution) and directly compares strengths/weaknesses.
Strengthen the "faculty involvement" section with more analysis.
Proofread carefully for articles, verb forms, and pronoun clarity.

You have solid research and real insight into this issue—the next step is to tighten the argumentative structure and polish the language so your voice comes through clearly and persuasively. I'd be happy to look at a revised draft once you've addressed these points.

Overall Impression

You have clearly done substantial research and can summarize the rhetorical strategies of two opposing "sides" (proposition/opposition) in this case with real sophistication. However, the essay currently reads more like a descriptive comparison of media rhetoric than an argumentative essay. Since your title question—"How should the Fourth Amendment be interpreted?"—promises an argument, this mismatch between framing and content is the most important issue to address in revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Stance

Your introduction poses a genuine question, but the essay never answers it. Instead, you spend the body paragraphs neutrally describing how "the proposition" and "the opposition" each construct persuasive arguments, and your conclusion explicitly refuses to take sides ("Whether one side's reasons seem more credible... is not the issue"). If your assignment calls for an *argumentative* essay, you need a clear, defensible claim—for example, that exigent-circumstances exceptions should be interpreted narrowly, or that this particular search was (or was not) justified. Right now, the essay is closer to a rhetorical analysis of press coverage. Decide which genre you are writing and revise the introduction and conclusion to match. If rhetorical analysis is in fact the assignment, make that explicit early on ("This essay examines how media coverage framed the two sides of the admissibility debate") rather than opening with a legal question that implies you will take a position.

Structure and Organization

The chronological background paragraph (bodies found, police at Simpson's home) is useful, but it is placed awkwardly *after* your introduction has already summarized the ethical dispute. Consider moving the factual narrative to the very beginning, before you discuss the two sides' arguments, so readers have the facts before the analysis.

Within the body, your paragraph-by-paragraph structure (proposition strategy 1, proposition strategy 2, opposition strategy 1, opposition strategy 2) is logical, but each paragraph would benefit from a topic sentence that names the *rhetorical technique* being discussed (eg, appeal to emotion, appeal to authority, ad hominem) rather than just narrating content. This would make your analytical framework more visible to the reader.

Evidence and Citation

Several quotations are missing entirely, marked only with . These need to be filled in with the actual quoted language and a proper in-text citation (author, source, date). As it stands, claims like "the detectives displayed a " are unverifiable and weaken your credibility. These are the kind of small errors that a reader may read as carelessness with the very evidence you are analyzing—ironic given your topic!

Logical Consistency

A few claims need tightening:
You state that because the detectives lacked a warrant, they "carelessly gathered" the evidence — but lacking a warrant does not by itself imply carelessness; these are two separate issues (legality vs. reliability). Separate them explicitly.
The conclusion's final suggestion ("if authorities obtain warrants... they will eliminate much of the problem with credibility") introduces a new claim not developed anywhere in the body. If this is your real position, it should be foreshadowed in the introduction and supported with reasoning in the body, not dropped in as an afterthought.

Language and Style

Your English is generally strong and fluent, with only minor issues typical of transfer from German:

Long, layered sentences: eg, "The judge ruled that the detectives who went into Mr. Simpson's home were confronted by a series of portentous questions that needed quick answers" — consider breaking such sentences into two for clarity in English, which tends to favor shorter, more direct sentences than German academic prose.
Capitalization after periods: "one way the opposition tries..." should begin with a capital letter.
Word choice: "portentous" is a striking but slightly unusual choice — "urgent" or "pressing" may fit better here. "Emotional statements" could be more precise as "emotionally charged testimony."
Article use: mostly correct, but watch constructions like "the past of the detectives" → more natural as "the detectives' past."

Priorities for Revision

Decide whether this is an argumentative essay or a rhetorical analysis, and align your introduction, thesis, and conclusion accordingly.
Fill in missing quotations and citations; correct factual/spelling errors (Goldman, Los Angeles).
Add explicit topic sentences naming the rhetorical strategy in each paragraph.
Tighten the logical link between "no warrant" and "unreliable evidence."
Revise the conclusion so it either commits to a position or, if purely analytical, summarizes your analytical findings rather than passing judgment neutrally.

You clearly understand the case and the arguments well — the next step is sharpening your own authorial stance and making sure every claim is fully supported with cited evidence.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

This essay tackles a difficult and sensitive topic with intellectual seriousness, and your central insight—that opponents of suicide often assert strong claims without empirical support—is a genuinely interesting angle for an argumentative essay. However, the essay needs revision in several areas: clarifying your thesis, tightening the structure, deepening the analysis, and correcting language issues.

Thesis and Purpose

Your opening paragraph is confusing. The claim that "suicide means the end of all problems" followed by "those problems are usually solved easily" seems to contradict itself, and I'm not sure what point you want to make there. More importantly, you announce that you will analyze "those who advocate rational suicide and those who oppose it," but then say you will "only discuss the opponents." This creates a mismatch between your stated framework and your actual essay. Consider revising your introduction so it clearly states: (a) that your essay is not really about whether suicide is right or wrong, but about the *quality of argumentation* used by suicide's opponents, and (b) what your thesis actually claims (eg, "Although opponents of suicide raise legitimate concerns, their arguments remain unpersuasive because they rely on assertion rather than evidence"). This is a valid and interesting thesis—make it explicit early on.

Structure and Repetition

Each body paragraph follows the same pattern: you present a scholar's claim, restate it, and conclude "this is a good point, but there is no evidence." This becomes predictable by the third or fourth repetition. To improve variety and depth:
Vary your critique. Instead of always saying "no evidence provided," sometimes analyze *why* evidence would be hard to obtain, or interrogate the logic itself (as you do nicely with the slippery-slope fallacy in the AIDS section—this paragraph is your strongest because it uses a different analytical tool).
Consider grouping your paragraphs thematically (selfishness, cry for help, family grief, religion, slippery slope) with a brief transition sentence explaining *why* you move from one to the next, rather than relying solely on rhetorical questions to shift topics.

Use of Sources

I noticed several  placeholders where quotations should be. Please ensure these are filled in before submission—without the actual quotes, a reader cannot evaluate whether your paraphrase is accurate or whether the source really does lack evidence.

Also, be cautious about your own credibility as an arguer: you write "assuming that they are two people with experience in dealing with grief" regarding Parks and Weiss. This is a moment where you should have done more research rather than assumed. If you cannot verify a source's credentials, either look them up or acknowledge this limitation more critically (you do address it, which is honest, but it also undermines your own argument if you haven't checked).

Depth of Analysis

Right now, your rebuttals mostly point out the *absence* of evidence rather than actively engaging with the logic of each claim. For instance, in your discussion of Rauscher's "selfishness" argument, you offer a genuinely good counterexample (the cancer patient), but then drop it without connecting it back to your evidence-based thesis. Push further: what *kind* of evidence would satisfy you? Psychological studies? Survivor testimonies? Statistics? Naming the kind of evidence lacking would strengthen your critique and show methodological sophistication.

Conclusion

Your conclusion shifts to a new claim—that we must "learn to understand" suicide—which is not quite the same as your essay's actual argument (that opponents' arguments lack evidence). Revise the conclusion so it circles back to your original thesis, perhaps ending with a call for what future argumentation on this topic *should* include (eg, psychological data, survivor interviews) rather than a broader philosophical statement that shifts your topic.

Language Notes (English/German transfer)

A few recurring issues:
Pronoun ambiguity: "They feel no pity for the person who committed suicide either" — unclear who "they" refers to.
Sentence fragments/comma splices: "In no part of their essay do Ingram and Ellis give credentials for these two people." (correct, but watch for similar constructions elsewhere that drift into fragments).
Word choice: "solved easily" (paragraph 1) is unclear—do you mean "avoided" or "resolved"?
Overuse of "state/states" as a reporting verb; vary with "argue," "contend," "claim," "assert," "maintain" for more natural academic style.

Next Steps

Focus your revision on: (1) sharpening the thesis, (2) filling in missing quotations, (3) varying your critique of each source, and (4) aligning your conclusion with your actual argument. This is a promising draft with a clear analytical angle—refining the execution will make it much stronger.

Feedback Report: Adolescent Suicide Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled a genuinely difficult and important topic, and your effort to connect sociological theory (Durkheim, Marx) to a real-world social problem shows ambition. The structure—moving from statistics to theoretical frameworks—is a reasonable approach. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas: grammar/syntax, argumentative clarity, source integration, and overall coherence. Below I address these systematically.

Grammar and Sentence-Level Issues

As a German speaker, you may be transferring some German sentence structures into English, which creates specific problems here. Some patterns to watch for:

Subject-verb agreement: "the worst problem concerning are young people" should be "concerning young people is"; "the attempts are even more phenomenal" — check number agreement throughout.
Article usage: German and English article rules differ significantly. Phrases like "when its a well known public figure" need "a well known public figure" (you have this one right, but check others like "over any other race").
Word confusion: "contraversal" (controversial), "programed" (programmed), "bondness" (should be "belonging" or "connectedness"), "publically broadcast problem" (should be plural or restructured).
Verb tense inconsistency: You shift between present and past tense when discussing Durkheim's theory ("Durkheim's major theory states" vs. "led him to write"). Keep theoretical claims in present tense (theories "state," they don't "stated") and historical facts in past tense.

Suggestion: Read your essay aloud, or use a grammar-checking tool, then manually verify each correction rather than accepting suggestions blindly, since automated tools sometimes miss nuances specific to academic register.

Structure and Paragraph Organization

Your introduction tries to do too much in run-on sentences. Consider breaking the first paragraph into 2-3 shorter paragraphs: one establishing the problem, one presenting the statistical evidence, one signaling your theoretical approach.

The transition sentence "Thus, relating the social problem..." is grammatically incomplete—it's a fragment functioning as if it were a full sentence. This is a good place to clearly state your thesis: something like "This essay will apply Durkheim's structural functionalist theory and Marx's conflict theory to explain why adolescent suicide occurs, arguing that both perspectives ultimately point to depression as a common underlying mechanism."

Citation and Source Integration

I notice many `<R>` and  placeholders throughout—these need to be filled in with actual citations before submission. More importantly, integrate your sources rhetorically, not just as add-ons. For example:

> "The suicide attempt rates for people under the age of 19 jumped from 50:1 in 1964 to 312:1 in 1984 <R>."

This just states a fact with a citation. Better integration would explain *why* this matters: "This dramatic increase suggests that whatever is driving adolescents to suicidal ideation, they are increasingly failing to complete the act—raising questions about intervention windows that Durkheim's theory might help explain."

Also, the anecdotal reference "a young lady in Rabkin's book Growing Up Dead" needs a direct quote or paraphrase—right now it's a citation with no actual content attached.

Argumentative Development

This is the most important area for revision. Currently, the essay describes Durkheim's and Marx's theories rather than arguing with them. Ask yourself:

Why are these two theories particularly suited to explain adolescent suicide, as opposed to others (eg, psychological theories)?
You dismiss altruistic suicide as inapplicable to adolescents—could you complicate this? Are there any exceptions (eg, adolescents dying for a cause, gang loyalty, family honor)?
Your Marx section explains dialectics but doesn't clearly connect it back to *why* adolescents are more affected. The claim "children and adolescents are the greatest affected by such changes" needs support—why would this be more true for adolescents than adults?
Your concluding move—that depression is the "common ground" between both theories—is your most original insight. Expand this. Does Durkheim see depression as a *cause* of egoistic/anomic suicide, or a *symptom* of social conditions? Is Marx's contribution to this "depression" argument different in kind?

Conclusion

Your final line—"maybe one has to look backwards to better understand their movements forward"—is poetic but vague. Given your essay's content, articulate a more concrete takeaway: What does understanding these theories actually help us *do* about adolescent suicide? Does it suggest particular interventions (eg, addressing isolation, family stability, social change)?

Priority Revisions

Fill in all citations properly.
Write a clear, one-sentence thesis at the end of the intro.
Revise verb tense consistency (theories = present tense).
Strengthen the depression argument as your unifying contribution.
Proofread for subject-verb agreement and word choice.

You have solid raw material here—the next draft should focus on sharpening your argument's edges and cleaning up the prose so your ideas come through clearly.
