Feedback on Your Essay: Water Pollution, Exchange Theory, and Rational Choice Theory

Overall Impression

You have engaged with challenging sociological concepts and connected them to a real-world environmental issue. The essay shows genuine effort to synthesize theory with evidence. However, there are several structural, argumentative, and stylistic issues that need attention before this piece is ready for submission.

Missing Citations — Most Urgent Issue

Throughout the essay, you have left placeholder markers such as  and `<R>` where citations or source material should be. This is the most pressing problem: as it stands, the essay is incomplete. Every statistic (eg, the CDC figures, the 7,700 beach closures, the Milwaukee outbreak) and every theoretical definition needs a proper in-text citation. Please go through the draft systematically and replace every placeholder with the actual reference before resubmitting. Without this, readers cannot evaluate the reliability of your evidence.

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis appears only in the second paragraph ("The focus of this paper is on water pollution and its relationship to...") and is stated descriptively rather than argumentatively. A strong thesis should tell the reader *what you claim*, not just *what you will discuss*. Consider revising to something like: "Water pollution persists not because solutions are unavailable, but because individuals and companies lack the incentives and awareness that Rational Choice Theory and Exchange Theory identify as necessary for behavioral change."

The essay's structure also needs tightening:
The long descriptive section on pollutant categories (infectious agents, organic/inorganic chemicals, point/non-point sources) is informative but disconnected from your theoretical argument. Either connect each category explicitly back to your theories, or trim this section significantly—it currently reads like a separate encyclopedia entry.
The Rational Choice and Exchange Theory sections are strong in places, but they overlap considerably (both use the "personalized, individualized" language) without clearly distinguishing what makes each theory unique. Consider a brief comparison sentence early on clarifying the key difference: Rational Choice emphasizes conscious belief-driven decisions, while Exchange Theory emphasizes reinforcement (reward/cost) without requiring conscious deliberation.

Use of Theory

Your application of theory to real-world behavior (eg, car engine washing, taste of water) is a good instinct, but the reasoning sometimes becomes circular—restating the theory's premise as if it were evidence. For example: "if the actor is deprived of quality water then she will recognize the course that has resulted." This sentence needs unpacking: *how* does deprivation lead to recognition? Push your analysis one step further by explaining the mechanism, not just asserting it.

Conclusion

The Milwaukee AIDS-patient example is powerful, but it appears for the first time in your final paragraph. Strong conclusions should synthesize points already made, not introduce new evidence. Consider moving this example earlier (perhaps into the Exchange Theory section, since it illustrates cost/consequence) and using your conclusion purely to draw connections between your two theories and your overall claim.

Language and Style Notes

Since English is your second language, here are some recurring issues to watch for:

Apostrophes for plurals/possessives: "its'" should be "its"; "ones' behavior" should be "one's behavior." German uses apostrophes differently—English possessive "its" never takes an apostrophe.
Article usage: "the Exchange Theory" is used consistently and correctly, but check phrases like "a societal phenomena" (should be "phenomenon," singular) and "this-water" (typo, remove hyphen).
Word confusion: "then" vs. "than" ("to look at fresh water pollutants, it is best to divide *then*..." should be "them"); "war pollution" is likely a typo for "water pollution."
Redundant phrasing: Sentences like "Rational Choice Theory is directed by personal beliefs and knowledge of a situation" repeat earlier statements almost verbatim. Look for repeated ideas and consolidate them.
Sentence fragments/run-ons: Some sentences are quite long with multiple clauses joined by "and" or "therefore." Consider breaking these into two sentences for clarity, eg, the sentence beginning "Some points to consider: The Centers for Disease Control..."

Suggested Next Steps

Fill in all missing citations.
Write a clearer, argumentative thesis statement and place it at the end of your introduction.
Either cut or explicitly connect the pollutant-classification paragraph to your theoretical framework.
Move the Milwaukee example earlier; use the conclusion to synthesize, not introduce.
Proofread for apostrophe errors, article use, and word confusions (then/than).

You have solid content here—the revision work now is about tightening the argument and ensuring every claim is properly supported and cited.

Feedback Report: Marijuana Legalization Essay

Overall Impression

Structure and Argumentation

Consider narrowing your thesis to 2–3 main arguments and developing them more deeply rather than covering seven topics superficially. A clearer thesis statement—one sentence stating your position and main reasons—would help readers follow your argument.

Your essay is also one-sided. A strong argumentative essay acknowledges counterarguments seriously before refuting them. You mention "opposition" and "negative aspects" but dismiss them quickly (eg, the carcinogen argument is dismissed in one sentence). Strengthening your engagement with the strongest counterarguments (addiction potential, gateway-drug debates, impact on youth, driving impairment) would make your case more persuasive rather than making the essay read as advocacy.

The concluding paragraph introduces a new claim ("not a single death...") rather than synthesizing your argument. Save new evidence for the body; use the conclusion to restate your thesis and synthesize your strongest points.

Use of Sociological Theory

This is the weakest element. You name Structural-Functionalism, Exchange Theory, and Conflict Theory in the introduction, but you don't *define* them before applying them. Readers need a brief explanation of each theory's core assumptions before you use them as analytical lenses.

Additionally, your application is formulaic—almost every section ends with "the rewards outweigh the costs" (Exchange Theory) or references to class conflict. This repetition makes the theoretical analysis feel mechanical rather than analytical. Choose the theory that best fits each specific issue and explain *why* it applies, rather than inserting all three theories into every paragraph. For instance, Conflict Theory fits the class-based prison argument well, but forcing Exchange Theory into the industrial-fabric paragraph feels artificial.

Citations and Sources

The placeholder `<R>` appears throughout instead of actual citations. Before submission, you must:
Replace every `<R>` with a proper citation (APA, MLA, or Chicago—check your instructor's requirement)
Include a complete bibliography/works cited page
Ensure quotations (eg, Grinspoon) are properly cited with page numbers

Uncited statistical claims (eg, "$4–24 billion," "30 billion spent fighting marijuana") are currently unverifiable and will undermine your credibility with an academic reader.

Language and Style

A few patterns to watch for, likely influenced by German:

Spelling/typos: "cannavis" → *cannabis*; "ganga" → *ganja*; "heroine" → *heroin*; "POP" → *PCP*; "win" → *will*; "mar on drugs" → *war on drugs*; "foe" → *for*.

Article usage: English often omits articles German requires, or vice versa (eg, "a Public Health Service spokesman" is correct, but check phrases like "the entire harmony and structure of society").

False friends/word choice: "our government" appears twice—since this is an academic paper, consider "the U.S. government" for a more objective, less nationalistic tone appropriate to argumentative writing.

Register: Phrases like "getting high" and "crazed on pot" are informal; academic writing calls for more neutral phrasing (eg, "under the influence of marijuana").

Sentence fragments/run-ons: Some sentences need revision for clarity, eg, "By legalizing pot the black market would also very soon disappear. Not only would pot be cheaper if it were legal; it would be monitored..." — the semicolon here doesn't connect independent clauses correctly.

Specific Suggestions

Rewrite the introduction to state one clear thesis and preview only your main arguments (perhaps 3, not 7).
Define each theory briefly in a short paragraph before applying it.
Add a counterargument section addressing the strongest opposition claims (eg, health risks, gateway drug theory) before refuting them.
Fix all citations and compile a bibliography.
Proofread carefully for the typos listed above—reading the essay aloud can help catch these.
Revise the conclusion to synthesize existing arguments rather than introduce new evidence.

Final Note

Your essay has real analytical potential and strong research. With tighter focus, deeper theoretical grounding, and more balanced argumentation, this could become a much more convincing piece of academic writing. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further before your revision.

Feedback Report on Homelessness Essay

Overall Impression

You have clearly invested significant research into this essay, and your grasp of sociological theory (particularly conflict theory and symbolic interactionism) is impressive. However, the essay currently reads as several loosely connected sections—a historical overview, a theoretical discussion, and a conclusion—rather than a unified argument. My feedback focuses on strengthening your thesis, improving structural coherence, and addressing some recurring language issues.

Thesis and Argument

Your introduction promises an argument but doesn't clearly state one. You write that "action must be taken immediately," but the essay itself is largely descriptive (history) and expository (theory) rather than argumentative. Ask yourself: what is your actual claim? Possible theses might be:
"Conflict theory offers a more useful framework than symbolic interactionism for understanding *why* homelessness persists, because it addresses structural causes rather than individual perception."
"Homelessness cannot be solved without addressing the labeling processes that both cause and perpetuate it."

Whichever direction you choose, state it explicitly in your introduction and return to it in each section. Currently, the essay's final paragraph introduces a new claim (micro- vs. macro-level analysis) that should have been signaled much earlier and developed throughout.

Structure

The essay has three distinct movements: (1) a history of homelessness in America, (2) causes of homelessness, and (3) application of two sociological theories. Each is reasonably well-developed on its own, but the connections between them are weak. Consider:

Cutting or condensing the historical section. Much of it (paragraphs 2–4) reads as background narrative without analytical payoff. If history is meant to support your argument, you need topic sentences that state *why* each historical period matters to your thesis (eg, "This period established the ‘deserving vs. undeserving poor’ distinction that still shapes policy today").
Integrating causes and theory. Right now, causes of homelessness (paragraphs 7–8) and theoretical explanation (paragraphs 10–17) are separate. Since conflict theory *is* essentially a causal explanation, consider merging these sections so the causes you list (housing costs, deinstitutionalization, discrimination) are explicitly analyzed through the theoretical lens.
The unresolved citation. The placeholder  after the Fantasia and Isserman citation needs to be filled with an actual quotation or paraphrase—this is a significant gap that undermines your source use.

Use of Sociological Theory

This is your strongest material. However:

You introduce "labeling theory" as part of symbolic interactionism but don't fully connect it to your earlier discussion of the "deserving vs. undeserving poor" (which is essentially the same phenomenon, historically). Making this link would unify your historical and theoretical sections.
The comparison between conflict theory and symbolic interactionism in your final paragraph is valuable but arrives too late. Consider setting up this comparison *before* you explain each theory, so the reader knows why both are being discussed.
Be careful with "Contact theorists" (should be "Conflict theorists")—a small typo, but check throughout for consistency in terminology.

Language and Style Notes (common patterns to watch)

As a German speaker, watch for:

Article use: "the ability to pinpoint" is correct, but phrases like "a percentage of the population of the citizens of America" are overloaded—simplify to "a percentage of Americans" or "a portion of the U.S. population."
Nominalization overload: Sentences like "This form of legislation was new, and it brought about the recognition of the actual nature of poverty" could be more direct: "This new legislation revealed the true, systemic nature of poverty."
False friends / word choice: "consent unrest socked endures" (paragraph 11) appears to be a significant error—possibly a mistranslation or typo that obscures meaning entirely. Please revise this sentence.
Redundant intensifiers: Words like "extremely," "immensely," "totally," and "dramatically" appear very frequently. English academic prose favors precision over intensity—try replacing some with specific data or more exact verbs.
Run-on lists without analysis: Sentences such as "The results of these inequities are poverty, homelessness, discrimination, and oppression" state conclusions without demonstrating the causal chain. Show *how* power leads to these outcomes.

Priority Revisions

Write a clear, arguable thesis and place it at the end of your introduction.
Fill in the missing citation.
Cut the historical narrative by roughly half, keeping only what serves your argument.
Merge the "causes" and "theory" sections so each cause is explicitly analyzed through a theoretical lens.
Move your micro/macro comparison earlier to frame the theory discussion.
Proofread for the specific language issues noted above, especially the unclear sentence in paragraph 11.

You have strong content here—the next draft should focus on sharpening the argument and tightening the connections between your historical, causal, and theoretical material.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay: The U.S. Welfare System

Overall Impression

You have tackled a complex, controversial topic with impressive command of factual detail and a clear conceptual framework (contrasting Spencer's Social Darwinism with Merton's Matthew Effect). This is an ambitious structure, and the historical overview at the beginning shows strong research skills. However, the essay needs revision in several areas: citation completeness, argumentative focus, and some language issues typical of English-German transfer.

Citation Gaps (Most Urgent Issue)

Throughout the essay, there are placeholder markers like `<R>` and  where quotations and source citations should appear (eg, the Ann Clark quote, the Aletha Townsend quote, the Bill Bennett quote, the Toby Herr quote). These must be completed before submission. An essay this heavily dependent on statistics and expert testimony absolutely requires full, consistent citations (author, year, page). Right now, a reader cannot verify any of your claims, which undermines your credibility no matter how strong your argument is. Please go through the entire draft and fill in every `<R>` and  with the actual source and quotation.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your introduction poses a good guiding question ("Why does welfare exist?"), but the essay doesn't clearly state *your own position* until the final two paragraphs. For an argumentative essay, readers should know your stance—or at least the shape of your argument—within the first paragraph or two. Consider revising your introduction to preview your conclusion: that welfare recipients face structural barriers (Merton), but that reform, not abolition, is the answer, and that reforms must be realistic and skills-based.

The essay currently reads more like a balanced report than an argument: you present Spencer's view with substantial supporting evidence, then Merton's view with even more supporting evidence, and only briefly synthesize at the end. This is a reasonable structure for exploring both sides, but you should signal earlier that you are *building toward* a rejection of Spencer and a qualified endorsement of Merton/reform. Otherwise, the reader may feel the essay is not "arguing" but merely "surveying" until the last few paragraphs.

Balance and Fairness

You give Spencer's position real weight (the Talent-Faircloth Bill, illegitimacy statistics, Bill Bennett), which is good practice—engaging seriously with the opposing view strengthens your eventual rebuttal. Make sure, though, that your rebuttal of Spencer is equally substantive. Currently the transition ("However, most liberals... disagree with Spencer's theory") moves quickly into Merton's theory without directly refuting Spencer's specific claims (eg, the Aletha Townsend case). Consider explicitly returning to that example later and reinterpreting it through Merton's lens—this would tighten the connection between your two "case studies" and make your argument feel more integrated rather than two parallel halves.

Structure and Paragraphing

Some paragraphs are only one or two sentences (eg, the ones introducing quotations). In academic English, very short paragraphs can feel choppy. Try combining these with the surrounding paragraph, or expand them with your own analytical commentary rather than letting the quotation stand alone. Academic writing expects you to *interpret* evidence, not just present it.

Language Issues (English/German Interference)

A few recurring patterns to watch for:

Typos/spelling: "Me United States" → "the United States"; "deferent" → "different"; "he no useful place" → "has no useful place."
Articles: You are generally strong here, but double-check phrases like "in to a large... program" → "into."
Word choice/false friends: "well-taken" is fine idiomatically, but check phrases like "candidly admit" — perhaps "openly admit" flows better in this context.
Sentence fragments/run-ons: eg, "In 1993, 14.1 million people... received welfare through AFDC. One in ten Americans now receives food stamps." These are fine as separate sentences, but elsewhere you have comma splices — read your sentences aloud to check where a full stop or semicolon is needed instead of a comma.
Repetition of "Thus": You begin many paragraphs with "Thus," which becomes monotonous. Vary your transitions (eg, "Consequently," "As a result," "This suggests that…").

Conclusion

Your ending is thoughtful and appropriately cautious ("complete equality will never exist... hopefully certain reforms can be made"), but it would be stronger if you explicitly returned to your opening question ("Why does welfare exist?") and answered it directly in light of your analysis of Spencer and Merton.

Priority Revisions

Complete all missing citations and quotations.
Add a clear thesis statement early on.
Strengthen the explicit rebuttal of Spencer using Merton's framework.
Combine short paragraphs; vary sentence openings.
Proofread for the typos noted above.

You have done substantial research—now the task is to sharpen the argumentative "spine" that connects it all. Well done overall; this has strong potential with revision.

Feedback on Your Essay on Divorce

Your essay demonstrates a solid grasp of sociological theory and impressive research on divorce statistics and causes. You've organized your discussion around four major theoretical frameworks (symbolic interactionism, functionalism, conflict theory, and exchange theory), which gives the essay a clear structural backbone. Below are areas to strengthen in revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

This is largely a descriptive/expository essay rather than an argumentative one. You present multiple theories and summarize what each says about divorce, but you rarely take a position or argue for one explanation over another. For an argumentative essay, you need a clear thesis that stakes a claim—for example: "While all four theoretical perspectives illuminate aspects of rising divorce rates, symbolic interactionism offers the most compelling explanation because..." Currently, your conclusion simply restates that "we have looked at" various things rather than asserting what you've concluded.

Revision suggestion: Decide on your central argument early, and let each section build toward defending it, rather than treating each theory as a separate, self-contained summary.

Structure and Coherence

The essay reads as four parallel mini-essays (one per theory) followed by four parallel "solutions" sections. This creates repetition without clear connections between sections. Consider adding transitional sentences that explicitly compare/contrast theories, showing how they complement or conflict with one another.
The paragraph beginning "If divorce is widespread, it is a sign..." introduces an important sociological concept (linking personal troubles to social problems) but drops it immediately. This idea deserves development—perhaps as your thesis foundation—rather than a passing mention.
The section on children/custody after divorce feels appended rather than integrated. Clarify how it connects to your main argument.

Citations

You've marked numerous points with `<R>`, indicating missing references. This is critical to fix before submission—an argumentative essay drawing on specific statistics, named studies (Ogburn; Burgess and Locke; Spanier and Thompson), and theoretical claims must cite sources properly (eg, APA or ASA style). Uncited statistics ("two-thirds of all first marriages," "one out of every three white children") will undermine your credibility.

Language and Grammar (common patterns to watch)

Since English is your second language, here are recurring issues to review:

Verb tense consistency: You shift between present and past tense when discussing ongoing sociological patterns. Decide whether you're describing historical findings (past tense: "Ogburn recognized...") or general truths (present tense: "conflict theorists argue...") and stay consistent within each context.

Article usage: German doesn't map directly onto English article rules. Watch for missing/incorrect articles, eg:
   - "may fall even further" → "may fall even further" (fine, but check others)
   - "the medieval society" → "medieval society" (no article needed for general historical periods)
   - "Piety needs to make" — this looks like a typo/autocorrect error for "People" — please proofread carefully.

Prepositions and phrasal verbs: eg, "cannot take for granted of what to expect" → "cannot take for granted what to expect"; "As a couple moves forward divorce" → "As a couple moves toward divorce."

Word choice/false friends: "coning to depend" is likely a typo for "coming to depend." "Fob" should be "job." Please proofread more carefully for typos, as these interrupt readability.

Pronoun agreement: "With then own job" → "With their own job." "A person must decided" → "must decide."

Sentence fragments/run-ons: eg, "It is interesting to see how different theories explain divorce and its consequences" at the very end feels tacked-on and weakens your conclusion's authority.

Content Suggestions

Define "symbolic interactionism," "functionalism," "conflict theory," and "exchange theory" more explicitly for readers unfamiliar with sociological jargon.
The Spanier and Thompson study is mentioned but not explained—what did they conclude, and how does it support your argument?
Your solutions sections are reasonable but generic ("couples need to communicate," "families should be stronger"). Push further: What specific policies, interventions, or social changes would address each theoretical cause you've identified?

Priority Revisions

Develop an explicit, arguable thesis statement.
Add proper citations throughout.
Strengthen transitions between theoretical sections to show argumentative progression, not just listing.
Proofread carefully for typos and grammar patterns noted above.
Sharpen your conclusion to state a clear claim, not merely a summary.

Overall, you have excellent raw material here—strong content knowledge and logical organization. Focusing revision on argumentative clarity and precise language will elevate this from a solid summary to a compelling academic essay.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay: "Running Up the Score"

Your essay tackles an engaging and specific topic, and your knowledge of college football gives you credibility with readers. You clearly have real expertise here, and your enthusiasm for the subject comes through. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas to strengthen its academic quality. Let me walk you through the main issues.

Thesis and Argumentative Structure

Your central claim—that coaches aren't really "guilty" of running up the score—gets lost among many different explanations. Right now you offer at least five separate justifications (second/third string players, running vs. passing plays, Heisman Trophy considerations, job security, and fan satisfaction) without clearly organizing them or showing how they relate to each other. Consider: are these all equally important? Could some be combined? A strong thesis statement early in the essay (perhaps replacing your current opening) should preview these main points so readers know where you're headed.

Suggestion: Try writing one clear sentence: "Although the NCAA criticizes coaches for running up the score, this practice is often unavoidable due to [X], [Y], and [Z]." Then organize your paragraphs around these points in order of importance.

Introduction and Hook

Your opening ("It's fourth down...") is a strong, vivid hook—well done. However, "or do they" as a follow-up sentence is confusing; readers don't yet know what "it" refers to. Consider revising this transition to more smoothly introduce your topic and thesis.

Use of Evidence

The Florida-Carolina example is detailed and effective—this is your strongest paragraph. However, notice that you use this single anecdote to support multiple different claims throughout the essay. Academic writing benefits from *varied* evidence. Do you have statistics on how often teams use bench players when running up scores? Data on Heisman winner correlations with playing time? Quotes from Spurrier, Bowden, or NCAA officials? Adding 2-3 additional sources would substantially strengthen your credibility.

Paragraph Coherence

Some paragraphs shift focus mid-stream. For example, the paragraph beginning "I don't believe..." starts discussing second-string players, then unexpectedly pivots to a placeholder  that seems to represent missing text—please check this and fill in your intended point. Each paragraph should have one clear controlling idea, stated in a topic sentence.

Sentence-Level Issues (Common German-to-English Patterns)

Since English and German differ in certain grammatical structures, watch for these recurring issues:

Comma splices/run-ons: "Florida did score with two seconds left. He wasn't done yet because..." Consider combining or restructuring: "Florida scored with two seconds left, but Spurrier wasn't finished."

Subject-verb agreement: "If one of these top ranked teams are winning" should be "is winning" (singular subject "one").

Article usage: English articles (a/an/the) work differently than German der/die/das. Check phrases like "give the ball and say" or "get first down" (should be "get *a* first down" or "get *the* first down").

Word choice/false friends: "unsurmountable lead" should likely be "insurmountable" or simply "commanding." Also "tables up" (paragraph 6) is unclear—did you mean "adds up" or "boils down"?

Comparative structures: "passing is quicker and more destructive than running" — consider more precise/academic word choice than "destructive" (perhaps "effective" or "explosive").

Tone and Register

Phrases like "getting their butts beat bad," "Steve was a nice guy," and "who could blame him" are conversational and inject personal opinion in ways that weaken academic credibility. For a more formal argumentative essay, consider:
"Carolina was suffering a significant defeat" instead of "getting their butts beat bad"
Removing direct address to the reader ("You may wonder...") in favor of more formal transitions

Conclusion

Your ending returns nicely to the rhetorical question format from your introduction—this creates good symmetry. However, it summarizes rather than synthesizes. Try explicitly connecting your strongest 2-3 pieces of evidence to reinforce why the "not guilty" verdict is justified.

Priority Revisions

For your next draft, focus on:
Writing a clear, specific thesis statement
Reorganizing paragraphs so each addresses ONE main reason, in logical order
Fixing the  placeholder
Proofreading for subject-verb agreement and article usage
Revising conversational phrases for a more academic tone

You have real subject-matter expertise and a genuinely interesting argument—now it's about giving that argument the structural support it deserves. Good work overall!

Feedback on "The Confederate Battle Flag" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling, controversial topic and you write with genuine passion. Your personal connection to Southern heritage adds an authentic voice. However, the essay's central argument becomes confusing by the end, and this needs to be your primary focus in revision.

Argument & Structure

The core problem: Your thesis states the battle flag "should not be flying" over the capitol, but by the final paragraphs, you're arguing that a *different* Confederate flag should fly in its place, accompanied by an explanatory plaque. This is a significant shift that changes your entire argument. Ask yourself: Is your thesis "remove the battle flag entirely" or "replace the battle flag with the historical Confederate flag"? These are two different essays. Decide which claim you're actually defending and restructure accordingly.

Suggestion: If you want to argue for replacement, your introduction needs to signal this from the start. Something like: "While the Confederate battle flag should come down, the true Confederate flag—one that represents heritage rather than warfare—should take its place." This gives your essay a clear, consistent direction.

Redundancy: Several paragraphs repeat the same claim (the battle flag = hatred/racism) without adding new evidence or nuance each time. Paragraphs 4 and 5, for instance, largely restate this point. Consider combining them and using the extra space to develop your strongest argument—the 1964 dating—with more historical detail and a source citation.

Weak transitions: Phrases like "One should not argue that..." are abrupt and slightly unclear in English. Consider more natural connectors: "Some might argue that the battle flag represents heritage rather than hatred. However, this claim overlooks..."

Content & Evidence

Your strongest point is the historical claim that the flag was raised in 1964, linked to the civil rights movement. This is compelling—but it needs a citation. Where did you learn this? Academic writing requires you to support factual claims with sources, especially ones central to your argument. Without a citation, a skeptical reader may doubt or dismiss this claim entirely.

Similarly, your description of the two flags (battle flag vs. "real" Confederate flag) is detailed, but consider whether all these visual details are necessary, or whether one clear sentence per flag would be more efficient, leaving room for historical/political context instead.

Language & Style

Your English is generally strong, but here are patterns to watch:

"Succeeded from the union" → This should be "seceded from the union." This is an easy word to confuse (succeed vs. secede) but changes the meaning significantly.

Repetition of "flag" — This word appears dozens of times. Vary your vocabulary: "the banner," "this symbol," "the emblem," or use pronouns more often once the referent is clear.

Overuse of "I agree/I believe" — In academic argumentative writing, especially in English-language conventions, you can state claims more directly without constantly flagging them as personal opinion: instead of "I agree that the flag is a sign of heritage for some people. I agree because I am one of those people," try: "For many, the flag represents heritage. As someone raised with respect for Southern tradition, I understand this perspective—but heritage does not require this particular flag."

German-influenced sentence structures: Some sentences place information in an order that feels translated rather than natively English. For example: "This 'way of life' was one that was cherished by those who resided in the South" is grammatically correct but slightly stiff—more natural phrasing might be "Southerners cherished this way of life."

Vague pronoun reference: "It holds many different beliefs for different groups" — a flag doesn't hold beliefs; people do. Try: "It carries different meanings for different groups."

Comma splices and run-ons: A few sentences could be tightened. For example: "This was the year that the civil rights movement started. Actually, the flag was set up the day after the civil rights movement started." These two sentences could be combined: "This was the same year the civil rights movement began—in fact, the flag was raised the very day after."

Concrete Next Steps

Clarify your thesis: Choose between "remove entirely" or "replace with historical flag," and make this clear in your introduction and conclusion.

Feedback on "Premarital Sex" Essay

Overall Impression

You tackle an ambitious, controversial topic and clearly have researched it—you cite several sources (Reiss, Gardner, Janus, Schur) and try to build a multi-pronged argument. However, the essay currently reads more like a personal moral statement than an academic argument. To move it toward academic writing, you need to work on (1) argument structure and logic, (2) use of evidence, and (3) language accuracy. Below are concrete suggestions in each area.

Argument and Structure

Your thesis promises three effects of premarital sex: loss of respect for women, "illegitimate communities," and STDs. This is a reasonable structure, but the essay doesn't follow it consistently. The paragraph on the sexual revolution, for instance, doesn't clearly connect to any of the three claims—it reads as background but is placed as if it were an argument itself. Consider adding a clear topic sentence to each paragraph that states which part of the thesis it supports.

More importantly, several claims are asserted rather than argued. Phrases like "It is without any doubt that premarital sex is overall destructive" state your conclusion as a premise. Academic writing requires you to demonstrate *why* a claim is true through evidence and reasoning, not to declare certainty upfront. Similarly, you often move from statistics to causal claims without justifying the link. For example, citing that many people have had multiple partners does not by itself prove that premarital sex "causes" disrespect or family breakdown—these could be correlations, or the causal arrow could run the other way (eg, changing social attitudes may cause both). Academic readers will expect you to acknowledge alternative explanations or at least qualify your causal claims ("this suggests," "this may contribute to," rather than "leads to").

The conclusion introduces a new, unsupported claim—that the solution is "moral and Christian values." This appears out of nowhere; nothing in your body paragraphs argued for Christianity specifically. If this is your real position, you need a paragraph developing that argument with evidence. Otherwise, consider a more neutral policy-oriented conclusion (eg, education, healthcare access) that follows more logically from your body paragraphs on STDs and family structure.

Use of Evidence

You use good sources, but the quotations need better integration:
Introduce each quotation with context (who is the author, what is their expertise/argument) rather than dropping a quote mid-paragraph.
After each statistic, explain what it means for your argument. For instance, the Janus Report percentages (32%, 39%, etc) are presented but not interpreted—tell the reader explicitly why this number matters.
Double-check your citations for accuracy (eg, "Janus 13 5" looks like a typo) and make sure you're using a consistent citation style (MLA, APA, etc) as required by your instructor.
Some evidence is emotionally loaded rather than analytical—for example, the Norman Rockwell family imagery and the "beer commercials" reference are vivid but anecdotal. These can support a point but shouldn't replace actual data or scholarly argument.

Tone

The essay currently uses casual, moralizing, and sometimes humorous language ("wedding night consummations have become post-game shows," "the dog of course," "din-finishing in number"). This undercuts the academic seriousness of your argument. Academic essays on sensitive social topics should maintain an analytical, respectful tone even when arguing a strong position. Try revising for a more formal register throughout.

Language Issues

As a German-speaking writer, watch for these recurring patterns:
False friends / word confusion: "affect" vs. "effect" (you consistently write "affect" for "effect"); "lost" vs. "loss"; "there" vs. "their"; "brazier" vs. "brassiere"; "least" vs. "at least."
Verb forms after modal/gerund constructions: "cut down on have sex" → "cut down on having sex"; "may have make" → "may have made."
Article usage: English requires articles more consistently than German in generic statements (eg, "the birth of illegitimate communities" is unclear—do you mean "illegitimate families"?).
Word order and idiomatic phrasing: Some sentences show German syntax patterns (eg, placement of adverbs). Reading sentences aloud can help you catch these.
Proofreading: There are several typos ("din-finishing," "relies" for likely "relationships," "contacted" for "contracted") that suggest the essay needs a careful final proofreading pass, ideally with a spell-checker and a native-speaker reader.

Priority Revisions

Rewrite the thesis and add clear topic sentences linking each paragraph back to it.
Remove or support the "Christian values" conclusion.
Soften causal language; acknowledge complexity.
Integrate quotations with signal phrases and analysis.
Do a dedicated proofreading pass for grammar/spelling.

You have real analytical instincts and good source material—tightening the logic and language will make this a much stronger academic essay.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely controversial and interesting question, and your voice comes through clearly — you write with conviction. However, the essay currently reads more like a personal reflection than an academic argument. To strengthen it, you need clearer structure, textual evidence, and more precise, consistent terminology. Below are specific areas to revise.

Repetition and Structure

The second paragraph contains a duplicated passage — the sentence about a woman's "intellectual capabilities" and "physical weakness" appears twice, almost word-for-word. This is likely a drafting error, but it signals a broader issue: the essay's structure needs tightening. Each paragraph should advance a *new* point rather than circling back to restate earlier claims (as happens again in the conclusion, which largely repeats the introduction rather than synthesizing your argument). Consider outlining your essay with one clear claim per paragraph:
Paragraph 1: Thesis
Paragraph 2: Physical standards argument
Paragraph 3: Philosophical claim about "difference vs. deficiency"
Paragraph 4: Emotional makeup argument
Paragraph 5: Conclusion — synthesis, not repetition

Argumentation and Evidence

This is the essay's biggest weakness for academic purposes. Your claims — that standards are "lowered," that women are the "weaker sex," that emotional differences would be a "serious impediment" in combat — are presented as self-evident truths, but an academic reader will expect evidence: studies on physical standards in the military, data on women in combat roles (many countries already permit this), or expert testimony. Right now the argument rests on assertion and personal feeling ("I love having feminine characteristics," "I have a difficult time trying to understand a woman who wants...").  Personal conviction is a legitimate starting point, but in an argumentative essay it must be supported, not simply repeated with more intensity.

I'd also encourage you to engage with the counterargument more rigorously. You gesture toward it ("The intentions seem honorable on the surface") but don't seriously grapple with, for example, the fact that women already serve in combat in several militaries, or that "physical standards" arguments have historically been used to exclude many groups. Addressing the strongest version of the opposing view — and then explaining why you still find your position more persuasive — will make your essay far more convincing.

Terminology and Loaded Language

Phrases like "the weaker sex" need to be used carefully. If you want to make a claim about average physical differences between men and women, phrase it in more precise, defensible terms (eg, "average differences in upper-body strength") rather than a phrase that sounds like an unexamined stereotype. This will make your argument seem more rigorous rather than reliant on cliché.

Language and Grammar (specific to English/German transfer)

A few recurring patterns to watch for:

Subject–verb agreement: "the feminists that support this equality is" → should be "are." Because "feminists" is plural, the verb must agree with it, not with the singular noun "equality." This kind of agreement error across long noun phrases is common for German speakers, since German verb agreement often follows different word-order cues.
Comma splices / run-on sentences: Many sentences are joined only by commas where English requires a period, semicolon, or conjunction (eg, "...weaker sex. and goes into the military..."). Reading your sentences aloud can help you hear where one complete thought ends and another begins.
Article usage: occasional missing or unnecessary articles ("create equality among the sexes" vs. "create an equal environment") — a common challenge since German article rules differ from English ones. Proofread specifically for "a/an/the" as a separate pass.
Word choice: "intentional purposeful created differences" is a bit redundant — try "intentional, meaningful differences."

Tone

For an academic argumentative essay, aim for a more measured, analytical tone even when discussing a topic you feel strongly about. Rhetorical questions ("Why not accept the differences...?") are effective in moderation but overused here; consider turning some of them into direct claims supported by reasoning.

Suggestions for Revision

Remove the duplicated passage in paragraph 2.
Add at least two or three concrete pieces of evidence (statistics, historical examples, expert opinion) to support your central claims.
Rewrite the conclusion so it synthesizes your argument rather than repeating the introduction.
Do a dedicated grammar pass focused on subject-verb agreement and comma splices.
Reconsider emotionally loaded phrases and replace them with more precise, defensible language.

You have a clear position and are clearly capable of sustained, passionate argument — now channel that energy into a more evidence-based, tightly structured essay.

Your essay tackles a complex and important topic, and you draw on relevant sources (Farrell, Masters, Friedan, Poilit) to support your points. However, there are several areas—concerning argument, structure, and language—that need revision before this essay is ready for submission.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay tackles an interesting and relatable topic—the tension between residence hall policies and student independence. You clearly have personal investment in this issue, and your specific examples (the roommate contract, the visitation hours, the quiet hours policy) show you know your subject well. Below are suggestions to help you strengthen the essay for revision.

Overall Structure and Argument

Your essay would benefit from a clearer thesis statement early on. Right now, the introduction meanders through several related ideas (maturation, rules, resident halls) before settling on your main argument. Consider stating explicitly in your first paragraph: something like "Resident hall policies, particularly visitation rules and quiet hours, undermine the very independence college is supposed to foster." This gives your reader a roadmap.

Each body paragraph does focus on one issue (visitation policy, gender/discrimination, quiet hours), which is good organizational instinct. However, within paragraphs, ideas sometimes drift. For example, in your visitation paragraph, you move between the policy itself, hypothetical scenarios, and emotional appeals about guests driving tired. Consider using topic sentences that clearly announce each paragraph's focus, and then stay tightly focused on developing that one point with evidence before moving to emotional or hypothetical elaboration.

Use of Evidence

Your strongest moments are when you cite specific policies (the USC visitation hours, the roommate contract process). These give your argument credibility. I'd encourage you to:
Find and cite actual statistics or quotes from university policy documents to strengthen your claims
Consider counterarguments more systematically—you raise the safety rationale ("policies are to protect residents") but then move past it quickly. Engaging more deeply with *why* institutions created these rules, and then refuting that reasoning specifically, would make your argument more persuasive.

Tone and Rhetorical Choices

You use second-person address ("you feel," "you know") throughout, which creates an informal, conversational tone. This can be effective for connecting with readers, but in academic writing, especially argumentative essays, it can undermine your credibility. Consider:
Shifting some "you" statements to third person ("students feel...") or first person plural ("we")
Reducing rhetorical questions (you have several: "What is too loud?" "How are you supposed to feel at home...?"). A few are effective for emphasis, but many in a row can weaken your argument by seeming to ask rather than assert.
The phrase "That's ridiculous!" and similar exclamations feel more appropriate for spoken argument than formal writing. Consider replacing these with reasoned statements that let your evidence make the point.

Grammar and Language (Common Patterns for German L1 Speakers)

Since English and German article and preposition systems differ significantly, here are patterns to watch for:

Subject-verb agreement: "guest have to be" → should be "guests have to be" (check plural nouns throughout—you drop the -s on "guest" repeatedly)

Articles: "Men are rape also" → this needs "Men are raped also" (verb form) and generally check where English requires "a/the" that German might not

Word confusion: "there opinions" should be "their opinions"; "your loudest hours" — check "your" vs. "the" usage for general statements

Sentence fragments/run-ons: Some sentences need clearer connection, eg, "With all of the rules and regulations it has makes it hard..." — this sentence has a grammar issue (subject unclear); consider: "The many rules and regulations make it hard..."

Comparative structures: "worse than your parents" should likely be "worse than your parents' rules" for clarity

Specific Suggestions

Introduction: Cut the meandering opening and get to your thesis faster
Conclusion: Your final paragraph raises good points (the "maturing station" vs. "party school" tension) but introduces new ideas rather than synthesizing what you've argued. Consider revising to summarize your three main points and end with a clear call to action (what should universities actually do?)
Paragraph on discrimination: This is your most original and thought-provoking section. Consider expanding it and moving it earlier, as it's your strongest argument
Proofread carefully for the subject-verb agreement issues noted above—reading your essay aloud can help catch these

Final Thoughts

You have a genuine argument here with real stakes for students. Focus your revision on: (1) sharpening your thesis, (2) maintaining paragraph focus, (3) reducing informal address and rhetorical questions in favor of assertive claims, and (4) careful proofreading for subject-verb agreement. These changes will help your strong ideas shine through more clearly.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay: College Football Playoff System

Overall Impression

You've chosen a strong, debatable topic and you clearly know the subject matter in impressive detail. Your case studies from 1992-93, 1993-94, and 1994 are well-chosen and effectively illustrate the recurring problem with the bowl system. However, the essay currently reads more like a chronicle of controversies than a tightly argued essay. Below are suggestions to help you sharpen the argument and clean up language issues.

Structure and Argumentation

Balance narrative and analysis. Roughly 70% of your essay is devoted to recounting three seasons' controversies. While these examples are valuable evidence, you need more analytical commentary connecting them explicitly to your three stated benefits (revenue, excitement, fairness). Right now, the connection is implicit—make it explicit. After each case study, add a sentence or two like: "This controversy demonstrates precisely why a playoff is necessary: no poll or computer ranking can substitute for teams settling the question on the field."

The counterargument section is too thin. You raise opposing views (bowl tradition, season length, financial concerns) in one short paragraph near the end, then dismiss them in a single sentence ("But which ever way you look at it..."). This weakens your credibility. Devote at least one full paragraph to genuinely engaging with the strongest counterargument—likely the season-length/student-athlete welfare concern raised by Bowden—and explain concretely why your 16-team, 4-week model addresses it.

Your proposal needs more development. The concrete plan (16 teams, bowls as neutral sites, 4 weeks) is one of your most original contributions, but it's compressed into one short paragraph. Consider moving this earlier, perhaps right after your thesis, so readers know your solution before wading through three seasons of examples. Then use the case studies to test/validate this specific model: eg, "Under this system, the 1993 debate between Florida State and Alabama would have been resolved because both teams would have earned playoff berths."

Strengthen your conclusion. Your final paragraph restates the thesis but doesn't synthesize the evidence or leave the reader with a forward-looking thought. Consider ending with a specific prediction or call to action rather than "college football will probably have to resort to..."

Language and Style Issues

Since English is your second language, here are recurring patterns to watch for:

Article usage: "the college football" vs. "college football" — check when definite articles are needed (a persistent issue for German speakers, since German uses articles differently). Example: "the 1992-93 college football season" is correct, but "college football also hoped" (no article) is also correct—the difference is definiteness.

Verb tense consistency: You shift between past and present tense when discussing the same events (eg, "Nebraska is in the Big Eight Conference" vs. "Nebraska was playing Miami"). Since these are historical events, maintain past tense throughout the narrative sections.

Word choice errors: 
  - "there was always some debated" → "some debate"
  - "couldn't really Florida State ahead" → missing verb, likely "rank"
  - "going to going to the Cotton Bowl" → repeated phrase, delete one
  - "installed a bowl alliance" → "established" or "created" fits better
  - "played liked chunky soup" → likely meant "played like chunky soup," but clarify with context since this is a colloquial insult (soup = weak/watery)

Sentence fragments: "Both teams won their bowl games on." — incomplete sentence, remove "on" or add missing information.

Comma splices: Several instances, eg, "Florida State won the title outright, but their was some controversy..." (also note "their" should be "there").

Quotation formatting: Your quotes have formatting issues (the "11" appears to be a typo/OCR error for quotation marks). Proofread all quoted material carefully and ensure citations are consistent (you have (18), (19), (C3), (C6) — clarify what these refer to in a Works Cited list).

Specific Recommendations

Add topic sentences that explicitly link each case study back to your thesis's three benefits.
Move your proposed playoff structure earlier in the essay.
Expand the counterargument paragraph and respond to it more substantively.
Proofread carefully for tense consistency, articles, and sentence completeness—reading the essay aloud can help you catch these.
Add a Works Cited page or at least clarify your citation system.

Final Note

Your command of complex subject matter and your ability to construct a sustained argument across multiple case studies is genuinely strong. With tighter organization and careful proofreading, this essay could be very persuasive. Focus your revision on making the analytical thread more visible—don't just show us the controversies; keep telling us *why* they prove your point.

Feedback on Your Go-Cart Safety Essay

Your essay has real strengths worth acknowledging first. You write with authority and firsthand experience, and your personal anecdotes (getting hit by a go-cart, watching kids get hurt) make abstract safety rules feel urgent and real. This is exactly the kind of concrete, experience-based writing that makes an argument persuasive. Below are suggestions to help you strengthen the piece further.

Introduction and Thesis

Your opening sentence is a bit apologetic ("I don't know how to express...") and buries your main point. Consider starting more directly with your authority and your claim. Something like: "Having worked at Frankie's Fun Park for a year and a half, I have seen firsthand what happens when people ignore go-cart safety rules." This immediately establishes your credibility and sets up your argument more confidently. Save the emotional appeal for later, after you've earned the reader's trust with your expertise.

Organization

Right now, your essay moves rule-by-rule (seatbelts, gas/brake, bumping, wrecks, pit entry), which is a logical structure. However, each paragraph would be stronger with a clearer topic sentence stating the rule and its purpose *before* you launch into examples. For instance, your bumping paragraph is your strongest and most detailed—consider whether it should come earlier, since it seems to be your most important point.

Also consider adding brief transitions between paragraphs ("In addition to seatbelts, another critical safety measure involves...") to help the reader move smoothly from one rule to the next, rather than each paragraph starting fresh.

Grammar and Language (Common German-to-English Transfer Issues)

A few recurring issues to address in revision:

Word order/verb placement: "The seatbelts shouldn't be taking off during the race" → should be "should not be taken off." Also check "When we start the annoucements" (spelling: *announcements*).
"their" vs. "there": "The bumpers are not their for people" → should be "there." This is a common mix-up, so proofread carefully for it.
Subject-verb agreement: "Having all these rules are for the safety" → should be "Having all these rules is for the safety" (gerund phrases take singular verbs).
Run-on sentences: Several sentences are joined with commas where a period or semicolon is needed. Example: "I was even lucky that when I got hit, I wasn't seriously hurt, I just bruised my arm real bad." This should be split into two sentences or use a semicolon before "I just bruised."
"you got to remember" is very informal/colloquial. For an argumentative essay, consider "you must remember" or "it is important to remember."
Article usage: German doesn't always map directly onto English article rules. Watch for missing or unnecessary articles, eg, "coming in the pit" (correct) vs. "riding on the track" (also fine)—but double-check phrases like "the workers are at great risk" for consistency throughout.

Tone and Audience

You shift between direct address ("you") and general statements about "people," which is fine for a persuasive piece, but be consistent. Decide whether you're writing directly to future riders (like a safety pamphlet) or writing an analytical essay *about* rule-following for an academic audience. Right now it reads like the former, which is engaging, but if this is for an academic assignment, you may want to frame it slightly more formally—for example, reducing phrases like "Believe me" and "you got to remember."

Strengthening the Argument

Your examples are vivid, but consider adding just one or two more concrete numbers or details to increase persuasive weight—you already do this well with "thirty-eight cars" and "twenty-five miles per hour." Look for opportunities to add similarly specific details elsewhere (eg, how many people you've seen injured, how often accidents happen per week).

Your conclusion is warm and appropriate, tying safety back to family fun. Consider ending with a stronger final sentence—something that echoes your opening line about the importance of listening, to create a sense of closure.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Revise the introduction for a more confident opening.
Add clear topic sentences to each paragraph.
Proofread carefully for *there/their*, subject-verb agreement, and run-on sentences.
Replace overly colloquial phrases ("you got to") with more formal alternatives.
Consider reordering paragraphs to lead with your strongest point (bumping).

You have a genuinely compelling personal voice and real expertise—the revision process should focus on tightening the language mechanics so your strong content shines through clearly. Keep going!

Feedback on Your Essay: Susan Smith Case Analysis

Thank you for sharing this draft. You have chosen a compelling case and clearly feel strongly about your position. Below are suggestions to strengthen the essay's argumentative structure, evidence, and clarity.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your introduction promises to evaluate "the justness of the sentence," but the essay gradually shifts into a personal reflection on religious forgiveness. This is an interesting angle, but as an *argumentative* essay, you need a single, clear thesis that you defend with evidence throughout. Consider deciding: is your central claim (a) that the sentence was legally/morally appropriate given her psychological history, or (b) that religious forgiveness matters more than earthly punishment? Right now both arguments are present but not integrated. I recommend narrowing to one main claim and using the other idea as supporting context.

Factual Accuracy

You state that "the Supreme Court did not impose the death penalty" and later that "the jury made the right decision." This is inconsistent—was it a jury or a court? In the actual case, a jury recommended the sentence. Please verify and correct this throughout, as factual precision is essential for credibility in academic writing.

Evidence and Sourcing

Several claims need citation:
The psychiatrist's testimony about her depression—cite where this information comes from (news report, trial transcript, etc).
Biographical details about her childhood and the affair—same requirement.
The Bible quotations are marked with placeholders () rather than actual text. Be sure to include the full quotations with proper citation (book, chapter, verse) before submitting.

Academic essays require you to show *where* your claims come from, especially when writing about a real legal case.

Unsupported Speculation

Several passages present your imagination of Smith's inner life as fact:
> "She will always live with a hideous picture etched in the back of her mind..."
> "Every morning she will wake up and see herself as a cold-hearted murderer..."

These are vivid, but they are speculative claims about someone else's psychological experience, not verifiable facts. Consider using hedging language ("she may," "it is likely that") or framing these as rhetorical effect rather than assertion.

Similarly, "Death would have been quick, easy, and more economical" needs support—economical compared to what? Cost of life imprisonment vs. execution is actually a debated empirical question with a real answer (life imprisonment is generally *cheaper* than the appeals process for death penalty cases)—you might look this up to make your point more precise and correct.

Structure and Paragraphing

Some paragraphs combine multiple ideas (biography, then a return to the crime, then a return to biography). Try to organize by function:
Paragraph 1: Background of the crime
Paragraph 2: Psychological/biographical explanation
Paragraph 3: Argument for why the sentence fits (legal reasoning)
Paragraph 4: Counterargument (why some believe death penalty was warranted) + your rebuttal
Paragraph 5: Broader reflection (religious perspective, conclusion)

Adding a paragraph addressing the opposing view (why many were angry she avoided execution) before you rebut it would strengthen your argumentative credibility—right now you only briefly mention "shocked" reactions without engaging them.

Audience and the Religious Argument

Your religious reasoning is heartfelt, but consider your audience: an academic argumentative essay is usually read by people of varied beliefs. If you want the forgiveness argument to be persuasive to non-Christian readers too, you might frame it more as "many ethical and religious traditions distinguish between legal punishment and personal redemption" rather than relying solely on Christian doctrine as proof. Alternatively, if this is meant as a personal/reflective essay from a Christian perspective, state that framing explicitly in your introduction so the reader knows what kind of argument to expect.

Language Notes (typical German-English transfer issues)

"recieve" → *receive*; "prision" → *prison* — proofread carefully for spelling.
"endowed with logical mental coherence" — this phrase is very formal/abstract; simpler phrasing ("her behavior was not rational" or "did not follow a coherent logic") will read more naturally in English academic style.
Watch redundant repetition: "Her boyfriend dumped her" appears twice in nearly the same words—combine into one sentence.
German-style capitalization of pronouns referring to God ("He," "Him") is a stylistic choice acceptable in religious writing, but be consistent throughout.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Sharpen and maintain one clear thesis.
Fix factual inconsistency (jury vs. Supreme Court).
Add citations for all biographical/testimonial claims and complete Bible quotations.
Reduce speculative claims about Smith's inner life; use hedged language.
Add a counterargument paragraph.
Proofread for spelling and revise overly abstract phrasing.

You have strong instincts for using narrative detail to build emotional appeal—now pair that with more rigorous evidence and structure to make the argument academically persuasive. Well done overall; this revision will bring it to the next level.

Citations and Source Credibility (Most Urgent Issue)

This is the area needing the most attention. Several of your citations are problematic:

"Rick Finley@rfinleygmail.vt.edu" and similar entries appear to be personal email addresses, not credible academic sources. These need to be replaced with peer-reviewed studies, government reports, or reputable organizations.
"nttp.www.kfu.com/nsayer" appears to be a broken or informal URL—this is not an acceptable academic citation.
You cite "US Department of Justice 3-62" and "Newman 506" but never explain what these sources are (a book? a report? a journal article?) or introduce the authors properly.

Recommendation: Use a consistent citation style (APA or MLA, as your instructor requires). Every source should be a recognizable, retrievable document—a journal article, government report, or book chapter. Before submitting, verify that each source actually exists and says what you claim it says.

Logical Gaps and Unsupported Claims

Several claims need stronger evidentiary support or more careful reasoning:

You assert that lowering the drinking age would make 18-year-olds "act responsibly" and that drinking would become "normal and ordinary" rather than "glamorous." This is a debatable psychological claim—you need evidence (perhaps comparative data from other countries, as you gesture toward with Canada) rather than assertion.
The claim that binge drinking happens *because* alcohol is scarce is an interesting causal argument, but it needs a source. Currently it reads as speculation.
The military/contract argument ("if they can be drafted, they should drink") is a common argument in this debate, but you should acknowledge the counterargument: opponents argue that brain development research shows 18-21 year-olds have different risk profiles than older adults. Engaging with the opposing view—even briefly—would strengthen your credibility.

Language and Grammar (German-to-English Interference)

A few recurring issues:

Apostrophes with plurals: You repeatedly write "eighteen-year old's," "old's," "candidates for reform programs are under" etc In English, plural nouns do NOT take an apostrophe: "eighteen-year-olds," "18-year-olds." The apostrophe is only for possession or contraction.
Hyphenation: "eighteen-year-old" should be hyphenated when used as an adjective (eg, "eighteen-year-old students"), but not when used as a plural noun ("eighteen-year-olds").
Word confusion: "effect" vs. "affect" (you wrote "Lowering drinking age... would effect the United States"—this should be "affect," since you mean "influence," not "produce a result").
Missing capitalization: After periods, some sentences start lowercase ("on most college campuses...").
"amoung" should be "among" (final paragraph).
Run-on and choppy sentences: Many sentences are short and disconnected. Try combining related ideas with subordinating conjunctions (because, although, since) to show relationships between ideas rather than just listing them side by side.

Suggestions for Revision

Rewrite your source list first. Find 4-5 credible, citable sources (CDC, NIAAA, peer-reviewed journals) and rebuild your evidence around them.
Add a counterargument paragraph. Acknowledge opponents' concerns (eg, brain development, drunk-driving statistics) and respond to them. This will make your argument feel more balanced and persuasive.
Tighten each paragraph to one main idea, with a clear topic sentence and 2-3 pieces of evidence.
Proofread for the apostrophe/plural issue—this appears many times and is easily fixed once you're aware of it.
Strengthen the conclusion by adding a forward-looking statement rather than repeating the introduction.

Overall

Your argument has real potential—you've identified genuine issues (crime, glamorization, health) that scholars debate on this topic. Focus your revision on sourcing credible evidence and engaging with counterarguments; this will elevate the essay from a persuasive opinion piece to a well-supported academic argument.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling topic and your narrative opening is genuinely effective—it draws the reader in and creates emotional investment before you present your argument. This is a sophisticated rhetorical strategy. However, the essay needs revision in several areas: paragraph structure, argument development, evidence, and language accuracy. Below are my main suggestions.

Structure and Organization

The thesis needs to be clearer and appear earlier. Currently, your main claim ("swimming should be mandatory in P.E.") doesn't fully emerge until paragraph 3. Consider adding a brief transitional sentence at the end of paragraph 2 that explicitly states your thesis, so readers know exactly what you're arguing before you develop your reasoning.

Paragraph 3 tries to do too much. It introduces the proposal, explains the school system logistics, and makes a claim about the topic being under-discussed—all before returning to why swimming matters. Consider splitting this: one paragraph to state your proposal clearly, and a separate paragraph for supporting reasons (safety benefits, life-skill value).

The essay lacks a counterargument. A strong argumentative essay anticipates objections. What would critics say? Perhaps: cost of implementation, liability concerns, students with disabilities, or time taken from other curriculum. Addressing at least one objection would strengthen your credibility significantly.

Evidence and Support

This is the essay's weakest area. You make several strong claims without support:
"This is a common story in the summer time" — Can you cite drowning statistics (CDC, WHO, etc)?
"In as little as four weeks a swim instructor can teach someone... how to swim" — Where does this figure come from?
"Many school systems have pools that are under-used" — This needs a source or concrete example.

Suggestion: Even one or two statistics (eg, "Drowning is the leading cause of accidental death for children ages 1-4 in the U.S.") would transform this from an appeal to emotion into a persuasive, evidence-based argument. Your opening anecdote is a good emotional hook, but academic argumentation needs facts to back it up.

Language and Mechanics

Since English is your second language, let's focus on patterns rather than every individual error:

Capitalization inconsistency: "the News" should be "the news" (not a proper noun here).

Spelling errors (likely typos, but proofread carefully):
   - "cericulum" → curriculum
   - "instuctor/instuctors" → instructor/instructors
   - "wuold" → would
   - "thaech" → teach
   - "drownding" → drowning (appears twice)
   - "panicing" → panicking
   - "simlpe" → simple
   - "remidied" → remedied
   - "evryone" → everyone
   - "employent" → employment

Comma splices/run-ons: eg, "You do not even think twice about it and you go put on your swim trunks..." — consider breaking into shorter sentences for clarity.

Missing periods/capitalization after periods: "This is not uncommon, most of your friends..." should likely be two sentences, or use a semicolon.

Article usage: "It would give evryone the chance... It is skill that could save..." — should be "a skill." German doesn't always require articles the same way English does, so double-check noun phrases for missing "a/an/the."

Word choice: "profits" for public pools might be better as "revenue" in this context, since many public pools are non-profit or municipal.

Style Suggestions

Avoid addressing the reader as "you" throughout the analytical sections (paragraphs 3 onward). The second-person narrative works well in your opening anecdote, but switching to more formal, general language ("students," "many people," "young swimmers") in the argumentative body paragraphs will sound more academic.

The line "How often have you heard of dodgeball saving someone's life?" is a nice rhetorical touch, but in a formal essay, consider whether informal humor fits your tone. You could keep the point but phrase it more formally: "Unlike other common P.E. activities such as dodgeball, swimming offers a life-saving skill."

Priority Revisions

If you only have time for three changes, focus on:
Add statistics/evidence to support your key claims.
Add a counterargument paragraph addressing at least one objection.
Proofread for spelling using a spell-checker, and fix comma splices.

Your instinct for storytelling and structuring a persuasive case is strong—now it's about grounding that instinct in evidence and polishing the technical execution. Good work overall!

Feedback on Your Essay: Gender Identity

Overall Impression
You've chosen an ambitious and socially relevant topic, and your essay shows a clear line of argumentation: distinct gender identities cause psychological harm and are increasingly impractical in modern life (single parenthood, changing societal roles). This is a solid foundation. However, the essay needs revision in several areas: source integration, sentence-level grammar, and precision of key terms.

Content and Argumentation

Define your key term earlier and more sharply. You acknowledge that "distinct gender identity" is not a common phrase, but your explanation in paragraph 2 is vague (partly due to the placeholder brackets, which I assume mark missing quotations). Give the reader a working definition in your introduction, not just in paragraph 2. Right now, readers must wait too long to understand exactly what you're arguing against.

Strengthen the logical connections between evidence and claims. Some of your causal statements are too absolute. For example: "by abolishing the distinct gender identity, there would be no psychological impact." This is an overstatement—removing gender norms would likely *reduce*, not eliminate, psychological strain. Similarly, in the conclusion, you assert that distinct gender identities "have proven themselves to be useless and harmful," which is stronger than what your evidence (a few examples and expert citations) can support. Consider qualifying these claims ("can be harmful," "often creates pressure") to make your argument more credible.

Watch for unsupported generalizations. Phrases like "traditionalists have stereotyped" and "these women were led to believe" need either a source or must be explicitly framed as commonly known assumptions. Right now, they read as assertions rather than argued points.

The single-parent paragraph is your strongest. It's concrete, relatable, and directly supports your thesis. Consider moving it earlier or using it as a model for how to develop your other paragraphs (Friedan, Farrell) with similarly specific, vivid detail rather than paraphrase alone.

Structure

Your paragraph organization is logical: definition → tradition/psychology → female perspective (Friedan) → male perspective (Farrell) → real-world consequence (single parents) → conclusion. This works well. To improve flow, add clearer transitional sentences at the start of each paragraph that explicitly link back to your thesis (eg, "This pressure to conform to a single identity is not only outdated but psychologically damaging, as the following examples show.").

Your conclusion summarizes but doesn't synthesize. Instead of repeating points from body paragraphs almost verbatim, try to draw a final insight—what should change in practice? What would a "post-distinct-gender" society look like?

Source Integration

This is the area needing most attention. Right now, quotations appear to be missing (marked with  and `<R>`), so I can't evaluate your actual use of sources, but based on the surrounding text:

Always introduce quotations with a signal phrase that tells the reader who is speaking and why the quote matters (you do this well with "Betty Friedan, a feminist organizer and writer, states that...").
Avoid dropped quotations—don't just insert a quote without grammatically integrating it into your sentence.
After a quotation, add a sentence of analysis, not just paraphrase. For example, after the Farrell quotation about Ralph, explain *why* this story illustrates psychological burden—don't just restate the plot.
Make sure all sources are cited according to your required format (MLA, APA, etc).

Language and Grammar (German-to-English Interference)

Since German capitalization and punctuation rules differ from English, watch for these patterns:

Sentence fragments after periods, lowercase start: "if these traits were abolished..." and "it would be ridiculous to think..." — these look like new sentences beginning with a lowercase letter. In English, always capitalize the first word of a sentence.

Comma splices: "Today, however, some women are thought to possess male gender qualities and some men are thought to possess female gender qualities" is fine, but elsewhere you join independent clauses with commas where a period or semicolon is needed. Check especially in the single-parent paragraph.

Spelling: "dependant" → "dependent" (English reserves "dependant" mainly for British legal/tax contexts; standard adjective is "dependent").

Word repetition: "distinct gender identity/identities" appears over 15 times. Vary your phrasing: "rigid gender roles," "traditional gender categories," "fixed gender expectations."

Article use: Check phrases like "on order to succeed" (likely a typo for "in order to").

Priority Revisions
Define your key term clearly and early.
Restore/integrate your quotations properly with signal phrases and analysis.
Fix capitalization and sentence-boundary errors.
Soften overly absolute claims.
Vary repeated vocabulary.

You have a clear argumentative voice and good examples—now it's a matter of tightening the logic and polishing the mechanics. I'd be happy to look at a revised draft.
