Feedback Report: Beauty Standards Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling, socially relevant topic, and your passion comes through clearly. The essay has a strong emotional core and makes a legitimate argument about the harms of media-driven beauty standards. However, the essay needs work in three main areas: source documentation, structural organization, and language precision. Below are specific suggestions for revision.

Content and Argument

Strengths: Your central claim—that societal beauty standards damage women's physical and mental health—is important and well-supported by real phenomena (eating disorders, cosmetic surgery risks, workplace discrimination). The comparison between average women's measurements, models' measurements, and a "realistic Barbie" is a genuinely effective rhetorical device.

Needs improvement: The essay currently reads as a list of related but separate problems (models' unrealistic bodies → eating disorders → plastic surgery → beauty product marketing → obesity discrimination) rather than a single, developing argument. Consider choosing a narrower thesis—for example, focus specifically on *how media creates unrealistic standards that lead to disordered eating*—and develop that one line of reasoning in depth, rather than surveying five loosely connected issues in a short essay.

Source Documentation (Priority Issue)

This is the most serious problem in the essay. You cite specific statistics (95% of anorexia cases, average model measurements, income gaps for obese women, etc) with no citations whatsoever. In academic writing, especially in English-language contexts, this is considered a serious flaw—readers cannot verify your claims, and using statistics without attribution can even raise concerns about plagiarism, even if unintentional. 

Action: For every statistic, add a source (author, publication, year). If you don't remember where you found these numbers, you must find them again or remove the claim. This is not optional for a revised draft.

Structure and Paragraphing

Your introduction is strong—the celebrity name-list is an engaging hook. However, the transition into your thesis ("The 1990's base these standards...") is somewhat abrupt and grammatically unclear. Revise this sentence for clarity: do you mean *"1990s culture imposes these standards"*?
Several paragraphs contain multiple sub-topics (eg, the paragraph combining anorexia, bulimia, plastic surgery, liposuction, *and* beauty products). Consider splitting this into two or three paragraphs, each with a clear topic sentence and focused evidence.
The paragraph on obese women and workplace discrimination feels somewhat disconnected from the rest of the essay—it's a valid point, but it shifts from *models causing insecurity* to *weight-based discrimination*, which is a related but distinct argument. Either connect it explicitly back to your thesis or consider whether it belongs in this essay at all.
Your conclusion has a nice, hopeful, empowering tone, but it relies heavily on rhetorical questions ("Do we have to go through the torment...?" "I think not."). Used sparingly, rhetorical questions are effective; used repeatedly, they can feel more like a speech than an academic essay. Consider replacing at least half of these with direct statements.

Language and Style

As you continue developing academic English, watch for these recurring issues:

Spelling: *Cambell* → Campbell; *adolecense* → adolescence; *enwnel* → enamel. Careful proofreading (or spell-check) will catch most of these.
Word choice/interference from German: "they are doing there job" → *their* job (there/their/they're confusion is common but easily fixed with careful proofreading). "inhibit symptoms" should likely be *exhibit* symptoms—these are false friends in English that don't map directly from German usage.
Run-on sentences and comma splices: Many sentences string together independent clauses with commas where a period or semicolon is needed. Example: *"They are obsessed with food, their body, and with being unrealistically thin. Above all of this though, is that there is no cure."* Consider revising for clearer sentence boundaries throughout.
Informality: Phrases like "(yeah right.)" and "I mean if the model..." are conversational and effective for building rapport, but in an academic essay, use them sparingly and deliberately—perhaps once for effect, not multiple times.
Number formatting: "1 10 pounds" appears to be a typo for "110 pounds"—check all numerical data carefully.

Priority Revision Checklist

Add citations for all statistics.
Narrow the thesis to one clear, focused argument.
Split overloaded paragraphs; ensure each has one topic sentence.
Proofread for spelling, their/there, and comma splices.
Reduce reliance on rhetorical questions in the conclusion.

You have real writing instincts—especially in your use of vivid, concrete comparisons. With tighter structure and proper sourcing, this essay could be very persuasive.

Feedback Report on Argumentative Essay: Talk Shows

Overall Impression

You've chosen an engaging and debatable topic, and your essay demonstrates real knowledge of talk show content and format. You clearly have opinions on this issue, and there's a good foundation here. However, the essay currently reads more as a *description* of talk shows than an *argument* about them. To strengthen this into a more effective piece of argumentative writing, I recommend focusing on the following areas: thesis clarity, structure, argumentation, and language/grammar polish.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your introduction sets up an interesting tension: some viewers see talk shows as harmless entertainment, others see them as vulgar and harmful. This is a strong basis for an argument—but you need to take a clear position. Currently, the essay presents both sides somewhat neutrally and then shifts into an extended description of how talk shows work, followed by an anecdote about a tragedy, and ends with a rather sudden claim about "responsibility."

Suggestion: Decide what you actually want to argue. Is it:
Talk shows are net positive (education, catharsis, connection) despite their flaws?
Talk shows are exploitative and irresponsible, and your murder anecdote is your key piece of evidence?
Responsibility should shift to guests/hosts (this seems to be your final point, but it arrives without enough set-up)?

Right now, the essay tries to do all three, which dilutes the impact. Choose one central claim and build every paragraph toward supporting it.

Structure

The essay's structure needs stronger signposting between sections:

Paragraph 2 describes the entertainment/educational value of talk shows.
Paragraph 3 describes the dramatic mechanics of talk shows (how conflict is manufactured) and gestures toward critics ("naysayers") without fully developing that counterargument.
Paragraph 4 introduces a shocking anecdote (the murder) that seems to belong to the "harmful" side of the argument, but it's not connected explicitly to what came before.
Paragraph 5 concludes with a claim about responsibility that feels like a new argument rather than a natural conclusion.

Suggestion: Add a clear thesis statement at the end of the introduction that previews your argument's structure. Then use topic sentences at the start of each paragraph to remind the reader how that paragraph supports your thesis. Right now, paragraph 4 (the murder story) is your most powerful piece of evidence — consider moving it earlier or making it central, since anecdotal evidence this vivid deserves more argumentative weight rather than functioning as a late-arriving example.

Development of Ideas

Some ideas are asserted but not fully argued:
The "naysayers" who "turn their heads" are dismissed rather quickly. If you're going to characterize critics this way, briefly explain *what* their concerns are (eg, exploitation of vulnerable guests, promoting stereotypes, glorifying conflict) so you can respond to them, rather than simply implying they are close-minded.
The murder anecdote is extremely serious and could anchor a strong argument about accountability, exploitation, or the ethics of "shock television." Right now, it appears as a dramatic aside without analysis. Ask yourself: What does this incident *prove*? Who was responsible? What should change?

Language and Grammar (German-to-English Interference Notes)

Since English capitalization, punctuation, and article use differ from German, here are recurring patterns to watch for:

Spelling/typos: "audeinces" → audiences; "agressively" → aggressively; "thier" → their; "fueding" → feuding; "heath" → health; "solitude" → likely you mean *solace* (solitude means being alone, which doesn't fit here); "dies deep" → likely *digs deep*.
Comma splices and stray periods: You frequently insert periods mid-sentence where a comma or no punctuation is needed (eg, "trashy or not. are for entertainment purposes"). Read sentences aloud to check whether a full stop truly marks the end of a complete thought.
Article use: German doesn't always require articles where English does, and vice versa. Watch phrases like "Members of the studio audience cheer on these actions" (good) vs. missing articles elsewhere.
Subject-verb agreement: "Many viewers... tune in" (correct) but "Members of the studio audience cheer on these actions making the discussion much greater" needs a comma before "making."
Capitalization inconsistency: "t.v." should be "TV," and sentence-initial capitalization should be checked throughout.

Suggestion: After revising for content, do a dedicated proofreading pass focused only on punctuation and spelling. Reading your essay aloud, sentence by sentence, will help you catch places where periods appear where they shouldn't.

Concrete Next Steps

Write one clear thesis sentence stating your position.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one visibly supports that thesis.
Expand your treatment of the counterargument (the "naysayers") so you can rebut it directly.
Develop the murder anecdote — connect it explicitly to your argument about responsibility or ethics.
Do a separate proofreading pass for punctuation, spelling, and capitalization.

You have compelling material here, especially the tragic anecdote — with a clearer argumentative throughline, this essay could be quite powerful. I'm happy to look at a revised draft.

Feedback on Your Essay: "The Legal Drinking Age Should Be Eighteen"

Overall Impression

You have a clear personal stake in this topic, and your central claim—that eighteen-year-olds should be allowed to drink because they are legally adults—is a real, debatable argumentative position. However, the essay needs significant revision in three areas: the clarity of your argument, the logical connections between your points, and language accuracy. Below I offer specific suggestions.

Content and Argumentation

Critical error in your conclusion: Your final paragraph states "Drinking should be prohibited to kids eighteen or older." This directly contradicts your thesis! I assume you meant "permitted" or "allowed." Always reread your conclusion against your thesis before submitting—this kind of slip can make a grader think you don't understand your own argument.

Strengthen your strongest point: Your best argument is the military service comparison (paragraph 2)—it's a classic and effective argument in this debate. However, you assert it rather than develop it. Consider adding: How many 18-20 year olds serve in the military? What logical principle is being violated (eg, "rights and responsibilities should be linked")? Can you cite any real statistics or expert opinions on this argument?

Fix the confusing diploma paragraph: Paragraph 3 is hard to follow. You seem to argue: (1) 18-year-olds should drink because a diploma proves maturity, but (2) some 18-year-olds without diplomas shouldn't drink. This undermines your main thesis (that age alone should determine the right to drink) and introduces a new, unsupported criterion (education level). Either drop this argument or explain clearly why educational attainment should determine drinking rights instead of age.

Support your claims with evidence: You make several factual claims ("Most crime among teenagers is drinking and driving," "there would be less crime") without any statistics or sources. For an argumentative essay, especially in an academic context, you need data—for example, comparing drunk-driving rates in countries with lower drinking ages, or referencing studies on the "hidden drinking" phenomenon you describe. Unsupported claims weaken your credibility.

Address the counterargument more seriously: You raise one counterargument (the diploma issue) but drop it quickly. A stronger essay would address the *strongest* opposing argument—eg, research on brain development and alcohol, or the effectiveness of the 21-law in reducing teen traffic deaths (this is well documented)—and explain why your position still holds despite this evidence.

Structure

Your introduction opens with a historical list (Wild West, Civil War, Declaration of Independence) that isn't explained or connected to your argument. Either develop this historical context with a real point (eg, "alcohol has always been part of American culture, yet legal restrictions have changed over time") or cut it and get to your thesis faster.
Each body paragraph needs a clearer topic sentence that states the main point before you dive into examples.
Watch your register: the essay is written in an academic, third-person argumentative style until the very last sentence, where you switch to personal ("I would feel more like an adult"). Either frame the whole essay with more personal voice from the start, or keep the conclusion in the same objective tone as the rest.

Language and Mechanics

You have many small errors typical of interference from German spelling/word patterns and homophones. A few examples to fix:

"Every since" → Ever since
"Declaration of Independance" → Independence
"dieing" → dying
"two young" → too young
"the affects of drinking" → the effects of (affect = verb, effect = noun)
"contain alcohol in the car" → this actually needs to be "possessing" or "carrying" alcohol, since "containing" is used differently in English
"eighteen year old was allowed" → eighteen year olds were allowed (subject-verb agreement, plural)
"gonvernment" → government (typo)

I recommend reading your essay aloud, or using a grammar-checking tool, to catch these small errors, since they're currently distracting readers from your argument.

Priority Revisions

Fix the thesis/conclusion contradiction immediately.
Cut or fully rework the diploma paragraph.
Add at least one piece of real evidence or statistic per body paragraph.
Address one serious counterargument (eg, drunk-driving death-rate data tied to the 21-law).
Proofread carefully for spelling and word-choice errors.

With these revisions, your essay will have a much clearer, more persuasive argumentative structure. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback on Your Essay: Gender Identity and Roles

Overall Impression

Your essay engages with an interesting and relevant topic, and you have clearly done the reading—you draw on Pollitt, Farrell, Friedan, and Gray to build a case about gender identity. However, the essay currently reads more like a summary of these sources strung together than a tightly argued essay with a clear central claim. My feedback below focuses on strengthening your thesis, improving structure, and cleaning up language issues.

Thesis and Argument

Your introduction contains several potentially strong claims (children learn gender roles early; media distorts these roles; adults suffer when they conform to rigid roles; gender differences should be embraced rather than erased), but they are not yet unified into one governing argument. By the end of the essay, you seem to be arguing that gender roles are necessary and even valuable, but should be more flexible—yet this conclusion appears rather suddenly in the final two paragraphs rather than being signaled from the start.

Suggestion: Revise your thesis statement so it explicitly states your position: something like, "While gender differences are natural and even valuable, the rigid, media-reinforced gender roles children learn from an early age cause lasting harm in adulthood; society must therefore preserve a flexible sense of gender identity rather than a fixed stereotype." Then make sure every paragraph visibly supports this claim.

Structure and Paragraphing

The essay would benefit from clearer signposting between sections. Right now, transitions between the discussion of children's media (Pollitt), adult regret (Farrell and Friedan), and the proposed solution (Gray) are abrupt. Phrases like "Now that our children are off to a wonderful start they are ready for adulthood" are meant to be ironic, but the sarcasm risks confusing readers rather than guiding them.

Suggestion: Add explicit transitional sentences at the start of each new section that tell the reader *why* you are moving to this next piece of evidence. For example: "If children absorb these distorted messages early, as Pollitt shows, what happens when they grow up? The following two accounts illustrate the long-term consequences."

Also, the line "Pollit pints out that even the more enlightened Public Broadca stations engage in male gender bias" appears to be an isolated fragment/heading that was not integrated into the paragraph—please merge it smoothly into the surrounding text.

Use of Sources

You reference your sources reasonably well, but a few issues weaken your credibility:

Names are misspelled inconsistently: "Pollit," "Pollitt," "Fredian," "Friedan," "Betty Frieden," "Warren Ferrell," "Farrell," "John Grey" (should be Gray), "Men are From Mars, Women are From Venue" (should be *Venus*). Double-check every proper noun against your source list.
Citations are incomplete: "(548)" and "(29)" appear without author names attached in the sentence or a Works Cited page. Follow your citation style consistently (eg, MLA: Masters and Johnson 548).
Summary vs. analysis: Much of the essay summarizes what each author says, but doesn't analyze *how* their evidence supports your argument or connects to the other sources. For instance, after summarizing Farrell's "Ralph's Story," explain explicitly how his experience proves your point about early gender conditioning causing adult unhappiness.

Clarity and Logic

Some claims need more support or are overstated. For example: "It's a bad message delivered at a bad time through a bad medium" is a nice rhetorical flourish but doesn't add evidence. Similarly, "Society's semiotics on how each gender is supposed to behave are everywhere" is a strong claim that would benefit from one concrete example.

The line "The successful role model that children can draw strength from is not going to be just Dad anymore, but mom as well" is a nice closing idea, but it appears without preparation—consider foreshadowing this point earlier in the essay so it doesn't feel like a new idea introduced at the very end.

Language and Mechanics

There are frequent spelling and typing errors that will distract an English-speaking reader and should be corrected in revision:

"amend" (not "ammend"), "relaxed" (not "relazed"), "scary" (not "scarry"), "spouse" (not "sponse"), "metaphor" (not "metophor"), "point" (not "piont"), "Californian" (not "Califorian").
Watch subject-verb agreement: "there are other types of behavior that is open to change" → "that are open to change."
Possessive vs. contraction: "boys lives are more important than their's" → "than theirs."
Comma splices appear frequently, eg, "Children should be taught that there is a core of behavior that makes them boys or girls but, there are other types..." — remove the comma after "but."

Since English is your second language, I recommend reading your essay aloud before submission, and running it through a grammar checker for smaller errors like these, so that the strength of your ideas is not undercut by surface-level mistakes.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Sharpen your thesis into one clear, arguable claim.
Add transitions that connect each source back to that thesis.
Correct all misspelled names and fix citation format.
Shift from summarizing sources to actively analyzing them.
Proofread carefully for spelling, agreement, and punctuation.

You have good instincts about how to use varied sources to build an argument—now it's a matter of tightening the thesis and making every paragraph work explicitly in service of it. I look forward to seeing your revision!

Feedback Report: Argumentative Essay on Drug Legalization

Overall Impression

You've tackled a genuinely complex and controversial topic, and you clearly have real convictions about it. Your essay contains many good individual points—the comparison to alcohol/tobacco, the overcrowded jails argument, the needle-sharing/AIDS point, and the constitutional liberty quotation are all strong material. However, the essay currently reads as a collection of loosely related points rather than a unified, logically developed argument. Below I outline the main areas for revision, organized from most to least important.

Structure and Organization (Priority #1)

Right now, each paragraph tends to introduce a new sub-topic without clear transitions, and some points appear, disappear, and reappear later (eg, the "decrease drug use" idea shows up in the introduction and then again in paragraph 5, somewhat contradicting your main claim). 

Revise by:
Writing a clear thesis statement at the end of your introduction that previews your main supporting reasons (eg, "This essay argues that legalizing marijuana would reduce crime, ease the burden on the justice system, generate government revenue, and protect individual liberty.")
Giving each paragraph ONE main idea, stated in a topic sentence
Using transitional phrases between paragraphs ("Furthermore," "In addition to economic benefits," "Another consequence would be...") so the reader can follow your logical progression

Argument Consistency

Some claims work against each other. For instance, you argue drugs should be legalized because people will use them anyway—but then you say legalization might *decrease* drug use. Pick one line of reasoning and follow it consistently, or explicitly acknowledge the tension ("While legalization may not reduce use, its other benefits outweigh this concern").

Similarly, the paragraph on age limits raises an interesting problem but doesn't resolve it or connect back to your thesis. Decide: are you arguing there *should* be an age limit, or that age limits are pointless? Make your position clear.

Paragraph Development

Several paragraphs contain strong ideas that are stated but not developed. For example:
"Most violent crimes stimulate from selling illicit drugs" — this is a big claim. Add evidence, statistics, or examples.
The AIDS/needle paragraph is only two sentences. Expand with reasoning or evidence.
The jobs/manufacturing point is asserted but not explained—what jobs? What resources would the money fund?

Aim for: claim → explanation/reasoning → evidence or example → link back to thesis.

Grammar and Language (Common Patterns to Watch)

Since German is your first language, here are recurring issues to self-edit for:

Subject-verb agreement: "The side effects of marijuana is" → are; "Most published works are" (correct) vs. "Drugs injure... The loss of liberty injuries us all" → *injures*
Article usage: English articles work differently than German. Check phrases like "stimulate from selling illicit drugs" → *stem from*
Word choice/false friends: "lease harmful" → *least harmful*; "hann your body" → *harm*; "use" at the end of the quotation → *us*
Preposition errors: "get high off of" is informal—consider "get high from"
Fragments: "Issues such as permanent damage to the brain and other parts of the anatomy" is not a complete sentence—it needs a verb (eg, "Health problems, such as permanent brain damage, are often cited as a concern.")

Suggestion: Read your essay aloud, or use a grammar-checking tool, then verify corrections rather than accepting them blindly, since some tools miss context-specific errors.

Citation and Sources

Your quotation ("If the zeal to eliminate drugs...") is excellent and adds credibility, but:
Check the spelling of the author's name (Wibotsky? Verify against your source)
Follow a consistent citation format (MLA style would use the author's name and page number, eg, (Wisotsky 17))
Consider adding 1–2 more outside sources (statistics on incarceration rates, drug-related deaths, or countries that have decriminalized marijuana) to strengthen your credibility beyond personal opinion

Tone and Register

Phrases like "get high off of" and rhetorical questions ("what is the big difference?") give the essay a conversational tone. This can be effective in argumentative writing, but make sure it's a deliberate choice rather than default informal phrasing. Academic essays generally benefit from a more measured, analytical tone even when arguing passionately.

Next Steps

Draft a one-sentence thesis statement.
Create an outline listing 4–5 main reasons, each becoming one paragraph.
Revise paragraph-by-paragraph, ensuring each has a topic sentence, evidence, and a link back to your thesis.
Do a final grammar pass focusing on the patterns listed above.

You have strong instincts about persuasive content—the revision now needs to focus on structure and precision. Good luck!

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a strong, relatable topic and clearly care about the issue. Your anecdote about your friend is vivid and effectively illustrates the real consequences of the problem you're addressing. However, the essay needs revision in several areas: thesis clarity, paragraph organization, evidence/support for claims, and sentence-level language issues. Below are specific suggestions organized by priority.

Sharpen Your Thesis and Argumentative Structure

Your thesis appears at the end of the second paragraph ("I completely agree that before a professor should be allowed domain over a classroom at USC, he should be able to speak English well enough to teach..."). This is good, but it arrives quite late—readers have to wade through a long narrative first. Consider stating your position more directly in your opening paragraph, then using the anecdote to *illustrate* rather than *delay* your argument. Academic essays in English typically prefer a clearer, earlier thesis statement than narrative-first approaches common in some other rhetorical traditions.

Additionally, your essay currently reads as a list of separate points (why professors should communicate well, why students deserve what they pay for, what the Board of Trustees should do) rather than a tightly connected argument. Try using clearer topic sentences that explicitly link each paragraph back to your central claim.

Strengthen Your Evidence

Right now, your argument rests heavily on one anecdote and general assertions ("Students confront problems with teacher-student relationships, grading scales..."). For an academic essay, you need more support:

Cite USC's actual policies on TA English proficiency requirements. You mention this policy exists but don't provide a source or specific detail (eg, what program, what test, what score is required). This is a key piece of your argument—the *contrast* between TA and professor requirements—so it needs to be concrete and, ideally, cited.
Consider adding at least one more example, statistic, or authoritative source (eg, a news article about instructor English proficiency debates at U.S. universities) to show this is a broader, recognized issue—not just one personal experience.

Address Counterarguments

You raise the TA-substitute solution but dismiss it quickly. Strengthen this section by acknowledging the strongest counterargument more seriously: universities value research output from international faculty, and requiring rigorous English testing might exclude some brilliant researchers. Address *why* your solution (testing Ph.D.s like TAs) is still the better trade-off. This will make your argument more persuasive and show critical engagement rather than dismissal.

Paragraph Organization

Some paragraphs try to do too much. For example, the paragraph beginning "Students come to school with the hope..." moves through several ideas: the value of professors, the "tuition as payment" analogy, and the TA-substitution problem. Consider splitting this into two paragraphs: one on the tuition/consumer argument, another on why TAs aren't a full solution.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

There are several recurring issues to address in revision (I'll note patterns rather than list every instance):

Sentence fragments and stray punctuation: eg, "She began-to have difficulty," "Along with other students in her class, she began. To ask questions..." These appear to be typing/formatting errors (stray periods, hyphens) rather than grammar mistakes—proofread carefully before submitting.
Pronoun and article inconsistency: You switch between "she" and "he" when referring to generic professors/students (eg, "the professor could hardly speak," then later "he should be able to speak"). Choose one convention (many writers now use "they" as a gender-neutral singular, or alternate consistently) and apply it throughout.
Run-on/comma splice sentences: eg, "When professors can't speak English, they can't communicate with English speaking students ; therefore they cannot teach them." Check punctuation before "therefore" (should be a semicolon directly before, not after a space) and generally review comma splices throughout.
Word choice: "squander through complicated material" should likely be "struggle through." "Invoke the student to ponder" might be better as "prompt the student to consider" or "encourage the student to explore."
Repetition: The phrase "speak English well enough to teach" appears many times. Vary your phrasing (eg, "possess adequate language proficiency," "communicate effectively in English") to make the prose more sophisticated and less repetitive.

Conclusion

Your conclusion restates your position well but could more powerfully end with a call to action or a memorable final image—perhaps returning to your friend's specific situation (did she graduate on time? What happened?) to give the essay narrative closure and emotional resonance.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Move/sharpen thesis earlier and make it more prominent.
Add concrete evidence (sources, policy details) beyond the single anecdote.
Engage more seriously with counterarguments.
Reorganize paragraphs so each has one clear focus.
Proofread carefully for fragments, pronoun consistency, and comma splices.

You have a genuinely compelling argument here—these revisions will help it read as polished and persuasive as the issue deserves.

Feedback on "Business is Business" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen an engaging, topical subject, and your passion for the topic comes through clearly. However, the essay currently reads more like an impassioned op-ed than an academic argumentative essay. To strengthen it, focus on three main areas: structure and thesis clarity, evidence and sourcing, and tone and language precision.

Argument and Structure

Your introduction poses several rhetorical questions ("Is business just business or do fans, tradition, and loyalty count?") but never explicitly answers them in a clear thesis statement. State your position directly early on—something like: "Although NFL franchises have the legal right to relocate, Art Modell's move of the Browns to Baltimore was a moral betrayal of the fans and city of Cleveland." This gives readers a clear roadmap.

The essay also needs better organization. Right now it moves somewhat randomly between other team relocations, Cleveland's civic history, and the Browns' specific case. Consider this structure:
Introduction with clear thesis
Brief context: the pattern of NFL relocations
Cleveland's investment and civic revival (your strongest evidence)
The specific betrayal by Modell (broken promises, lack of negotiation)
Counterarguments and your rebuttal
Conclusion

Importantly, your counterargument paragraph (para. 4) introduces the opposing view but dismisses it in one sentence ("Others say the city needs to suck it up") without seriously engaging it. A strong argumentative essay demonstrates that you understand the opposing position before refuting it with evidence.

Evidence and Sourcing

This is the area needing the most attention. You cite specific figures (the $175 million sin tax, the $200 million stadium offer, attendance numbers) and quotations (Modell, Bob Costas) but provide no sources. In an academic essay, every statistic and direct quote needs a citation. Where did you find these numbers? Newspaper articles? This is essential not just for credibility but for avoiding the appearance of fabrication.

Additionally, some claims are stated as fact but read as assumptions—for example, "Arthur B. Modell is a liar" and "he is greedy, pure and simple." Even if you believe this, academic writing requires you to build the case through evidence rather than assertion. Show *why* his actions constitute lying or greed by connecting his broken public promise ("I will never take the team out of Cleveland") to the actual relocation, and let the evidence speak rather than name-calling.

Tone and Language

Several phrases are too informal or colloquial for academic writing:
"suck it up and take it like a man"
"gave Cleveland the one finger salute"
"scream rapid" (unclear—do you mean the fans are loud/rowdy?)

Replace these with more precise, formal language. Academic argument can still be passionate, but it should persuade through evidence and reasoning rather than emotionally charged colloquialisms.

Language Accuracy (Note for a German speaker)

A few specific corrections:
"it's" vs. "its": "it's" is a contraction ("it is"); "its" is possessive. You need "its" in "NFL and its tax subsidized teams."
"inwhich" is not a standard English word—likely influenced by German "worin." Use "in which" or restructure the sentence.
"consciously devastated" should likely be "collectively devastated"—these words look similar but mean very different things.
"abdication" means giving up a throne/formal position; you likely mean "betrayal" or "abandonment."
"profoundly refused" — "profoundly" doesn't fit here; try "flatly refused" or "categorically refused."
"snowballed" typically means something grew larger, not "became confused/distorted"—perhaps you mean "the facts have been obscured" or "blurred."
Check team names: was it the Titans (not "Cherokees") and the Rams (not "Rains")? These factual/spelling errors will undermine credibility with informed readers.
"novel Science Museum"—"novel" means new/original in a literary sense; for a building, use "new" instead.

Mechanics

Watch comma splices: "The Rock'n Roll Hall of Fame opened this year, and now they are building..." is fine, but elsewhere you have run-on sentences that need periods or semicolons.
The sentence "They said that when people talk Cleveland, they only talk of the Cleveland Browns" has a grammar issue—remove the extra "they" ("Some try to blame the city... by saying that they said...").
Proofread for missing/extra words: "inwhich they were not even given time" should be "in which."

Next Steps

Write a clear, explicit thesis statement.
Reorganize paragraphs chronologically or thematically (not both).
Add citations for every statistic and quotation.
Revise emotionally loaded language into more formal, evidence-based phrasing.
Proofread carefully for the language issues noted above.

You have compelling material here—now it needs the scaffolding of academic argumentation to fully persuade your reader.

Feedback on Your Essay

Your essay explores a genuinely interesting and relatable theme—how societal and personal expectations can derail people from lives that would truly satisfy them. You do a good job connecting two different sources (Friedan and Farrell) across generations to show a persistent pattern. Below are suggestions to strengthen the essay in four areas: argument, structure, language, and mechanics.

Argument and Thesis

Your introduction raises a compelling question ("But what if it's wrong?") but never states a clear, explicit thesis. Readers must infer that your argument is something like: *"Both men and women suffer when they allow others' expectations to override their own desires, and society needs to make room for more flexible life choices."* State this directly at the end of your introduction so readers know what claim you will defend.

Additionally, some of your reasoning relies on hypotheticals ("What if Ralph had..."; "It seems to me she would have gained much") rather than evidence. This is fine as a persuasive technique, but be careful not to let speculation substitute for actual support from your sources. When you cite Friedan and Farrell, dig deeper into *how* their texts support your claims rather than mostly summarizing plot.

Structure and Organization

Missing quotations: You've marked two spots with  where a quotation should appear ("wrote Friedan" and "Ralph confessed"). These need to be filled in before submission—right now the essay looks unfinished.
Uneven paragraphing: Some paragraphs (especially the Friedan and Ralph summaries) are very long and cover multiple ideas. Consider breaking them so each paragraph makes one clear point.
Conclusion is too brief: Your final two sentences ("There has to be a middle road... search his or her own heart") feel abrupt after such a detailed body. Expand the conclusion to synthesize your argument—remind readers of the tension between societal pressure and individual choice, and perhaps suggest concretely what a "middle road" might look like.
Transitions: Some paragraphs jump between ideas (career women, then abruptly to Ralph) without a clear linking sentence. Add short transitional phrases like "A similar pattern appears in men's lives" to guide the reader.

Language and Style (common German-English interference points)

Since German syntax often differs from English in punctuation and word choice, watch for these patterns:

Comma before "pressured": "as young people being ,pressured..." — remove the stray comma; also reconsider the phrasing: "especially vulnerable as young people who are pressured into deciding what they want to do for the rest of their lives."
Word choice errors: "erected of them" should likely be "expected of them." "reouired" should be "required." These look like typos, but proofread carefully—spellcheck won't catch every real-word error (eg, "fear women" instead of "few women").
Long, layered sentences: Sentences like "Many women followed this advice and at the same time did their best to have a successful marriage and be a perfect mother" reflect a German tendency toward complex subordination. English readers often prefer shorter, more direct sentences. Try splitting long sentences into two when they contain more than one main idea.
Register consistency: Phrases like "worn to a frazzle" or "corporate cog-in-the-wheel" are vivid and idiomatic — good! But mixing very colloquial expressions with more formal academic phrasing ("The jury's still out") can create tonal inconsistency. Decide whether you want a more conversational persuasive essay style or a more formal academic one, and apply it consistently.
Punctuation with quotation marks: Check comma placement before quotation marks (eg, "The Feminine Mystique", that they were...") — in American English, punctuation typically goes inside the closing quotation mark.

Citations and Mechanics

Fill in the missing quotations marked with .
Provide full citations (author, title, page number if required by your citation style) the first time you mention each source.
Proofread for typos: "reouired," "actuality" (should be "actually"), "become a." (stray period), "tine-in" (likely "tie-in" or "hook-up").
Double-check hyphenation and capitalization of titles (eg, "Ralph's Story" is consistently capitalized correctly, good).

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Add a clear thesis statement at the end of the introduction.
Fill in the missing quotations.
Break up long paragraphs and add transitions between sections.
Expand your conclusion to more fully synthesize your argument.
Proofread carefully for typos and stray punctuation.
Review comma placement with quotation marks per English convention.

You have a strong, thoughtful argument here with good use of source material — the revision work needed is mostly about clarity, proofreading, and structural polish rather than rethinking your ideas. Good work overall!

Feedback on Your Essay: "Enjoying Sexual Differences"

Overall Impression

You've chosen a provocative and interesting topic, and your writing shows real passion and a willingness to challenge conventional views. That said, the essay currently reads more like a series of loosely connected opinions and anecdotes than a tightly argued academic essay. My feedback below focuses on the areas that will most improve the essay's persuasive power: thesis clarity, argument structure, evidence, and language accuracy.

Sharpen Your Thesis

Your introduction actually contains two different claims: (a) men and women should be treated equally under the law, and (b) we should "enjoy" and even "hold sacred" the differences between the sexes. These are not the same argument, and the essay never clearly explains how they relate. Is your point that legal equality and the enjoyment of difference can coexist? Or that current anti-discrimination efforts have gone too far in denying difference? Right now, readers must guess. Write one clear sentence stating your central claim, and make sure every paragraph supports *that specific claim* rather than a general theme of "difference."

Reconsider Your Examples

Several of your examples work against each other or don't clearly support your thesis:

Sports funding: This example is about unequal *revenue* and *fan investment*, not about "enjoying differences." It actually seems to argue that funding *should* be unequal (a meritocratic argument), which contradicts your opening claim about legal equality. Clarify what conclusion you want the reader to draw.
Male vs. female athletic competition: This paragraph argues that men and women are different in physical ability, which is a *biological* argument, not really about "enjoying" difference. It feels disconnected from the strip club/advertising examples, which are about *attraction and desire*, not athletic performance. Consider whether this belongs in the essay at all, or reframe it to connect explicitly to your thesis.
Strip clubs, Hooters, and Calvin Klein ads: These are your strongest and most relevant examples, since they directly address enjoyment of physical difference. Consider building the essay primarily around these and cutting or revising the sports material.
Madonna vs. Charles Barkley: This is a compelling comparison, but you assert claims (Barkley "brings an aura of violence," Madonna succeeded through "hard work and determination") without evidence. Academic writing needs you to support such claims, not just state them.

Engage the Other Side

Right now, you dismiss opposing views very quickly (eg, "This is not discrimination" regarding Hooters lawsuits) without explaining the *reasoning* behind the lawsuits or why you disagree with it. A stronger essay would briefly and fairly present the counterargument (eg, objectification concerns, unequal treatment of male/female sexuality in media) before explaining why you find your own position more convincing. This will make your argument feel more credible and less one-sided.

Improve Paragraph Transitions and Topic Sentences

Each paragraph currently opens with a new topic ("Recently there has been a trend...," "Another problem...," "Globally a contrast...") without explicitly connecting back to your thesis. Add a sentence at the start or end of each paragraph that ties the example back to your central claim about enjoying difference.

Watch Your Tone

Phrases like "I say a strip joint is a place where...", "Let us lust after the opposite sex," and "Maybe open one called buttocks for women" are engaging but too informal and joking for an academic argument. Rhetorical questions are used effectively in your introduction but appear too often throughout, weakening their impact. Aim for a more measured, analytical voice, especially when discussing sensitive topics like discrimination lawsuits.

Language and Proofreading

You have many small but frequent errors that distract the reader:

Homophone confusions: *there/their*, *hear/here*, *scene/seen*, *sexes/sexist*, *since/sense*
Spelling slips: *underlining* → *underlying*; *resent* → *recent*; *tearn* → *team*; *trask* → *trash*; *warring* → *wearing*; *Cline* → *Klein*
Missing apostrophes: *it's self* → *itself*; *land's* laws, etc

I recommend reading your essay aloud or using a grammar-checking tool, since many of these are easy to catch on a careful re-read.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Write one precise thesis statement and cut/reframe any example that doesn't clearly support it.
Decide whether the athletic-competition paragraph belongs; if kept, connect it explicitly to your main argument.
Add at least one paragraph engaging seriously with the opposing view (eg, discrimination concerns).
Revise topic sentences so each paragraph clearly connects to your thesis.
Adopt a more formal, analytical tone.
Proofread carefully for homophones and spelling.

You have an interesting and original argument here — the revision process should focus on making that argument sharper, fairer to opposing views, and more precisely supported.

Feedback on Your Welfare Reform Essay

Thank you for submitting this essay. You've tackled a complex policy issue and have clearly done research to support your argument. Below is feedback organized by category to help you revise.

Strengths

Your thesis is clear: you propose a compromise solution combining Democratic and Republican welfare proposals. You've also organized your body paragraphs around distinct policy elements (training, time limits, funding cuts), which gives the essay a logical structure. Your use of multiple sources shows genuine engagement with the topic.

Areas for Improvement

Citation Format and Accuracy

This is the most urgent issue to address. There are several inconsistencies:
The author's name is spelled two different ways: "Silverstein" and "Sliverstein." Please check your source and use one consistent spelling throughout.
Your quotation marks are inconsistent and sometimes appear as odd symbols (eg, `,how,`, `11`, `,,`). This looks like a formatting/encoding error from copying and pasting. Please review each quotation carefully against standard English punctuation: opening quotes (") and closing quotes (") should look identical, not like commas.
Check whether you're using MLA, APA, or another required citation style consistently, including how you introduce quotations and format parenthetical citations.

Overreliance on Quotations

Your essay currently reads more like a collection of quotations stitched together than an argument in your own voice. For example, in the paragraph about AFDC, four of the sentences are direct quotations. This raises two concerns:

Your analytical voice gets lost. The reader wants to know *your* interpretation of the evidence, not just the evidence itself.
Overuse of quotations can weaken your credibility as a writer, since it appears you are relying on others to make your points rather than synthesizing information yourself.

Suggestion: For many of these quotations, consider paraphrasing instead, and reserve direct quotes for particularly striking or authoritative language. After each quote or paraphrase, add a sentence of your own analysis explaining *why* this evidence supports your argument.

Logical Gaps and Unsupported Claims

Some claims need stronger reasoning or evidence:

You state that abolishing "the law that states that women should get more money for having more children" is part of your solution, but this appears only briefly in your thesis and isn't developed as its own paragraph with dedicated evidence. Consider either expanding this point with its own evidence or integrating it more clearly into the AFDC discussion.
The claim "mothers on welfare are having more babies at the government's expense" is a strong causal claim. Your source only says AFDC recipients have more children *on average* — this is correlation, not necessarily evidence that welfare is *causing* people to have more babies. Be careful about overstating what your sources actually claim.
Your concluding sentence — "The welfare problem could easily be solved if both parties would quit butting heads for power" — oversimplifies a complex issue. This kind of language undermines your credibility, since you've just spent the essay showing how complicated the issue is. Consider a more measured concluding claim.

Language and Sentence-Level Issues

As a German-speaking writer, watch for these patterns common in English-as-second-language writing:

Comma splices and run-on sentences: eg, "This would save the government a lot of money and make people support themselves" — check whether ideas need separate sentences or connecting words (however, therefore, in addition).
Article usage: English articles (a/an/the) work differently than German ones. Review sentences like "would give less people on welfare" — check if "fewer" is more appropriate than "less" here, since you're referring to a countable noun (people).
Word order and phrasing: Some sentences show German syntactic influence, such as placing time markers or clauses in patterns that read awkwardly in English. Reading your essay aloud can help you catch these.

Transitions Between Paragraphs

Your paragraphs sometimes shift abruptly between topics (eg, from training programs to time limits) without a transitional sentence connecting the two ideas back to your thesis. Consider adding a sentence at the start of each new paragraph that explicitly connects it to your overall argument about compromise.

Priority Revision Steps

Fix quotation marks and standardize the author's name.
Reduce direct quotations by roughly half; replace with paraphrase + your own analysis.
Strengthen the paragraph on "abolishing incentives for more children" or integrate it more clearly.
Revise your conclusion to avoid oversimplifying the political conflict.
Proofread for comma splices and article usage — reading aloud will help.

You have a solid foundation and clear structure. With more of your own analytical voice and cleaner citations, this essay will be much stronger. Good work so far — keep revising!

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Thank you for sharing this draft. Your topic—curfew and visitation policies in dormitories—is a strong choice for argumentative writing because it's personal, relatable, and has clear stakeholders and consequences. You have real experiences to draw on, which is a genuine strength. Below I offer feedback organized by area, with the goal of helping you strengthen the next draft.

Content and Argument Structure

Your essay contains several good supporting points: the inconsistency between co-ed dorms and visitation rules, the double standard that students already sneak around anyway, the safety concerns of walking at night, and the emotional case of your cousin's visit. However, these points currently feel like a list of separate grievances rather than a unified argument. Consider organizing your essay around 3-4 clear reasons why curfews should be abolished, and dedicate one paragraph to each. For example:

Curfews are inconsistently applied and easily circumvented (the Bates House example)
Curfews prevent meaningful connection with family (your cousin's visit)
Curfews may create safety risks rather than prevent them
College students are legal adults capable of self-governance

Right now, the piano-playing/loud-talking anecdote is confusing—it seems to argue *against* unrestricted freedom (since it shows the downsides of unsupervised behavior), which undermines your own position. Either cut this example or reframe it to serve your argument more clearly (perhaps: "curfews don't even solve the real problem of thoughtless dorm behavior, so they're not achieving what they claim to").

Clarity and Logical Consistency

A few moments create contradictions:
You argue curfews are dangerous because "leaving a dormitory at 2 a.m. is dangerous"—but this could equally support keeping people *inside* at night, which is closer to what a curfew does. Clarify whether your concern is about being forced to walk alone at night to *escort guests out*, which is a sharper, more defensible point.
The intoxication paragraph is unclear in its purpose. Are you saying curfews cause dangerous walks home while intoxicated? Make this causal link explicit.

Grammar and Mechanics (Common Patterns to Review)

Since English is your second language, here are recurring issues to watch for:

Subject-verb agreement: "his mate guest" and "she her mate guest" (paragraph 2) are unclear—check whether you meant "sign her male guest in."
Verb tense consistency: You shift between present and past tense often (eg, "I thought if I had... they could spend"). Decide whether you're describing ongoing policy (present tense) or past experience (past tense), and stay consistent within each section.
Article usage (a/an/the): German doesn't require articles in the same contexts as English. Watch for missing articles, eg, "spend quality time with someone" (fine) vs. "leaving a dormitory at 2 a.m. is dangerous" (correct) vs. "Nor did I know I would he treated" (typo: "be").
Homophone/typo errors: "he" should be "be"; "sigu.her mate guest" is garbled and needs rewriting; "bad" should be "had."
Comma splices and fragments: Several sentences run two independent clauses together without proper punctuation (eg, "We're not at home we don't have certain luxuries anymore like a car"). Consider: "We're not at home, and we don't have certain luxuries anymore, like a car."

Tone and Persuasive Strategy

Your personal voice is engaging, but for an *argumentative* essay, you'll want to:
Anticipate counterarguments. Why might administrators impose curfews? (Safety liability, noise complaints, parental expectations?) Address one or two of these directly to strengthen your credibility.
Use evidence beyond personal anecdote. Do other universities have different policies? Is there research on student autonomy and safety?
Clarify your final proposal. Your conclusion suggests a compromise (weekend visitation) after arguing for no curfew at all. This is a good rhetorical move, but introduce it as a deliberate "if my main proposal isn't accepted, here's a fallback" rather than letting it appear as an afterthought.

Next Steps

Draft a clear thesis statement for your introduction (eg, "Because curfews are inconsistently enforced, disconnect students from family, and may not even improve safety, dormitory curfews should be eliminated for college students.")
Reorganize paragraphs around distinct, well-labeled arguments.
Proofread carefully for typos and tense shifts—reading aloud can help you catch these.
Add at least one acknowledgment of the opposing view.

You have compelling material here—the revision work is mostly about structure and polish rather than substance. Good luck!

Feedback on "Recognizing and Rewarding Teachers"

Your essay tackles a topic you clearly care about, and your personal connection to your subject matter comes through in the anecdotes about your favorite teacher. This is a strong foundation to build on. However, the essay currently reads more like a series of related reflections than a tightly argued essay. Below are the main areas to focus on for revision.

Thesis and Overall Argument

Your introduction states the claim (teachers should be recognized and rewarded) but doesn't give the reader a roadmap of *how* you'll support this claim. Essays are strongest when the thesis previews the main lines of argument. Consider revising your thesis to something like: "Teachers deserve greater recognition through verbal appreciation, material rewards, and fair pay, because of the emotional, academic, and social burdens they carry that society often overlooks." This gives you three clear pillars (appreciation, pay, social respect) that map onto your existing content and will help you organize your paragraphs more logically.

Paragraph Organization and Coherence

Right now, several paragraphs are very short (some just one or two sentences) and function more as transitional fragments than developed arguments. For example, "For as long as I can remember, people have been rewarded when they do good, so why aren't teachers rewarded when they do good? People don't realize the good that teachers do" is only two sentences and doesn't develop the claim with evidence. Combine this with the surrounding paragraph on responsibility, or expand it with a concrete example.

Similarly, the anecdote about your favorite teacher appears in the second paragraph and is repeated again later (the elephant pin story). Consolidate these into one place—ideally your introduction or second paragraph—so the essay doesn't feel repetitive.

Each paragraph should open with a clear topic sentence stating the point you will argue, followed by evidence, and closing with a link back to your thesis. Currently, some paragraphs (eg, the one on doctors) raise an interesting comparison but don't fully develop it before moving to the next idea.

Evidence and Support

Much of your support comes from personal experience and general impressions ("I've heard that...", "I've been told that..."). This is appropriate for a personal reflective piece, but for an *argumentative* essay, you should supplement anecdote with more objective evidence: statistics on teacher pay, studies on teacher retention, or expert opinions on teacher workload. Even a brief statistic (eg, average teacher salary compared to other professions requiring similar education) would strengthen your claim that teachers are underpaid.

The doctor comparison is a good rhetorical move, but right now it remains abstract. Push it further: What specific parallels exist (years of training, licensing, high-stakes responsibility for others' futures)? This would make the comparison persuasive rather than merely asserted.

Language and Grammar

Since English is your second language, here are some specific patterns to watch for:

Sentence fragments: "A material gift or a kind word of expression." This is not a complete sentence. Combine it with the previous sentence: "The reward can be big or small—a material gift or a kind word of expression."
Subject-verb agreement and word choice: "it teachers that have helped made these figures" should likely be "it is teachers who have helped make these figures." Reread sentences aloud to catch these slips.
Typos/missing text: There's a placeholder  in the paragraph about "Teacher of the Year"—make sure to fill this in before submitting.
Comma splices: Several sentences join independent clauses with only a comma, eg, "These teachers really care and.try to work with their students" (also note the stray period after "and"). Watch for these throughout; use periods, semicolons, or coordinating conjunctions instead.
Idiomatic phrasing: "deserve their flowers while they live" is a colorful but informal idiom; in an academic essay, either explain the metaphor more explicitly or replace it with more direct phrasing like "deserve recognition while they are still working, not only in retrospect."

Tone Consistency

The essay shifts between formal argumentative tone and casual, conversational tone ("I'm guilty of being like society," "so why aren't teachers rewarded when they do good?"). Rhetorical questions can be effective, but use them sparingly and deliberately, perhaps one per section, rather than scattered throughout.

Suggested Next Steps

Rewrite your introduction with a three-part thesis.
Reorganize body paragraphs so each addresses one pillar (appreciation, pay, respect/status) with a clear topic sentence.
Add at least one external source or statistic per major claim.
Proofread carefully for fragments, typos, and comma splices—reading aloud will help you catch these.
Remove redundant anecdotes and choose the most powerful one to develop fully.

You have a genuine, persuasive voice—now it needs a clearer structural frame and stronger evidence to match. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback on "Television Violence" Essay

Thank you for this draft. You have gathered relevant sources and touch on an important social issue, but the essay's argumentative structure needs significant revision before the ideas can convince a reader. Below are the main areas to work on.

Clarify Your Thesis and Argumentative Stance

This is the most pressing issue. Your introduction sets up television violence as a "serious concern," which leads the reader to expect an essay arguing *against* violent programming or *for* stricter regulation. However, by the end you seem to argue the opposite: that violence "adds texture" to shows and that children will eventually "have to deal with it" regardless. This shift is confusing and undermines your credibility as a writer.

Before revising further, decide: What is your actual claim? Possible options include:
Television violence should be more strictly regulated because of its effects on children.
Current regulatory proposals (V-chip, report cards) are ineffective/unnecessary because [reason].
Parental responsibility, not government regulation, is the appropriate solution.

Once you choose a position, every paragraph should support it. Right now, the essay reads like a list of facts and quotations rather than a persuasive argument.

Improve Paragraph Structure and Topic Sentences

Each paragraph should open with a clear topic sentence that states its purpose and connects to your thesis. Currently, paragraphs jump between topics abruptly:
Paragraph 2 introduces the V-chip and Senate bills but doesn't explain *why* this matters for your argument.
Paragraph 4 shifts to defending violent content as necessary for plot ("If a certain plot involves a murder, then the murder must happen") — this reads as a new argument, not a continuation.
Paragraph 5 discusses cartoons with almost no transition from the previous paragraph about adult programming.

Consider using an outline before your next draft: write one sentence per paragraph stating its main point, and check that each point logically builds toward your conclusion.

Strengthen Your Use of Evidence

You cite Hepburn, Stern, and Jensen, but the reader gets little sense of *who these authors are*, *what kind of texts these are* (news articles? reports? studies?), or *how reliable they are*. Academic writing requires you to introduce sources properly, eg:

> According to journalist [First Name] Hepburn, writing in [Publication], American television is considered the most violent among industrialized nations.

Additionally, you present quotations (eg, the NAB's statement) without analysis. After a quotation, explain its significance: Do you agree with the NAB's criticism? Why or why not? Quotations should support *your* reasoning, not replace it.

Also double-check your in-text citation format — "Hepburn 1" is acceptable in MLA style, but confirm this matches your assignment's required citation system (MLA, APA, etc), and make sure a Works Cited/References page is included.

Revise the Conclusion

Your final paragraph introduces a new claim ("The cause for television violence is to add texture to the show") that has not been argued anywhere else in the essay. A conclusion should *summarize* and *reinforce* your argument, not introduce fresh, unsupported ideas. Revise this paragraph to restate your thesis and briefly recall your strongest points.

Language and Mechanics

Your English is generally strong, but here are some specific points to review (common patterns for German-speaking writers):

Article use: "The V-chip is an electrical device" — good, but watch phrases like "Television programming in the United States is considered the most violent" — this is correct, but check similar constructions throughout for missing/extra articles.
Sentence fusion: Several sentences use dashes or commas where a period or semicolon would be clearer, eg, "Violence is common in TV entertainment-- the dramas that portray stories about crime..." Consider restructuring as two sentences.
Word choice: "Some proposals to antiviolence" should be "Some proposals *against* violence" or "*anti-violence proposals*."
Date typo: "the 1995-1995 season" — likely should be 1995–1996.
Vague quantifiers: "How many parents actually watch television with their teenager?" is a strong rhetorical question, but it appears in a paragraph that otherwise argues violence is justified — this creates tonal inconsistency. Make sure rhetorical questions serve your actual argument.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Choose and clearly state one argumentative position.
Rewrite paragraphs so each has a clear topic sentence linked to that position.
Introduce and analyze your sources rather than just quoting them.
Revise the conclusion to match your thesis, not introduce new claims.
Proofread for sentence-level clarity and consistency.

You have solid research material here — the next step is to organize it around a single, defensible argument. I'd be happy to discuss your revised thesis before you write the next draft.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay: Government Funding for the Arts

Overall Impression

You have chosen a thoughtful, debatable topic and you clearly care about it—your personal investment in art comes through. The essay contains many good ideas (anthropological value, social bonding, economic benefit, psychological benefit), but at the moment they read more like a list of separate points than a unified argument. Revising for structure, focus, and precision will make this a much stronger piece.

Structure and Argumentation

Your introduction promises a clear thesis (arts funding should be maintained or increased), but the body paragraphs sometimes drift from this claim into general reflections on "what art is" or "what is art versus pornography." This last paragraph, in particular, feels like a digression: it doesn't support your central argument about *government funding* and risks distracting the reader with a controversial side issue. Consider cutting it, or—if you feel it's essential—reducing it to two sentences that clarify your definition of art before you make your funding argument, rather than placing it in the middle of your evidence.

Each paragraph should open with a clear topic sentence that ties back to your thesis ("The arts deserve government funding because…"). Right now, several paragraphs (eg, the one on children's drawings, or the Louis XIV example) explain *what art does* without explicitly connecting this back to why *government* should pay for it. Add a sentence at the end of each paragraph that draws the connection explicitly: eg, "Because art performs this unique communicative function, public investment ensures that future generations retain the ability to interpret our era, just as we interpret cave paintings today."

Your conclusion is too short and introduces a new, somewhat vague idea ("counteract negative influences") without synthesizing your main points. Strengthen it by briefly restating your three or four strongest arguments and returning to the "storm cloud" image from your introduction to create a sense of closure.

Content and Evidence

Many claims are asserted rather than supported: "psychologists and psychoanalysts have told us for years," "researchers have found," "continual research has been done." Academic writing requires you to name sources or at least be more specific (which researchers? what study?). Since this appears to be an argumentative essay, even one or two concrete statistics (eg, economic impact figures for museums, or a named study on arts programs in prisons) would significantly strengthen your credibility.

The personal anecdote ("As somewhat of an artist, I myself have found…") is engaging but may be too informal for an academic argumentative essay—check your assignment guidelines on whether first-person testimony is appropriate here.

Language and Style

Your English is generally strong, but there are recurring patterns typical of German-to-English transfer that are worth reviewing systematically:

Articles: German often omits articles where English requires them. Watch for phrases like "man has seen a need," "leave an impression," "team sports" (should be "a need," "an impression," "team sports" is fine, but check similar constructions throughout). Read each noun phrase and ask: does English need "a," "the," or nothing?

Comma splices and run-on sentences: Several sentences join independent clauses with only a comma (eg, "Certainly, large museums can attract a large number of tourists, which directly can influence…"). Consider breaking long sentences into two, or using semicolons/conjunctions.

Word choice/false friends: "Summarily" (from German *summarisch*?) doesn't mean "in summary" in English—use "In summary" or "To summarize." "Necessarily" is used oddly ("Necessarily, it's interesting to note…")—this adverb doesn't fit; try "Notably" or simply delete it.

Spelling: "pschosanalysts" → "psychoanalysts"; "oftentiines" → "oftentimes."

Overused hedging phrases: "Certainly," "Obviously," "Perhaps" appear very frequently and can weaken your authority. Vary your sentence openings and reduce hedges where your evidence is strong.

Concrete Next Steps

Write one sentence stating your thesis; check that every paragraph's final sentence loops back to it.
Cut or drastically shorten the pornography paragraph.
Add at least two concrete, cited examples (statistics, named studies, or specific programs).
Proofread specifically for comma splices and article use—read each sentence aloud.
Rewrite the conclusion to synthesize your three strongest points and return to your opening image.

You have rich material here—now it needs tighter architecture and more precise, sourced evidence to convince a skeptical reader. Good luck with the revision!

Feedback Report: Salary Cap Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a topic you clearly feel strongly about, and your passion comes through in the writing. You raise several genuinely interesting points—about the disparity between athlete and doctor salaries, the neglect of fan facilities, and free agency—that could form the basis of a compelling argument. However, the essay currently reads as a collection of loosely connected complaints rather than a unified, logically developed argument. Below are the main areas to focus on for revision.

Structure and Focus

Your thesis mentions three reasons for a salary cap (comparison to college graduates, facility spending, and player movement), but the essay develops into five or six different themes, including greed, the O.J. Simpson trial, and the history of football since the 1950s. Many of these points, while interesting, don't actually support your central claim about salary caps.

Suggestion: Choose your three strongest arguments and dedicate one paragraph to each. Cut material that doesn't directly connect to the salary cap issue—for example, the O.J. Simpson reference and the paragraph about broken stadium seats support different arguments (celebrity privilege and revenue mismanagement) that aren't really about salary caps at all.

Logical Connections

Several of your points need clearer reasoning to connect the evidence to your conclusion. For instance:
You argue football players are overpaid compared to doctors—but this argument would support *redistributing* pay, not necessarily a *salary cap* specifically. A cap only limits the *maximum*; it doesn't address the underlying doctor/athlete comparison.
The stadium disrepair paragraph suggests a *revenue transparency* problem, not a salary problem. A salary cap wouldn't necessarily fix broken seats—that argument needs a clearer bridge to your thesis.

Suggestion: After each supporting point, ask yourself: "How does this specifically prove a salary cap is needed?" Make that connection explicit for your reader.

Tone and Argument Strength

Some claims are overstated and could weaken your credibility with a skeptical reader:
"football players don't do anything that is considered rewarding" — this dismisses the athletic skill and physical risk involved.
"A person could not be smart enough to pass the second grade, but as long as he can play ball he is going to be rich" — this is an exaggeration that may alienate readers rather than persuade them.

Suggestion: Acknowledge the skill and effort involved in professional sports, then pivot to your actual argument (eg, "While football requires genuine athletic skill, the compensation scale is disproportionate to compensation in other demanding professions..."). This makes your argument more balanced and credible.

Grammar and Language (German-English Interference)

A few recurring issues to watch for:

Article usage: German and English differ in when articles are required/omitted. Check phrases like "as good as they should be" and generic noun references throughout—English often needs "a" or "the" where you've omitted it, or vice versa.
Word choice: "wee" should be "we"; "ant" should be "any"; "affective" should be "effective."
Verb tense consistency: You shift between present and past tense somewhat unpredictably (eg, "Football use to be" should be "used to be").
Comparative constructions: "make more money than a college graduate does" is good, but check constructions like "as important, nor make as much money as" — English typically prefers "or" after a negative rather than "nor" in this position ("not as important, or...").

Suggestion: After revising for content, do a separate proofreading pass focused only on articles, verb tense, and spelling. Reading your essay aloud can help catch some of these issues.

Evidence and Examples

Your Deion Sanders example is excellent and directly relevant—more of this kind of concrete, specific evidence would strengthen the whole essay. The doctor/football player comparison is compelling but relies on assertion rather than data. 

Suggestion: Where possible, include approximate figures (even general ones, like "top players earn tens of millions annually while the median physician earns...") to give your comparisons more persuasive weight.

Revision Priorities

Narrow your focus to 3 clear, well-connected arguments.
Strengthen logical bridges between evidence and thesis.
Tone down overstatements to build credibility.
Proofread separately for grammar/vocabulary.
Add concrete evidence where possible.

You have real conviction and some strong intuitions here—the Deion Sanders paragraph shows you can build a focused, relevant argument. Apply that same precision to the rest of the essay, and this will become a much more persuasive piece of writing.
