Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay: Gun Control

Overall Impression

You've tackled a complex, controversial topic with genuine conviction, and your research is extensive—you clearly engaged with multiple sources (Cozic, Cole, Close Up Foundation). The essay contains solid factual content and makes a coherent overall argument for gun regulation. However, the essay needs significant revision in three areas: structure and argumentation, use of sources, and language/mechanics. Below are concrete suggestions.

Structure and Argumentation

Your introductory paragraph actually contains your entire argument in condensed form (availability of guns → crime, constitutionality, regulation helps). This is a strength, but it means the rest of the essay largely *repeats* these points rather than developing them with new depth. For instance, the constitutionality argument appears in paragraph 1, then again in paragraph 2, and again in the conclusion. Consider restructuring so each paragraph adds a new dimension to the argument:

Paragraph 1: Thesis only (shorter, sharper)
Paragraph 2: Constitutional analysis
Paragraph 3: Historical case (Brady/Reagan) and NRA opposition
Paragraph 4-5: Statistical evidence
Paragraph 6-7: Refutation of counterarguments (self-defense, slippery slope)
Paragraph 8-9: Proposed policy solutions
Conclusion: Synthesis

Right now, similar material (statistics, constitutional interpretation, calls for "reasonable" legislation) is scattered across multiple paragraphs. Try outlining your essay after writing it and checking whether each paragraph has one clear, distinct job.

Also, strengthen your topic sentences. Several paragraphs start with a fact or quotation rather than a claim (eg, the paragraph beginning "In 1981, Jim Brady..."). Start instead with a sentence that tells the reader *why* this example matters to your argument: "The 1981 assassination attempt on President Reagan illustrates how gaps in background checks can have devastating consequences."

Use of Sources

This is the area needing the most attention:

Integrate quotations smoothly. You often drop quotations in without a clear signal phrase (eg, "The NRA debates ll the 2nd Amendment guarantees..."). Introduce quotes properly: "The NRA argues that 'the Second Amendment guarantees the right of any American to own anything from handguns to machine guns' (Cozic 84)."
Avoid citation inconsistency. You cite "Cozic" and "Cosec"—these appear to be the same source misspelled. Double-check every citation against your bibliography.
Don't over-rely on direct quotation. Many ideas (eg, about licensing programs, statistics) could be paraphrased in your own words, reserving direct quotes for particularly striking or authoritative statements. This will also make the essay sound more like *your* argument rather than a patchwork of sources.
Statistics need clearer framing. When you cite the 12,000 murders, 1,750 accidental deaths, etc, explain *what these numbers prove* in relation to your thesis, not just list them.

Language and Mechanics

As a German native speaker, watch for these recurring patterns:

Capitalization of common nouns: "Gun Control," "Constitutional Rights," "Firearms," "Federal Legislation"—English capitalizes only proper nouns, not general concepts, even important ones. This is a common German-to-English transfer error (German capitalizes all nouns).
Spelling: "definatly" → *definitely*; "riffles" → *rifles*; "ant-gun" → *anti-gun*; "licencing" is British spelling (fine if consistent, but "license" as a noun and "licence" as a verb is the British split—American English uses "license" for both).
The garbled sentence beginning "lnl981,JimBrady,W'hiteHousePresssecretarywasshot..." appears to be a formatting/copy-paste error where spaces were lost. Please proofread carefully for these technical glitches—they seriously disrupt readability.
Comma splices and run-ons: eg, "Federal Legislation is the only realistic answer. Even with the 20,000 gun control laws already in existence, the serious problems due to firearm misuse continue" — this is fine, but elsewhere you have long sentences that could be split for clarity (eg, the sentence about the first Congress and the militia is trying to do too much at once).
Article usage: Check "the Constitution," "the Second Amendment" (correct with "the"), but also watch generic plural nouns without articles: "Firearms are certainly needed" is correct, but be consistent.

Suggestions for the Next Draft

Write a one-sentence thesis at the end of your introduction that previews your main line of argument clearly.
Create a reverse outline: for each paragraph, write in the margin what its single main point is. If two paragraphs share a point, merge or differentiate them.
Read your essay aloud to catch missing spaces, awkward phrasing, and capitalization errors.
Reduce direct quotations by roughly one-third; paraphrase instead, and reserve quotes for genuinely quotable language (like the Supreme Court's phrasing).
Strengthen your conclusion by connecting it explicitly back to your specific proposals (licensing, registration, waiting periods) rather than ending in general appeals to "common-sense" without recalling the specifics.

You have a strong foundation of research and a clear position—now it's a matter of tightening the organization and polishing the language so your argument shines through as clearly as your evidence does.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay: Abortion Regulation

Overall Impression

You clearly have strong personal convictions on this topic, and your willingness to share a painful family story shows real courage. However, the essay currently reads more like a passionate opinion piece than an academic argument. Below are suggestions to help you sharpen your thesis, strengthen your logic, and adjust your tone for an academic audience.

Clarify and Stabilize Your Thesis

Your introduction claims that abortion should be governed by "strict rules," but your essay then carves out two significant exceptions (rape and medical danger to the mother). This is not necessarily a weakness—nuanced positions are often stronger—but you need to state this nuance clearly in your thesis from the start. Right now, readers may feel whiplash between your absolute statements ("all... should be put into jail for murder") and your later concessions. Revise your introduction to preview the actual shape of your argument: for example, "While abortion should generally be restricted by law, exceptions must exist in cases of rape or medical necessity."

Support Your Statistics

You cite specific numbers (98%, 95%, less than 1%) for contraceptive effectiveness. In academic writing, statistics require citations. Without sources, readers cannot verify your claims, which weakens your credibility. Find and cite reputable sources (eg, CDC, Planned Parenthood, WHO) for these figures.

Address Logical Gaps

Several parts of your argument would benefit from more careful reasoning:

The "no excuse" claim: You argue that because contraception is available, there is "no excuse" for abortion. But you yourself note that no method except abstinence is 100% effective. This means unwanted pregnancies *will* occur even among responsible, contraceptive-using people. You need to address this contradiction directly rather than passing over it.
The adoption argument: You suggest adoption as an alternative but don't acknowledge the physical, emotional, and financial burdens of carrying a pregnancy to term, which a rigorous essay should at least briefly consider.
The anecdote: Your cousin's story is emotionally powerful, but be cautious about the causal claim that the abortion caused her infertility. A doctor's informal comment is not strong evidence of causation—many factors affect fertility. Presenting this as established fact weakens your credibility. Consider softening the language ("her doctor believed this *might* be connected to...") or acknowledging the uncertainty.
The punitive proposal: Calling for jail time for "murder" is a serious legal and moral claim that needs far more development—what about the exceptions you just described? Would rape victims who abort also go to jail? This proposal needs to be reconciled with the rest of your argument or removed.

Reconsider Tone and Word Choice

This is the most important area for revision. Academic writing on a sensitive topic like abortion requires restrained, precise language—even when your feelings are strong. Several phrases will strike readers as inflammatory or unprofessional:

"if a person decides to play the game" / "the game" (used twice) — replace with "engage in sexual activity"
"horseplay" — too casual for describing sexual consequences
"kill it" — this phrasing, referring to the fetus, reads as deliberately provocative rather than argumentative
"wild maniac," "vicious sperm," "this fiend" — these phrases, while emotionally understandable, are melodramatic and undermine your argument's seriousness. A calmer description of the trauma of rape will actually be more persuasive.
"like a bottle ripped from a baby's mouth" — this metaphor is confusing and mixes with your subject matter awkwardly

Aim for a tone that is passionate but composed—this will make your argument more convincing to readers who don't already agree with you.

Language and Structure Notes

As a German-speaking writer, watch for these patterns:

Comma splices and run-ons: eg, "Sure, it is supposed to be a woman's choice to say no I don't want this child, kill it, but that not very fair to the child." Break this into shorter, clearer sentences.
Article usage: "the death of two people is a justified reason" — check article/preposition combinations throughout; a few sentences need "a" or "the" added or removed.
Verb agreement: "I does think," "persons how are" (should be "who are") — these look like typing slips rather than systematic errors, but proofread carefully.
Paragraph transitions: Each paragraph currently stands somewhat alone. Add transitional sentences that explicitly connect exceptions back to your main thesis (eg, "These exceptions do not weaken the general rule, but rather...").

Next Steps

I recommend: (1) rewriting your introduction to state your nuanced thesis clearly, (2) finding sources for your statistics, (3) revising emotionally charged language into measured academic prose, and (4) reconciling your punitive proposal with your stated exceptions. Your personal investment in this topic is an asset—channel it into precise, well-supported argumentation.

Feedback Report on Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a relevant and engaging topic—the pervasiveness of sexual content in media and its potential effects on teenage behavior. You provide good concrete examples (specific TV shows, songs, and advertisements), which strengthens your argument. However, the essay would benefit from clearer organization, a more precise thesis, and attention to grammar and word choice. Below are specific areas to focus on during revision.

Thesis and Argument Clarity

Your title phrase "sex is greatly overrated" is repeated but never fully explained. Do you mean sex is *overused* in media, or that its *importance* is exaggerated? These are different claims. Consider revising your thesis to something like: "The media's excessive and unrealistic portrayal of sex contributes to poor decision-making among teenagers regarding sexual health." This gives your essay a clearer analytical direction rather than just cataloguing examples.

Structure and Paragraph Organization

Your essay moves through television, radio, magazines, and then teenage behavior—this is a logical progression. However, each paragraph currently functions more as a list of examples than an analytical argument. For each media type, try this structure:
State a claim (eg, "Radio normalizes sex through lyrics")
Provide 1-2 specific examples
Explain *why* this matters (this is currently missing)

For instance, after mentioning "Sex Me" and "I'll Make Love to You," explain *how* these songs shape listener expectations, rather than just noting the titles are obvious.

Transitions Between Paragraphs

Your transition sentences ("Television isn't the only place...") are repetitive and mechanical. Try more varied, analytical transitions that connect ideas, such as: "This trend extends beyond television into the auditory realm of radio" or "The visual saturation of sex is not confined to television and radio; print media follows the same pattern."

Grammar and Language Issues (Common Patterns)

As a German speaker, watch for these recurring issues:

Article usage: "Sex Is used to do everything" — check capitalization and consider "Sex is used to sell everything."
Verb tense consistency: You shift between present and future ("has or will have slept") — clarify your timeframe.
Word confusion: "saids" should be "says" (this appears to be a typo, but proofread carefully). "Soak" should be "soaked." "Precautions measures" should be "precautionary measures" or simply "precautions."
Sentence fragments: "Nothing but a sheet." is stylistically fine as emphasis, but ensure such fragments are intentional, not accidental.
Repetitive sentence starters: You use "You can't..." five times in the concluding paragraphs. While repetition can be rhetorically effective, here it feels unintentional. Vary sentence structure to maintain reader engagement.

Specific Sentence-Level Revisions

"R. Kellye" should be "R. Kelly" — double-check proper nouns.
"" appears to be a placeholder—remember to fill in the actual slogan you're citing.
"Soak and wet" — likely means "soaked and wet"; also consider whether both words are needed.
"The only things else that needs to be said" — this should be "The only other thing that needs to be said."

Logical Development

Your causal claim—that media sexual content leads to teen pregnancy and STDs—is compelling but asserted rather than argued. Consider:
Acknowledging counterarguments (Could other factors contribute to these problems? What about sex education, family communication, or peer influence?)
Citing at least one statistic or study to support the causal link, even briefly
Clarifying whether you're arguing correlation or direct causation

This will make your argument more persuasive to an academic audience, who will expect evidence beyond personal observation.

Conclusion Strengthening

Your conclusion introduces a useful concept ("understood sex") but does so quite late. Consider moving this idea earlier, perhaps in your introduction as part of your thesis, so you can develop it more fully as a *solution* rather than a last-minute suggestion. Right now, the essay ends somewhat abruptly without truly synthesizing your argument.

Suggested Next Steps

Rewrite your thesis statement to clearly state your argument and its stakes.
Add analytical sentences after each example explaining *why* it matters.
Proofread for tense consistency and article usage (a common German-English transfer issue).
Consider adding one external source (survey, study) to support your claims about teenage sexual behavior.
Revise the conclusion to explicitly tie back to your (revised) thesis.

Overall, you have strong observational skills and clear personal conviction—now focus on developing the *analysis* behind your examples and tightening the grammatical precision. This will elevate the essay from description to argumentation.

Feedback on "The Psychology of Fashion" Essay

Overall Impression

You've clearly done substantial research and have gathered interesting material from multiple sources (Ryan, Flugel, Davis, Herrick, Hollingworth). However, the essay currently reads more like an annotated summary of sources than an argumentative essay. To strengthen this piece, you need a clearer central claim and tighter organization around that claim.

Thesis and Argument

Your introduction raises an interesting idea—that clothing reveals identity and emotion—but the essay never commits to a single, arguable thesis. Instead, it moves through several loosely related topics (fashion identity, adolescents, consumer behavior, the elderly, modesty) without showing how they connect to one central argument.

Suggestion: Decide on ONE claim you want to defend—for example: *"Clothing choices are shaped less by personal expression than by the psychological need for social approval."* Every paragraph should then work to support this specific claim, and you should explicitly connect each section back to it.

Structure and Paragraph Organization

The essay currently reads as a sequence of topics: identity → adolescents → consumers → elderly → modesty. Each section is interesting on its own, but the transitions between them are abrupt, and it's unclear why these particular topics were chosen or how they build on one another.

The Herrick poem (Delight in Disorder) appears without introduction or analysis. Why is it here? What does it prove? You need at least 2–3 sentences explaining its relevance and connecting it to your argument, not just quoting it.
The section on the elderly and the section on modesty feel like separate mini-essays rather than parts of a developed argument. Consider whether they truly serve your thesis, and if so, make the connection explicit.
Your conclusion largely restates topics rather than synthesizing an argument or drawing a conclusion beyond "clothes are important."

Suggestion: After drafting your thesis, outline each paragraph's function: What point does it make? How does it support the thesis? What is the transition from the previous paragraph?

Use of Sources

You rely heavily on paraphrase and quotation from Ryan, sometimes stringing together multiple citations from the same source without your own analysis in between (eg, the long block of "Ryan 82... 84... 85... 91"). This makes the essay feel like a book report.

Suggestion:
After every quotation or paraphrase, add a sentence in your own voice explaining *why* this evidence matters to your argument.
Vary your sources more evenly throughout rather than clustering all your Ryan citations together.
The five-point list from Flugel is quoted verbatim without discussion. Since you say a "full description... would require an entire paper," consider cutting this list down to just the point relevant to your thesis, or omit it altogether.

Language and Grammar (Specific Points)

Since English is your second language, here are recurring patterns to watch for:

Subject-verb agreement: "What a person wears can be a good indicator on how that person feel" → *feels*. "If she wears a mini-skirt, she probably feel sexy" → *feels*.
Pronoun consistency: You switch between "he," "she," "one," and "they" within the same paragraph (eg, "one doesn't know what style he/she wants... in order to get people to buy their clothes"). Choose one approach (eg, consistently "they/their" for gender-neutral reference) and stick with it.
Word choice: "Clothing has strong affect" → *effect* (noun form). "Combat the growing need... to try and dress alike" — this is imprecise; do you mean *reduce social pressure* or *reduce visible economic difference*? Clarify.
Missing article/preposition issues: "feed" in "this is where fashion designers feed" needs an object—*feed on this uncertainty* or similar.
Run-on/incomplete transition: "Dress codes were formulated in order to / combat..." — this sentence break across paragraphs suggests a formatting error; check for accidental line breaks.

I'd recommend reading your essay aloud, sentence by sentence, to catch verb agreement and pronoun shifts—these are very common when translating structures from German, where pronoun and verb agreement rules differ.

Citation Formatting

Ensure consistent in-text citation style (author name only needed once per source cluster, then just page numbers, per MLA convention) and double check that all sources cited are also listed on a Works Cited page.

Revision Priorities

Write one clear thesis statement and revise every paragraph to support it directly.
Add analytical sentences after each piece of evidence—don't let quotations speak for themselves.
Strengthen transitions between sections so the essay reads as a unified argument, not a list of topics.
Proofread for subject-verb agreement and pronoun consistency, especially in sentences describing hypothetical individuals ("if she/he/one...").
Reconsider the conclusion—synthesize your argument rather than simply restating your topics.

With a sharper thesis and tighter integration of your excellent research, this essay has strong potential. Good luck with your revision!

Thank you for tackling such a complex and sensitive topic. You clearly have a position and some good instincts for argumentation (eg, addressing counterarguments). Global Issues

Revise your intro so it ends with a one-sentence thesis stating your position.

Factual accuracy. You state the debate "began over two decades ago," but the abortion debate has a much longer history (well over a century in various forms, and decades before *Roe v. Wade* in the U.S. context, for example). Double-check factual claims like this—an inaccurate historical claim undermines your credibility from the first sentence.

"there would be higher death rates from self-induced abortions"
"a larger number of unwanted children"
"he is automatically psychologically damaged for life"

These are exactly the kind of claims that need statistics or citations to be persuasive in an academic essay. Consider adding 2–3 citations from credible sources.

Capitalization inconsistency: "Slippery Nazi Slope" is capitalized as a proper term—make sure this is an actual recognized term you're citing, not a term you coined; if the latter, don't capitalize it, or explain that you are naming it yourself.

Feedback on Your Essay: Children, Television, and Sexuality

Thank you for sharing this essay. Your topic is timely and you clearly care about it—the personal examples involving your own children give the essay warmth and credibility that many student essays lack. Below is feedback organized by argument, organization, and language, followed by suggestions for revision.

Argument and Evidence

Your central claim—that television exposes children to sexual content they are not developmentally ready to process—is worth making, but it needs to be stated clearly and early. Right now, the thesis emerges only gradually across several paragraphs. Consider adding one or two sentences near the beginning that state explicitly: *what* the problem is, *why* it matters, and what you think should change.

A more serious issue is your use of evidence. You state that "approximately ninety-eight percent of television programs are not for children," but you don't cite a source. Unsupported statistics weaken your credibility rather than strengthen it—readers may assume you invented the number. Either find a real source (a media study, ratings report, etc) or replace the statistic with a qualitative claim you can support with examples, such as your own hour-by-hour breakdown of afternoon programming, which is actually quite persuasive on its own.

Similarly, be careful about generalizing from personal experience. Your anecdote about your four-year-old assuming a kissing couple must be married is charming and illustrative, but it is one data point, not proof that "every child" draws such conclusions. Frame these moments explicitly as illustrations ("For example, in my own experience...") rather than as evidence for universal claims.

You also repeat the same core idea—that children are too young to understand what they see—many times throughout the essay (in the introduction, the Ricki Lake example, the Simpsons paragraph, and the homosexuality paragraph). Consolidate this point once, argue it well with your strongest example, and then move forward to new material rather than restating it.

Organization

The essay would benefit from clearer paragraph divisions built around one idea each. Currently, several paragraphs shift between multiple sub-topics (parents' schedules, statistics, the daily programming schedule, and psychological effects all appear in one paragraph). Consider this structure:

Introduction – the problem and your thesis
Why children are exposed – parents' schedules, TVs in bedrooms
What children are exposed to – your hour-by-hour example, cartoons like *The Simpsons*
Why this is harmful – children's cognitive immaturity, your daughter's example, the Ricki Lake example
The special problem of homosexuality/sexuality – perhaps merge this into the harm section rather than treating it separately
Conclusion – your proposal (more children's programming after school) and a final call to action

Also, your conclusion introduces a new idea—that networks should offer more children's programming in the after-school hours—that deserves more development. This is actually your most concrete, actionable proposal; consider moving it earlier and building your evidence toward it, so the essay ends with a solution the reader has been anticipating, not one that appears in the final paragraph almost as an afterthought.

Language and Grammar

Since English article and infinitive rules differ from German, here are patterns to watch for:

Infinitives after "make": "make the children to want" should be "make children want" (bare infinitive after *make*, *let*, *have*).
Hyphenation of compound adjectives: "four year old" should be "four-year-old" child; "thirteen year old girls" → "thirteen-year-old girls."
Relative pronouns: "the Ricki Lake show who has..." should be "which features" or "on which appear" (use *which/that* for programs, not *who*).
Run-on sentences and comma splices: Many sentences string together independent clauses with only commas (eg, "Children draw their own conclusions... Only adults do not usually show..."). Read your paragraphs aloud and break long sentences at natural pause points, or use conjunctions like *because*, *while*, *however*.
Apostrophes and contractions: "mom's cooking," "it's," "don't"—check these consistently; a few are missing (eg, "mom is" needs an apostrophe in "moms cooking" → "mom's cooking").
Capitalization: "Opra" should be "Oprah"; check proper nouns throughout.
Register: For an academic argumentative essay, reduce contractions ("that's," "don't") and phrases like "I feel that," which sound conversational. Try "I would argue that" or simply state the claim directly.

Summary of Priorities

Write an explicit thesis statement early on.
Cite or remove unsupported statistics.
Reorganize paragraphs around single ideas; reduce repetition.
Develop your final proposal about children's programming earlier and more fully.
Proofread for hyphenation, apostrophes, and run-on sentences.

You have a genuine, well-observed argument here—tightening the structure and supporting your claims more carefully will make it much more persuasive.

Feedback on "Recycling" Essay

Your essay demonstrates strong research effort and genuine engagement with an important topic. You've gathered substantial evidence about recycling practices across multiple materials (plastic, glass, batteries, motor oil, etc). However, the essay needs significant revision in several key areas to strengthen its argumentative power and academic quality.

Structure and Argumentation

The essay currently reads more as an informative catalog of recycling facts than a focused argument. Consider these issues:

Thesis clarity: Your introduction promises an argument about why recycling "needs to become commonplace," but the body paragraphs simply move through different materials one by one without building toward a cumulative argument. Each section (plastic, glass, batteries, oil, etc) feels like a separate mini-essay rather than a building block of one argument.

Missing analytical thread: Try adding a sentence at the start of each paragraph explaining *why* this particular material matters to your overall argument, and a transition sentence connecting it to the next section. Right now the essay reads as a list.

The plastic vs. paper claim: You make a strong claim ("Plastic bags are much better for the environment than paper bags") based on a single source (Superbag Corporation), which has an obvious commercial interest in promoting plastic. This is a significant logical weakness—you're using a biased source to support a controversial claim without acknowledging counterarguments (eg, plastic's contribution to ocean pollution, its non-biodegradability). Consider either qualifying this claim more carefully or removing it, as it undermines your credibility.

Source Integration and Citation

This is the area needing the most attention:

Citation format: Your citations are inconsistent and unclear ("Recycler's 1," "One Earth 1," "Superbag 1"). Check with your instructor about which citation style is required (MLA, APA, etc) and apply it consistently, including a Works Cited/References page.

Long block quotation: The extended quotation from "Superbag" in the middle of the essay is not properly integrated—it appears without an introductory sentence explaining its relevance, and it interrupts the flow. Long quotes should be introduced, contextualized, and followed by your own analysis.

Over-reliance on quotation: Much of the essay relies on direct quotes rather than your own synthesis of the information. Try paraphrasing more and reserve direct quotations for particularly striking statistics or phrases. This will make the writing feel more like *your* argument.

Language and Mechanics

Since English is your second language, here are some specific patterns to watch for:

Typographical errors: There are several distracting errors, likely from OCR or autocorrect issues: "fun" (should be "full"), "fight bulb" (should be "light bulb"), "m" instead of "in" (multiple instances), "win" instead of "will," "@" instead of a word. Please proofread carefully or read the essay aloud to catch these.

Comma splices and run-ons: eg, "Glass bottles should not be mixed with other types of glass such as windows, light bulbs, mirrors, glass tableware, Pyrex or auto glass" — check sentences like this for proper punctuation with lists and clauses.

Article usage: As is common for German speakers, watch for missing or unnecessary articles (a/an/the), eg, "the human population needs to educate themselves" — consider "people need to educate themselves" for a smoother, more natural phrase.

Sentence fragments: eg, "Recycling is not only useful for saving room in the landfills but for saving" — this sentence is incomplete, likely cut off before the source citation.

Specific Suggestions for Revision

Rewrite your introduction to include a clearer thesis statement that previews your specific argument (not just "recycling is good" but perhaps "small individual actions in recycling specific materials can have a disproportionately large collective impact").

Add a counterargument section: Acknowledging why some people don't recycle (inconvenience, cost, lack of infrastructure) and refuting these points would strengthen your argument considerably.

Strengthen your conclusion: Currently it introduces new statistics rather than synthesizing your argument. Save the landfill statistics for the body and use the conclusion to circle back to your thesis and call to action.

Verify your sources: Some statistics (eg, the Freon/ozone molecule ratio, the "twin towers" comparison) seem striking but should be verified for accuracy, as some appear to be popular but potentially exaggerated claims circulating on early recycling-advocacy websites.

Summary

Your factual research is a real strength—you clearly did substantial work. Now focus on: (1) building a cohesive argument rather than a list of facts, (2) improving source integration and citation consistency, (3) careful proofreading for language errors, and (4) engaging with counterarguments to make your case more persuasive. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Feedback Report

Thank you for this essay on color stereotypes, particularly the gendered associations of pink and blue. You've chosen an accessible, relatable topic and use concrete examples effectively. Below is feedback organized by key areas for revision.

Thesis and Structure

Your essay currently lacks a clear thesis statement that would guide the reader through your argument. Right now, the essay reads more as a list of observations ("pink is associated with women," "commercials show this," "babies wear pink") rather than a structured argument. Ask yourself: What is the larger claim you want to make? Is it that these color associations are socially constructed rather than natural? That they reinforce limiting gender norms? That there's an asymmetry in how flexible these rules are for men versus women? Your final paragraph gestures toward this last idea (that women have more freedom to cross color boundaries than men do), but this insight arrives too late and isn't developed. Consider making this asymmetry your central thesis and restructuring the essay to build toward it.

I recommend an opening paragraph that states your thesis clearly, followed by body paragraphs that each support a specific aspect of that argument, with your commercial and baby-clothing examples serving as evidence rather than as the organizing principle itself.

Development of Ideas

Your examples (Skintimate, Secret, Huggies, Gerber) are good, but they need more analysis. Currently you describe the commercials rather than explain their significance. For each example, ask: *What does this example prove? How does it support my thesis?* For instance, after describing the Secret deodorant commercial, you could add a sentence explaining what the pairing of "strong" language with pink packaging reveals about how femininity is marketed—perhaps as an attempt to make strength "acceptable" for women only when visually softened.

Similarly, your claim that "women are usually pictured as being soft and tender" is an assumption you should either support with evidence or acknowledge as a stereotype itself (which seems to be your point, but this isn't explicit).

Paragraph Organization

Several paragraphs contain a mix of ideas that could be separated for clarity. For example, your third paragraph moves between commercials, sanitary napkins, and general observations about babies. Consider grouping your evidence more deliberately: one section on commercials for adult products, one on baby products, and one on the double standard you identify at the end.

Language and Style Issues

Capitalization: Sentences beginning with lowercase letters after periods need correction (eg, "why? because," "on the Secret," "once a baby," "of course," "As for males, baby boys especially, blue is the").

Informal language: "(jam referring to babies)" appears to be a typo for "I am referring to babies," but even corrected, parenthetical asides like this feel too casual for academic writing. Integrate this clarification into your sentence structure instead.

Word choice and phrasing: 
   - "get away with it" is colloquial; consider "face fewer social consequences" or "are not stigmatized."
   - "you would not catch a baby boy with pink on" is informal; try "baby boys are rarely dressed in pink."
   - "once a baby has developed its looks" is unclear—do you mean once physical features become more distinct? Clarify this.

Repetition: You repeat "soft colors" and "stereotypical" frequently. Vary your vocabulary—consider synonyms like "muted," "pastel," "gentle" for soft, and "conventional," "normative," "socially expected" for stereotypical.

Sentence-level clarity: Some sentences run together ideas awkwardly, eg, "The Skintimate shaving gel commercial shows a woman wearing pink holding a pink razor. The background has soft colors." These could be combined more smoothly: "In the Skintimate shaving gel commercial, a woman wearing pink holds a pink razor against a backdrop of soft pastel colors."

Suggestions for Revision

Write a clear thesis statement for your introduction that frames the entire essay's argument, likely centered on the double standard you identify at the end.
Reorganize paragraphs thematically, ensuring each one has a clear purpose tied to your thesis.
Deepen analysis of each example—don't just describe, but explain what it reveals about gender norms.
Proofread carefully for capitalization and punctuation, especially after periods.
Revise the conclusion to fully develop your point about asymmetry between how men and women can navigate these color codes; this is potentially your most original insight and deserves more space.

Overall, you have strong observational material and an interesting angle in your conclusion. With a clearer argumentative structure and more analytical depth, this essay will be significantly stronger. I'd be happy to discuss revision strategies further in office hours.

Overall Impression

However, the essay currently reads more like an outline than a finished argument: several paragraphs contain gaps (marked with ) where evidence, examples, or logical connections should be. Structure and Argumentation

But each paragraph needs a complete cause-and-effect chain. Ask yourself: *Why exactly would lower prices make dealing less attractive? What specific mechanism connects legalization to less street dealing?* Spell this out explicitly rather than leaving the logical steps implicit.

Similarly, the paragraph on public health suddenly shifts from a general claim to detailed statistics about substitution effects (drugs replacing alcohol/tobacco) without citing a source. This is a strong point, but as written it appears unsupported—readers will wonder where these figures and generalizations come from.

You cite Duke once (231), which shows you are engaging with sources, but this is the *only* citation in the whole essay. Right now, strong claims (eg, "millions of people... every day," "one third of people") are asserted without a source, which weakens your credibility. Please add in-text citations wherever you present a statistic or a claim that is not your own original reasoning.

Paragraph Development

Several paragraphs end abruptly or contain only one or two developed sentences (eg, the paragraph on personal responsibility/debts). Try to develop each paragraph with: (1) a clear topic sentence, (2) supporting evidence/example, (3) explanation of how it connects to your thesis, and (4) a concluding link back to your argument. This will also help you meet a stronger word count with substance rather than repetition.

Counterargument

A notable weakness is the total absence of counterarguments. A strong persuasive essay acknowledges opposing views (eg, concerns about increased drug use, effects on children, enforcement of quality control) and then refutes them. Adding at least one paragraph addressing the strongest objection to legalization would make your argument much more convincing and show critical engagement with the issue.

Language and Style

Since English is your second language and German is your first, here are some patterns to watch for:

Comma splices / run-on sentences: eg, "They have come to reality and decided that ,drug prohibition has not worked..." Missing/incorrect articles: English requires articles more consistently than German in generic statements (eg, "helping the homeless" is fine, but check phrases like "socially and economically margin" — this should likely be "socioeconomic marginalization" or "their marginalized social and economic status").

Word choice: "beneficiary" should be "beneficial." "This really appeals" is vague—specify what "this" refers to.

Verb agreement and tense shifts: "Similarly, when heroin, they become alcohol abusers" is incomplete/garbled—likely missing a clause ("when heroin [is unavailable], [users] become alcohol abusers"). Reread sentences aloud to catch missing words.

Sentence fragments: "Also in the cities, ." needs completion before submission.

Concrete Next Steps

Fill in every  with a complete, logically connected sentence.
Add at least 4–5 citations from credible sources (studies, government statistics) to support your claims.
Add one paragraph presenting and refuting a counterargument.
Proofread for comma splices and article usage—reading your essay aloud can help you catch these.
Revise your conclusion to briefly reference your rebuttal of the counterargument, not just repeat your main points.

You have a solid foundation and a clear passion for this topic—the next revision should focus on *completing* your argument and *supporting* it with real evidence.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay on Capital Punishment

Overall Impression

You've constructed a clear, well-organized essay with a logical structure: introduction, three counterarguments addressed in turn, and a conclusion. Your use of the classic three-reasons refutation structure works well, and you clearly feel strongly about your topic, which comes through in your writing. However, the essay needs significant revision in three areas: tone/register, grammar and spelling, and argumentative rigor. Let's go through each.

Tone and Academic Register

This is the most important issue. Academic writing requires a more formal, measured tone than what you've used here. Several phrases undermine your credibility:

"one by one I will show you that these reasons are as ridiculous as the source they are trying to endorse" — avoid insulting your opposition; it makes you appear biased rather than analytical.
"Let me throw a couple of statistics at you" — too conversational.
"Correct me if I'm wrong but..." — this is spoken-language filler, not appropriate for essays.
"this obscene form of punishment," "midevil philosophy" — again, loaded language.

Suggestion: Academic argumentation persuades through evidence and logic, not through emotional language or direct address to the reader ("you"). Try rephrasing claims neutrally: instead of "these reasons are ridiculous," say "these reasons do not withstand closer examination." This is actually *more* persuasive to an academic reader, because it signals objectivity.

Grammar, Spelling, and Punctuation

There are frequent errors that a native German speaker commonly makes when writing in English, plus some typos. Please proofread carefully or use a spellchecker; I'll flag patterns rather than every instance:

Apostrophes with "its": "it's existence," "it's use," "it's intended purpose" — these should be "its" (possessive, no apostrophe). "It's" only means "it is."
Homophone errors: "untill" → until; "Ounce society is enlightened" → Once; "el,,,actly" → exactly; "statels" → states; "prols and con's" → pros and cons; "murdeer(s)" → murder(s); "deference" → deterrence (this is a meaning-changing error—please check this word again, as it appears in your final key sentence); "midevil" → medieval; "inprison" → imprison; "recieves" → receives (i before e except after c); "detramental" → detrimental.
Comma splices/run-ons: eg, "This may seem plausible, but under close scrutiny it ends up being completely false" is fine, but elsewhere sentences run together without clear connectors.
Subject-verb agreement: "There are a couple of reason's" — "reason's" should be "reasons" (plural, not possessive).

Suggestion: Read your essay aloud slowly, or use a tool like Grammarly in addition to your own proofreading. Since German and English share false friends and different apostrophe conventions, pay special attention to "its/it's" and homophones.

Argumentative Strength

Your reasoning is generally sound, but needs more rigor:

Citations: You mention "statistics from a paper I did last year" and "studies in states"—but you don't cite any actual sources. Academic essays require specific, attributable evidence (author, study name, year, or at least a general reference like "according to the Death Penalty Information Center"). Vague references weaken your argument, since readers cannot verify your claims.
The retribution paragraph is your most philosophically interesting point, but it conflates "revenge" and "retribution" without fully explaining the distinction many philosophers make (retribution ≠ simple revenge in some ethical theories). Engaging with the strongest version of the opposing argument (this is called "steelmanning") will make your rebuttal more convincing.
The deterrence paragraph could be strengthened by naming a specific study or comparing specific states/countries.
Consider acknowledging a counter-counterargument at least once—eg, "Some might argue that even without measurable deterrence, retribution alone justifies the practice"—and then responding to it. This shows deeper engagement with the debate.

Structural/Stylistic Notes

Your topic sentences are functional but could be sharpened (eg, "The first claim of supporters is..." could become more analytical: "Proponents' first argument rests on a cost comparison that misrepresents the true financial burden of capital punishment.")
The conclusion restates points adequately but could end with a stronger final claim or call to action rather than trailing off with "it seems there is no use for it."

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Revise tone: remove direct address to reader, sarcasm, and emotionally loaded language.
Proofread carefully for spelling/apostrophe errors—these are frequent and distracting.
Add specific, citable sources for all statistics and studies mentioned.
Deepen engagement with opposing views, especially on retribution.

You have a strong foundation and a clear, persuasive structure—these revisions will elevate the essay to meet academic standards of tone and rigor.

You have chosen an interesting and genuinely debatable topic, and it is clear that you have done real research (the Kevorkian case, William F. May's typology, the Hippocratic Oath, Joseph Fletcher's critique). However, in its current form the essay reads more like an informative report than an argumentative essay. To meet the requirements of the genre, you need a clearer thesis, a more consistent line of argument, and stronger integration of your sources into that argument. Below are the main areas to revise.

Thesis and Argumentative Stance

Your introduction ends with a genuine research question ("Should it be legalized...or should they be bound by their Hippocratic Oath?"), but you never clearly commit to an answer until the final short paragraph, which arrives abruptly and almost contradicts your opening framing. In your first paragraph, suicide is described as "an easy way out," which carries a negative judgment; but your conclusion defends the patient's right to choose and criticizes doctors who "decide the fate of the patient." These two framings pull in different directions. Decide early what your central claim is (eg, "Physicians should be permitted to assist terminally ill patients in dying, but only as a response to patient choice, not as an initiative of their own") and make sure every paragraph supports that claim rather than simply reporting facts.

Structure and Paragraph Development

The essay currently has very long, dense paragraphs that mix definitions, historical narrative, and argument without clear topic sentences. Consider this structure:
Introduction with a clear, arguable thesis
One paragraph clearly defining and distinguishing the forms of euthanasia (this material is good but needs tightening)
One paragraph presenting the opposing view (May's four social forces, the Hippocratic Oath) — this is your counterargument
One paragraph responding to that counterargument (Fletcher's critique, the Kevorkian case as evidence)
A conclusion that returns explicitly to your thesis

Right now the Kevorkian narrative is very detailed but its purpose is unclear — is it evidence supporting legalization, or an example of a doctor overstepping ethical lines? Make the function of this example explicit.

Missing Citations

There are several  placeholders where you clearly intended to insert direct quotations (from May, Judge Breck, the Hippocratic Oath, and Fletcher). These must be filled in with the actual quoted text and given an in-text citation (author, page number) and a Works Cited entry. As it stands, a reader cannot evaluate your evidence, and this will be read as unfinished work.

Clarifying Key Distinctions

You introduce three types of euthanasia (withdrawal of life support, refusal of treatment, and self-inflicted suicide) but then shift focus almost entirely to physician-assisted suicide, which is technically different from all three categories you defined. Add a sentence explicitly connecting physician-assisted suicide to your typology, so the reader understands where it fits.

Language and Style Notes

Since English is your second language, here are recurring patterns worth checking:

Generic "one": Phrases like "one's expertise," "guided by his conscience... to initiate or suggest euthanasia as an option, but it is one's option as a physician" mix "one," "his," and "a physician" inconsistently. English tends to avoid the generic "one" in this register; prefer "physicians" or "a doctor" consistently, and keep pronoun reference consistent (do not switch between "he," "one," and "a physician" within the same idea).
Comma splices/run-ons: eg, "If medical conduct to end the patient's life is prohibited, the patient is allowed maximum opportunity to change his mind..." — check sentence boundaries throughout; some sentences need periods or subordinating conjunctions instead of commas.
Apostrophe errors: "patient's who desire" should be "patients who desire" (no apostrophe needed for a simple plural).
Spelling: "Christains" → "Christians"; "break throughs" → "breakthroughs."
Word choice/false friends: "leave in their will" should likely be "state in their will" or "leave instructions in their will." "Re unmanageable pain" appears to be a typo/artifact — check this sentence.
Verb tense: The historical narrative about Kevorkian shifts tenses; keep past tense consistent throughout that narrative section.
Redundant phrasing: "die than to continue to live in pain" — "than continue" without "to" is more idiomatic after "would rather."

Suggested Revision Priorities

Write a clear, one-sentence thesis and place it at the end of your introduction.
Fill in all missing quotations and add proper citations.
Reorganize paragraphs so each has one clear purpose and a topic sentence.
Resolve the tension between your opening framing (suicide as "an easy way out") and your closing argument (defense of patient autonomy).
Proofread for the specific grammar/spelling issues noted above, ideally reading the essay aloud to catch run-on sentences.

With a sharper thesis, complete citations, and tighter paragraph structure, this essay has strong potential — the research is solid; it now needs a clearer argumentative spine.

Feedback on "The Wild Card in Baseball" Essay

Overall Impression

This is a well-researched and clearly organized essay with a strong grasp of the subject matter. You demonstrate good structural instincts—background, then three supporting arguments, then conclusion—and you support your claims with specific statistics and examples (MVP names, run totals, the detailed last-day scenarios). However, the essay would benefit from tightening the argumentative focus, refining several grammar issues common among German-English speakers, and improving citation consistency. Below are concrete suggestions.

Balance Explanation and Argument

The first two sections (background on realignment and the mechanics of home-field advantage) are very detailed—almost half the essay is devoted to explaining the *rules* before you get to your actual argument. While context is necessary, consider trimming this substantially. A reader doesn't need the full home-field-advantage rotation explained in depth; a sentence or two suffices. Save your word count for analysis, which is what argumentative essays are graded on.

Strengthen the Analytical Voice

Much of the essay *lists* evidence rather than *analyzes* it. For example, naming eleven players who would have missed the playoffs is effective, but you could strengthen this by explaining *why* their absence would have specifically hurt baseball (ratings? attendance? historical narratives lost?). Similarly, the closing paragraph's repeated "Without the wild card..." structure is a nice rhetorical device, but repeating it five times reduces its power. Consider condensing to three uses, or varying the sentence structure to avoid monotony.

Also consider addressing a counterargument. Purists might argue the wild card dilutes the meaning of a division title (as you hint by comparing the Rockies' one win against Atlanta to the Reds' four losses—doesn't this actually complicate your claim of "competitiveness"?). Engaging with this tension would deepen your analysis rather than presenting the case as one-sided.

Grammar and Language Issues

You write English very capably, but there are several recurring issues typical of German-English transfer:

Sentence fragments/garbled syntax: "The wild card…is decided by taking the team from each league with the best record the three Division winners" — this sentence lost a word or connector somewhere. Revise to something like: "…is decided by comparing the second-place teams in each division and selecting the one with the best record among them."

Article usage: German lacks the same article system as English, and this shows in places like "the team with the better record receives home field advantage" (correct) vs. inconsistent article use elsewhere. Read through and check each noun phrase for whether "the," "a," or no article is needed.

Comma splices and run-ons: Several sentences string together independent clauses with only commas, eg, "More teams in the playoffs lets people watch more of their favorite players in postseason action, as well as involving more cities in the playoff excitement." Break these into shorter sentences or use semicolons/conjunctions.

Verb agreement: "More teams in the playoffs lets people watch" — "teams" is plural, so this should be "let," not "lets." Watch for subject-verb agreement especially when the subject is a longer noun phrase.

Typos/misspellings: "Galaragga" (Galarraga), "Leagued" (League), "car" (card), "one game" needs a hyphen ("one-game"). A careful proofreading pass will catch these.

Citation Consistency

Your citations mix styles: sometimes you name the source in-text (Major League Baseball), sometimes you use a title in quotation marks and ellipses ("The Wildcard..."), and once you cite what appears to be a standings report without full attribution ("Final 1995 Wild Card Standings"). Choose one consistent citation style (likely MLA, given the discipline) and apply it uniformly. Also include a Works Cited page listing full source information—this is currently missing and is essential for an academic essay using direct quotations.

Paragraph Transitions

Your topic sentences are generally strong (eg, "The wild card provides more competition for the league championship for many reasons"), but transitions *between* paragraphs could be smoother. Consider linking sentences explicitly back to your three-part thesis (more teams, more races, more competition) so the reader always knows which argument you're currently supporting.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Cut the introductory explanation of rules by roughly half.
Add at least one paragraph addressing a counterargument.
Proofread carefully for typos, article usage, and subject-verb agreement.
Standardize citations and add a Works Cited page.
Vary repetitive sentence structures, especially in the conclusion.

You have excellent command of the subject and a logical structure already in place—these revisions will polish the prose and sharpen the argument. Well done overall!

Feedback on Your Essay: Media and the "Right to Know"

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling and timely topic, and your passion is evident throughout. The personal anecdote that opens the essay (the phone call from "The State") is an effective hook, and you clearly care about this issue. However, the essay would benefit from a clearer argumentative structure, more explicit connections between your examples and your central claim, and a final proofreading pass. Below are suggestions organized by area.

Thesis and Structure

Right now, your central argument—that the media's pursuit of "the right to know" increasingly violates personal privacy and damages public life—doesn't appear explicitly until well into the essay. Consider adding a clear thesis statement near the end of your first or second paragraph that tells the reader exactly what you will argue and what evidence you'll use. Something like: "While the media justifies its intrusions with the public's 'right to know,' this principle has been stretched to endanger lives, ruin reputations, and erode the privacy that should be a basic right of every citizen." This gives readers a roadmap for the examples that follow.

Your essay currently reads as a *list* of grievances (Grenada, the Surgeon General nominee, the USC professor, O.J. Simpson, Colin Powell, Reagan/Ford) rather than a *build* toward a conclusion. Each example is strong on its own, but you could strengthen the essay by grouping them thematically—for instance, one paragraph on cases where media reporting caused *concrete harm* (Grenada, troop safety), one on cases of *personal/reputational damage* (the Surgeon General nominee, the professor, Powell), and one on the *commercial motives* behind sensationalism (O.J. Simpson trial). This would make your argument feel more like a logical progression and less like a series of separate complaints.

Developing Your Argument

Several of your points would be more persuasive with a bit more explanation or evidence:

The Grenada/Desert Storm/Bosnia example is powerful, but you could clarify *how* the reporting specifically endangered troops (what was broadcast, when). A brief factual anchor here would strengthen your credibility.
The Surgeon General example — you don't name Dr. Henry Foster, which weakens the historical specificity. Consider adding his name and a brief note on how this fits your broader argument about privacy versus public interest.
The Colin Powell paragraph contains an assumption ("would make a terrific President, as most military leaders have been in the past") that some readers might challenge. Since this is an argumentative essay, anticipating this kind of objection—or softening the claim—would make your reasoning more persuasive.

Addressing Counterarguments

Your essay would be stronger if you directly acknowledged the strongest form of the opposing view. You gesture toward this once ("I suppose this might come under the people's 'right to know'"), but you could develop this more: Why do people believe the media *should* have this right? Is there a legitimate public interest in knowing about a Surgeon General's medical history, for example? By taking the counterargument seriously and then explaining *why* it doesn't outweigh your concerns about privacy and safety, your own position will seem more carefully reasoned rather than simply reactive.

Style and Tone

Your voice is engaging and conversational, which suits an opinion piece, but for an academic argumentative essay, consider tightening some rhetorical questions and exclamations (eg, "Watch out, future warriors of this country") so the tone remains persuasive rather than editorializing. A few sections—like the comparison to Reagan's Alzheimer's or Ford's golf game—could be reworded to sound less flippant and more pointed, since these are actually strong examples of the erosion of dignity you're arguing against.

Language and Proofreading

There are numerous typographical errors that likely resulted from a scanning or transcription process (eg, "successfiil," "fives," "inunune," "gwne," "dodt," "immune"). Since English is your second language, I recommend reading the essay aloud slowly, or having someone else read it aloud to you, to catch these. Pay particular attention to:
Consistent capitalization of proper nouns ("the media's" vs. "The Media's")
Article usage ("a wonder" vs. "it is a wonder")
Verb tense consistency, especially when moving between past examples and present-day commentary

Conclusion

Your final paragraphs effectively return to your opening anecdote, which creates nice symmetry. To strengthen the ending further, consider explicitly restating your thesis in new words and perhaps proposing what responsible journalism *should* look like, giving the reader a constructive takeaway rather than only a critique.

Overall: This is a passionate, example-rich draft. Focus your revision on sharpening the thesis, organizing examples thematically, engaging with counterarguments, and proofreading carefully.

Feedback on Your Essay: "Marijuana Legalization"

Overall Impression

You clearly have a personal investment in this topic, and your essay conveys genuine passion. However, the essay currently reads more like a personal reflection or spoken narrative than an academic argument. To meet the expectations of argumentative writing, you'll need to strengthen your thesis, tighten your structure, and support your claims with credible evidence rather than anecdotes and rhetorical questions. Below are specific areas to work on.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your opening ("It is a plant, and I think it should be legalized") states a position but doesn't tell the reader *why*. A strong thesis should preview your main reasons (eg, medical benefits, economic factors, inconsistent government policy) so the reader knows what argument to expect. Right now, the essay unfolds as a chronological account of your personal experiences with weed, rather than a structured defense of a claim. Consider rewriting your introduction to state clearly: "Marijuana should be legalized because [reason 1], [reason 2], and [reason 3]." Then organize your body paragraphs around those reasons.

Structure and Coherence

The essay moves fluidly between childhood memories, family history, and claims about government corruption, but the connections between these sections aren't always clear. Ask yourself: does this paragraph advance my argument, or is it a story that feels relevant to me but isn't clearly tied to my claim? For example, the anecdote about "Black Moses" is engaging, but you need to extract the *argument* he made (marijuana is natural, unlike processed drugs) and develop it with more than his personal opinion.

Suggestion: Try outlining your essay with clear sections: (1) Introduction with thesis, (2) Medical/practical benefits, (3) Economic and legal inconsistencies, (4) Counterargument and rebuttal, (5) Conclusion. This will help you avoid repeating ideas (eg, the government "getting its cut" appears twice) and give the essay a logical flow.

Evidence and Credibility

This is the area needing the most attention. Right now, your evidence consists mainly of:
Personal anecdotes (family, friends, classmates)
Assertions without sources ("Colombian suppliers make millions every year")
Rhetorical questions ("Did you know marijuana can be used to slow down certain eye ailments?")
Celebrity examples (rappers, the President)

While anecdotes can add color, an academic argument needs verifiable evidence: statistics, studies, or expert testimony. For instance, instead of asking "Did you know marijuana can treat eye ailments?", cite a specific study or medical organization that supports this claim. Similarly, claims about who gets arrested for drug offenses should be backed by criminal justice data, not just personal observation ("You rarely hear about people getting busted bringing drugs into this country").

Also, be cautious with generalizations like "politicians who set these laws up use drugs themselves" — this is an assumption, not a fact, and using it as a reason undermines your credibility unless you can support it.

Logical Consistency

There are a few places where your argument contradicts itself or relies on weak reasoning:
You say "I'm not trying to say that it is some grand scale cover up" right after implying exactly that. Decide whether you're making this claim or not, and be consistent.
The comparison "it only mellows them out much like an advil would" is a reasonable analogy, but it needs elaboration — how is this evidence for legalization specifically?
Naming individuals who used marijuana (Snoop, Biggie, the President) doesn't prove marijuana is harmless; this is closer to an appeal to popularity than solid evidence.

Tone and Register

Academic argumentative essays generally avoid direct address to the reader ("Do you ever notice...", "Did you know...") and casual phrasing ("talking out of his head," "mega bucks," "nice clothes and drove nice cars"). While these expressions give your writing personality, they read as informal in an academic context. Try to maintain a more measured, analytical tone, even while expressing conviction.

Language and Grammar (common patterns to watch)

As a German native speaker, watch for these recurring issues:
its / it's and their / there / they're confusion (eg, "and their are nearly no long lasting side effects")
Comma splices and run-on sentences — many sentences string together independent clauses without proper punctuation (eg, "I always told myself that even though they did it back in the 70's it still wasn't right.")
Capitalization inconsistency (eg, "european countries" should be "European")
Missing apostrophes in possessives and contractions ("in it's leafy form" should be "in its leafy form")
Proofread carefully for typos like "c ession" (session) and "critisize" (criticize).

Final Thought

Your voice and passion are strong assets — don't lose them in revision. Instead, channel that energy into a more focused, evidence-based argument. Start by rewriting your thesis, then reorganize your paragraphs around 2–3 clear reasons, each supported by credible sources. This will transform your essay from a personal narrative into a persuasive academic argument.

Feedback on Your Essay: Shield Laws and Journalist-Source Confidentiality

Overall Impression

You've clearly done substantial research on this topic, and your case studies (Maryland, South Carolina, Ohio, Watergate, Boston, California) provide rich, specific evidence. This is a real strength. However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of well-researched examples than a unified argument. To strengthen this into a persuasive academic essay, you need to sharpen your thesis, improve structural organization, and tighten your use of sources. Below are the main areas to focus on.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your concluding paragraph reveals your actual position clearly: shield laws should be protected and strengthened. However, this argument doesn't appear clearly at the beginning of the essay. Readers must wait until the final paragraphs to understand your stance. 

Revision suggestion: State your thesis explicitly in the introduction—something like: "While shield laws exist in 28 states to protect the reporter-source relationship, these laws are inconsistently applied and frequently undermined by courts. This essay argues that shield laws must be strengthened and standardized to protect both journalistic integrity and the public's right to information."

Structure and Paragraph Transitions

The essay moves through many examples (Maryland 1896, South Carolina 1993, Ohio, Watergate, Boston, California) but doesn't clearly explain *why* each example is included or how it supports your argument. Right now, the organization feels chronological/encyclopedic rather than persuasive.

Suggestion: Group your examples by the *point* they prove:
Examples showing shield laws working well (Watergate, Boston case)
Examples showing shield laws failing or being circumvented (South Carolina/Decker, Ohio/Swasy, California/Serrano)
Then use this contrast to argue why stronger, more uniform protection is needed

Add transitional sentences like "This case illustrates how..." or "In contrast to the previous example..." to guide your reader through your reasoning, not just through the facts.

Analysis vs. Summary

Many paragraphs (especially the case studies) are almost pure summary. You explain *what happened* but not *what it means* for your argument. For instance, after the Decker/South Carolina case, you could add 2-3 sentences analyzing why the court's distinction (trial court vs. plaintiff/defendant) is problematic or reasonable, and how it weakens the shield law's protective function.

Ask yourself after each example: "So what? How does this support my thesis?"

Quotations and Citations

You have several formatting errors with quotation marks (eg, "his sense of humor would not permit him to do so", stray commas, inconsistent italics/quotes for terms like *Deep Throat*). Proofread carefully for consistent punctuation.
The long block quote from the 1896 Maryland law is useful but should be introduced with a sentence explaining what to look for in it, and possibly followed by analysis (eg, "Notice that this law makes no exception for criminal cases—a gap that later legislation would need to address").
Check that every quotation has correct in-text citation formatting (page numbers, consistent style).
The statistic about subpoenas ("almost 4,500... has definitely increased") needs a citation. Where does this number come from?

Sentence-Level and Language Issues

As a German-English writer, watch for these specific patterns:

Article usage: "the trust bond" vs. "a trust bond" — English articles function differently than German der/die/das. Reread sentences aloud to check if articles sound natural.
Comma splices and punctuation: Several sentences have stray commas or missing ones (eg, "In a very recent example,, Twila Decker..." has a double comma; "Alecia Swasy,, was researching" also has an error).
Word choice: "the way other states handle cases... is varied greatly" → "varies greatly" (subject-verb agreement).
Register: The concluding paragraph shifts to short, punchy imperative sentences ("Protect the Shield Laws. Don't make people scared to talk.") This works rhetorically but feels stylistically different from the rest of the essay. Consider whether this fits the academic register expected, or whels revising it to sound less like a slogan and more like a reasoned conclusion.

Counterargument

Your essay would be stronger with an explicit counterargument section. Why might courts and prosecutors *need* to compel disclosure sometimes (eg, enforcing court orders, criminal investigations)? Address this directly, then explain why you still believe shield laws should prevail. This will make your argument more persuasive and show critical engagement with the issue, which is expected in academic argumentative writing.

Priority Action Steps for Revision

Add a clear thesis statement in your introduction.
Reorganize examples into thematic groups that build your argument.
Add analytical sentences after each case study explaining its significance.
Proofread punctuation carefully, especially quotation marks and commas.
Add a counterargument paragraph before your conclusion.
Check all citations for completeness and consistency.

You have excellent raw material here—now it's a matter of shaping it into a persuasive, analytically driven essay rather than a report of facts. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback on Your Essay on Women at the Citadel

Overall Impression

You have chosen a strong, debatable topic and you clearly have a personal stake in the argument, which gives your writing energy and conviction. You also do a good job identifying the main counterarguments (tradition, physical strength, distraction, public funding) and organizing your essay around refuting them one by one. This is a solid essay structure for argumentative writing. However, the essay needs work in three main areas: thesis clarity, paragraph organization/transitions, and language accuracy. Below are suggestions for revision.

Thesis and Introduction

Your introduction states the debate but never clearly commits to your position. Readers should know your stance by the end of paragraph one. Consider a sentence like: *"Despite claims of tradition and physical difference, women should be admitted to the Citadel because equal access to public education is a fundamental right."* This gives your essay a clear argumentative spine that each paragraph can support.

Structure and Paragraph Development

Each body paragraph tackles a different objection, which is a good strategy, but the connections between paragraphs are weak. Add transitional sentences that link back to your thesis ("Another argument against admitting women concerns..."). Also, each paragraph would benefit from a clear topic sentence stating the objection you are about to refute, followed by your rebuttal, then a concluding sentence that ties back to your main argument.

Your essay currently has no conclusion. This is a significant gap — you need a final paragraph that restates your thesis, briefly summarizes your main points, and leaves the reader with a final thought (eg, about equality, opportunity, or societal progress).

Logical Development

Some of your reasoning needs tightening:

The comparison between the Citadel's policy and slavery is a strong rhetorical move, but it may feel like an overstatement to some readers. Consider using a comparison closer to the topic, such as other historically single-sex public institutions that became coeducational (eg, VMI, which is directly relevant to the Citadel case).
The paragraph about physical strength makes a good point (not all men or women meet the same standard), but you could strengthen it with a concrete example or statistic instead of general statements.
The paragraph on public funding and discrimination is one of your strongest — the "hispanic/Polish" hypothetical works well to illustrate the absurdity of discrimination. Keep developing this kind of concrete analogy elsewhere too.

Language and Style

As a German-speaking writer, you are dealing with several common English challenges. Here are patterns to watch for:

Spelling errors: appearred → appeared; alao → also; desciniinate → discriminate; pubfically → publicly; learening → learning; eqaul → equal. Please proofread carefully or use a spellchecker before submitting.
Capitalization: "hispanic" and "poliish" should be capitalized as proper adjectives: *Hispanic*, *Polish*.
Subject-verb agreement and articles: "That questions is" → "That question is"; "Everyone does deserve" is fine, but "Every deserves a good education" should be "Everyone deserves a good education." German article use differs from English, so double-check phrases like "the Citadel" (correct) vs. missing articles elsewhere.
Word choice/false friends: "excellence discipline" → "excellent discipline"; "less superior" is redundant — you likely mean "inferior."
Register: Phrases like "Yes, it has been tradition..." and "Sure, there may a few cases" are conversational. For academic writing, aim for a more formal tone: "While it is true that the Citadel has traditionally admitted only men, tradition alone does not justify continued exclusion."
Run-on sentences and comma splices: For example, "That may be true in some cases, but others might be able to handle the challenges, Everyone does deserve the chance to try" needs a period or semicolon before "Everyone."

Suggestions for Revision

Write a clear thesis statement and place it at the end of your introduction.
Add topic sentences to each paragraph that name the counterargument you're refuting.
Write a conclusion paragraph.
Proofread systematically for spelling, capitalization, and subject-verb agreement — reading the essay aloud can help you catch errors.
Where possible, replace general claims with specific examples or evidence (court cases, statistics, or the VMI precedent) to strengthen your credibility.

Final Note

Your argument is compelling and your instincts as a writer are good — you organize your rebuttals logically and you write with conviction. With attention to structure (especially a strong thesis and conclusion) and careful proofreading, this essay could be very persuasive. I encourage you to revise with these points in mind and feel free to share your next draft for further feedback.
