Feedback on Your Essay: "The Status of the Stay-at-Home Wife"

Overall Impression

You have chosen a topic you clearly feel strongly about, and your passion comes through. However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of related thoughts than a tightly organized argument. My feedback below focuses on structure, development, and language, with the goal of helping you produce a more focused and polished revision.

Argument and Structure

Your central claim—that a stay-at-home wife should be treated as an equal partner, not a subordinate—is a strong and defensible thesis. However, it is buried in the middle of a long opening paragraph rather than stated clearly at the outset. Consider opening with a single, clear thesis sentence, then use your introduction to preview the main lines of argument (eg, unpaid domestic labor has real value; equality should be legally formalized; social judgment of stay-at-home wives is unfair).

The essay's structure is currently associative rather than logical: paragraphs shift between the television anecdote, your own family, legal/financial arguments, and social criticism without clear transitions. Each paragraph should have one controlling idea, stated in a topic sentence, and should connect explicitly back to your thesis. For example, the paragraph about your mother's exhausting daily schedule is one of your strongest pieces of evidence—but it needs a clearer topic sentence explaining *why* you are including it (to show that paid work and domestic work both deserve equal respect) and a concluding sentence linking it back to the equality argument.

Development and Evidence

Your strongest argument is the practical one: that unpaid domestic labor has real economic value (illustrated by the housekeeper's hourly wage) and that legal protections (joint ownership on mortgages/contracts) are necessary to protect wives in case of a husband's death. This is a concrete, persuasive point—develop it further and consider moving it earlier, as it gives your essay a legal/economic backbone rather than relying only on personal opinion.

The television anecdote is currently unusable in its present form: the dialogue is missing (marked only with  placeholders). Please either reconstruct the dialogue from memory or remove the anecdote and replace it with a hypothetical example, since a reader cannot follow the argument without knowing what was actually said.

Some claims are asserted without support and should be qualified or removed: "In most marriages when the wife stays home it's usually the consent of both partners" and "Lots of people seem to view a stay home wife as inferior" are broad generalizations. Since this is an argumentative essay, you don't need statistical proof, but avoid absolute words like "most," "always," or "lots of people" unless you can support them—consider phrases like "it is often argued that…" or "in my experience…"

Language and Style

A few patterns typical of German-influenced English appear throughout and are worth addressing systematically:

Comma splices and run-on sentences: Many sentences join independent clauses with only a comma (eg, "The husband then went on to say... He then struck back by saying..."). English requires a period, semicolon, or conjunction. Please review comma-splice rules specifically.

Placement of "however": You frequently begin sentences with "However," followed by a comma, which is correct, but you also sometimes insert "however" mid-sentence with incorrect punctuation ("she is,doing"). Check comma placement carefully.

Article usage: Occasional missing or misplaced articles ("this is a source of income" vs. "these are sources of income")—common for German speakers, since German article rules differ from English ones. Proofread specifically for "a/the" omissions.

Register: The essay mixes casual expressions ("the best of both worlds," "watch the soaps," "what's good for the goose isn't good for the gander") with more formal argumentative prose. For an academic essay, aim for consistent formal register—either remove these idioms or use them sparingly and deliberately for effect.

Repetition: The phrase "utmost respect/sincerity" appears twice in close proximity; vary your vocabulary.

Outdated/specific detail: The $4.25/hour minimum wage reference dates the essay; if this is not deliberate, consider using a general phrase like "minimum wage" instead.

Suggestions for Revision

Write a one-sentence thesis and place it at the end of your introduction.
Reorganize paragraphs so each has one clear topic sentence and ends with a link back to the thesis.
Fix or remove the incomplete TV dialogue.
Strengthen the legal/economic argument as your central evidence.
Proofread specifically for comma splices, article errors, and "however" punctuation.
Tighten the conclusion so it restates your thesis and central argument, rather than introducing a new idea (career choice) at the very end.

You have real insight and personal experience to draw on—channeling it into a tighter structure will make your argument much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely controversial and interesting topic, and you've clearly done research to support your points. You bring in historical facts, statistics, and even literary/scholarly sources, which shows good effort. However, the essay has a significant structural problem: you announce one purpose but then pursue another. This report will focus on this core issue along with argumentation, evidence, and language concerns.

Contradiction Between Stated Purpose and Actual Argument

This is the most important issue to address. In your introduction, you explicitly state: *"The purpose of this passage is not to convince you to legalize marijuana... Rather, its purpose is to inform you on all the facts."* You even say readers should be free to choose either position "as long as you have justifiable reasons."

However, your essay does not remain neutral. By the second half, you are clearly arguing *for* legalization: you discuss "what is to be gained," describe how legalization would "elevate much of the crime," bring in "billions of dollars," and you close with "there can only be one logical answer" — strongly suggesting there is only one correct conclusion.

Recommendation: Decide which essay you actually want to write:
Option A: A genuinely informative/balanced essay. If you choose this, you must present counterarguments too (eg, why some people believe marijuana should remain illegal, potential downsides of legalization) and remove language that pushes readers toward one conclusion.
Option B: An argumentative essay in favor of legalization. If you choose this, revise your thesis to state this position clearly, and you can keep much of your existing evidence, but you should also address counterarguments to make your argument more persuasive and credible.

Given the strength of your ending and structure, Option B seems more natural for you. Consider rewriting your introduction to reflect this.

Structure and Paragraph Organization

Your paragraph structure follows a clear, logical progression (history → physical effects → mental effects → comparison to alcohol → crime/economic reasons → conclusion), which is a strength. However, some paragraphs contain only one or two undeveloped ideas. For example, the paragraph on mental effects is very short and could be expanded with more explanation of *why* memory loss occurs or its severity compared to other effects.

Recommendation: Consider merging very short paragraphs with related ones, or expanding them with more analysis rather than just facts. Don't just state facts — explain their significance for your argument.

Use of Evidence

You cite several sources (DEA, FDA, Department of Agriculture, a psychology textbook), which is excellent for building credibility. However:

You need proper in-text citations and a works cited list. Academic writing requires you to show exactly where a quote or statistic comes from (author, year, page number if possible).
Some statistics need updating or double-checking — "400,000 die from diseases related to smoking cigarettes" and "150,000 from alcohol" should be verified for accuracy and cited to a specific, credible source.
The claim "NO ONE has ever died from marijuana" is an overgeneralization. Even if direct overdose deaths are rare, this claim needs a qualifying source or softer phrasing (eg, "no confirmed direct overdose deaths have been recorded").

Recommendation: Add a Works Cited/References page and in-text citations (eg, APA or MLA, depending on your instructor's requirement).

Language and Grammar

As a German speaker, watch for these recurring issues:

Typos: "goverwnent," "eanount," "Pgychology," "high'" (with a curly quote mark issue), "suffers" (should be "sufferers"). Proofread carefully — these are likely typing errors rather than language knowledge gaps.
Comma splices: "The next step in formulating an opinion, is to examine..." — remove the comma before "is."
Article usage: German doesn't always require articles where English does, and vice versa. Check phrases like "the president admits to smoking it" (needs context — which president, when).
Capitalization: "Marijuana" doesn't need to be capitalized throughout (it's not a proper noun) — pick one convention and apply it consistently.
Word choice: "elevate crime" should likely be "reduce" or "alleviate" crime — "elevate" means to increase, which contradicts your intended meaning.

Tone

The essay sometimes uses a conversational tone ("People often ask themselves...") which is common in spoken argument but should be adapted for academic writing. Consider paraphrasing rhetorical questions into declarative topic sentences.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Resolve the purpose contradiction — this is essential and should be fixed first.
Add proper citations for all quoted or statistical material.
Address counterarguments to strengthen persuasive credibility.
Proofread carefully for typos and grammar.
Adjust tone for academic register.

You have strong raw material here — with revision, this could become a compelling argumentative essay. Focus first on clarifying your purpose, then refine the details.

Feedback on Your Essay: Double Standards for Women

Thank you for tackling such an important and complex topic. Your passion for the subject is clear, and you raise valid points about how history, religion, media, and childhood socialization have shaped double standards for women. Below are some observations to help you strengthen the essay in a revision.

Clarify Your Thesis and Structure

Right now, the essay moves chronologically through history, religion, childhood, media, and rights lists, but it's not always clear what central argument connects these sections. Consider stating a clear thesis early on—for example: "Double standards for women, rooted in historical and religious tradition, persist today through media representation and childhood socialization, despite the gains of the feminist movement." Then organize each paragraph to visibly support one part of that claim. Right now, some paragraphs (like the one on the "Bill of Rights") feel like a list inserted without being integrated into your argument—explain *why* this list matters to your point before or after presenting it.

Missing Text and Citation Gaps

There's a placeholder  where a quote should appear, and the following paragraph refers to "this quote" without the reader ever seeing it. Make sure the actual quotation is inserted before you comment on it. Also, your citation "(Bloom, 24)" needs a proper introduction—tell the reader who Bloom is and why this source is credible (eg, "According to psychologist Lynn Bloom, women have basic rights, including..."). This will make your paper feel more academically grounded.

Watch for a Key Word Confusion: "Warm" vs. "Women"

I noticed the word "warm" appears several times where you clearly mean "women" (eg, "the equality of warm," "what society deems a warm," "warm are expected to behave"). This looks like a spelling autocorrect error rather than a language issue tied to German–English differences, but please proofread carefully and use "Find and Replace" to catch every instance—this happens repeatedly and is very distracting for the reader.

Grammar and Word Choice (Common German-to-English Interference)

A few patterns to watch:
Missing subjects: "It stems back from the past when were strictly in the home" — you need a subject: "when *women* were strictly in the home." This happens elsewhere too (eg, "the young might also be chaperoned m her date" should likely be "the young *woman*... on her date").
Article usage: English requires "a" or "the" more consistently than German in some contexts—for instance, "women were treated cared of" should be "women were taken care of."
False friends/word choice: "hard tire" should be "hard time"; "particulary" → "particularly"; "appropiate" → "appropriate"; "nuturing" → "nurturing"; "intellingence" → "intelligence."
Verb agreement and tense shifts: You move between present and past tense frequently within the same paragraph (eg, discussing history in present tense: "This is why early society placed..."). Decide whether you're narrating history (past tense) or describing ongoing patterns (present tense), and be consistent within each section.

I recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, or using a grammar-checking tool like Grammarly, to catch these smaller errors before submission.

Logical Consistency

In your conclusion, you write that double standards are unjust, but then compare them to school rules that "should be adhered to." This creates a contradiction—are double standards good structure (like rules) or unjust oppression? Revise this comparison; perhaps you mean to say that *fair* rules (like equal treatment) should be adhered to, whereas *double standards* should be abolished. Make this distinction explicit.

Strengthen Your Evidence

Much of the essay relies on generalizations ("men felt," "society believes") without specific historical examples, statistics, or named sources. Consider incorporating:
A specific date or event from the suffrage movement.
A cited example of media double standards (a specific TV show, advertisement, or study).
Reference to a scholarly source on gender socialization in children's literature (you could cite specific research on fairy tales and gender roles, since you allude to this idea already).

This will move your essay from personal opinion toward persuasive, evidence-based argumentation.

Tone

Your personal voice is engaging, especially in the conclusion where you speak from experience. However, phrases like "fine and dandy" and "uppity" work well as ironic commentary on historical attitudes, but make sure your reader understands you're being ironic, not reflecting your own view. Consider adding a signal like "as men of the time might have said" to clarify tone.

Summary of Priorities for Revision:
Insert the missing quotation.
Fix "warm" → "women" throughout.
Strengthen thesis and paragraph transitions.
Add specific, cited evidence.
Resolve the contradiction about double standards vs. rules.
Proofread for subject omission and article usage.

You have a strong, opinionated voice—now let's make sure the structure and language fully support it!

Feedback Report: Censorship Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling, socially relevant topic and clearly feel passionate about it. This passion comes through in your writing, and you've done research to find specific court cases and statistics. However, the essay currently reads as a draft rather than a polished piece: the argument needs sharper focus, the evidence needs to be integrated more smoothly, and there are numerous language issues that obscure your ideas. Below are the main areas to work on.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your introduction poses several rhetorical questions but never crystallizes into a clear thesis statement. What exactly are you arguing? Is it that:
book banning violates the First Amendment,
censorship is hypocritical because children encounter "adult" content elsewhere anyway, or
specific institutions (school boards, parent groups) overstep their authority?

You gesture at all three but never commit to one central claim. Revise your introduction so that your last sentence is a clear, arguable thesis (eg, "This essay argues that book censorship in American schools and libraries is both unconstitutional and ultimately futile, since it fails to protect children from the very content it seeks to suppress"). Every paragraph should then visibly support this thesis.

Structure and Paragraph Coherence

Currently, your body paragraphs are organized by *example* (Ms. magazine, Arapahoe books, Madonna's *Sex*) rather than by *argument*. This makes the essay feel like a list of cases rather than a developed argument. For each paragraph, ask: "What point does this case prove, and how does it connect to my thesis?" Add a topic sentence stating the point *before* the example, and a concluding sentence explaining what the example demonstrates.

Your conclusion introduces new material (Vonnegut quote, Maya Angelou's memoir) that would be better placed in the body, where it can be analyzed. Save the conclusion for synthesizing your argument, not adding new evidence.

Evidence and Citation

You cite specific court cases and statistics, which is excellent—but you need to:
Cite your sources properly. Where does the PAW statistic come from? Which edition/page of *Attacks on the Freedom to Learn*? Academic writing requires you to credit sources formally (eg, MLA or APA), not just mention organization names.
Explain the cases more fully. In *Cary v. Board of Education*, you state the board banned books "without reason" and that teachers' First Amendment rights were violated, but then say "nothing could be done"—this is confusing. Clarify the legal reasoning: why did the court rule this way despite the rights violation? This nuance would actually strengthen your argument by showing the complexity of the issue.
Analyze, don't just report. After each case, add a sentence of analysis connecting it explicitly to your thesis.

Language and Grammar

As a German speaker, watch for these recurring issues:

Article usage: German lacks the English distinction in usage that trips up learners (eg, "the Constitution of the United States provided all people with the fireedom of speech" — should be "provides" [present tense, since the Constitution still does this] and "freedom," not "fireedom").
Verb tense consistency: You shift between past and present unnecessarily. Since you're discussing an ongoing societal issue, prefer the present tense for general claims ("censorship groups attempt to ban books") and past tense only for specific historical events (the Gutenberg press, specific court rulings).
Word order/calques: "the go as far as to bum them" should be "they go as far as to burn them." Watch for missing subjects and typos throughout—this suggests the essay needs a careful proofreading pass.
False friends/word choice: "provide with the authority" is slightly awkward; "grants authority" is more natural. "Waived under an previous agreement" — check article usage ("a previous agreement").
Punctuation: There are many stray commas, quotation marks, and typographical errors (eg, "New Hampshire,," "case,,"). These likely stem from editing in a word processor—do a dedicated proofreading pass focused only on punctuation.

I recommend reading your essay aloud, or having someone else read it aloud to you—this often catches missing words and awkward phrasing that silent reading misses.

Tone and Persuasive Strategy

Phrases like "try to stop the world from turning" are vivid but overly casual for academic writing. Similarly, rhetorical questions ("What right do we... have to say...") can work in moderation but shouldn't replace argumentation. Consider converting some rhetorical questions into declarative claims backed by evidence.

Suggested Next Steps

Write a one-sentence thesis and test whether every paragraph supports it.
Reorganize paragraphs so each begins with a claim, not an example.
Properly cite all sources (check with your instructor which style is required).
Do a dedicated grammar/proofreading pass, paying special attention to articles, tense consistency, and punctuation.
Strengthen the conclusion by synthesizing your argument rather than introducing new evidence.

You have real analytical potential here—the raw material is strong. The next revision should focus on *argumentative clarity* and *language precision*.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Confederate Flag Controversy

Thank you for tackling this complex and sensitive topic. You've clearly done research and gathered a range of perspectives, which is commendable. However, the essay needs significant revision to become a strong piece of argumentative writing. Below are my main concerns, organized by priority.

Unclear and Underdeveloped Thesis

Your essay lacks a clear argumentative thesis. An argumentative essay must take a position and defend it with reasoning—yours currently reads more like a report that catalogs various opinions without synthesizing them into your own argument. Your conclusion states, "there is no reason to fight over something that was established so many years ago" and warns that removing the flag "will only lead to bigger controversy down the road." This is your actual thesis, but it appears only in the final paragraph, unsupported by the analysis that precedes it. You need to state this position clearly in your introduction and then structure every subsequent paragraph to build toward it.

Imbalanced Use of Evidence

This is the most serious structural problem. You present multiple quotations defending the flag (Lunford, Cavera, Lukes, Campbell) but your conclusion doesn't actually engage with the strongest counterarguments—particularly the historical evidence you yourself include. Most notably, the Alexander Stephens quotation is extremely damning: he explicitly states the Confederacy was founded on the principle that Black people are inherently inferior. This is a direct, primary-source refutation of the "heritage, not hate" argument your essay seems to lean toward. Yet you don't analyze this quotation or reconcile it with your final claim that the flag represents ancestors who "fought for what they thought were their rights." You must directly address this tension, not simply let contradictory evidence sit side by side.

Similarly, Senator Campbell's comparison of the Confederate flag to the US flag (both representing violence) is a logical move called a *tu quoque* argument ("you also do it")—it doesn't actually defend the Confederate flag; it just says another flag is equally bad. Point this out if you use it, rather than letting it stand as effective proof.

Overreliance on Quotation, Underdeveloped Analysis

Long stretches of your essay are simply strings of quotations from your sources (Riley, *The Post and Courier*, *USA Today*) with minimal transition or interpretation. For example, the paragraph with Laverne Thomson, Elena Minor, Al Lukes, Patricia Lucas, and Charles Blair simply lists five opinions and then says, "Those are some different views of people around the USA." This sentence adds nothing—readers can see that themselves. After each quotation (or small group of quotations), add 1-2 sentences explaining *why* this quotation matters and *how* it supports or complicates your argument.

Citation Format

You cite sources as (Riley 30), (Davis 10), etc, but these appear to be page numbers from articles, not full bibliographic references. Check with your instructor which citation style is required (likely MLA, given the format). You will need a Works Cited page listing full source information (author, article title, publication, date). In-text citations should correspond exactly to entries there.

Grammar and Mechanics

As a German speaker, watch for these recurring issues:
Comma splices and fragments: "There is always going to be controversy for since we live in this world. Whether it is over a flag or anything else." The second sentence is a fragment; combine it with the first.
Article usage: "the united Daughters of the Confederacy" should be capitalized ("United Daughters of the Confederacy") throughout.
Homophone/spelling errors: "fory" (forty), "siad" (said), "todl" (told), "empathizes" (should likely be "concurs" or "agrees"—empathize doesn't fit this context), "Charles Lunford" is misspelled as "Carles."
Verb agreement and word order: "That slavery subordinates to the superior race" is a garbled quotation—check the original Stephens speech for accuracy, as this sentence doesn't make grammatical sense in English.

Please proofread carefully or use a grammar-checking tool, then do a final human read-through, since automated tools often miss context-specific errors like these.

Paragraph Organization

Consider organizing by argument rather than by source. For example, group all "heritage" arguments together, analyze them, then group all "racism" arguments together, analyze them, then move to your synthesis/rebuttal. This will help you avoid the current "list of quotations" feel.

Suggested Next Steps
Write a clear one-sentence thesis and place it in your introduction.
Go through each source and ask: "Does this support or complicate my thesis? How?"
Directly address the Stephens quotation—it's your biggest challenge and cannot be ignored.
Revise for grammar and citation format.

You have strong raw material here—now it needs a clear argumentative spine. I'm happy to discuss further in office hours.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a topical and engaging subject—frivolous lawsuits and their costs to taxpayers—and you clearly have real-world knowledge and passion for this issue. Your essay presents a logical chain of cause and effect (frivolous lawsuits → court backlogs → higher costs → slower justice for legitimate cases). This is a solid argumentative structure. However, the essay needs revision in several areas: paragraph organization, evidence/sourcing, tone, and language precision. Below are specific areas to focus on.

Structure and Organization

Your essay currently reads as one long argument without a clear roadmap. Consider using a more explicit structure:

Introduction with a clear thesis statement. Right now, your thesis is implied ("changes could cut costs") but never stated precisely. Try something like: "Frivolous civil lawsuits waste taxpayer money and delay justice for legitimate cases; therefore, lawmakers should implement reforms to discourage baseless litigation."

Body paragraphs organized by *category of problem* (eg, one paragraph for prison lawsuits, one for staged accidents, one for workplace/malpractice effects, one for court backlogs). Currently, these ideas blend together within paragraphs, making it harder for readers to follow your logic.

Conclusion/Solutions — your final paragraph offering solutions is strong; consider expanding it slightly, since solutions are often what distinguish a persuasive essay from a complaint.

Evidence and Credibility

This is the area needing the most attention. You make many factual claims ("It costs millions of tax dollars," "hundreds of lawsuits," "several rings of accident stagers arrested recently") without citing sources. For an academic argumentative essay, you need:

Statistics from credible sources (state judicial reports, insurance industry studies, academic research on litigation costs)
Specific examples with citations rather than "I heard of a case last week." Vague anecdotes weaken your credibility. If you reference the inmate lawsuits (ice cream flavor, hair, underwear), find and cite actual case names or news reports.
Attribution for numbers like "three to five years for a civil case to be tried" — where does this come from? Is this true nationally, or in a specific state?

Without sourced evidence, your argument sounds like opinion rather than a documented case. This is the single most important revision needed.

Tone and Word Choice

Your tone occasionally slips into casual or emotionally charged language, which undermines academic credibility:
"Seems like everybody is suing everybody these days" — replace with more formal phrasing and data.
"ridiculous cases," "ridiculous lawsuits," "begging for people to call them" — while your frustration is understandable, academic writing benefits from measured language even when arguing a strong position. Consider "meritless," "unsubstantiated," or "frivolous" (which you already use well elsewhere).
"It cost you and I millions" — check grammar (should be "you and me" as object of the verb) and avoid the very informal direct address; consider "taxpayers" instead of "you and I" for consistency with academic register.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are numerous typos that suggest this draft wasn't fully proofread (likely from OCR or scanning issues): "wn t~g" (probably "writing"), "fiLI1" (probably "fall"), "ft~tes" (probably "inmates"), "bmtes," "wnount," "cems," "govermnent," "lie insurance" (probably "The insurance"), "payoff" (should be two words: "pay off"), "corporation" (should likely be "cooperation"). Please proofread carefully or run a spell-check before submitting your final draft—these errors distract from otherwise clear ideas.

Also watch subject-verb agreement: "the trend s cems to be moving" should be "the trend seems." And "Judges must also be mandated to waste no time with these cases" is a bit awkward—consider "Judges must be required to resolve these cases without delay."

Logical Connections

Your causal chain in the fourth paragraph (fewer civil cases → freed courts → faster criminal trials → fewer people in jail awaiting trial) is a compelling argument, but each link needs support. Is there evidence that civil and criminal courts actually share resources this directly in most jurisdictions? Acknowledging this assumption or providing evidence would strengthen your argument.

Addressing Counterarguments

A stronger essay would acknowledge the *other side*: Why might someone support broad access to lawsuits, even if some are frivolous? For example, some "frivolous-sounding" cases (like inmate rights cases) sometimes reveal real constitutional violations. Briefly addressing this would show critical thinking and strengthen your credibility, rather than weakening your argument.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Add citations and concrete evidence for all statistical/factual claims
Reorganize into clearer topic-based paragraphs
State an explicit thesis
Formalize tone — remove casual phrases
Proofread carefully for typos and grammar
Consider a brief counterargument for balance

You have a strong argumentative instinct and real conviction in this piece—now it needs the scholarly infrastructure (sources, structure, precision) to match. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback Report: Government Shutdown Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a genuinely interesting topic, and your engagement with a real historical event (the 1995 government shutdown) gives your essay concrete grounding. Your ability to research specific details—numbers of laid-off workers, dates, names of officials—shows real effort. However, the essay currently reads more like a factual report with occasional opinions inserted rather than a focused argumentative essay. Below are the main areas to work on, roughly in order of priority.

Sharpen Your Thesis and Argument

Your opening quotation from Jefferson suggests your argument will be about partisanship as the root cause of government dysfunction. This is a strong, debatable claim. However, the essay drifts between several different arguments:
Partisanship causes shutdowns (Jefferson's point)
The constitutional design (checks and balances) is flawed
Ordinary Americans unfairly suffer for politicians' mistakes
The shutdown will recur because the "pact" is temporary

Suggestion: Choose ONE central claim and make every paragraph serve it. Given your strong material on economic consequences and the "kids fighting on a playground" comparison, I'd suggest making your thesis something like: *"Partisan gridlock, not the structure of American government itself, caused unnecessary suffering for ordinary citizens during the 1995 shutdown."* Then cut or subordinate material that doesn't support this.

Reorganize for Logical Flow

Right now the essay moves: Jefferson → 1995 shutdown (general) → fiscal year explanation → pros/cons of party division → constitutional design → 1995 bill details → 1962 shutdown → job loss statistics → "who's right?" → veto process → economic effects → resolution. This creates a back-and-forth structure where background information appears in multiple places (you introduce the 1995 shutdown twice, for example).

Suggestion: Try this structure:
Introduction with thesis (keep Jefferson quote)
Background: how the fiscal year/budget process works (combine your two explanations)
Narrative: what happened in 1995, step by step, chronologically
Analysis: why this happened (partisanship, veto standoff)
Consequences: effects on workers and the economy
Conclusion: connect back to Jefferson, warn about the future

Distinguish Fact from Opinion More Clearly

You often signal opinion with "I feel," which is appropriate for an argumentative essay, but in academic English, "I feel" is used for emotions, not intellectual judgments. Use "I believe" or "I argue" instead. Also, try to integrate your opinions more smoothly into the analysis rather than dropping them as separate sentences.

Instead of: *"I feel the American people have been unfairly made to pay..."*
Try: *"The 800,000 furloughed workers were, I argue, unfairly forced to pay for a political conflict they had no role in creating."*

Language and Grammar Notes

Since English article and preposition usage differs significantly from German, here are patterns to watch for:

Articles: "the constitution," "the government" (definite article needed even when general, unlike German's more flexible usage) — you do this well in most places, but check consistency.
Verb tense consistency: You shift between past tense (narrating 1995 events) and present tense (general claims). Keep narration in simple past and analysis in present tense, and be consistent.
Sentence fragments: "Starting with the release of all nonessential personnel and ending with the president of the United States." This is not a complete sentence—attach it to the previous sentence with a comma.
Comma splices: "Both sides then proceeded to argue and call each other names, 1, the November deadline had passed." This sentence is unclear—check what you meant here and rewrite as two separate sentences.
Apostrophes: "congress'" should be "Congress's"; watch for stray apostrophes in words like "'m seven years" (likely a typo for "in seven years") and "TheShutdown'" (missing space, stray apostrophe).
Capitalization: "govermnent's" (typo), inconsistent capitalization of "Congress" vs. "congress" — pick one convention and use it throughout (capitalize when referring to the specific U.S. Congress).

Strengthen the Conclusion

Your ending ("I say no...") is a good instinct—predicting that the problem will recur—but it needs to loop back to your thesis and the Jefferson quote from the introduction. Right now the essay just stops after the informational point about the pact being temporary. Add 2-3 sentences explicitly connecting this back to your argument about partisanship.

Next Steps

Rewrite your thesis statement clearly at the end of paragraph 1.
Create an outline using the structure suggested above, and place each existing paragraph into the appropriate section.
Do a separate proofreading pass focused only on grammar (dates, apostrophes, tense).
Read your conclusion and introduction side-by-side—do they match?

You have excellent raw material here. The revision work now is about shaping and focusing, not generating new content.

Feedback on Your Essay: "The Right to Abortion"

Overall Impression

You have chosen a genuinely controversial and important topic, and your passion for the subject is evident throughout the essay. You raise several distinct arguments (trauma from rape, socioeconomic factors, legal precedent, government overreach, and practical consequences of criminalization), which shows you have thought about the issue from multiple angles. However, the essay currently reads as a list of loosely connected points rather than a unified argument. Below are suggestions to help you strengthen the content, structure, and language.

Thesis and Structure

Your introduction states your position ("Abortion is the right of the people and it should remain so"), but the preview of your arguments is confusing — the sentence beginning "Hapeo povertyg personal choice..." appears to be a fragment or typing error and should be revised so your reader knows exactly which arguments to expect and in what order.

Each of your paragraphs raises a different point, but they are not connected with transitions, and several lack a clear topic sentence that states the paragraph's main claim before providing support. For example, your paragraph on socioeconomic status jumps from "children born into poverty remain poor" to family size statistics without explaining *why* this matters for your argument about abortion rights. Make the logical connection explicit: are you arguing that abortion reduces poverty, or that poor women need access to abortion for their own autonomy? These are different claims and should not be blurred together, especially since the poverty argument as written risks sounding like you are suggesting abortion should reduce the number of poor people — a claim with troubling historical associations you likely do not intend. Consider revising this section carefully.

The essay also ends abruptly after the point about adoption costs. You need a concluding paragraph that restates your thesis and synthesizes your strongest arguments — do not let the essay simply stop.

Evidence and Sources

You use several statistics (one in four women experiencing assault, average family size by income, cost of raising a child) and a legal case (which should be "*Roe v. Wade*," not "Rowe versus Way" — please double-check proper nouns and legal terminology). None of these claims are cited. In academic writing, especially on a topic this contentious, every statistic and factual claim needs a source. Without citations, a reader can easily dismiss your evidence as unsupported opinion. Please add footnotes or in-text citations (check which citation style your instructor requires) for every statistic and factual assertion.

Logical Reasoning

Some of your arguments would benefit from more careful reasoning:
The rape/trauma paragraph is emotionally powerful, but the rhetorical questions ("Would be able to love him or her?") feel manipulative rather than persuasive in an academic essay. Consider replacing at least one rhetorical question with a factual or expert claim.
The "slippery slope" argument in the government-interference paragraph (if government removes this right, other rights will follow) is a recognized logical fallacy unless you can support it with historical evidence. If you want to keep this point, cite a specific historical example.
The paragraph on illegal abortions (coat hangers, underground clinics) is a strong, evidence-based argument — consider moving this earlier, as it is one of your most persuasive points, and expanding it with historical data (eg, mortality rates before *Roe v. Wade*).

Language and Grammar

Since English articles ("a," "the") often differ from German usage, watch for these specific errors:
"being force to give birth" → "being forced to give birth"
"This traumatic event takes months or years" — check subject-verb agreement throughout; some sentences shift between singular and plural unexpectedly.
Typos to fix: "eightteen" → "eighteen," "sky racket alsop" → "skyrocket also," "Rowe versus Way" → "*Roe v. Wade*."
"if the remove this right" → "if they remove this right"
Watch comma splices, eg, "Women deserve the right to have an abortion. they won this right..." — this should be one sentence or properly separated with a period and capital letter.

I recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, or using a grammar-checking tool, to catch these smaller errors before submission.

Tone

A few phrases (eg, implying poor families should have fewer children through abortion access) could be read as insensitive or classist even if unintended. Rephrase these sections to focus on *choice and autonomy* rather than population control, which will make your argument more ethically consistent and persuasive.

Next Steps

Rewrite the introduction with a clear preview of your three strongest arguments.
Add topic sentences and transitions between paragraphs.
Cite all statistics and the legal case correctly.
Write a conclusion.
Proofread carefully for grammar and word choice.

You have good instincts for argumentation — with revision, this essay can be much stronger. Well done on tackling a difficult topic.
